### [1] [Uncertainty-Aware Consistency Distillation for Few-Step Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39132)
*Lingyu Liu,Yaxiong Wang,Li Zhu,Zhedong Zheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Uncertainty-Aware Consistency Distillation (UACD) for few-step video generation, which reweights consistency supervision at each spatiotemporal region using a local uncertainty estimate to handle regions with rapid temporal variation (e.g., moving shadows, flowing water) that are harder to learn and produce larger perceptual errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-step video generators require tens of sampling steps, incurring substantial latency and compute. While consistency distillation can distill these into few-step students, the teacher-guided targets are not equally trustworthy—regions with rapid temporal variation are harder to learn and produce larger perceptual errors. The key insight is that supervision reliability follows local difficulty of content rather than semantic complexity.

Method: UACD constructs two independently perturbed teacher-guided consistency paths, whose student endpoint predictions provide a consensus target. The discrepancy between the student's direct prediction and this target serves as an uncertainty proxy. An exponential weight relaxes the consistency penalty on high-uncertainty regions while keeping full penalty elsewhere. Feature-space adversarial training with semantic alignment preserves perceptual quality. The method uses parameter-efficient LoRA adaptation of the 50-step Wan model.

Result: The method achieves state-of-the-art 4-step generation on VBench 2.0 with a mean score of 0.556 and is preferred over competing methods in user studies.

Conclusion: UACD effectively handles the challenge of distilling multi-step video generators into few steps by dynamically adjusting supervision based on local uncertainty estimates, enabling aggressive step reduction while preserving perceptual quality.

Abstract: We study few-step video generation, i.e., distilling a multi-step video generator, which typically requires tens of sampling steps, incurring substantial latency and compute, into a few-step student. Consistency distillation is a common recipe, in which a multi-step teacher provides the consistency targets for a few-step student. However, these teacher-guided targets are not equally trustworthy, and the content is harder to learn where it varies rapidly over time, e.g., moving foliage shadows or flowing water. We observe that supervision reliability follows the local difficulty of the content rather than semantic complexity: regions that change little yield consistent endpoint predictions, whereas regions with large temporal variation produce larger discrepancies that coincide with the largest perceptual errors. Motivated by this observation, we propose Uncertainty-Aware Consistency Distillation (UACD), which reweights consistency supervision at each spatiotemporal region using a local, parameter-free uncertainty estimate. Specifically, we construct two independently perturbed teacher-guided consistency paths, whose student endpoint predictions provide a consensus target; the discrepancy between the student's direct prediction and this target is the uncertainty proxy. We then relax the consistency penalty on high-uncertainty regions through an exponential weight, while keeping the full penalty elsewhere, since the student cannot be expected to match targets that are hard to learn. To preserve perceptual quality under aggressive step reduction, we integrate feature-space adversarial training with semantic alignment. With parameter-efficient LoRA adaptation of the 50-step Wan model, our method achieves state-of-the-art 4-step generation on VBench 2.0 (0.556 mean score) and is preferred over competing methods in a user study.

</details>


### [2] [Evaluating Multi-Task Morphological Concept Learning for Pulmonary Nodule Malignancy Assessment in 3D CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38271)
*Namitha Narayanan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study investigates whether learning radiologist-annotated morphological features (spiculation, lobulation) alongside malignancy risk from 3D CT volumes improves pulmonary nodule classification. Using the LIDC-IDRI dataset (3,918 annotations from 742 patients), a multi-task 3D CNN was compared to a single-task model. The multi-task model showed no significant improvement over single-task classification (ROC-AUC difference: 0.005, 95% CI: -0.087 to 0.090).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Morphological features like spiculation and lobulation are important for assessing pulmonary nodule malignancy risk on CT scans. The study aimed to explore whether learning these radiologist-annotated features jointly with malignancy risk could improve classification performance, leveraging the potential correlation between morphological characteristics and malignancy.

Method: The study used the LIDC-IDRI dataset with 3,918 reader-level nodule annotations from 742 patients (after excluding indeterminate malignancy ratings). Patient-level splitting was used for train/validation/test sets, with 112 patients (628 annotations) in the held-out test set. A single-task 3D CNN predicting malignancy risk was compared against a multi-task 3D CNN predicting malignancy risk, spiculation, and lobulation simultaneously.

Result: The single-task model achieved balanced accuracy of 0.548 and ROC-AUC of 0.552. The multi-task model achieved balanced accuracy of 0.539 and ROC-AUC of 0.558. Bootstrap analysis showed ROC-AUC difference of 0.005 (95% CI: -0.087 to 0.090) and balanced-accuracy difference of -0.009 (95% CI: -0.067 to 0.043). The auxiliary tasks (spiculation, lobulation) were highly imbalanced and showed limited predictive performance.

Conclusion: Including morphological features (spiculation, lobulation) as auxiliary tasks did not clearly improve malignancy-risk classification. The study highlights the importance of considering class balance, label formulation, and reader-level annotation structure when designing multi-task learning approaches for pulmonary CT analysis.

Abstract: Morphological characteristics such as spiculation and lobulation play an important role in assessing pulmonary nodules on computed tomography (CT), particularly in relation to malignancy risk. This study examines whether learning radiologist-annotated morphological features together with malignancy risk from lesion-centred 3D CT volumes improves classification performance. The Lung Image Database Consortium and Image Database Resource Initiative (LIDC-IDRI) dataset was used, comprising 3,918 reader-level nodule annotations from 742 patients after excluding indeterminate malignancy ratings. Patient-level splitting was used for training, validation, and testing, with 112 patients and 628 reader annotations in the held-out test set. A single-task 3D convolutional neural network was compared with a multi-task model predicting malignancy risk, spiculation, and lobulation. The single-task model achieved a balanced accuracy of 0.548 and receiver operating characteristic area under the curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.552, while the multi-task model achieved 0.539 and 0.558, respectively. Patient-level bootstrap analysis showed an ROC-AUC difference of 0.005 (95% confidence interval (CI): -0.087 to 0.090) and a balanced-accuracy difference of -0.009 (95% CI: -0.067 to 0.043). The auxiliary tasks were strongly imbalanced and showed limited predictive performance. Overall, including morphological features did not clearly improve malignancy-risk classification, showing the importance of class balance, label formulation, and reader-level annotation structure in multi-task pulmonary CT analysis.

</details>


### [3] [Masked Swingers: Harnessing Data Augmentation to Advance Autoencoders for Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38278)
*Anthony Fuller,Scott C. Lowe,Daniel G. Kyrollos,Graham W. Taylor,Evan Shelhamer,James R. Green*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Masked Swingers is a self-supervised learning method that improves upon Masked Autoencoders (MAE) by augmenting images in two ways, masking and encoding each view separately, then exchanging global representations (CLS tokens) between views before decoding the masked patches. This encourages learning view-agnostic image summaries for efficient transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve masked autoencoders for better representation learning and transfer performance across various downstream tasks, particularly fine-grained recognition and instance retrieval tasks where MAE underperforms.

Method: The method augments an image in two different ways, then masks and encodes each view separately. Before decoding the masked patches, it exchanges the global representations (CLS tokens) between the two views. This design encourages learning a view-agnostic summary of the image.

Result: Masked Swingers outperforms MAE by +3-5% on ImageNet-1K kNN. It provides large gains on fine-grained tasks: +45% relative gain on instance retrieval, +22% on animal re-ID, and +76% on Omniglot character recognition. Additionally, it reduces error by -64% relative to MAE on three new state-probing datasets.

Conclusion: Masked Swingers significantly outperforms MAE across various tasks, with particularly strong improvements on fine-grained recognition tasks. The method also shows promising results for world modeling applications as evidenced by performance on state-probing datasets.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) removes the need for annotations and makes models that are capable across more domains than supervised learning. The autoencoder SSL framework learns by reconstructing its own input after information loss through a bottleneck or noise injection. Masked autoencoders (MAE) are the most successful instantiation of this framework: they encode a random subset of patches, then decode the masked-out patches. In this work, we introduce key modifications to improve MAEs. Our method augments an image in two different ways, then masks and encodes each view separately. It then exchanges the global representations (CLS tokens) between views before decoding the masked patches. By design, our Masked Swingers encourages learning a view-agnostic summary of the image to facilitate efficient transfer. We perform extensive experiments, and find Masked Swingers outperforms MAE by +3-5% on ImageNet-1K kNN and provides large gains on fine-grained tasks, e.g., relative gains of +45% on instance retrieval, +22% on animal re-ID, and +76% on Omniglot character recognition. To boot, Swingers reduces error -64% relative to MAE on three new state-probing datasets, opening the door to world modeling. Welcome to our Swingers party.

</details>


### [4] [Strike a Chord! Modal Kinetic Typography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38325)
*Maham Tanveer,Jiyeon Han,Nanxuan Zhao,Hao Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A method for animating vector typography to express semantic concepts while maintaining legibility, using modal analysis (finite-element eigenmodes) combined with a frozen video diffusion model to drive smooth, looping animations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on kinetic typography has two key weaknesses: (1) Free-form point optimization under video score distillation (SDS) causes glyph tearing and jitter due to independent noisy gradients per point/frame; (2) Structured alternatives rely on category-specific priors (skeletons/keypoints). The authors seek a method that produces smooth, articulated motion without tearing and without requiring category-specific priors.

Method: 1. Build motion from glyph's natural vibration modes via finite-element eigenproblem on vector outline
2. Use softest modes for whole letters and individual parts to allow bending
3. Apply zero-energy solutions (rigid translations/rotations) in closed form for part movement
4. Use frozen video diffusion model to supervise only the modes' amplitudes and phases
5. Use language model to generate a list of each letter's moving parts once for the entire alphabet

Result: Produces more articulated and smoother motion than Dynamic Typography and AniClipart with comparable or better concept alignment, less glyph tearing than Dynamic Typography, and is preferred by human raters. Also preferred over Astra (GPT-6) in user studies. The method works even in a frozen-shape setting where motion alone must carry the concept.

Conclusion: Modal kinetic typography successfully disentangles shape and motion by construction - a base outline is sculpted toward the concept, and the modal drive cannot alter it. This allows letters to be animated without reshaping. The approach eliminates tearing and jitter issues while avoiding category-specific priors, achieving superior human preference ratings.

Abstract: We introduce modal kinetic typography, which animates a vector glyph to express a semantic concept while keeping it legible. Our key idea is to build motion from the glyph's natural vibration modes. Specifically, a finite-element eigenproblem assembled from the vector outline yields the glyph's softest modes, for the whole letter and for each of its parts, allowing it to bend. The problem's zero-energy solutions, i.e., rigid translations and rotations, are applied in closed form to each part, allowing parts to also move as blocks. To animate the glyph, a frozen video diffusion model supervises only the modes' amplitudes and phases. Our modal approach addresses two weaknesses of prior work. Free-form point optimization under video score distillation (SDS) moves each point and frame independently along noisy gradients, tearing the outline and causing jitter. In contrast, our modes are smooth along the outline and driven by a few whole-cycle harmonics, which restricts these gradients to smooth, seamlessly looping motion. On the other hand, structured alternatives rely on skeletons or keypoints from category-specific priors, whereas our modes come from the glyph itself; the only prior is a list naming each letter's moving parts, generated once for the whole alphabet by a language model. In modal kinetic typography, shape and motion are disentangled by construction: a single base outline is sculpted toward the concept, and the modal drive cannot alter it, so a letter can also be animated without being reshaped. Our method produces more articulated and smoother motion than Dynamic Typography and AniClipart at comparable or better concept alignment, with less glyph tearing than Dynamic Typography, and is preferred by human raters, including in a frozen-shape setting where motion alone must carry the concept. Our results were also preferred over Astra (GPT-6) by human raters.

</details>


### [5] [ShieldCLIP: Selective Safety Alignment for Harmful Content Mitigation in Multimodal Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39688)
*Tobia Poppi,Silvia Cappelletti,Samuele Poppi,Marcella Cornia,Lorenzo Baraldi,Diego Garcia-Olano,Rita Cucchiara*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ShieldCLIP introduces modality-specific safety alignment for CLIP encoders using a novel four-way conditional objective and a 195k-quadruplet dataset (ViSUv2), achieving consistent harmful output reduction across multiple downstream tasks while preserving benign content representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety alignment datasets label all generated samples as unsafe, even when one modality is individually safe. This overly broad labeling unnecessarily modifies benign representations. The core challenge is that ethical constraints prevent collecting real unsafe content at scale, necessitating synthetic datasets with precise safety labeling.

Method: ShieldCLIP uses a four-way conditional objective: (1) anchoring safe content, (2) redirecting unsafe modalities to safe counterparts, (3) updating only unsafe branch for mixed pairs, and (4) enforcing coherence when both modalities are unsafe. The method conditions alignment on observed safety state of each modality rather than sample origin.

Result: ShieldCLIP consistently reduces harmful outputs over prior safety-aligned encoders and strong mitigation baselines across cross-modal retrieval, text-to-image generation (Stable Diffusion v1.4, SDXL), and image-to-text generation (LLaVA), while preserving the utility of the original embedding space. Ablation studies confirm both modality-specific supervision and selective alignment objectives contribute to performance gains.

Conclusion: ShieldCLIP effectively reduces harmful outputs across multiple downstream tasks while preserving embedding space utility. Both modality-specific supervision and selective alignment objectives contribute to these gains. The approach represents a significant advancement in safety alignment for multimodal encoders.

Abstract: Multimodal encoders such as CLIP underlie many downstream systems, but their web-scale training data embed harmful associations that safety alignment must suppress without unnecessarily changing benign representations. Because ethical and practical constraints prevent collecting real unsafe content at scale, existing datasets pair safe real samples with generated counterparts, but label every generated sample unsafe, even when one modality is individually safe. To address this, we introduce ShieldCLIP, the first framework to condition safety alignment on the observed safety state of each modality rather than the origin of a sample, preserving safe content while redirecting only what is unsafe. We also introduce ViSUv2, a 195k-quadruplet dataset with independent per-modality safety labels across 578 concepts and 28 categories. Using these labels, ShieldCLIP defines a four-way conditional objective beyond pair-level supervision: safe content is anchored, unsafe modalities are redirected to their safe counterparts, mixed pairs update only the unsafe branch, and coherence is enforced when both are unsafe. We evaluate ShieldCLIP on cross-modal retrieval, text-to-image generation with Stable Diffusion v1.4 and SDXL, and image-to-text generation with LLaVA. Across these settings, ShieldCLIP consistently reduces harmful outputs over prior safety-aligned encoders and strong mitigation baselines, while preserving the utility of the original embedding space. Extensive ablation studies further show that both modality-specific supervision and the selective alignment objective contribute to these gains. Source code, trained models, and ViSUv2 (under a controlled-access protocol) will be made publicly available at https://aimagelab.github.io/ShieldCLIP/.

</details>


### [6] [GaugeVLM: Structuring Spatial Supervision with Measured Geometric Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38285)
*Hongbo Wang,Zihan Lin,Wenkui Yang,Shiran Ge,Yuang Ai,Jie Cao,Huaibo Huang,Ran He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GaugeVLM addresses VLM failures in spatial reasoning across different views by introducing controlled 3D scene interventions and a new training objective (GaugeDPO) that converts measured errors into preference margins, achieving state-of-the-art results on spatial benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models can contradict themselves when viewing the same spatial relation from different perspectives and fail to respond when that relation changes. Existing supervision methods (individual answers or ordinal preferences) don't capture error magnitude and geometric dependencies across observations.

Method: 1) GaugeVLM uses controlled object and camera interventions in explicit 3D scenes to produce linked observations with measured differences between spatial relations and shared truths across views. 2) GaugeDPO converts measured errors into preference margins, directly supervises correct canonical rankings across views, and links intervention-induced answer-odds contrasts to measured relation changes with view-specific scales.

Result: GaugeVLM improves all 10 established spatial metrics over supervised fine-tuning across three VLM backbones. The main 7B model gains 15.0 percentage points on MSMU distance and 18.9 percentage points on QSpatial+.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates robust generalization across domains, with gains extending to autonomous driving and embodied reasoning tasks, validating the effectiveness of cross-view and intervention-based supervision for spatial reasoning.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can contradict themselves across views of the same spatial relation and fail to respond when that relation changes. Addressing these failures requires supervision that captures error magnitude and geometric dependencies across observations, both of which remain implicit in training on individual answers or ordinal preferences. Therefore, we introduce GaugeVLM, which makes this structure explicit through controlled object and camera interventions in explicit 3D scenes, producing linked observations with measured differences between spatial relations and shared truths across views. To translate this structure into learning signals, its core objective, GaugeDPO, converts measured errors into preference margins, directly supervises correct canonical rankings across views, and links intervention-induced answer-odds contrasts to measured relation changes with view-specific scales. Our analysis bounds canonical prediction error and establishes that the cross-view and intervention constraints can be jointly satisfied. Empirically, GaugeVLM improves all 10 established spatial metrics over supervised fine-tuning across three VLM backbones, with the main 7B model gaining 15.0 and 18.9 percentage points on MSMU distance and QSpatial+, respectively. These gains also extend to autonomous driving and embodied reasoning, demonstrating the robust generalization across domains.

</details>


### [7] [Lens Flare Removal and Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39527)
*Tarun Yenamandra,Jonathon Luiten,Daniel Cremers,Nathan Matsuda*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses lens flare removal in images and proposes a novel method for 3D scene decomposition into lens flares and the actual scene. The authors compile a dataset for large-flare removal, fine-tune a diffusion-based model for flare removal, and introduce a flare representation model that leverages symmetry about the camera's principal point. This model is jointly optimized with Gaussian splatting (3DGS) to enable flare/scene decomposition, allowing reconstructed flares to be edited and transferred to novel views.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lens flares degrade image quality and negatively impact downstream applications like 3D scene reconstruction, as they are artifacts of the camera system rather than the actual scene. While existing methods handle small flares near light sources, they struggle with large flares that fill the entire image. Additionally, lens flares are valuable artistic tools but lack consistent representation across multiple views.

Method: 1) Compile a novel dataset combining real-world data with procedural generation for large-flare removal; 2) Fine-tune a diffusion-based model on this dataset for removing complex, large lens flares; 3) Introduce a flare representation model exploiting symmetry about the camera's principal point; 4) Jointly optimize the flare model with Gaussian splatting (3DGS) for 3D scene decomposition; 5) Use the flare-removal model to decompose the scene into flares and actual content.

Result: The method enables effective removal of large lens flares on established benchmarks and a new benchmark for large reflective flares. It successfully decomposes 3D scenes into lens flares and the underlying scene, with the reconstructed flare being explicit and re-renderable for editing and transfer to novel images and new 3D scenes. The pipeline demonstrates robustness to errors in automatic light-source localization.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully addresses large lens flare removal and enables consistent flare representation across multiple views. By combining diffusion-based removal with a symmetry-based flare representation and 3DGS optimization, the method achieves robust flare/scene decomposition that supports editing and transfer capabilities, while being resilient to localization errors.

Abstract: The presence of lens flares in images can significantly reduce the quality of downstream application results for tasks such as 3D scene reconstruction. This is because lens flares are a property of the camera imaging system, and not a part of the underlying scene being modeled. There are previous methods that tackle the removal of small flares focused around a light source. However, existing methods struggle with large flares, such as those that fill the entire image. In this work, we compile a novel dataset for large-flare removal, combining publicly available real-world data with a procedural generation pipeline. We fine-tune a diffusion-based model on our dataset to remove complex, large lens flares. On the other hand, lens flares remain effective artistic tools, widely used in the media. While there are ways to simulate 2D flares, representing and reconstructing lens flares consistently across multiple views has not yet been explored. To achieve this, we introduce a flare representation model that leverages the symmetry of lens flares about the camera's principal point. We propose a computational pipeline to jointly optimize this flare model and a Gaussian splatting model (3DGS). This enables the decomposition of a 3D scene into lens flares and the scene itself, using our flare-removal model. Because the reconstructed flare is explicit and re-renderable, it can be edited and transferred to novel images and new 3D scenes. We evaluate removal on an established benchmark and a new one for large reflective flares, quantify the flare/scene decomposition directly, and show that the pipeline is robust to errors in automatic light-source localization.

</details>


### [8] [WARP: A Unified Benchmark for Invisible Image Watermarking -- Robustness and Protection Against Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40031)
*Khaled Abud,Aleksey Yakushev,Aleksandr Akimenkov,Irina Serzhenko,Kirill Aistov,Egor Kovalev,Dmitry Obydenkov,Sergey Lavrushkin,Anastasia Antsiferova,Dmitriy Vatolin,Yury Markin,Kirill Lukianov*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WARP is a unified benchmark framework for evaluating the robustness of invisible digital image watermarks, incorporating 32 watermarking methods and 34 attack techniques to address the need for updated benchmarking practices in the era of AI-generated content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing need to mark AI-generated content and ensure traceable sources to prevent manipulation or misuse, coupled with recent advances in invisible watermarking methods that require updated benchmarking practices.

Method: WARP provides standardized, reproducible, and scalable protocols for evaluating perceptual quality, watermark readability, and attack resilience. It incorporates 32 recent classical, deep, and generative watermarking methods, along with 34 different erasing techniques ranging from traditional distortions to adversarial, purification, and re-embedding attacks.

Result: The study collected the largest robustness benchmark in the field, identifying the most robust approaches under both distortion and adversarial conditions, and revealing consistent relationships between watermarking methods and effective attack strategies. Key finding: some watermarking methods are highly vulnerable to re-embedding attacks despite being robust to standard distortions.

Conclusion: The benchmark successfully identifies robust watermarking approaches and exposes critical vulnerabilities, particularly to re-embedding attacks, providing a comprehensive evaluation framework for the watermarking community.

Abstract: Digital image watermarking is increasingly critical in media contexts, as emerging regulations and industry practices require marking AI-generated content and ensuring traceable sources to prevent manipulation or misuse. Recent advances in invisible watermarking methods highlight the need to update existing benchmarking practices to reflect current techniques and evaluation criteria.
  We address this by introducing WARP -- a unified framework and benchmark for evaluating the robustness of invisible watermarks. WARP incorporates 32 recent classical, deep, and generative watermarking methods, as well as 34 different erasing techniques, ranging from traditional distortions to more sophisticated adversarial, purification, and re-embedding attacks. It provides standardized, reproducible, and easily scalable protocols for evaluating perceptual quality, watermark readability, and attack resilience.
  Using WARP, we extensively evaluate current invisible watermarking techniques, collecting the largest robustness benchmark in the field. Results identify the most robust approaches under both distortion and adversarial conditions, and reveal consistent relationships between watermarking methods and the attack strategies most effective against them. Our experiments also highlight that some of the watermarking methods considered are highly vulnerable to reembedding, even if they are robust to standard distortions. The code is made available at https://github.com/ispras/wibe.

</details>


### [9] [It Takes Little to Rewrite Perception: Targeted Semantic Substitution in Vision-Language Models at $ε\leq 4/255$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38298)
*Binchi Zhang,Atrisha Sarkar,Apurva Narayan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that Vision Language Models (VLMs) are more vulnerable to adversarial attacks than previously thought. While existing representation-alignment attacks show limited success at small perturbation budgets (ε ≤ 4/255), the authors propose a new targeted semantic substitution attack that achieves significant success within the same range. By aligning source image tokens with target image tokens in the VLM's post-merger token space under white-box conditions, the attack can make VLMs perceive target semantics at ε = 2/255 and achieve complete semantic replacement in 38% of images at ε = 4/255. The attack is even more effective on video, reaching 35.9% complete replacement at ε = 1/255. A notable phenomenon called "semantic fusion" is also observed, where the LLM component reconciles contradictory visual signals into a coherent narrative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research suggested VLMs are relatively robust to small adversarial perturbations (ε ≤ 4/255) when using representation-alignment attacks. However, this perceived robustness may be misleading. The motivation is to demonstrate that VLMs are actually vulnerable to semantic manipulation within the same perturbation range, challenging the assumption of their robustness and highlighting a critical security concern for safety-critical deployments.

Method: The proposed attack aligns each stream of the source image with its counterpart in the target image within the victim VLM's post-merger token space. This operates under a white-box threat model where the attacker has full knowledge of the model's architecture and parameters. The evaluation uses a strict success criterion requiring the model to simultaneously: (1) name the target object, (2) confirm its presence, and (3) deny the source object.

Result: The attack achieves significant success: target semantics appear at ε = 2/255, complete replacement reaches 38% at ε = 4/255 on images, and 35.9% at ε = 1/255 on video. The "semantic fusion" phenomenon is observed, where the LLM rationalizes contradictory visual signals into a coherent narrative.

Conclusion: VLMs are more vulnerable to adversarial attacks than previously believed. The targeted semantic substitution attack succeeds within small perturbation budgets (ε ≤ 4/255) that were thought to be safe. This vulnerability poses significant risks for safety-critical deployments of VLMs. The discovered "semantic fusion" phenomenon further demonstrates the complexity of how VLMs process conflicting visual information.

Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) are widely deployed in safety-critical scenarios, and understanding to which extent they can be controlled by adversarial perturbation is a prerequisite for evaluating their trustworthiness. Existing representation-alignment attacks, which make a VLM perceive a target image, achieve limited success at $\varepsilon \leq 4/255$. Therefore, VLMs seems robust to perturbations in this range. We show that this robustness does not hold, as targeted semantic substitution succeeds within the same range. Specifically, we align each stream of the source image with its counterpart in the target image in the victim VLM's post-merger token space, operating under a white-box threat model. We evaluate under a strict success criterion, requiring the model to simultaneously name the target, confirm its presence, and deny the source. In images, target semantics appear at $\varepsilon = 2/255$ and complete replacement reaches 38\% at $\varepsilon = 4/255$. On video, complete replacement reaches 35.9\% at $\varepsilon = 1/255$. We also observe a phenomenon of \textit{semantic fusion}, where Large Language Model (LLM) rationalizes contradictory visual signals into a coherent narrative.

</details>


### [10] [MatLoom: Layered Text-to-Material Generation in a Compact Program Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40322)
*Anson Y. Lam,Shuqing Li,Michael R. Lyu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MatLoom is a layer-oriented programming language for text-to-material generation that produces executable programs (not just images) representing material construction. It uses pretrained language models without task-specific fine-tuning, incorporating parser-guided repair, preview-based critique, and noise seed search. It outperforms diffusion baselines on prompt-alignment metrics and human preference tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-material generation produces only visual outputs without retaining the construction rules. This limits editability, debugging, and understanding of the generation process. The authors propose that materials should be represented as compact, editable programs that explicitly define dependencies between patterns, color, and relief.

Method: MatLoom uses a layer-oriented language where programs compose alpha-masked layers with shared spatial expressions defining coverage and PBR channels. The pipeline includes: (1) parser-guided repair to fix syntax errors, (2) preview-based critique for revision, and (3) noise seed search while keeping source fixed. No task-specific fine-tuning is required - uses pretrained language models directly.

Result: On 141 prompts with 6 backbones: best configuration outperforms 3 diffusion baselines on all 4 flat-layout prompt-alignment metrics. Initial programs already exceed all baselines on mean BLIPScore. Median retained program length is 21 lines. In blind human evaluation (30 participants, 20 prompts), MatLoom renders receive 59.2% of choices vs 19.3% for the best baseline.

Conclusion: Generating materials as compact executable programs is viable and superior to diffusion-only approaches for prompt alignment. The program representation retains construction logic, enabling subsequent authoring while achieving higher human preference than state-of-the-art diffusion baselines.

Abstract: Material generation should produce not only an appearance, but also the rules that construct it. We introduce MatLoom, a compact, layer-oriented language for text-to-material generation with pretrained language models. Each program composes alpha-masked layers whose shared spatial expressions define coverage and physically based rendering (PBR) channels, making dependencies between patterns, color, and relief explicit. A standalone interpreter evaluates the program into material maps, while the source retains named fields and layer parameters for subsequent authoring. Without task-specific fine-tuning, our pipeline uses parser-guided repair and preview-based critique to revise material designs, then searches noise seeds while keeping each candidate's remaining source fixed. On a curated benchmark of 141 prompts evaluated with six backbones, our best-performing configuration achieves higher mean scores than three diffusion baselines on all four flat-layout prompt-alignment metrics. Its initial programs already exceed all three baselines on mean BLIPScore, before critique or seed search. Retained programs have a median length of 21 lines when pooled across backbones. In a blind four-way comparison involving 30 participants and 20 prompts, our renders receive 59.2% of choices, compared with 19.3% for the most-preferred baseline. Compact executable programs thus offer a way to generate prompt-aligned materials while retaining their construction as part of the asset.

</details>


### [11] [ExploreNet: Learning Where to Explore in Diffusion GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38329)
*Shuyue Stella Li,Xiaochuang Han,Yulia Tsvetkov,Luke Zettlemoyer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EXPLORENET introduces adaptive exploration for group-relative RL in image generation, learning to predict optimal noise scales for each latent element rather than using uniform isotropic Gaussian noise. This approach improves Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium by 14% on GenEval2 and achieves 67.2% human preference win-rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current group-relative RL methods (like Flow-GRPO) use isotropic Gaussian noise added uniformly at every denoising step, treating all latent elements equally. However, latent elements differ in how much they affect the generated image, so uniform exploration is suboptimal.

Method: EXPLORENET is a policy network that predicts a noise scale for every latent element from the current latent, denoising step, and prompt—before any reward is observed. It is trained on the reward spread of each rollout group and discarded after training, leaving inference unchanged.

Result: On Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium: 14% improvement on held-out GenEval2 over Flow-GRPO; transfers to two independent compositional benchmarks and five preference/image-quality models; achieves 67.2% human preference win-rate.

Conclusion: Exploration is learnable in group-relative diffusion RL; the shape of the exploration distribution outweighs its magnitude; and rollout quality is more effective than rollout quantity.

Abstract: Group-relative RL methods such as Flow-GRPO post-train image generators by exploring with isotropic Gaussian noise added at every denoising step. This noise decides which rollouts the model learns from, yet it perturbs every channel and spatial position of the latent equally. In this paper, we instead show that latent elements differ in how much they change the generated image, so exploration should adapt to these differences. We introduce EXPLORENET to learn an adaptive exploration distribution. EXPLORENET is a policy that predicts a noise scale for every latent element from the current latent, the denoising step, and the prompt, before any reward is observed; it is trained on the reward spread of each rollout group and discarded after training, leaving inference unchanged. On Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium, EXPLORENET improves held-out GenEval2 by 14% over Flow-GRPO, transfers to two independent compositional benchmarks and five preference and image-quality models, and reaches a 67.2% human preference win-rate. Overall, across our group-relative diffusion RL experiments, we find that exploration is learnable, the shape of the exploration distribution outweighs its magnitude, and rollout quality is more effective than rollout quantity.

</details>


### [12] [Learning Semantic Inpainting for Animatable Gaussian Head Avatars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38343)
*Pilseo Park,Fizza Rubab,Yiying Tong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SInGA is a novel method for creating animatable 3D head avatars from a single image by using semantic inpainting in UV space to complete unobserved facial regions, with stacked Gaussians for enhanced detail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing avatar approaches require multi-view observations and lack effective handling of unobserved regions in single-view settings, limiting their applicability. The goal is to create a method that can generate high-quality, animatable head avatars from a single image while preserving identity and handling unseen views.

Method: The method proposes a semantic inpainting framework defined in UV space for completing unobserved facial regions. It leverages the structured topology of UV representation which provides consistent spatial correspondences. Features are extracted from observed regions and used to complete unobserved regions using facial symmetry cues. The completed representation regresses Gaussian attributes (Gaussian inpainting). Multiple Gaussians are stacked at each surface/pixel location to enhance detail.

Result: The method generates high-quality head avatars with improved completeness and identity preservation, supports realistic animation, and enables consistent rendering from unobserved views. It generalizes across identities without requiring per-identity optimization.

Conclusion: SInGA successfully addresses the challenge of creating animatable 3D head avatars from single images by introducing semantic inpainting in UV space. The approach achieves better completeness, identity preservation, and view consistency compared to existing methods, with the ability to generalize across different identities.

Abstract: We present SInGA, a novel method for learning Semantic Inpainting for animatable Gaussian head Avatars from a single image. Existing avatar approaches often rely on multi-view observations and lack effective handling of unobserved regions in single-view settings, limiting their applicability in such scenarios. To address this, we propose a semantic inpainting framework defined in UV space for completing unobserved facial regions. Our key insight lies in the structured topology of the UV representation, which provides consistent spatial correspondences and enables reliable completion of identity-specific features using the inherent symmetry cues of human faces. We extract features from observed regions and use them to complete unobserved regions. The completed representation is then used to regress Gaussian attributes, effectively performing Gaussian inpainting. In addition, instead of relying on a single Gaussian at each surface or pixel location, we stack multiple Gaussians to enhance detail. The resulting avatar generalizes across identities without requiring per-identity optimization and can be animated with driving inputs. Experimental results show that our method generates high-quality head avatars with improved completeness and identity preservation, while supporting realistic animation and consistent rendering from unobserved views.

</details>


### [13] [TrackFish3D: Self-Supervised 3D Tracking of Schooling Fish from Multi-view Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38347)
*Patt Phurtivilai,Zhiyang Dou,Yifan Wu,Kinfung Chu,Yuan Liu,Lei Yang,Wenping Wang,Taku Komura*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TrackFish3D is a geometry-driven self-supervised framework for dense multi-camera 3D tracking of schooling fish that uses multi-view geometry as supervision without requiring identity annotations, appearance features, or test-time optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantifying collective fish behavior requires accurate trajectories, yet multi-view 3D tracking remains challenging due to frequent occlusions, visually similar individuals, and the long-standing scarcity of identity annotations.

Method: TrackFish3D uses triangulation and reprojection consistency from calibrated multi-view geometry as pseudo-associations for supervision. It employs a geometric encoder and global association transformer for all-to-all cross-view correspondence within each frame. A self-supervised contrastive objective separates co-visible individuals in embedding space, while a temporal predictor preserves identities and bridges short occlusions across frames.

Result: On the benchmark, TrackFish3D improves 3D Multi-Object Tracking Accuracy from 87.7% to 95.8%. On the 3D-ZeF zebrafish benchmark, it achieves 81.1% MOTA versus 77.4% for the best geometric baseline. It also generalizes to real-world bird tracking.

Conclusion: TrackFish3D can be trained once on unlabeled footage and applied directly to unseen test videos without requiring cross-view identity labels, temporal annotations, 3D ground truth, appearance features, or test-time optimization, demonstrating strong generalization beyond fish to other species.

Abstract: Quantifying collective fish behavior requires accurate trajectories, yet multi-view 3D tracking remains challenging due to frequent occlusions, visually similar individuals, and the long-standing scarcity of identity annotations. We present TrackFish3D, a geometry-driven self-supervised framework for dense multi-camera 3D tracking of schooling fish. Instead of relying on appearance-based re-identification or manually annotated identities, TrackFish3D turns calibrated multi-view geometry into supervision: triangulation and reprojection consistency provide pseudo-associations, while a geometric encoder and global association transformer learn all-to-all cross-view correspondence within each frame. To make these associations identity-aware, TrackFish3D introduces a self-supervised contrastive objective that separates co-visible individuals in the embedding space, together with a temporal predictor that preserves identities and bridges short occlusions across frames. The resulting model is trained once on unlabeled footage and applied directly to unseen test videos, requiring no cross-view identity labels, temporal annotations, 3D ground truth, appearance features, or test-time optimization. On our benchmark, TrackFish3D improves 3D Multi-Object Tracking Accuracy from 87.7% for the strongest baseline to 95.8%. On the 3D-ZeF zebrafish benchmark, it achieves 81.1% MOTA, compared with 77.4% for the best geometric baseline. TrackFish3D also generalizes beyond fish, achieving strong results on real-world bird tracking.

</details>


### [14] [Inductive Visual Logic for Few-Shot Out-Of-Distribution Adaptation in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38362)
*Hung-Jen Chen,Yu-Heng Ho,Ting-Yao Huang,Po-Hsiang Hsu,Li-Yu Chen,Chun-Yi Lee,Min Sun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses distant out-of-distribution (OOD) classification in vision-language models (VLMs) where standard models fail due to lack of discriminative features. The authors propose Inductive Visual Logic (IVL), a training-free framework that exploits the asymmetry between VLMs' descriptive and discriminative abilities by extracting visual traits from few-shot support images via dual-mode prompting and organizing them into per-class trait dictionaries for hierarchical filtering at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard VLMs like Qwen-VL and LLaVA achieve strong zero-shot performance on tasks within their pretraining distribution but fail on specialized domains where required discriminative features were never learned. This "distant OOD" regime cannot be addressed by standard adaptation methods because they operate within the encoder's existing feature space. The key insight is that VLMs retain descriptive capacity even when discrimination fails - they can articulate visual patterns but cannot classify them.

Method: IVL constructs classification knowledge from the model's surviving descriptive ability through: (1) Dual-mode prompting combining semantic descriptions with primitive visual observations to extract visual traits from few-shot support images; (2) Organizing extracted traits into per-class trait dictionaries; (3) Hierarchical filtering at inference to identify spatially grounded trait evidence for classification.

Result: IVL achieves the highest aggregate accuracy across multiple distant-OOD benchmarks under two VLM backbones while producing interpretable, trait-traceable predictions.

Conclusion: The proposed IVL framework effectively addresses distant OOD classification by leveraging the asymmetry between descriptive and discriminative abilities in VLMs. This training-free approach provides a practical solution for deploying VLMs in specialized domains without requiring additional training or fine-tuning.

Abstract: Generative vision-language models (VLMs) such as Qwen-VL and LLaVA achieve strong zero-shot performance on tasks overlapping with their pretraining distribution, yet fail on specialized domains where the required discriminative features were never learned, a regime we term distant out-of-distribution (OOD). Standard adaptation methods cannot overcome this representational absence because they operate within the encoder's existing feature space. However, VLMs retain a robust descriptive capacity even when discrimination collapses: a model that cannot classify a medical scan can still articulate its visual patterns. Exploiting this asymmetry, we introduce Inductive Visual Logic (IVL), a training-free framework that constructs classification knowledge from the model's surviving descriptive ability. IVL extracts visual traits from few-shot support images through dual-mode prompting, combining semantic descriptions with primitive visual observations, and organizes them into per-class trait dictionaries. At inference, hierarchical filtering identifies spatially grounded trait evidence for classification. Across multiple distant-OOD benchmarks, IVL achieves the highest aggregate accuracy under two VLM backbones while producing interpretable, trait-traceable predictions.

</details>


### [15] [Composition, Not Conversation: VLMs Lose the Scene, Not the Thread](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38368)
*L. D. M. S. Sai Teja,Ufaq Khan,N. Siva Gopala Krishna,Satyajit Tourani,Ashshak Sharifdeen,Fida Mohammad Thoker,Bernard Ghanem,Muhammad Haris Khan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Layered-VQA, a benchmark that tests how Vision-Language Models (VLMs) perform when visual evidence is fragmented into layers rather than presented as complete images. The study evaluates 13 models across 187,200 conversations and identifies three consistent failure modes: scene fragmentation significantly degrades performance (composition loss), oracle-selected evidence can paradoxically perform worse than complete images (oracle inversion), and grounding ability degrades faster than answer accuracy as more evidence is required. The key insight is that presenting evidence in the right way matters as much as having the right evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VQA benchmarks present complete images with questions all at once, but real-world VLM applications often involve fragmented visual evidence (cropped, segmented, retrieved, or revealed over time). This gap between benchmark conditions and practical use motivates understanding what models lose when visual information is fragmented rather than presented as a complete scene.

Method: The authors created Layered-VQA with 93 scenes and 300 questions. Each image is decomposed into ordered RGBA layers that exactly recompose the original scene. Questions are annotated with supporting layers (needed to answer), minimal-sufficient layers (oracle evidence), and distractor layers. They evaluated 11 open-weight VLMs (3B to 32B parameters) and 2 proprietary models using 187,200 conversations, graded by 1.74M open-model cross-judgments.

Result: Three consistent failure patterns were identified: (1) Loss in Composition - fragmenting the scene substantially reduces accuracy (more than fragmenting the question), but recomposing the same layers largely restores performance; (2) Oracle Inversion - even oracle-selected sufficient evidence can perform worse than the complete scene; (3) Loss in Grounding - as more evidence is required, grounding degrades much faster than answer accuracy.

Conclusion: Having the right visual evidence is not sufficient for VLMs. How that evidence is composed and presented determines whether models can effectively use and ground it. The study concludes that "the right evidence is not enough: VLMs need the scene it came from" - suggesting that presenting evidence in context of the complete scene is crucial for VLM performance.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly reason over visual evidence that is cropped, segmented, retrieved, or revealed over time. Yet most VQA benchmarks present the complete image and question at once. We ask what models lose when the same information is fragmented. We introduce Layered-VQA, with 93 scenes and 300 questions. Each image is decomposed into ordered RGBA layers that exactly recompose the original scene, and each question is annotated with supporting, minimal-sufficient, and distractor layers. We evaluate eleven open-weight VLMs from 3B to 32B parameters and two proprietary models with a scale of 187,200 conversations, graded by 1.74M open-model cross-judgments. We find three consistent failures. Loss in Composition: fragmenting the question has a small effect, but fragmenting the scene substantially reduces accuracy; recomposing the same layers largely restores performance. Oracle Inversion: even oracle-selected sufficient evidence can perform worse than the complete scene. Loss in Grounding: as more evidence is required, grounding degrades much faster than answer accuracy. Together, these results show that having the right visual evidence is not enough. How that evidence is composed and presented determines whether models can use and ground it. The right evidence is not enough: VLMs need the scene it came from.

</details>


### [16] [PhyProbe: Rethinking Physical Consistency Evaluation in Generated Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38377)
*Max Ku,Jiaojiao Fan,Zekun Hao,Francesco Ferroni,Heng Wang,Wenhu Chen,Ming-Yu Liu,Prithvijit Chattopadhyay*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PhyProbe is a physical consistency evaluator for generated videos that uses a frozen pretrained spatio-temporal encoder with a lightweight scoring head, trained via a unified objective combining pairwise ranking, regression on noisy annotations, and anchor-based calibration. It outperforms prior methods on pairwise benchmarks and achieves strong correlation with human judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating physical consistency in generated videos is challenging because existing approaches rely on vision-language models that miss physical dynamics, or fine-tuned evaluators that overfit to specific datasets. A key problem is that existing supervision provides either relative ordering or absolute scores, but not both reliably.

Method: PhyProbe extracts features from a frozen pretrained spatio-temporal encoder and maps them to a scalar physical consistency violation score via a lightweight scoring head. Training uses a unified objective combining: (1) pairwise ranking, (2) regression on noisy scalar annotations, and (3) anchor-based calibration over heterogeneous supervision sources.

Result: PhyProbe outperforms prior methods on most pairwise benchmarks (real-generated and generated-generated pairs) with largest gains in no-correspondence and generated-generated settings. It achieves strong correlation with human judgments, with close agreement between rank-based and linear metrics. Scores are well-ordered and anchored to [0,1] scale. Despite training only on physics-related supervision, it also performs competitively on human preference benchmarks.

Conclusion: PhyProbe successfully provides both relative ordering and absolute scoring for physical consistency evaluation. The approach demonstrates that physical consistency violations are entangled with broader quality degradations, enabling cross-domain generalization. The method addresses the key limitation of existing supervision sources by combining multiple training signals in a unified framework.

Abstract: Evaluating the physical consistency of generated videos remains a fundamental challenge. Existing approaches rely on off-the-shelf vision-language models, which can often be myopic to physical dynamics, or fine-tuned evaluators trained on human annotations, which overfit to dataset-specific cues and fail to generalize. A key challenge is that existing supervision sources provide either relative ordering or absolute scores, but not both reliably and consistently across varied settings. To this end, we introduce PhyProbe, an evaluator that extracts features from a frozen pretrained spatio-temporal encoder and maps them to a scalar physical consistency violation score via a lightweight scoring head. PhyProbe is trained through a unified objective combining pairwise ranking, regression on noisy scalar annotations, and anchor-based calibration over a curated set of heterogeneous supervision sources. Experiments show that PhyProbe outperforms prior methods on most pairwise benchmarks spanning real-generated and generated-generated pairs under varying correspondence, with the largest gains in no-correspondence and generated-generated settings where existing fine-tuned evaluators degrade sharply. PhyProbe achieves strong correlation with human judgments, with close agreement between rank-based and linear metrics, indicating that scores are both well ordered and anchored to a stable [0, 1] scale. Further, despite being trained on supervision indicative of physical consistency, without explicit general-preference labels, PhyProbe also performs competitively on human preference benchmarks: consistent with the observation that physics violations are entangled with broader quality degradations.

</details>


### [17] [Team MSU GenText-Forensics Challenge 2026 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38391)
*Kirill Koltsov,Aleksandr Gushchin,Dmitriy Vatolin,Anastasia Antsiferova*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A decomposed chain-of-thought pipeline for document text forgery detection that combines a tampering detector with two LoRA-adapted Qwen3-VL-32B models (Filterer and Semantic Detective) to localize manipulations, identify attack types, and generate forensic reports. Secured 3rd place in ACM MM 2026 GenText-Forensics challenge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Document forgery has evolved beyond pixel-level manipulation to include semantic alterations targeting OCR & LLM pipelines, requiring systems that can not only detect forgeries but also localize manipulation points, identify attack types, and produce human-readable forensic reports.

Method: Three-component pipeline: (1) Document Tampering Detector (DTD) produces probability maps converted to numbered candidate regions; (2) First Qwen3-VL-32B model (Filterer) validates regions and assigns preliminary forgery type; (3) Second Qwen3-VL-32B model (Semantic Detective) merges/re-grounds regions, detects semantic anomalies invisible to pixel-level detectors, and writes final report. Both models are LoRA-adapted and trained via distillation from a privileged Qwen3-VL-235B teacher with ground-truth masks and reports.

Result: Third place in ACM MM 2026 GenText-Forensics challenge. The paper details data preparation, test-time augmentation, region rendering, distillation protocol, training configuration, and reports ablations over detector thresholds, prompt designs, and pipeline decompositions.

Conclusion: The decomposed CoT approach with specialized vision-language models achieves strong performance in multilingual document forgery detection by combining pixel-level tampering detection with semantic anomaly identification, demonstrating the effectiveness of task decomposition and model distillation for complex forensic tasks.

Abstract: Document text forgery has evolved beyond simple pixel-level manipulation: modern attacks alter not only the appearance of a document but also its meaning, and increasingly target the OCR & LLM pipelines that consume such documents. The ACM MM 2026 GenText-Forensics challenge therefore requires systems that not only decide whether a multilingual text image is forged, but also localize the point of manipulation, identify the attack type, and produce a human-readable forensic report with supporting evidence. We present our solution, a decomposed chain-of-thought (CoT) pipeline that combines a document tampering detector (DTD) with two Qwen3-VL-32B vision-language models, each LoRA-adapted to a distinct sub-task. DTD produces tampering probability maps that are converted into numbered candidate regions; a first model (the Filterer) validates these regions and assigns a preliminary forgery type, while a second model (the Semantic Detective) merges and re-grounds the surviving regions, searches for purely semantic anomalies that are invisible to pixel-level detectors, and writes the final report. Both models are trained by distilling chain-of-thought traces from a privileged Qwen3-VL-235B teacher that has access to ground-truth masks and reports. Our approach secured third place in the ACM MM 2026 GenText-Forensics challenge. We describe the data preparation, test-time augmentation, region rendering, distillation protocol, and training configuration in detail, and report ablations over detector thresholds, prompt designs, and pipeline decompositions.

</details>


### [18] [VidHarness: Evolving Agent Harnesses for Cost-Efficient Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38413)
*Susan Liang,Jianmin Wu,Daxiang Dong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VidHarness automates the design of video agent harnesses (programs wrapped around VLMs) for cost-efficient long video understanding. It uses MCTS-based evolutionary search with uncertainty-aware multi-fidelity validation to find optimal harness designs, and includes a mixture-of-harness mechanism for budget-adaptive routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Processing every frame of hour-long videos is prohibitively expensive, yet evidence for questions usually spans only a few seconds. Existing video agents (VLMs wrapped with harnesses) are hand-crafted by experts through slow build-and-test cycles, and there is no automated framework for harness design.

Method: 1) MCTS-based evolutionary search to escape greedy refinement local optima
2) Uncertainty-aware multi-fidelity validation to reduce evaluation cost by screening new harnesses on few questions and promoting only promising ones
3) Mixture-of-harness mechanism that routes each question to a budget-specialized harness

Result: Sets new state-of-the-art on LongVideoBench, Video-MME, and Video-Holles. Outperforms strongest hand-crafted video agent by up to 11.2 points. Generalizes to knowledge-intensive benchmarks (Video-MMMU, MMVU) with fewer than half the frames of uniform sampling.

Conclusion: VidHarness successfully automates harness design for VLM-based video agents, achieving superior performance with significantly reduced computational cost compared to both hand-crafted agents and uniform sampling baselines.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can answer questions about hour-long videos, but processing every frame is prohibitively expensive, even though the evidence for a question usually spans only a few seconds. Video agents, i.e., harness programs wrapped around a frozen VLM, address this by observing the video selectively, yet existing harnesses are hand-crafted by experts through slow build-and-test cycles. We propose VidHarness, a framework that automates harness design for cost-efficient long video understanding, in which a harness proposer iteratively evolves harnesses based on execution feedback from an evolution environment. To escape the local optima of greedy refinement, we organize the evolution as Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS), and to reduce the evaluation cost, we integrate uncertainty-aware multi-fidelity validation, which screens new harnesses on a few questions and promotes only the promising ones. Since the best harness varies with the frame budget, we further introduce a mixture-of-harness that routes each question to a harness specialized for its budget. VidHarness sets new state-of-the-art results on LongVideoBench, Video-MME, and Video-Holmes, outperforms the strongest hand-crafted video agent by up to $11.2$ points, and generalizes to the knowledge-intensive benchmarks Video-MMMU and MMVU with fewer than half of the frames of uniform sampling.

</details>


### [19] [LoopVL: Recurrent Visual Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38426)
*Zhe Qian,Ziyang Gong,Zhongxing Xu,Hehan Li,Zhonghua Wang,Fei Luo,Mingxuan Wang,Xue Yang,Shiwei liu,Yanbiao Ma,Junchi Yan,Jungong Han*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LoopVL introduces recurrent vision-language modeling by extending Loop Transformers to create a unified vision-language state updated through shared modules via Module-Loop and Model-Loop computation. It outperforms non-recurrent models of similar and larger sizes on multimodal benchmarks and exhibits "Visual Aha Moments" - significant shifts in visual attention across iteration loops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses whether Loop Transformers can be effectively extended to vision-language models, exploring recurrent mechanisms in multimodal learning rather than relying solely on non-recurrent (e.g., transformer) architectures.

Method: LoopVL combines Module-Loop and Model-Loop computation to iteratively update a unified vision-language state through shared modules. Training proceeds in three stages: language pre-training, multimodal training, and post-training.

Result: LoopVL outperforms a range of similarly sized and larger non-recurrent models on multimodal understanding and visual reasoning benchmarks. The model exhibits "Visual Aha Moments" characterized by pronounced shifts in visual attention across loops.

Conclusion: LoopVL provides practical evidence for recurrent vision-language modeling and demonstrates how shared parameters can support deeper multimodal computation over continuously evolving visual-language states.

Abstract: We introduce LoopVL to study whether Loop Transformers can be effectively extended to vision- language models. LoopVL combines Module-Loop and Model-Loop computation to iteratively update a unified vision-language state through shared modules. We train LoopVL from scratch through language pre-training, multimodal training, and post-training. LoopVL outperforms a range of similarly sized and larger non-recurrent models on multimodal understanding and visual reasoning benchmarks. We also observe Visual Aha Moments in LoopVL, characterized by pronounced shifts in visual attention across loops. LoopVL provides practical evidence for recurrent vision-language modeling and offers an intuitive perspective on how shared parameters can support deeper multimodal computation over continuously evolving visual-language states.

</details>


### [20] [MOBA-VL: Event-Localized Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning for Real-Time MOBA Commentary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38428)
*Shengyun Zhong,Xinkang Zhao,Ziyuan Chu,Linchao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MOBA-VL is a 9B-parameter vision-language model that generates real-time esports commentary for MOBA games by using game telemetry as supervision signal with event-localized multi-turn reinforcement learning, achieving 63.25 Overall score on MOBACast-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming VLMs for esports commentary sound natural but frequently miss critical events like kills and objectives, reducing the quality and accuracy of live match narration.

Method: The approach uses game telemetry (which records exact timing of events) as a supervision signal, combined with event-localized multi-turn reinforcement learning that rewards commentary turns describing each specific event. Training data includes MOBACast: 860 professional matches (~460 hours) across three MOBA games with word-level timestamped commentary.

Result: MOBA-VL achieves the highest Overall score on MOBACast-Bench: 63.25 on full matches (vs. 55.12 for StreamingVLM) and 63.45 on clips (vs. 56.22 for DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash). Event-localized reinforcement learning improves event recall from 34.5 to 42.1 compared to supervised fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The telemetry-supervised approach with event-localized credit assignment significantly improves event coverage in real-time esports commentary while maintaining natural language fluency. The method addresses the key limitation of missing events while preserving the natural sound of existing VLMs.

Abstract: Real-time commentary for Multiplayer Online Battle Arena (MOBA) esports requires a vision-language model (VLM) to narrate a live match second by second, both fluently and accurately. Existing streaming VLMs sound natural but often miss key events such as kills and objectives. To address this limitation, we use game telemetry, which records exactly when each event occurs, as a supervision signal. We introduce MOBA-VL, a 9B-parameter model trained on this signal with event-localized multi-turn reinforcement learning, which rewards the turns that describe each event. We also collect MOBACast, 860 professional matches (about 460 hours) across three MOBA games with word-level timestamped commentary, and MOBACast-Bench, a benchmark from held-out tournaments. On MOBACast-Bench, MOBA-VL achieves the highest Overall score on full matches (63.25 vs. 55.12 for StreamingVLM) and clips (63.45 vs. 56.22 for DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash). Event-localized credit also raises event recall from 34.5 to 42.1 over supervised fine-tuning. Code and data will be released, and demos are available on an anonymous project page at https://moba-vl.github.io.

</details>


### [21] [Audible World Models: Spatially Aware Sound Generation for 3D Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38444)
*Duowen Chen,Jinjin He,Gouthaman KV,Sandeep Bangalore Venkatesh,Bo Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Audible World Models is a training-free framework that incorporates spatial audio into text-to-3D generated environments by constructing panoramic 3D proxies, separating semantic layers, synthesizing dry audio for sound sources, and rendering listener-dependent spatial audio using geometric acoustic propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text- and image-conditioned world generators create visually rich 3D environments but produce audio that lacks explicit spatial representation - they don't model where sound sources are located or how perceived sound varies with listener movement. Current audio generation lacks spatial awareness and motion-dependent behavior.

Method: The framework consists of: (1) Constructing a panoramic 3D proxy from text prompt; (2) Separating the scene into semantic layers; (3) Identifying sound-producing foreground objects and ambient background regions; (4) Synthesizing dry audio for each sound label; (5) Anchoring sources to reconstructed geometry; (6) Rendering listener-dependent spatial audio using geometric acoustic propagation.

Result: Experiments across 80 generated scenes show substantial gains in spatial consistency over text-, video-, and panorama-conditioned baselines while preserving competitive semantic alignment. VLM-based assessments and human evaluations indicate preferred soundtracks for audio-visual consistency, spatial plausibility, and motion-dependent behavior.

Conclusion: The framework successfully maintains persistent source locations while adapting rendered audio to changes in listener viewpoint and motion by explicitly linking semantics, geometry, and sound propagation. It represents a significant advance in creating immersive, spatially-aware audio-visual 3D environments.

Abstract: Text- and image-conditioned world generators can create visually rich 3D environments, yet these worlds often remain silent or rely on soundtracks synthesized solely from text or rendered video. Although such audio can convey what should be heard, it lacks an explicit representation of where sound sources are located and how their perceived sound should vary with listener movement. We introduce Audible World Models, a training-free framework that incorporates sound into the generated world state. Starting from a text prompt, our system constructs a panoramic 3D proxy, separates it into semantic layers, identifies sound-producing foreground objects and ambient background regions, and synthesizes dry audio for each sound label. It then anchors these sources to reconstructed geometry and renders listener-dependent spatial audio using geometric acoustic propagation. By explicitly linking semantics, geometry, and sound propagation, the framework maintains persistent source locations while adapting the rendered audio to changes in listener viewpoint and motion. Experiments across 80 generated scenes demonstrate substantial gains in spatial consistency over text-, video-, and panorama-conditioned baselines, while preserving competitive semantic alignment. VLM-based assessments and human evaluations further indicate that our soundtracks are preferred for their audio-visual consistency, spatial plausibility, and motion-dependent behavior.

</details>


### [22] [PAMI: Part Anchored Motion for Text to Human-Object Interaction Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38466)
*Chuqiao Li,Xianghui Xie,Yong Cao,Andreas Geiger,Gerard Pons-Moll*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PAMI introduces a Part-Anchored Motion framework for text-conditioned full-body human-object interaction generation. It uses body-part anchors to vote for object motion, creating a structured latent space for coarse generation (PamiGen) and fine-grained contact refinement (PamiRefiner), achieving 14.5% higher contact recall than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-conditioned HOI generation requires synthesizing coordinated human motion and object trajectories. Existing methods represent human and object separately and predict global couplings implicitly, leading to issues like object drift, missed contact, and penetration. The authors sought a more explicit way to model the dynamically changing human-object relationship.

Method: Inspired by the Hough Transform, PAMI localizes object motion via body-part anchors that vote for it. Object motion is expressed relative to multiple body-part anchors. PamiVAE learns an interaction latent space decoding frame-wise weights to aggregate part-specific votes. Generation follows a coarse-to-fine hierarchy: PamiGen generates coarse human-object interaction from text in the structured latent space, then PamiRefiner recursively resolves fine-grained contact geometry using a hybrid surface-sensing representation combining long-range probes (overall body-part influence) and short-range sensors (detailed contacts near object surface).

Result: On InterAct dataset, PAMI generates more faithful interactions and more accurate human-relative object motion than previous methods, achieving 14.5% higher contact recall than the previous state of the art.

Conclusion: The part-anchored voting representation and hybrid surface-sensing refinement both contribute significantly to the improved performance, validating the proposed approach for addressing coordination challenges in HOI generation.

Abstract: Text-conditioned full-body human-object interaction (HOI) generation requires synthesizing human motion and object trajectories that match the input text while remaining precisely coordinated over time. Most methods represent the human and object as separate trajectories and predict the global human-object couplings. Learning this complex, dynamically changing relationship implicitly, however, often yields object drift, missed contact, and penetration. We introduce PAMI, a Part-Anchored Motion framework for Interaction generation. Inspired by the classic Hough Transform, our key idea is to localize object motion by letting body-part anchors vote for it: we express object motion relative to multiple body-part anchors and use PamiVAE to learn an interaction latent space, decoding frame-wise weights that aggregate these part-specific votes. Building on this representation, PAMI generates interactions in a coarse-to-fine hierarchy. PamiGen first generates a coarse human-object interaction from text in this structured latent space, and PamiRefiner then recursively resolves fine-grained contact geometry using a hybrid surface-sensing representation, combining long-range probes that capture overall body-part influence with short-range sensors that resolve detailed contacts near the object surface. Experiments on InterAct show that PAMI generates more faithful interactions and more accurate human-relative object motion than previous methods, achieving 14.5% higher contact recall than the previous state of the art. Extensive ablations validate the contributions of both the part-anchored voting representation and hybrid surface-sensing refinement.

</details>


### [23] [OP-CAD: On-Policy Clean-Audio Distillation for Robust Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39150)
*Xingming Shui,Dapeng Chen,Bowei Liu,Jingqi Tian,Minfu Li,Kun Yi,Jiapeng Hong,Yansong Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OP-CAD is a curriculum-based privileged self-distillation framework that improves robustness of omni-modal large language models in audio-visual question answering under environmental noise and competing speech. It uses selective token-level supervision that emphasizes positions sensitive to acoustic interference, comparing teacher predictions under clean, corrupted, and visual-only contexts to allocate supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omni-modal large language models deployed in real-world environments encounter external noise that interferes with their perception and understanding of multimodal inputs. Current on-policy distillation methods use uniform token weighting that does not explicitly prioritize positions affected by acoustic interference, limiting their robustness in audio-visual understanding under noisy conditions.

Method: OP-CAD employs a curriculum-based training approach progressing from mild to severe environmental noise and competing speech. At each stage, selective token-level supervision is applied where the student generates responses from corrupted audio-visual input while a frozen teacher uses clean audio and verified answers to supervise the same response prefixes. Supervision is allocated by comparing teacher predictions under clean, corrupted, and visual-only contexts without revealing the answer. A bounded weighting rule emphasizes positions identified by either signal while retaining supervision throughout the response.

Result: OP-CAD outperforms compared methods across all evaluated noise conditions. Paired analyses show improved preservation of clean-correct answers under strong interference, with no observed aggregate clean-accuracy penalty.

Conclusion: Directing clean-teacher supervision toward acoustically sensitive predictions is effective for robust audio-visual reasoning. The method successfully resists acoustic interference while preserving useful audio evidence without compromising clean audio performance.
