Abstract: Omni-modal large language models deployed in real-world environments encounter external noise that can interfere with their perception and understanding of multimodal inputs. We study their robustness in audio-visual understanding, focusing on question answering under environmental noise and competing speech. The challenge is to resist acoustic interference while preserving useful audio evidence. On-policy distillation provides dense teacher feedback on student-generated responses, but uniform token weighting does not explicitly prioritize positions affected by acoustic interference. We introduce OP-CAD (On-Policy Clean-Audio Distillation), a curriculum-based privileged self-distillation framework for robust audio-visual understanding. Training progresses from mild to severe environmental noise and competing speech, with selective token-level supervision at each stage. The student generates responses from corrupted audio-visual input, while a frozen teacher uses clean audio and the verified answer to supervise the same response prefixes. To allocate this supervision, OP-CAD compares teacher predictions under clean, corrupted, and visual-only contexts without revealing the answer. These matched comparisons measure sensitivity to audio removal and corruption; a bounded weighting rule emphasizes positions identified by either signal while retaining supervision throughout the response. OP-CAD outperforms the compared methods across all evaluated noise conditions. Paired analyses further show improved preservation of clean-correct answers under strong interference, with no observed aggregate clean-accuracy penalty. These results demonstrate the value of directing clean-teacher supervision toward acoustically sensitive predictions for robust audio-visual reasoning.

</details>


### [24] [Curating Synthetic Data for Task-Specific Visual Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38476)
*Saptarshi Neil Sinha,Paul Julius Kühn,Michael Weinmann*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper argues for curated synthetic data (deliberately designed scene content, appearance variations, sensing characteristics, and annotations) over general-purpose synthetic data for specialized vision systems. It examines three curation paradigms—procedural rendering, physics-based simulation, and generative AI—with applications in industrial defect detection, autochrome plate restoration, and 6DoF pose estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose datasets cannot provide domain-specific priors, and manual annotation is expensive, imprecise, or infeasible. The central question should be "which data to generate" not "how to generate more data."

Method: The paper examines three complementary curation paradigms: (1) Procedural rendering offers explicit control over scene parameters with annotations following by construction; (2) Physically-based simulation encodes mechanisms behind observed effects, yielding exactly aligned supervision pairs; (3) Generative AI learns sensor-specific appearance from small real seed sets to attain plausible realism, though prone to hallucination and inaccurate annotation. The authors analyze different data regimes and training strategies combining synthetic and real data across these paradigms.

Result: Curated synthetic data is best understood as a complement to real observations. Hybrid pipelines combining controllable supervision with learned appearance represent the most promising direction for reliable sim-to-real transfer.

Conclusion: For specialized vision systems, the focus should shift from generating more data to generating the right data through curation. The three paradigms (procedural rendering, physics-based simulation, generative AI) offer complementary strengths: procedural rendering provides explicit control, physics-based simulation ensures mechanism-aligned supervision, and generative AI achieves plausible realism. Hybrid pipelines that combine controllable supervision with learned appearance are the most effective approach for reliable sim-to-real transfer.

Abstract: Synthetic data are most valuable where general-purpose datasets cannot provide the domain-specific priors a task requires, and where manual annotation is expensive, imprecise, or infeasible. In this article we argue that the central question for specialized vision systems is not how to generate more data, but which data to generate. We therefore discuss curated synthetic data, whose scene content, appearance variations, sensing characteristics, and annotations are deliberately designed around a given task. We examine three complementary curation paradigms. Procedural rendering offers explicit control over scene parameters and the annotations follow by construction. Physically-based simulation encodes the mechanism behind an observed effect and yields exactly aligned supervision pairs. Generative AI learns sensor-specific appearance from small real seed sets and attains plausible realism, though it remains prone to hallucination and to inaccurate annotation. These paradigms are illustrated with examples from industrial surface defect detection, restoration of degraded digitized autochrome plates, and 6DoF pose estimation from RGB and event data. Using these examples, we analyze different data regimes and training strategies that combine synthetic and real data across these paradigms. We conclude that curated synthetic data are best understood as a complement to real observations, and that hybrid pipelines combining controllable supervision with learned appearance are the most promising direction for reliable sim-to-real transfer.

</details>


### [25] [Caption-Mediated Perceived-Safety Estimation for Pedestrian Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38479)
*Simon Parkinson,Paloma Liu,Wei Zheng,Mohammadreza Sheikhfathollahi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents an explainable pedestrian routing system that estimates perceived safety from street-level imagery using vision-language model captions as an intermediate representation. The approach was deployed across 654,115 images in Manchester and Huddersfield, UK, and validated through field testing with 3,669 ratings from 494 images. The caption-mediated approach matched direct image embeddings, and while benchmark improvements did not translate to field gains, the system showed meaningful routing behavior changes for journeys of 3-6 km.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pedestrian routing typically optimizes for distance or time, ignoring perceived safety which is crucial for vulnerable road users. Existing approaches using image embeddings lack transparency. This paper aims to create an explainable system where safety scores are derived from interpretable text captions rather than opaque embeddings.

Method: The approach uses vision-language models to generate captions for street-level imagery, then derives perceived-risk classes from structured caption features. Nine captioning conditions across five model families were benchmarked against a direct CLIP image-embedding baseline. The system was deployed over 654,115 images covering 36 electoral wards in Manchester and Huddersfield. Validation used 3,669 independent field ratings across 494 images from 70 participant sessions.

Result: Caption-mediated representation achieved parity with direct image embeddings (not worse). Field validation showed r=0.262 against a noise ceiling of 0.737. A confirmatory test found that a pipeline 44% stronger on supervised benchmarks did not improve field results (r=0.250, p=0.84), indicating benchmark gains did not predict deployment gains. Routing behavior showed negligible change below 1 km, but median increase of 12.78% in low-risk route length with 2.73% median detour for 3-6 km journeys.

Conclusion: The explainable caption-based approach is viable for pedestrian safety routing, achieving comparable performance to direct image embeddings while maintaining interpretability. However, supervised benchmark improvements failed to transfer to real-world deployment, highlighting a significant generalization gap. The system meaningfully influences routing for medium-length journeys (3-6 km) while having minimal impact on short trips.

Abstract: This paper presents an explainable approach to pedestrian routing, in which perceived safety is estimated from street-level imagery through an explicit natural-language intermediate representation. A vision--language model caption is generated and stored before any scoring is undertaken, and the perceived-risk class is derived entirely from structured features of that stored text, so that every segment score remains inspectable by the user. Nine captioning conditions across five model families are benchmarked against a direct Contrastive Language--Image Pre-training (CLIP) image-embedding baseline under an identical downstream pipeline, and the caption-mediated representation is found to reach parity with the image embedding rather than to trail it. The approach was deployed over 654,115 images covering 36 electoral wards in two locations in Northern England (Manchester and Huddersfield). Independent field validation against 3,669 locally collected ratings of 494 images across 70 participant sessions established agreement that is statistically significant but modest, at $r=0.262$, against a measured noise ceiling of 0.737 imposed by disagreement between raters. A single-use confirmatory test then found that a pipeline 44\% stronger on the supervised benchmark did not produce measurable improvement in the field ($r=0.250$, $p=0.84$), so the benchmark gains did not predict the deployment gains in this case. Routing behaviour varies systematically with journey length. There is negligible change below 1\,km, reaching a median increase of 12.78\% in low-risk route length for a median detour of 2.73\% on journeys of 3 to 6 km.

</details>


### [26] [Beyond Layers: Position-Resolved Gradient Conflict and Position-Aware Modulation for Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38485)
*Shuyang Jiang,Fucheng Deng,Yuchuan Luo,Zhenyu Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates interference between image understanding and autoregressive image generation in Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs). The key insight is that gradient conflict depends on token position within the sequence, not just layer depth. The authors introduce a position-resolved interference map and propose position-aware modulation (PAM) to selectively remove anti-aligned generation gradients at high-conflict positions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods diagnose and remedy understanding-generation interference at the layer/expert level, but this misses an important axis: the position of visual tokens in the raster sequence. Generation gradients from different positions interfere differently with understanding, which layer-based analysis cannot capture.

Method: The authors develop a position-resolved interference map that attributes gradient conflict to visual-token positions within each layer, computed from a single backward pass at 1.2× standard cost. They then propose Position-Aware Modulation (PAM), which removes anti-aligned components of generation gradients only at high-conflict positions without architectural changes.

Result: Position explains significant conflict variance (partial η²=0.31 on Show-o, 0.15 on Janus-Pro). The first quarter of sequences shows mean gradient cosine of -0.18 against understanding vs -0.02 for the last quarter. After normalization, 80% of effect size remains. Conflict correlates with semantic content (Spearman ρ=0.64). PAM improves +21 MME and +2.4 GenEval points over layer-wise separation while matching on POPE and FID. A random-position control recovers 31% of the gain.

Conclusion: Token position is a crucial, previously overlooked dimension of understanding-generation interference in UMMs. Position-based and layer-based separation are complementary and can be combined. PAM provides a simple, effective solution under matched parameter budgets.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) train image understanding and autoregressive image generation on shared parameters, and the two objectives are known to interfere. Existing diagnoses and remedies operate at the resolution of layers or experts, measuring conflict per layer and resolving it by separating parameters. We argue that this resolution hides an orthogonal axis. Generation in a UMM is next-token prediction over a raster sequence of visual tokens whose roles vary systematically with position, so how strongly a generation gradient interferes with understanding should depend on where in the sequence it originates. We introduce a position-resolved interference map that attributes understanding-generation gradient conflict to visual-token positions within every layer, computed from a single backward pass at $1.2\times$ the cost of a standard backward pass. On Show-o and Janus-Pro, position explains a large share of conflict variance after controlling for depth (partial $η^2=0.31$ vs. $0.35$ for layer on Show-o; $0.15$ vs. $0.30$ on Janus-Pro): the first quarter of the sequence has a mean gradient cosine of $-0.18$ against understanding, the last quarter $-0.02$. The dependence survives per-position gradient-norm normalization, retaining $80%$ of its effect size, and conflict strength tracks semantic content (Spearman $ρ=0.64$). Building on the map, we propose position-aware modulation (PAM), which removes the anti-aligned component of generation gradients only at high-conflict positions without changing the architecture. Under a matched trainable-parameter budget, PAM improves over layer-wise separation by $+21$ MME and $+2.4$ GenEval points on Show-o while matching it on POPE and overall FID; a random-position control recovers about $31%$ of the gain. Position-based and layer-based separation are complementary degrees of freedom and can be combined.

</details>


### [27] [Multidimensional Observer Model and Perceptual Dimensions of Human Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38487)
*Sheng Zhao,Weikai Lin,Yuhao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multi-dimensional observer model to understand the perceptual space underlying human image quality judgment. The model represents images as distributions in a latent perceptual space and models human judgment as comparing noisy samples, constrained by primate ventral stream neural representations and fitted to large-scale behavioral data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding human image quality perception is ecologically relevant to everyday tasks and foundational for machine vision tasks like image generation. However, the inherent perceptual space underlying quality judgment remains poorly understood.

Method: The researchers developed a multi-dimensional observer model representing images as distributions in a latent perceptual space. Human judgment was modeled as comparing noisy samples. The model was constrained by neural representations in the primate ventral stream and fitted to large-scale behavioral data.

Result: Perceptual spaces needed to account for human image quality judgment are extremely low-dimensional compared to the image space, even when considering sparsity. The structure (dimensionalities, information encoded) varies between low-level and high-level quality judgments.

Conclusion: Despite shared retinal encoding at the beginning of visual processing, humans selectively construct task-dependent perceptual spaces in visual decision making. This suggests a divergence in how the brain processes quality judgments at different levels.

Abstract: Judging image quality is not only ecologically relevant to everyday human tasks, but also underpins many machine vision tasks such as image generation. This paper proposes a framework to understand the inherent perceptual space underlying image quality judgment in humans. We propose a multi-dimensional observer model that represents images as distributions in a latent perceptual space and that models human judgment as comparing noisy samples. Being constrained by neural representations in the primate ventral stream and fit to large-scale behavioral data, the model enables analysis of perceptual structure while matching the predictive power of existing metrics. Using this model, we find that the perceptual spaces needed to account for image quality judgment in humans are extremely low-dimensional compared to the image space even when considering its sparsity. The exact structure of the space (e.g., dimensionalities, information encoded) varies between low-level and high-level quality judgments, suggesting that, despite a shared retinal encoding in the beginning, humans selectively construct task-dependent perceptual spaces in visual decision making.

</details>


### [28] [GazeFlow: From Human Gaze Behavior to Generative Egocentric Gaze Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38519)
*Sheng Zhao,Weikai Lin,Yuhao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GazeFlow introduces a novel framework for egocentric gaze prediction by modeling gaze as a joint distribution using conditional flow matching (CFM). The method uses a learned velocity field to transform Gaussian noise into plausible gaze trajectories, conditioned on both bottom-up visual features and top-down task information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Egocentric gaze prediction enables many downstream applications but remains challenging due to the inherent stochasticity of human gaze. However, this stochasticity is constrained by structured temporal dynamics (fixations and saccades), top-down task influences, and bottom-up visual saliency. Existing methods may not adequately capture these constraints.

Method: GazeFlow uses conditional flow matching (CFM) to model gaze as a joint distribution of temporal gaze positions. A learned velocity field iteratively transports a Gaussian noise sample into a plausible gaze trajectory. The velocity field is conditioned on: (1) bottom-up visual features extracted by a video encoder, and (2) top-down task information obtained by globally querying these features.

Result: GazeFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance on per-frame metrics on standard datasets. Additionally, the generated trajectories align better with human gaze temporal dynamics (fixations and saccades).

Conclusion: The conditional flow matching approach effectively captures both the stochastic nature and the structured temporal dynamics of human gaze. By conditioning on both bottom-up and top-down information, GazeFlow produces more realistic gaze predictions that better match human gaze behavior.

Abstract: Egocentric gaze prediction enables many downstream applications but remains challenging, as human gaze is inherently stochastic. This stochasticity is constrained by structured temporal dynamics alternating between fixations and saccades, top-down influences from tasks, and bottom-up visual saliency. Based on this observation, we introduce GazeFlow, a framework that directly models gaze as a joint distribution of temporal gaze positions conditioned upon both top-down and bottom-up information. In particular, GazeFlow uses conditional flow matching (CFM): a learned velocity field iteratively transports a Gaussian noise sample into a plausible gaze trajectory drawn from this joint distribution. The velocity field is conditioned on bottom-up visual features extracted by a video encoder and on top-down task information obtained by globally querying these features. On standard datasets, GazeFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance on per-frame metrics, and the generated trajectories align better with human gaze temporal dynamics.

</details>


### [29] [ThinkV2V: Unleashing the Reasoning Capability of MLLMs for Instruction-Guided Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38541)
*Donghao Zhou,Haoyang He,Fan Zhang,Hao Yang,Guisheng Liu,Xin Gao,Zhongwei Wan,Xingyuan Bu,Jie Wang,Qiangpeng Yang,Shilei Wen,Chi-Wing Fu,Pheng-Ann Heng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ThinkV2V is a reasoning-driven video editing framework that activates multimodal LLM thinking before visual generation. It uses an MLLM-to-DiT architecture to convert explicit reasoning over source videos and instructions into refined conditioning signals for video editing, combined with Progressive Curriculum Training and Inference-Time Thinking Scaling techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instruction-guided video editing methods use MLLMs primarily as semantic encoders, falling short in handling implicit edits requiring causal or semantic reasoning. This represents a fundamental gap in video editing capabilities.

Method: ThinkV2V employs an MLLM-to-DiT architecture that turns explicit reasoning over source video and instruction into refined conditioning signals for video editing. It uses Progressive Curriculum Training (gradually cultivates model from basic editing to reasoning-intensive cases) and Inference-Time Thinking Scaling (iteratively refines candidate prompts and selects most reliable one). Training and evaluation supported by ThinkV2V-150K dataset and ThinkV2V-Bench.

Result: ThinkV2V demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on both complex and standard editing scenarios. A 5B-scale DiT model substantially outperforms larger 10B-scale baselines.

Conclusion: ThinkV2V successfully bridges the gap in video editing by explicitly activating MLLM thinking before visual generation. The reasoning-driven approach with dedicated training and inference recipes enables effective handling of implicit intent and causal reasoning in video editing tasks.

Abstract: Instruction-guided video editing has made significant progress, yet existing methods use multimodal large language models (MLLMs) primarily as semantic encoders, so they often fall short in working with implicit edits that require causal or semantic reasoning. To bridge this fundamental gap in video editing, we propose ThinkV2V, a reasoning-driven framework for complex instruction-guided video editing, explicitly activating MLLM thinking before visual generation. At its core, ThinkV2V builds on a practical MLLM-to-DiT architecture to turn explicit thinking over the source video and instruction into refined conditioning signals for video editing. Further, we equip it with a dedicated training and inference recipe, combining Progressive Curriculum Training, which gradually cultivates the model from basic editing to reasoning-intensive cases, with Inference-Time Thinking Scaling, which iteratively refines candidate prompts and selects the most reliable one, to better elicit reasoning in challenging editing scenarios. We also curate the ThinkV2V-150K dataset and introduce ThinkV2V-Bench to support training and evaluation of video editing with implicit intent and causal reasoning. Experimental results demonstrate the state-of-the-art performance of ThinkV2V on both complex and standard editing scenarios, in which our 5B-scale DiT model substantially outperforms larger 10B-scale baselines.

</details>


### [30] [Detail in Context: A Dual-Scale Machine Learning Framework for Mycosis Fungoides Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38560)
*Mohamed Hazem,Tarek Waleed,Omar Khaled,Nada Omar,Mahmoud Raslan,Marwa Mohamed Fawzy,Aya Fahim,Rania M. Mogawer,Ahmed Mourad,Kariman Mansour,Muhammad Rushdi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multimodal diagnostic framework for automated Mycosis fungoides (MF) detection combining dual-scale histopathological image analysis (10x and 20x magnification) with deep learning CNNs and a random forest classifier on 16 clinical features. Testing on 6,267 images from 463 patients achieved 83.58% accuracy/89.13% sensitivity for the image model and 96.6% accuracy/93.8% sensitivity for the clinical model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mycosis fungoides is a rare cutaneous T-cell lymphoma often misdiagnosed in early stages due to visual similarity to benign inflammatory dermatoses. Early and accurate diagnosis is critical for improving patient outcomes, but current diagnostic methods are challenging.

Method: The proposed approach uses a late-fusion ensemble of dual-magnification (10x and 20x) convolutional neural networks (CNNs) for histopathological image analysis, complemented by a random forest classifier trained on 16 clinical features. The framework addresses two objectives: image-based early diagnostic screening (MF vs non-MF) and clinical metadata model for staging (patch/plaque vs tumor).

Result: Experimental results on 6,267 images (4,306 MF; 1,961 Non-MF) across 463 patients show the image-based late-fusion model achieves 83.58% accuracy and 89.13% sensitivity, while the clinical random forest model achieves 96.6% accuracy and 93.8% sensitivity. Higher-resolution cytological details (20x) within broader architectural context (10x) provided strong detection performance.

Conclusion: The multimodal framework demonstrates potential as a robust clinical decision support system in dermatology. The dual-scale image pipeline is optimized for early diagnostic screening, while the clinical metadata model complements for staging of confirmed MF cases.

Abstract: Mycosis fungoides (MF) is a rare form of cutaneous T-cell lymphoma that is often misdiagnosed in early stages due to its visual similarity to benign inflammatory dermatoses. Early and accurate diagnosis is critical for improving patient outcomes. In this paper, we propose a comprehensive diagnostic framework for automated MF detection that combines dual- scale histopathological image analysis with deep learning. To distinguish MF from other lymphoproliferative skin conditions, the proposed approach leverages a late-fusion ensemble of dual- magnification (10x and 20x) convolutional neural networks (CNNs), complemented by a random forest classifier trained on 16 clinical features. Experimental results on an expanded dataset of 6,267 images (4,306 MF; 1,961 Non-MF) across 463 patients demonstrate that strong detection performance is obtained by prioritizing higher-resolution cytological details (20x) within broader architectural context (10x). The image-based late-fusion model achieves an accuracy of 83.58% and a sensitivity of 89.13%, while the clinical random forest model achieves an accuracy of 96.6% and sensitivity of 93.8%, highlighting the po- tential of this multimodal framework as a robust clinical decision support system in dermatology. This framework addresses two distinct clinical objectives: an image-based dual-scale pipeline optimized for the early diagnostic screening of MF versus non- MF dermatoses, and a complementary clinical metadata model designed for the subsequent staging of confirmed MF cases (patch/plaque versus tumor)

</details>


### [31] [LongTake: Learning to Sustain Dynamics in Long-Horizon Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38562)
*Byoungwoo Park,Jaemoo Choi,Juho Lee,Yongxin Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LongTake addresses the problem of quality degradation in autoregressive video diffusion when generating long videos (30-60 seconds). It introduces a two-stage training pipeline with Long-Horizon Teacher Forcing that extends supervision beyond short training horizons to help models sustain dynamics and preserve visual quality during extended rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive video diffusion for long-horizon generation (30-60s) often produces near-static videos or loses visual quality. The authors hypothesize this failure stems from limited guidance provided by short-video supervision on how scene dynamics develop over longer durations.

Method: Long-Horizon Teacher Forcing (TF): trains the AR model to predict later frames conditioned on long ground-truth video prefixes, extending direct supervision beyond the short training horizon. This supervision strengthens direct initialization for Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) under student self-rollout, without the intermediate few-step distillation stage. Hybrid DMD further extends supervision to later frames of self-rollout while retaining bidirectional joint supervision over the initial window.

Result: Under the same five-second DMD training setup, LongTake initialization yields substantially higher dynamic degree than short horizon TF initialization on 30-second rollouts at comparable aesthetic quality, and surpasses evaluated baselines in both measures. Hybrid DMD attains the highest dynamic degree among evaluated methods at both 30s and 60s.

Conclusion: Long-Horizon TF effectively addresses the quality degradation problem in long video generation by extending supervision beyond short training horizons. The method achieves superior performance on the Pareto front of dynamic degree and aesthetic quality for 30-60 second video generation, with Hybrid DMD achieving state-of-the-art results.

Abstract: World models, game simulators, and long-take video creation require coherent scene evolution and sustained dynamics over extended durations. Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion provides a natural framework for long-horizon generation, yet extended rollouts often become near-static or lose visual quality. We hypothesize that these failures reflect the limited guidance provided by short-video supervision on how ongoing scene dynamics develops over longer durations. This motivates us to introduce LongTake, a two-stage training pipeline built around Long-Horizon Teacher Forcing (TF) on curated real long videos. Long-Horizon TF trains the AR model to predict later frames conditioned on long ground-truth video prefixes, extending direct supervision beyond the short training horizon. This supervision is designed to help the model sustain dynamics and preserve visual quality during long-horizon generation. Our central finding is that this training stage strengthens direct initialization for distribution matching distillation (DMD) under student self-rollout, without the intermediate few-step distillation stage used in standard pipelines. Under the same five-second DMD training setup, our initialization yields substantially higher dynamic degree than short horizon TF initialization on 30-second rollouts at comparable aesthetic quality, and surpasses the evaluated baselines in both measures. Hybrid DMD further reuses this teacher to extend supervision to later frames of the self-rollout while retaining bidirectional joint supervision over the initial window. On long-horizon self-rollouts, LongTake lies on the Pareto front of dynamic degree and aesthetic quality, and Hybrid DMD attains the highest dynamic degree among evaluated methods at both 30s and 60s.

</details>


### [32] [Retargeting Motions to Diverse Skeletons via Learnable Flattening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38578)
*Kia-Jüng Yang,Fabian H. Sinz,Paweł A. Pierzchlewicz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a Transformer Autoencoder for cross-structural motion retargeting that learns a topology- and translation-invariant latent space. The method uses learnable skeletal graph flattening with multiplicative graph-based positional encodings, enabling zero-shot retargeting to unseen skeletal topologies without paired training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing state-of-the-art models struggle with reliability in zero-shot settings for skeletons with different topologies unseen during training. Recent Transformer-based attempts have failed to outperform specialized geometric methods, indicating a need for a new architectural approach that can handle diverse skeletal topologies within a unified framework.

Method: The method employs a Transformer Autoencoder with a learnable flattening of skeletal graphs that captures both local dependencies and global structure. Graph-based positional encodings are integrated multiplicatively (rather than additively as in standard transformers), which follows directly from the flattening formulation. The model trains in a fully unsupervised manner requiring no paired retargeting data.

Result: In zero-shot evaluations, the method reduces global joint position error by 43-47% over current benchmarks. A user study (n=37) including expert animators ranks the approach highest in motion alignment and physical plausibility (p < 0.05).

Conclusion: The model design (graph encodings, multiplicative formulation, and Transformer backbone) is critical for making transformer architectures effective for motion retargeting. This approach outperforms existing methods, demonstrating that the architectural choices are key to bridging the gap between Transformers and specialized geometric methods for cross-structural motion retargeting.

Abstract: Cross-structural motion retargeting aims to transfer motion between different skeletal topologies. Despite recent progress, existing state-of-the-art models struggle with reliability in zero-shot settings, i.e. skeletons with different topologies which were unseen during training, and recent Transformer-based attempts have failed to outperform specialized geometric methods. We bridge this gap with a Transformer Autoencoder that learns a topology- and translation-invariant latent space. Our core contribution is a learnable flattening of skeletal graphs that captures both local dependencies and global structure. Unlike the standard transformer architecture, which adds positional information to token content, we integrate graph-based positional encodings multiplicatively, a design choice that follows directly from our flattening formulation. The resulting model handles diverse skeletal topologies within a single unified architecture and trains in a fully unsupervised manner, requiring no paired retargeting data. Ablation studies show, that the graph encodings, multiplicative formulation, and Transformer backbone is critical for the performance. In zero-shot evaluations, our method reduces global joint position error by $43-47\%$ over current benchmarks. A user study ($n = 37$), including expert animators, further ranks our approach highest in motion alignment and physical plausibility ($p < 0.05$). These results demonstrate that our model design is key to making transformer architectures effective for motion retargeting, outperforming existing approaches.

</details>


### [33] [Restoring without Forgetting: Filter-Level Continual Image Restoration via Parameter-Space Integrated Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38591)
*Xin Feng,Jin Zhao,Yizhen Zhang,Wenjie Pei,Fanglin Chen,Guangming Lu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RwF is a filter-level continual adaptation framework for image restoration that addresses catastrophic forgetting by isolating task-specific knowledge in a small subset of filters. It uses integrated gradients for filter localization, generates task-specific filters via low-rank transformations with cross-task attention and prototypical contrastive learning, and assembles them only at localized positions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continual adaptation of image restoration models to new tasks without revisiting past data causes catastrophic forgetting. Existing methods struggle to balance avoiding forgetting while maintaining restoration quality, and parameter-efficient methods like LoRA require too many additional parameters.

Method: 1) Parameter-space integrated gradients attribution to localize degradation-critical filters in coarse-to-fine manner; 2) Generate task-specific filters from a filter bank using compact factorized low-rank transformations; 3) Augment with cross-task attention and prototypical contrastive learning; 4) Assemble filters back only at localized positions.

Result: RwF effectively avoids forgetting and achieves competitive restoration quality against all-in-one methods with full data access, while outperforming LoRA-style adaptation with ~10× fewer additional parameters.

Conclusion: Task-specific knowledge in image restoration models is concentrated in a small subset of filters, which can be separated from general content reconstruction. This enables effective continual adaptation with minimal parameters, offering a promising direction for filter-level model adaptation in image restoration tasks.

Abstract: Adapting image restoration models to a stream of new tasks without revisiting past data remains challenging due to catastrophic forgetting. In this work, we propose Restoring without Forgetting (RwF), a filter-level continual adaptation framework for image restoration built upon a critical observation: task-specific knowledge is centered in a small subset of filters and can be separated from those reconstructing general content. RwF first performs parameter-space integrated gradients attribution to localize degradation-critical filters in a coarse-to-fine manner. It then adapts to new tasks by generating task-specific filters from a filter bank using compact factorized low-rank transformations, further augmented with cross-task attention and prototypical contrastive learning, and lastly assembles them back only at localized positions. Experiments on six restoration tasks show that RwF effectively avoids forgetting and achieves competitive restoration quality against all-in-one methods that have full data access, and outperforms LoRA-style adaptation with $\sim$10$\times$ fewer additional parameters. Code is available at https://github.com/funkdub/Restoring-without-Forgetting.

</details>


### [34] [StereoGaussians: Feed-Forward 3D Gaussian Splatting from Stereo Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38592)
*Boyuan Tian,Huangying Zhan,Zhan Li,Shin-Fang Chng,Hanwen Yang,Zirui Wang,Yi Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StereoGaussians is a method that predicts a metric 3D Gaussian Splatting representation from a single calibrated stereo pair, enabling nearby-view extrapolation for stereo-camera applications. It leverages frozen pretrained stereo networks for Gaussian attribute prediction while using calibrated disparity for geometry anchoring. A second Gaussian layer and expanded canvas handle disoccluded and outside-FOV content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feed-forward 3DGS enables reconstruction without per-scene optimization, but practical stereo-camera applications require nearby-view extrapolation beyond input views. While stereo disparity anchors visible surfaces, rendering newly exposed regions (disocclusions and outside-field-of-view content) requires learned appearance and additional scene capacity.

Method: StereoGaussians predicts a metric 3DGS representation from a single calibrated stereo pair by: (1) reusing intermediate representations from frozen pretrained stereo networks to predict Gaussian attributes; (2) using calibrated disparity to anchor geometry; (3) adding a second Gaussian layer and expanded image canvas for disoccluded and outside-FOV content. Training uses SceneSplat-Stereo dataset (803 quality-filtered 3DGS teachers with stereo inputs paired to nearby target views).

Result: Demonstrates improvements over strong view-synthesis baselines on unseen real and photorealistic stereo benchmarks. Ablation studies support the main design choices.

Conclusion: The approach successfully enables feed-forward 3DGS from stereo pairs with proper handling of disoccluded regions and outside-FOV content, achieving superior performance compared to existing view-synthesis methods.

Abstract: Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables reconstruction without per- scene optimisation, but practical stereo-camera applications require nearby-view extrapolation beyond the input views. Stereo depth anchors visible surfaces, yet rendering newly exposed regions also requires learned appearance and additional scene capacity. We introduce StereoGaussians, which predicts a metric 3DGS representation from a single calibrated stereo pair. It reuses intermediate repre- sentations from frozen pretrained stereo networks to predict Gaussian attributes, while calibrated disparity anchors the geometry. A second Gaussian layer and an expanded image canvas provide capacity for disoccluded and outside-field-of- view content. For training, we construct SceneSplat-Stereo from quality-filtered 3DGS teachers, pairing stereo inputs with nearby target views across 803 training scenes. Experiments on unseen real and photorealistic stereo benchmarks demon- strate improvements over strong view-synthesis baselines, while ablation studies support our main design choices.

</details>


### [35] [PixelUMM: Encoder-Free Unified Image and Video Understanding and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38597)
*Cong Wei,Xuanchi Ren,Bryan Chu,Weiming Ren,Huan Ling,Jiahui Huang,Laura Leal-Taixé,Sanja Fidler,Wenhu Chen,Zian Wang,Jay Zhangjie Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PixelUMM is an encoder-free unified multimodal model that handles both image and video understanding and generation directly in pixel space, using a Mixture-of-Transformers architecture with shared attention and task-specific parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) use separate visual representations for understanding and generation, which increases visual context length and complicates integration with vision-language pretraining pipelines. While pixel-space modeling offers an encoder-free alternative for images, extending this to video is challenging due to different temporal representations used for understanding vs. generation.

Method: PixelUMM represents images as spatial patches and videos as spatiotemporal tubelets, connecting raw pixels to a shared multimodal backbone through single-layer linear projections. It uses a Mixture-of-Transformers architecture combining shared attention with task-specific parameters, and extends clean-pixel prediction to video generation while jointly supporting autoregressive text prediction and pixel-space flow matching.

Result: PixelUMM achieves competitive performance across image and video understanding and generation tasks. The paper also provides empirical studies on key design choices including decoder design and spatial-temporal patch size.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that encoder-free unified multimodal models can effectively handle both image and video tasks in pixel space, providing insights for future pixel-space unified multimodal models through studies of decoder design and spatiotemporal patch size.

Abstract: Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) often rely on separate visual representations for understanding and generation, increasing visual context length and complicating integration with established vision-language pretraining pipelines. Recent advances in pixel-space modeling offer an encoder-free alternative, but extending this paradigm from images to videos is non-trivial: video understanding and generation adopt different temporal representations, leaving the design of a unified visual interface an open question. We present PixelUMM, an encoder-free model for unified image and video understanding and generation directly in pixel space. PixelUMM represents images as spatial patches and videos as spatiotemporal tubelets, connecting raw pixels to a shared multimodal backbone through single-layer linear projections. Its Mixture-of-Transformers architecture combines shared attention with task-specific parameters and extends clean-pixel prediction to video generation, jointly supporting autoregressive text prediction and pixel-space flow matching. Experiments show that PixelUMM achieves competitive performance across image and video understanding and generation tasks. We further conduct empirical studies of key design choices, including decoder design and spatial-temporal patch size, providing insights for future pixel-space unified multimodal models.

</details>


### [36] [Aperture: Training-Free Multiscale Concept Bottlenecks for Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38603)
*Rishabh Mondal,Nipun Batra,Utkarsh Mall*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: APERTURE is a training-free interpretable earth observation model that uses multiscale concept bottlenecks with greedy quadtree routing and pre-trained MLLMs to achieve state-of-the-art performance on a new fine-grained concept dataset (SiFC), outperforming both training-free baselines and supervised concept bottleneck models by over 10 percentage points in macro F1-score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Earth observation models lack interpretability, and existing concept-bottleneck models are either too expensive to train for remote sensing or perform poorly without annotations. The authors argue that training-free models in expert domains like remote sensing require fine details in both image and concept space.

Method: 1) In image space: multiscale concept bottleneck using greedy quadtree routing to locate small concepts. 2) In concept space: replace contrastive vision-language models with pre-trained MLLMs and extract reliable concept scores. 3) Blend concept scores at global image and native concept-scale level. 4) Introduce SiFC dataset: fine-grained concept-centric dataset across three countries with human-reviewed class-level concept maps.

Result: APERTURE outperforms the best training-free baselines by more than 10 percentage points in macro F1-score on SiFC, and also outperforms supervised concept bottleneck models. Component-removal tests show concept scores respond to visual evidence changes. Temporal experiments demonstrate descriptor updates improve recognition of technological changes without retraining.

Conclusion: The proposed APERTURE framework successfully achieves training-free interpretable earth observation by combining multiscale concept bottlenecks with MLLMs, setting a new state-of-the-art for training-free models while maintaining interpretability through human-understandable concepts.

Abstract: While earth observation models have advanced substantially, they still lack interpretability. While concept-bottleneck models provide interpretability and expert interaction, they are either too expensive to train for the remote sensing domain or perform poorly without annotation. We posit that in expert domains like remote sensing, such training-free models require both fine details in both image and concept space. In image space, we propose a multiscale concept bottleneck using greedy quadtree routing to locate small concepts. In concept space, we replace contrastive vision language models with pre-trained MLLMs and present a way to get reliable concept scores from them. We introduce APERTURE that blends concept scores at the global image and native concept-scale level to give state-ofthe-art training-free model performance. To test these models, introduce SiFC, a fine-grained concept-centric dataset across three countries, with human-reviewed class-level concept maps. On SiFC, APERTURE outperforms the best training-free baselines by more than 10 percentage points in macro F1-score, and notably also outperforms supervised concept bottleneck models. Targeted component-removal tests examine whether concept scores respond to changes in visual evidence, while temporal experiments show that descriptor updates improve recognition of technological changes without retraining.

</details>


### [37] [After a Decade: Bringing Shadow Removal into the Real World with Agentic Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38607)
*Shilin Hu,Jingyi Xu,Dimitris Samaras,Hieu Le*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Shadow removal models perform poorly in real-world scenarios due to limited and outdated paired training data. This paper proposes an offline agentic workflow to generate diverse shadow-free targets from existing shadow detection datasets, creating AgenticShadow - a dataset of 17,138 image-mask-target triplets spanning general scenes, faces, and remote sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that shadow removal models rely on paired training data (shadow image + corresponding shadow-free target) that hasn't evolved in nearly a decade. Capturing diverse paired data is difficult because it requires removing the occluder while keeping scene, camera, and illumination unchanged. However, large shadow detection datasets exist with diverse images and masks but lack shadow-free targets. This work aims to transform this abundant but incomplete data into useful paired supervision.

Method: The proposed method is an offline agentic workflow combining five components: (1) Physics-motivated generation - using physical models of shadow formation to generate shadow-free targets; (2) Failure detection - identifying generation failures; (3) Feedback-driven retry - iteratively improving outputs; (4) Candidate selection - choosing best results; (5) Deterministic correction - applying corrections to ensure quality.

Result: The workflow reduces Color Distribution Difference by 50.5% compared to previous shadow removal work. When training existing shadow removal models on AgenticShadow, cross-domain LAB RMSE improves by 19.7-37.5%, demonstrating better generalization to real-world scenarios.

Conclusion: The AgenticShadow dataset with 17,138 triplets enables shadow removal models to generalize better to diverse real-world scenarios across general scenes, faces, and remote sensing domains. The agentic workflow approach successfully addresses the data scarcity problem in shadow removal by creating high-quality paired training data from existing detection datasets.

Abstract: Shadow removal looks nearly solved on established benchmarks, yet remains brittle in the real world. Models have advanced; the paired training data they rely on have barely changed in nearly a decade. The reason is simple: obtaining a shadow-free target requires removing the occluder while keeping the scene, camera, and illumination otherwise unchanged, making diverse paired data difficult to capture. Meanwhile, large shadow detection datasets already contain diverse real-world images and masks, but no shadow-free targets. To turn this abundant but incomplete data into paired supervision, we propose an offline agentic workflow combining physics-motivated generation, failure detection, feedback-driven retry, candidate selection, and deterministic correction. Using this workflow, we construct AgenticShadow, a dataset of 17,138 image-mask-target triplets spanning general scenes, faces, and remote sensing. Our construction workflow reduces Color Distribution Difference by 50.5% over previous shadow removal work, while training existing shadow removal models on AgenticShadow reduces cross-domain LAB RMSE by 19.7-37.5%.

</details>


### [38] [Exo2EgoHOI: Hand-Object-Interaction Aware Exocentric-to-Egocentric Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38615)
*Hongjia Zhai,Xiyu Zhang,Haoran Zhang,Zhichao Ye,Haomin Liu,Guofeng Zhang,Ian Reid,Xingxing Zuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Exo2EgoHOI is a framework for transforming exocentric (third-person) manipulation videos into egocentric (first-person) views while preserving hand-object interactions (HOI) and object consistency. It addresses the challenge of large viewpoint changes that often lose fine-grained interaction details.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Egocentric videos are valuable for embodied intelligence but costly to collect at scale. Exocentric-to-egocentric generation offers a scalable alternative using abundant third-person videos. However, existing methods fail to preserve hand-object interactions (HOI) across large viewpoint changes due to insufficient fine-grained interaction guidance and weak object-centric anchoring.

Method: The framework introduces three key components: (1) Unified 4D HOI prior combining scene geometry, articulated hand renderings, and dense hand-object relation fields; (2) Dual-branch residual adapter for injecting structural and relational cues into the video generation backbone; (3) Decomposed Gated Cross-Attention that separately encodes object and background references and adaptively integrates global semantic and local appearance features as object-centric anchors.

Result: On ARCTIC-HOI, Exo2EgoHOI improves object mIoU by 32.3% and reduces MPJPE and PA-MPJPE by 34.7% and 50.0% respectively, relative to the best baseline results. The method maintains competitive visual fidelity while substantially improving object consistency and HOI preservation.

Conclusion: Exo2EgoHOI successfully preserves fine-grained hand-object interactions and object consistency during exocentric-to-egocentric video translation, achieving significant improvements in both quantitative metrics and visual quality. The approach demonstrates the effectiveness of HOI-aware guidance and object-centric anchoring for viewpoint transformation in manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Egocentric videos of human manipulation provide valuable visual experience for embodied intelligence, yet collecting such data at scale is costly. Exocentric-to-egocentric video generation offers a scalable alternative by transforming abundant third-person manipulation videos into first-person observations. However, existing methods often struggle to faithfully preserve demonstrated hand-object interactions (HOI) across large viewpoint changes due to insufficient fine-grained interaction guidance and weak object-centric anchoring. We present Exo2EgoHOI, an HOI-aware video generative framework for interaction-preserving exocentric-to-egocentric translation. To preserve fine-grained HOI, we introduce a unified 4D HOI prior that combines scene geometry, articulated hand renderings, and dense hand-object relation fields, together with a dual-branch residual adapter for injecting structural and relational cues into the video generation backbone. To preserve object consistency, we introduce Decomposed Gated Cross-Attention, which separately encodes object and background references and adaptively integrates global semantic and local appearance features as object-centric anchors. Experiments on ARCTIC-HOI and Ego-Exo4D demonstrate substantial improvements in object consistency and HOI preservation while maintaining competitive visual fidelity. In particular, on ARCTIC-HOI, Exo2EgoHOI improves object mIoU by 32.3% and reduces MPJPE and PA-MPJPE by 34.7% and 50.0%, respectively, relative to the respective best baseline results. Project page: https://rcl-robotics.github.io/Exo2EgoHOI/.

</details>


### [39] [HIGS: Hierarchical Implicit Grids for Joint Geometric and Semantic Scene Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38620)
*Hanwen Cao,Wenqiang Wu,Kuang-Ting Tu,Mathias Otnes,Jeffrey Delmerico,Rui Wang,Yulun Tian,Nikolay Atanasov*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hierarchical neural field representation for large-scale scene reconstruction that combines geometric and semantic (vision-language) features. It addresses two key challenges: lack of semantic information in existing methods and computational inefficiency at scale. The approach uses multiresolution submaps with hierarchical optimization and feature encoders for fast initialization, achieving efficient and scalable implicit representation for robot spatial understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural implicit representations for scene reconstruction focus primarily on geometric reconstruction and lack semantic information needed for high-level spatial understanding and task planning. Additionally, as environment scale increases, neural representations struggle with computational efficiency in optimization.

Method: The authors introduce: (1) A hierarchical neural field with multiresolution submaps for scalable computation; (2) A unified query and decoding mechanism supporting both geometric and semantic features; (3) Feature encoders that predict initial hierarchical grid features to reduce optimization time; (4) Submap alignment and fusion entirely within the implicit feature space to avoid decoding for final output. The approach embeds both geometric features (SDF) and vision-language latent features into the map.

Result: The approach significantly improves computation and memory efficiency while maintaining high estimation accuracy. It enables both Signed Distance Field (SDF) construction and open-vocabulary object grounding on large-scale real-world benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed hierarchical neural field successfully achieves efficient and scalable implicit representation with both geometric and semantic capabilities, endowing robots with spatial awareness for large-scale real-world applications.

Abstract: Neural implicit representations have had a significant impact on scene reconstruction by enabling robots to build continuous, differentiable, and high-fidelity 3D maps. Most existing works focus on geometric reconstruction and lack semantic information for high-level spatial understanding and task planning. Also, as the scale and complexity of the environment increase, neural representations face the challenge of maintaining computational efficiency in back-end optimization. To resolve these two challenges, we introduce a hierarchical neural field that leverages multiresolution submaps to achieve an efficient and scalable implicit representation, and a unified query and decoding mechanism to support both geometric and semantic features. More specifically, the learnable map features can be converted to the output with the query and decoding process for both training and inference. For large-scale representation, we decompose a scene into overlapping submaps and do hierarchical optimization within each local submap, thus enabling scalable computation. To further improve efficiency, we design feature encoders that predict initial hierarchical grid features to substantially reduce the time needed to optimize the submap features from scratch. To correct estimation drift among submaps, we align and fuse them entirely within the implicit feature space, leading to substantial acceleration by avoiding the need to decode the final output. Building upon this efficient hierarchical representation, we embed both geometric features and vision-language latent features into the map, and demonstrate it on both Signed Distance Field (SDF) construction and open-vocabulary object grounding. Our approach significantly improves computation and memory efficiency, maintains high estimation accuracy, and endows the robot with spatial awareness on large-scale real-world benchmarks.

</details>


### [40] [Eulerian Motion Reconstruction for Water Scenery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38622)
*Chuhan Chen,Yen-Chi Cheng,Ayush Saraf,Rajvi Shah,Tuotuo Li,Johannes Kopf,Chen Gao,Hung-Yu Tseng,Deva Ramanan,Matthew O'Toole,Changil Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a 3D framework for reconstructing and animating water scenery from a single non-looping 2D source video, creating a looping 4D dynamic reconstruction that can be rendered from novel viewpoints. The method uses a 3D static Eulerian motion field with cyclically reborn Gaussian splats, supplemented by a non-periodic residual term to capture stochastic dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work on water scenery animation focused on 2D video textures for looping videos. This work aims to tackle the problem from a 3D perspective, enabling interactive rendering from novel viewpoints using only a single non-looping 2D source video.

Method: The method represents motion as a 3D static Eulerian motion field that advects canonical Gaussian splats cyclically reborn at fixed time periods, supervised using rendering losses. A non-periodic, time-varying residual term is added to capture deviations from the static Eulerian motion field for modeling non-periodic and stochastic dynamics in real-world scenes.

Result: The framework enables photorealistic animation of water scenes. Both quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate superior performance compared to prior art.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully creates looping 4D dynamic reconstructions of water scenery from a single 2D video, enabling novel viewpoint rendering with photorealistic quality, outperforming existing methods.

Abstract: Reconstructing and animating water scenery from nature produces compelling and immersive visual experiences. Previous work examined this task from the perspective of 2D video textures, with the goal of creating a looping video. In our work, we tackle the problem from a 3D perspective, creating a looping 4D dynamic reconstruction which can be interactively rendered from novel viewpoints from a single non-looping 2D source video. We represent motion as a 3D static \textit{Eulerian} motion field that advects canonical Gaussian splats that are cyclically reborn at fixed time periods, supervised using rendering losses. To model non-periodic and stochastic dynamics present in real-world scenes, we add a non-periodic, time-varying residual term to capture deviations from the static Eulerian motion field. We show quantitatively and qualitatively that our framework enables photorealistic animation of water scenes better than prior art.

</details>


### [41] [STEPS: Scene Text Editing with Preserved Style Using Diffusion and Contrastive Style Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38636)
*Nicolas Thiebaut,Nameer Hirschkind,Xiao Yu,Kyle Spence*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: STEPS is a novel diffusion model architecture for scene text editing that preserves font, colors, orientation, and background while changing textual content. It introduces a style encoder that captures visual text style independently of content, combined with semantic conditions (target text encoding and rendered glyphs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scene Text Editing (STE) aims to change text in images while preserving original style, but existing methods struggle with style preservation. This work addresses the need for improved quality in text replacement, focusing on better style preservation, output readability, and subjective quality.

Method: STEPS introduces: 1) A style encoder for visual text that captures style independently of textual content; 2) A model architecture combining the style encoder with multiple semantic conditions including target text characters encoding and rendered glyphs. The approach uses a diffusion model architecture.

Result: STEPS achieves superior results compared to previous STE methods in three key areas: style preservation, output readability, and subjective quality.

Conclusion: STEPS advances the state of the art in scene text editing through directed focus on improved style preservation, demonstrating that independent style encoding combined with semantic conditions can effectively replace text while maintaining visual consistency with the original image.

Abstract: We introduce Scene Text Editing with Preserved Style (STEPS), a novel diffusion model architecture for quality text replacement in images. Scene Text Editing (STE), also known as Visual Text Editing, consists of changing the textual content in an image while conserving the original style, e.g. font, colors, orientation, background, etc. STEPS advances the state of the art in STE through directed focus on improved style preservation. We introduce a style encoder for visual text that captures style independently of textual content, and a model architecture that combines the style encoder with multiple semantic conditions (target text characters encoding and rendered glyphs). STEPS achieves superior results to previous STE methods in style preservation, output readability, and subjective quality.

</details>


### [42] [Template-Search Domain Adaptation via Multi-Stage Feature Alignment for Cross-Modal Object Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38637)
*Fereshteh Aghaee Meibodi,Amir Mehdi Soufi Enayati,Shadi Alijani,Homayoun Najjaran*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TSDA-Track is a Template-Search Domain Adaptation framework for cross-modal visual object tracking, where template and search frames originate from different modalities. It addresses the representation gap between modalities by applying feature alignment strategies, with two variants: Pre-AFA (adversarial alignment before transformer interaction) and Enc-CFA (contrastive alignment after interaction). Both variants use a shared inference pipeline without modality-specific branches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual object tracking typically assumes template and search frames share the same sensing modality. In practice, sensor availability or operation may change over time, creating a substantial representation gap between modalities. Unlike conventional multi-modal tracking with paired modalities, cross-modal tracking requires localization when template and search frames originate from different active modalities.

Method: TSDA-Track investigates two feature alignment strategies: 1) Pre-AFA applies adversarial alignment before transformer's template-search interaction to suppress modality-specific bias; 2) Enc-CFA applies contrastive alignment to encoder representations after interaction to strengthen target-level cross-modal correspondence. Both variants retain a shared inference pipeline without modality-specific branches.

Result: Experiments on LasHeR, and zero-shot evaluations on RGBT234 and GTOT under multiple cross-modal protocols demonstrate improvements over state-of-the-art trackers. Under the modality-switch protocol on RGBT234, Pre-AFA TSDA-Track achieves SR/PR of 43.2%/56.0%, compared with 36.8%/50.0% for ToMP-101 baseline. Study on Anti-UAV-024 further verifies applicability to aerial tracking.

Conclusion: Feature alignment domain adaptation is effective for cross-modal tracking. The proposed TSDA-Track framework successfully reduces modality discrepancy during training through adversarial or contrastive alignment strategies, achieving superior performance across multiple benchmark datasets and cross-modal protocols.

Abstract: Visual object tracking typically assumes that the initial template and subsequent search frames share the same sensing modality. In practice, sensor availability or operation may change over time, creating a substantial representation gap between template and search frames. Unlike conventional multi-modal tracking where paired modalities are simultaneously available, cross-modal tracking requires localization when template and search frames originate from different active modalities. Accordingly, we introduce TSDA-Track, a Template-Search Domain Adaptation framework to reduce modality discrepancy during training. We investigate two feature alignment strategies. Pre-AFA TSDA-Track applies adversarial alignment before transformer's template-search interaction to suppress modality-specific bias. Enc-CFA TSDA-Track applies contrastive alignment to encoder representations after interaction to strengthen target-level cross-modal correspondence. Both variants retain a shared inference pipeline without modality-specific branches. Experiments on LasHeR, and zero-shot evaluations on RGBT234 and GTOT under multiple cross-modal protocols demonstrate improvements over representative state-of-the-art trackers. For instance, under the modality-switch protocol on RGBT234, Pre-AFA TSDA-Track achieves an SR/PR of 43.2/56.0, compared with 36.8/50.0 for ToMP-101 baseline. In addition, a study on Anti-UAV-024 further verifies the applicability of TSDA-Track to aerial tracking. Our study highlights the effectiveness of feature alignment domain adaptation for cross-modal tracking.

</details>


### [43] [Vision-Language-Action Autonomous Driving Agent with Language-based Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38641)
*Kai Yan,Xiangyu Chen,Yulong Cao,Alex Naumann,Peter Karkus,Yan Wang,Jef Packer,Alex Schwing,Yuxiong Wang,Boris Ivanovic,Wenjie Luo,Marco Pavone*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AD-Memo is a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) driving agent that uses language-based memory as an extension of Chain-of-Thought reasoning. It addresses the limitation of VLAs that can only process limited frames due to high token costs, enabling memory-dependent tasks like all-way stop coordination and long-horizon scene understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) foundation models are limited in the number of frames they can process as visual input due to high token costs. This is problematic for memory-dependent tasks such as determining arrival order at all-way stops and long-horizon driving scene understanding. Existing solutions using latent vector memories are neither interpretable nor portable.

Method: The paper proposes AD-Memo with a two-stage training recipe: (1) Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on curated memory-based datasets, and (2) Da Capo, a novel semi-closed-loop Reinforcement Learning algorithm that uses trajectory-level advantage for memory and step-level advantage for driving for better credit assignment. Memory is output as an extension of Chain-of-Thought to record surrounding objects critical to driving, and becomes part of the agent's future input.

Result: AD-Memo improves driving quality across scenarios such as all-way stops and general driving, enables better question answering on driving scenes, and produces plug-and-play memory for other models.

Conclusion: AD-Memo successfully addresses the frame limitation of VLAs through language-based memory, demonstrating improved performance in memory-dependent driving tasks while providing interpretable and portable memory that can be used by other models.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) foundation models have recently emerged as one of the prevailing solutions for autonomous driving, as they can utilize knowledge acquired during vision-language pretraining for accurate and interpretable driving. However, VLAs can take only a limited number of frames as visual input due to the high token cost of an image, which is problematic for memory-dependent tasks such as determining the arrival order at all-way stops and long-horizon driving scene understanding. Existing solutions use latent vector memories accessed through cross-attention, which are neither interpretable nor portable. In this paper, we propose AD-Memo, a general-purpose VLA driving agent with language-based memory. The agent outputs memory as an extension of its Chain-of-Thought (CoT) to record surrounding objects critical to driving; this memory becomes part of the agent's future input. We curate memory-based datasets and train VLAs with a two-stage recipe: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and \textit{Da Capo}, a novel semi-closed-loop Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithm which uses trajectory-level advantage for memory and step-level advantage for driving, leading to better credit assignment. Across scenarios such as all-way stops and general driving, AD-Memo improves driving quality, enables better question answering on driving scenes, and produces plug-and-play memory for other models.

</details>


### [44] [ReGain: Restoring Subject Fidelity in Personalization on Synthetic Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38680)
*Shubhang Bhatnagar,Ishan Bhatnagar,Viraj Shah,Narendra Ahuja*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Fine-tuning DreamBooth on synthetic images (generated by diffusion models) rather than real photos degrades subject fidelity. The root cause is classifier-free guidance (CFG) inflation, particularly at high frequencies. ReGain is proposed as a training-free correction that scales down inflated frequency bands, closing 51-64% of the fidelity gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As synthetic images increasingly replace real photos for DreamBooth personalization, there's a degradation problem in subject fidelity (oversaturation, excess high-frequency detail). The goal is to identify the cause and develop a solution without requiring real photos.

Method: Controlled experiment: fine-tuned two models from the same base with same DreamBooth recipe—one on real photos, one on synthetic images. Analyzed CFG behavior (angle and norm between conditional/unconditional noise predictions) across frequency bands and semantically related prompts. Proposed ReGain: training-free sampling-time correction that measures CFG inflation per frequency band relative to base model and scales accordingly.

Result: Degradation traced to CFG inflation. For synthetic-fine-tuned model: angle between conditional/unconditional noise predictions is much larger than for real-photo model; inflation grows toward high frequencies; appears at semantically close prompts (e.g., class noun) but not unrelated ones. ReGain closes 51-64% of subject-fidelity gap on SDv1.5 (measured by DINO, DINOv2, CLIP-I), improves SDXL and SD 3.5, preserves text alignment.

Conclusion: CFG inflation is the root cause of subject fidelity degradation when fine-tuning on synthetic images. ReGain effectively corrects this without real photos, maintaining compatibility across multiple Stable Diffusion versions while preserving text alignment.

Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models are personalized to a subject by DreamBooth fine-tuning on a handful of its images. Increasingly, these images come from a diffusion model rather than a camera. We show that fine-tuning on such synthetic images degrades subject fidelity, producing oversaturated color and excess high-frequency detail. To isolate the cause, we fine-tune two models from the same base model with the same DreamBooth recipe, one on real photos of a subject and one on synthetic images of that subject generated by the first. We trace the degradation to classifier-free guidance (CFG). For the model personalized on synthetic images, the angle between the conditional and unconditional noise predictions, and with it the norm of their difference, is much larger than for the model personalized on real photos. This inflation grows toward high frequencies and also appears at other prompts semantically close to the subject, such as its class noun, but not at unrelated ones. We propose ReGain, a training-free correction applied at sampling time that measures how much each frequency band of the guidance is inflated relative to the base model and scales that band down accordingly. ReGain needs no real photos. On Stable Diffusion v1.5, ReGain closes 51-64% of the subject-fidelity gap to the model personalized on real photos, as measured by DINO, DINOv2 and CLIP-I. It also improves subject fidelity on SDXL and SD 3.5 and preserves text alignment on all three backbones.

</details>


### [45] [Unveiling the Value of Motion for Cinematic Camera Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38683)
*Ziqi Zhou,Yujian Yuan,Laura Sevilla-Lara*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper addresses camera trajectory generation and alignment by decomposing traditional pose-centric representations into direction and speed components. The authors introduce CineGEN (a novel generative model), a new evaluation protocol, and CineScript (a new dataset) to demonstrate the benefits of this decomposed representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current camera trajectory methods use per-frame pose representations, but the authors observe that networks may not effectively learn direction and speed from these representations. This motivated them to explore explicit decomposition of camera trajectories into direction and speed components.

Method: The key insight is decomposing camera trajectory representation from traditional per-frame poses into direction and speed. Building on this, they propose CineGEN, a novel generative model for camera trajectories. They also introduce a simple evaluation protocol for trajectory-to-text alignment and a new dataset CineScript containing movie clips with scene descriptions and metadata.

Result: The decomposed representation (direction and speed) achieves superior performance across multiple metrics for both trajectory-to-text alignment and text-to-trajectory generation tasks. The approach also inherently encodes complex directorial intent.

Conclusion: Representing camera trajectories through direction and speed not only improves numerical performance in alignment and generation tasks but also captures high-level cinematographic information, demonstrating that explicit decomposition is more effective than implicit learning from pose-centric representations.

Abstract: Cinematic camera motion is a fundamental storytelling tool, defined not only by where the camera is positioned in the scene, but also by how it moves in terms of direction and speed. Recent work on camera trajectory generation and alignment to text relies on pose-centric representations. While in principle a network could derive direction of movement and speed, we find that in practice this might not happen. In fact, in this paper we discover that decomposing the camera trajectory representation from the traditional per-frame poses to direction and speed has surprising benefits across multiple tasks, including trajectory-to-text alignment as well as text-to-trajectory generation. To accurately evaluate the former, we introduce a simple and reliable protocol that overcomes the limitations of prior evaluation baselines. For the latter, building on this representational insight, we propose a novel generative model for camera trajectories, CineGEN, that achieves superior performance across a variety of metrics. We also propose a novel dataset, CineScript, containing movie clips that are enriched with scene descriptions as well as higher-level metadata. This novel data allows us to test models' ability to capture high-level cinematographic information. We show that, despite its simplicity, representing camera trajectories through direction and speed not only helps numerically to achieve better alignment and generation, but also inherently encodes complex directorial intent.

</details>


### [46] [EPIC: Epipolar-Consistent 360° Immersive Stereo Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38689)
*Debabrata Mandal,Dongdong Fu,Jonathon Miller,William Villareal,Xi Peng,Praneeth Chakravarthula*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A zero-shot generative pipeline that extends existing video diffusion models to generate 4K stereoscopic 360° videos for immersive displays, using an epipolar-aware 360° image matching metric for direct preference optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manually authoring immersive content is prohibitively expensive and unscalable. While generative AI could help, existing models cannot generate high-resolution stereoscopic 360° content required for immersive viewing, and temporal/stereo inconsistencies become highly disruptive in headsets.

Method: Developed an epipolar-aware 360° image matching metric inspired by binocular vision and depth perception to capture temporal and stereo geometric inconsistencies across views. This metric serves as a preference signal for direct preference optimization with limited training data.

Result: Enables 360° stereo video generation and provides a scalable path for bringing generative content to immersive displays, allowing diverse mixed reality experiences on demand.

Conclusion: The work bridges the gap between conventional video diffusion models and immersive display requirements, offering a zero-shot solution that addresses both quality and consistency challenges in stereoscopic 360° content generation.

Abstract: Immersive displays can enable rich and diverse virtual experiences.
