Manually authoring every possible experience to realize this potential, however, is prohibitively expensive, difficult to scale, and impractical. Generative AI models could remove this bottleneck, but today's models are built for conventional displays and cannot generate the high-resolution, stereoscopic $360^\circ$ content required for immersive viewing. Further, temporal and stereo inconsistencies that may be tolerable on conventional displays can become highly disruptive when viewed through an immersive headset.
  Here, we address this gap with a zero-shot generative pipeline that extends existing video diffusion models into 4K stereoscopic $360^\circ$ videos. Inspired from binocular vision and depth perception, we develop an epipolar-aware $360^\circ$ image matching metric that captures the temporal and stereo geometric inconsistencies across views. We then use this metric as a preference signal for direct preference optimization with limited training data. Our work enables $360^\circ$ stereo video generation and provides a scalable path for bringing generative content to immersive displays, allowing diverse mixed reality experiences on demand.

</details>


### [47] [No Corners Cut: State-Grounded Transitions for Mid-Stream Prompt Switches in Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38691)
*Zejing Rao,Ketong Ren,Xiaoqiang Liu,Yiping Meng,Guoxin Zhang,Fan Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SEGUE is a framework for streaming video generators that enables faithful mid-stream prompt switching by using a training-free planner to write segue prompts with roles and durations, combined with SPANDMD for temporal context evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming video generators take heuristic shortcuts when responding to mid-stream prompt switches, bypassing necessary intermediate state changes needed for plausible transitions.

Method: SEGUE uses a training-free planner that parses each switch frame and prompts, writes segue prompts with roles and durations, then returns control to the user's prompt. SPANDMD evaluates active prompts using full rollout as temporal context while retaining DMD residual only within the prompt's assigned span.

Result: On OpenTrans-360 (1,800 switches), SEGUE ranks first on all eight transition metrics and improves overall score from 0.866 to 0.887. On StreamAV-Bench, it ranks first on four of six instruction-response metrics. The planner transfers to frozen autoregressive generators without retraining.

Conclusion: SEGUE successfully enables streaming video generators to execute faithful transitions during mid-stream prompt switching, with the planner being transferable to other generative architectures.

Abstract: Streaming video generators allow users to dynamically modulate video synthesis via mid-stream prompt switching. Existing streaming methods can respond to the updated instruction while still cutting corners, prematurely realizing goals or taking heuristic shortcuts that bypass necessary intermediate state changes needed for a plausible transition. In this study, we present SEGUE, a novel framework that makes this process explicit and trains the generator to execute these transitions faithfully. At each switch, a training-free planner parses the latest frame and prompts, writes a few segue prompts with roles and durations, and then hands control back to the user's prompt. Furthermore, to address the inherent difficulty of training causal models on short-lived temporal schedules without corrupting preparatory supervision, we introduce SPANDMD, which evaluates each active prompt using the full rollout as temporal context while retaining its DMD residual only within the prompt's assigned span. On OpenTrans-360, a benchmark of 1,800 switches that scores how the old state exits and the new one begins, SEGUE ranks first on all eight transition metrics and raises the overall score over the strongest baseline from 0.866 to 0.887. It also ranks first on four of six instruction-response metrics of StreamAV-Bench, while the planner transfers to frozen autoregressive generators without retraining.

</details>


### [48] [SCALE: Synthetic Calibration via Agreement Labeling in Embedding Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38705)
*Wenjun Liu,Saeed Hassanpour*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses calibration in computational pathology foundation models, showing that models assign overly confident probabilities to difficult cases. The authors propose "synthetic agreement calibration" - a method that uses interpolation between class anchors to create synthetic agreement signals for improving calibration without multi-annotator labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models for computational pathology are typically evaluated using AUC and accuracy, but calibration (the alignment between predicted probabilities and actual outcomes) is often untested. A model can be accurate on average but assign overly confident probabilities to cases that are difficult even for pathologists. This is problematic for clinical deployment where confidence calibration matters.

Method: The authors (1) used pathologist agreement as a measure of diagnostic difficulty across eight pathology foundation models; (2) proposed synthetic agreement calibration - a method using high-confidence embeddings as class anchors, interpolating between anchors from opposite classes, and using the interpolation weights as synthetic agreement signal to retrain the probe with agreement-aware label smoothing.

Result: Calibration error is consistently higher on low-agreement cases than high-agreement cases, a pattern not apparent from aggregate Expected Calibration Error (ECE) alone. On MHIST (with 7 pathologist annotations), synthetic agreement calibration recovers most of the calibration improvement from real agreement-based label smoothing while substantially reducing low-agreement ECE. Discrimination metrics are preserved. On PatchCamelyon and BreakHis (without multi-annotator labels), the method improves calibration across evaluated foundation models.

Conclusion: Synthetic agreement calibration effectively improves model calibration without requiring multi-annotator labels, making it practical for public histopathology datasets. The method addresses the problem of overconfident predictions on difficult cases while preserving discrimination performance.

Abstract: Foundation models for computational pathology are usually evaluated using AUC and accuracy, while calibration is often left untested. This matters because a model can be accurate on average but still assign overly confident probabilities to cases that are difficult even for pathologists. We study calibration across eight pathology foundation models. Using pathologist agreement as a measure of diagnostic difficulty, we find that calibration error is consistently higher on low-agreement cases than on high-agreement cases. This pattern is not apparent from aggregate expected calibration error (ECE) alone. We then propose synthetic agreement calibration, a method for improving calibration without collecting multi-annotator labels. Given a trained linear probe, we select high-confidence embeddings as class anchors and interpolate between anchors from opposite classes. The interpolation weights encode a continuous notion of diagnostic ambiguity, which we use as a synthetic agreement signal to retrain the probe with agreement-aware label smoothing. On MHIST, which includes annotations from seven pathologists, synthetic agreement calibration recovers most of the calibration improvement obtained by label smoothing based on real pathologist agreement, while substantially reducing low-agreement ECE relative to the uncalibrated baseline. Discrimination metrics are preserved. On PatchCamelyon and BreakHis, public histopathology datasets without multi-annotator labels, the method improves calibration across the evaluated foundation models, whereas annotator-dependent approaches cannot be used without additional expert annotation.

</details>


### [49] [Hard-Region Supervision: #1 on the Waymo Open Dataset 2D Video Panoptic Segmentation Leaderboard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38714)
*Jinghan Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents the winning solution to the Waymo Open Dataset 2D Video Panoptic Segmentation Challenge, which requires pixel-wise semantic segmentation with instance identity tracking across 100 frames and 5 cameras. The authors build on DVIS++ baseline and propose Hard Region Supervision (HRS) to improve performance by targeting error regions. Additional test-time improvements include model ensemble, output merging, and cross-camera identity linking. The method achieved 0.3547 wSTQ, 0.2071 wAQ, and 0.6075 mIoU, ranking first on all metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Waymo Open Dataset 2D Video Panoptic Segmentation Challenge requires not only semantic segmentation but also instance identity tracking across 100 frames and 5 overlapping cameras. The authors aimed to improve upon their DVIS++ baseline by addressing where it makes mistakes.

Method: The proposed method includes: (1) Hard Region Supervision (HRS) - uses baseline predictions to identify error regions, designs a loss and auxiliary prediction head for these hard regions (auxiliary head removed at test time); (2) Three test-time improvements: two-model ensemble, merging segmenter output into final panoptic map, and cross-camera identity linking.

Result: The entry achieved 0.3547 wSTQ, 0.2071 wAQ, and 0.6075 mIoU on the challenge test set, ranking first on all three metrics. It outperformed the second place by 3.6 wSTQ points and the DVIS++ baseline by 2.4 wSTQ points.

Conclusion: The proposed HRS method effectively improves segmentation by focusing on hard regions, and the test-time augmentation strategies further boost performance. The combination achieves state-of-the-art results on the Waymo Open Dataset 2D Video Panoptic Segmentation Challenge.

Abstract: We describe our winning entry to the Waymo Open Dataset 2D Video Panoptic Segmentation Challenge. The task asks for a semantic class at every pixel of every frame and, for countable objects, an identity that holds across 100 frames and across five overlapping cameras. We build on DVIS++, a cascade of a segmenter, a tracker, and a refiner, as our baseline. We propose hard region supervision (HRS) to improve the baseline. In particular, we use the baseline to define the hard region as where it makes mistakes, and design a loss and an auxiliary prediction head for this region. The auxiliary head is used only in training and removed at test time, so at inference the model trained with HRS has the same architecture as the baseline. In addition, we propose three test-time steps that further improve the results: a two-model ensemble, a merge of the segmenter's output into the final panoptic map, and cross-camera identity linking. On the challenge test set, our entry reaches 0.3547 wSTQ, 0.2071 wAQ, and 0.6075 mIoU, ranking first on all three metrics. It is 3.6 wSTQ points ahead of the second entry and 2.4 points ahead of our DVIS++ baseline.

</details>


### [50] [SpatialCORE: Confidence-Aware Grounded Spatial Reasoning in Large Vision--Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38716)
*Rafi Ibn Sultan,Xiangyu Zhou,Md. Sajid Alam Chowdhury,Chengyin Li,Prashant Khanduri,Marco Brocanelli,Dongxiao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SpatialCORE is a post-training framework that addresses spatial reasoning weaknesses in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) by using the model's own confidence in generated grounding as a learning signal. It reinforces accurate and confident grounding through a self-regulating spatial reward that weights bounding box quality by coordinate-token confidence, with an answer gate linking grounding optimization to final-answer correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial reasoning remains a persistent weakness in LVLMs, especially for questions requiring reasoning over visual space. Existing methods incorporating generated grounding (bounding boxes, masks) optimize only final-answer correctness, allowing correct answers to be rewarded even when the model does not reason from confidently localized task-relevant objects.

Method: SpatialCORE introduces a self-regulating spatial reward that weights each predicted bounding box's matching quality by its coordinate-token confidence, and an answer gate that ties grounding optimization to final-answer correctness. The framework encourages the model to reason from confidently localized task-relevant objects.

Result: SpatialCORE achieves state-of-the-art results among open-source and specialized spatial reasoning models across diverse benchmarks, and transfers effectively in zero-shot settings to unseen data distributions.

Conclusion: The proposed SpatialCORE framework successfully addresses spatial reasoning weaknesses in LVLMs by leveraging the model's own confidence in grounding as a learning signal, demonstrating superior performance and generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have made remarkable progress across visual perception tasks, yet spatial reasoning remains a persistent weakness, especially for questions that require reasoning over visual space. Recent spatial-reasoning methods incorporate generated grounding, where models predict bounding boxes, masks, or other localization outputs for task-relevant objects as part of their reasoning trace. However, these approaches typically optimize final-answer correctness alone, allowing correct answers to be rewarded even when the model does not reason from confidently localized task-relevant objects. We introduce SpatialCORE (Spatially COnfident REasoning), a post-training framework that turns the model's own confidence in generated grounding into a learning signal for spatial reasoning. Its central idea is to reinforce grounding that is both accurate and confident, encouraging the model to reason from confidently localized task-relevant objects. SpatialCORE realizes this through a self-regulating spatial reward that weights each predicted bounding box's matching quality by its coordinate-token confidence. An answer gate further ties grounding optimization to final-answer correctness. SpatialCORE achieves state-of-the-art results among open-source and specialized spatial reasoning models across diverse benchmarks, and transfers effectively in zero-shot settings to unseen data distributions. The source code is available at https://github.com/rafiibnsultan/SpatialCORE.

</details>


### [51] [Soft Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38717)
*Rafi Ibn Sultan,Md. Sajid Alam Chowdhury,Saleh Zare Zade,Chengyin Li,Prashant Khanduri,Marco Brocanelli,Dongxiao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Soft Spatial Reasoning, a post-training framework for LVLMs that addresses "premature discretization" in chain-of-thought spatial reasoning. Instead of committing to single tokens at each step, the method uses continuous soft states by mixing token embeddings, with an adaptive controller (AdaptSoft) that adjusts the degree of softness at each reasoning step based on predictive uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LVLMs perform spatial reasoning through chain-of-thought using discrete language tokens, which requires committing to a single token at each step even when uncertain. This "premature discretization" causes incorrect token selections to propagate errors through subsequent reasoning steps.

Method: The proposed Soft Spatial Reasoning framework introduces soft thinking for spatial tasks. At each intermediate reasoning step, the LVLM forms a continuous soft state by mixing token embeddings rather than selecting a single token. The core component is AdaptSoft, a controller that uses the current hidden state and predictive uncertainty to adapt the degree of softness at each reasoning step. Training uses a gradient-alignment learning objective that provides step-specific learning signals without requiring intermediate reasoning supervision.

Result: Soft Spatial Reasoning outperforms hard and fixed-soft CoT baselines using the same backbone, as well as a range of existing LVLMs, across diverse spatial benchmarks.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that adaptive soft reasoning is more effective than discrete or fixed-soft approaches for spatial reasoning in LVLMs. The gradient-alignment learning objective enables training without intermediate reasoning supervision, making the approach practical for deployment.

Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) commonly perform spatial reasoning through chain-of-thought (CoT), encoding intermediate reasoning as autoregressive sequences of discrete language tokens. Such hard thinking requires committing to a single token at each step, even when the correct spatial interpretation remains uncertain. This early commitment constitutes premature discretization: an incorrect token selection can propagate errors through subsequent reasoning. We propose Soft Spatial Reasoning, a post-training framework that introduces soft thinking for spatial tasks in LVLMs. At each intermediate reasoning step, the LVLM forms a continuous soft state by mixing token embeddings rather than selecting a single token, allowing multiple candidate continuations to influence the next step. The appropriate degree of softness, however, can vary across reasoning steps: retaining multiple candidates may preserve a useful spatial interpretation, but if those candidates imply conflicting spatial relations, mixing them may interfere with subsequent reasoning. At the core of Soft Spatial Reasoning is AdaptSoft, a controller that uses the current hidden state and predictive uncertainty to adapt the degree of softness at each reasoning step. To train AdaptSoft, we introduce a gradient-alignment learning objective that provides a step-specific learning signal for softness control without intermediate reasoning supervision. Across diverse spatial benchmarks, Soft Spatial Reasoning outperforms hard and fixed-soft CoT baselines using the same backbone, as well as a range of existing LVLMs. The source code is available at https://github.com/rafiibnsultan/Soft_Spatial_Reasoning

</details>


### [52] [Matisse: Evidence-Space Reasoning for Active 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38746)
*Xihang Yu,Kaichen Zhou,Lorenzo Shaikewitz,Clément Jambon,Xiao Zhan,Rajat Talak,Luca Carlone*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Matisse is a training-free active 3D reconstruction framework that uses a pretrained generative 3D model to estimate evidential uncertainty and guide both view acquisition and keyframe selection for efficient scene reconstruction under limited computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing active view acquisition methods cannot reason about unseen structure due to uncertainty limited to observed geometry, while long-horizon reconstruction methods retain redundant observations, leading to inefficient use of computational resources.

Method: Matisse estimates Evidential Uncertainty from cross-attention evidence associated with 3D latent tokens, derives Evidential Information Gain based on expected posterior entropy reduction, and uses occlusion-aware object-balanced aggregation for multi-object scenes. Uncertainty propagates through intermediate latents to avoid full reconstruction during planning.

Result: Matisse reduces Chamfer distance by 12.7%, 3.8%, and 9.2% on GSO30, YCB-V, and Replica respectively, relative to the best baseline. It achieves 1.50× end-to-end speedup over the best active reconstruction baseline on GSO30. For keyframe selection, it achieves comparable Chamfer distance using only 14% of input views compared with Stream3D.

Conclusion: Matisse effectively unifies active reconstruction and keyframe selection by leveraging pretrained generative 3D models, demonstrating superior reconstruction quality and computational efficiency across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: How can a 3D reconstruction system acquire and retain useful information to understand the geometry of a scene from partial views under a limited computation budget? Existing active view acquisition methods typically estimate uncertainty over observed or instantiated geometry, limiting their ability to reason about unseen structure, while long-horizon reconstruction methods often retain redundant observations. We introduce Matisse, a training-free framework that unifies active reconstruction and keyframe selection by leveraging evidence provided by a pretrained generative 3D model. Matisse estimates Evidential Uncertainty from cross-attention evidence associated with 3D latent tokens and derives an Evidential Information Gain to guide both view acquisition and keyframe selection based on the expected reduction in posterior entropy. Matisse supports multi-object scenes through occlusion-aware, object-balanced aggregation and propagates uncertainty through intermediate latents to avoid full reconstruction during planning. Matisse reduces Chamfer distance by 12.7%, 3.8%, and 9.2% on GSO30, YCB-V, and Replica, respectively, relative to the best baseline on each dataset, and achieves a $1.50\times$ end-to-end speedup over the best active reconstruction baseline on GSO30 with the same reconstruction backend. In the GSO30 keyframe selection experiment for long-horizon reconstruction, Matisse achieves comparable Chamfer distance using 14% of the input views compared with Stream3D.

</details>


### [53] [Consensus-Aware Multi-Source Fusion for Reference-Guided Camouflaged Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38747)
*Junyang Xia,Luocheng Zhang,Wenwen Pan,Chifeng Zhu,Yang Yang,Xinchun Liu,Jiajun Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes RCDF (Reference-Conditioned Dual-Backbone Fusion), a consensus-aware multi-source fusion framework for reference-guided camouflaged object detection. It addresses challenges of inconsistent reference samples and misaligned visual representations by coupling trainable PVTv2 query features with frozen DINOv3 representations, using reference-conditioned correlation for evidence selection, and aggregating multiple references through cross-reference consensus aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reference-guided camouflaged object detection is challenging because target objects closely resemble their surroundings, reference samples contain inconsistent target cues, and generic visual representations are not aligned with reference-specified targets.

Method: The proposed RCDF framework: 1) Couples trainable PVTv2 query features with frozen DINOv3 representations; 2) Uses reference-conditioned correlation to select foundation-model evidence before multi-scale fusion; 3) Aggregates multiple references through cross-reference consensus aggregation; 4) Injects reference information at semantic depths matched to query features; 5) Employs hierarchical decoding.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. Reference consensus, target-conditioned foundation features, and hierarchical decoding provide complementary improvements under the evaluation protocol.

Conclusion: The RCDF framework successfully addresses the challenges in reference-guided camouflaged object detection through consensus-aware multi-source fusion, with each component (reference consensus, target-conditioned features, hierarchical decoding) contributing complementary improvements.

Abstract: Reference-guided camouflaged object detection aims to segment a target whose visual appearance closely resembles its surroundings by exploiting auxiliary reference samples. The task remains difficult because reference samples contain inconsistent target cues, while generic visual representations are not inherently aligned with the target specified by the references. To handle these problems, we present a consensus-aware multi-source fusion framework. Reference-Conditioned Dual-Backbone Fusion (RCDF) couples trainable PVTv2 query features with frozen DINOv3 representations and uses reference-conditioned correlation to select foundation-model evidence before multi-scale fusion. The framework also aggregates multiple references through cross-reference consensus aggregation and injects reference information at semantic depths matched to the query features. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. The results further show that reference consensus, target-conditioned foundation features, and hierarchical decoding provide complementary improvements under the evaluation protocol. The source code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [54] [Here the World in Stereo: Learning Dynamic Spatial Correspondence for Immersive Joint Video-Audio Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38748)
*Hanmo Chen,Chengcheng Liu,Tianxiao Chen,Zheyu Zhang,Siming Zheng,Jinwei Chen,Xu Yang,Cheng Deng,Bo Li,Peng-tao Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StereoBind addresses the overlooked task of stereo audio generation that corresponds to visual source motion. The framework uses motion tracks to coordinate visual motion and stereo audio through three mechanisms: Visual Motion Binding, Spatial Track Encoder, and Residual Track RoPE. The authors also contribute StereoWorld-29K dataset and StereoWorldBench benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing joint video-audio generation models achieve semantic correspondence and temporal synchronization but overlook stereo audio generation. Applications like AR/VR and interactive gaming require stereo audio for immersive experiences, where sound location must evolve consistently with visual source motion (termed "Dynamic Spatial Correspondence").

Method: StereoBind uses motion tracks to coordinate visual motion and stereo audio through three complementary mechanisms: (1) Visual Motion Binding establishes source-aware audiovisual correspondence; (2) Spatial Track Encoder captures absolute source positions; (3) Residual Track RoPE models relative motion. The authors also construct StereoWorld-29K (large-scale stereo audio-video dataset with paired motion tracks) and StereoWorldBench (benchmark for measuring audiovisual spatial consistency).

Result: StereoBind substantially improves spatial alignment in stereo audio generation over existing models while preserving overall audiovisual quality.

Conclusion: The proposed StereoBind framework effectively addresses dynamic spatial correspondence in stereo audio generation by leveraging motion tracks. The introduced datasets and benchmarks provide valuable resources for future research in this direction.

Abstract: Recent joint video-audio generation models have achieved strong semantic correspondence and temporal synchronization. However, applications such as AR/VR and interactive gaming further require stereo audio to provide an immersive sense, which remains largely overlooked. Effective stereo audio requires the perceived sound location to evolve consistently with the motion of its corresponding visual source. We refer to this property as Dynamic Spatial Correspondence and propose StereoBind, a framework that binds visual source motion to stereo sound generation. StereoBind uses motion tracks to coordinate visual motion and stereo audio through three complementary mechanisms. Visual Motion Binding establishes source-aware audiovisual correspondence, the Spatial Track Encoder captures absolute source positions, and Residual Track RoPE models relative motion. For supervision and evaluation, we construct StereoWorld-29K, a large-scale stereo audio-video dataset with paired motion tracks, and StereoWorldBench for measuring audiovisual spatial consistency. Experiments show that StereoBind substantially improves spatial alignment in stereo audio generation over existing models while preserving overall audiovisual quality.

</details>


### [55] [Agentic Relative Camera Pose Estimation via Learned Ranking and Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38755)
*Zhining Gu,Shangjie Du,Weimin Qiu,Carl Olsson,Ping Liu,Meng Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PoseAgent is an agentic framework for relative camera pose estimation that dynamically selects and verifies pose estimators using learned ranking and verification agents. It profiles image pairs, ranks multiple estimators, executes the top-ranked one, and verifies the pose - falling back to lower-ranked estimators if verification fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No single camera pose estimator is optimal for all challenges (wide baselines, lack of texture, appearance changes, occlusions). Performance varies substantially across benchmarks and individual image pairs, with different estimators exhibiting complementary strengths.

Method: PoseAgent uses three key components: (1) A profiling agent that extracts appearance, semantic, and geometric features (e.g., scene type) from image pairs; (2) A learned ranking agent that predicts the relative competence of multiple pose estimators given the profile; (3) A learned verification agent that estimates pose error for the predicted pose. The top-ranked estimator is executed first, and if verification fails, lower-ranked estimators are adaptively invoked until acceptance or budget exhaustion.

Result: PoseAgent improves AUC@5° by up to 4.2% over the strongest standalone estimator on ARKitScenes, MegaDepth, ScanNet++, and RealEstate10K. On ARKitScenes, it also outperforms VLM-based agents (including a VLM ranker with the same verifier and fallback policy). The verification network predicts pose errors more accurately than prior models.

Conclusion: The learned ranking and verification approach is effective for dynamic pose estimator selection, demonstrating that adaptively orchestrating multiple estimators outperforms any single estimator or naive VLM-based selection.

Abstract: A wide range of approaches have been developed for camera pose estimation, including correspondence-based methods, end-to-end pose regression, and recent 3D geometric foundation models. Our key observation is that no single estimator is optimal for diverse challenges, such as wide baselines, lack of texture, appearance changes, and occlusions. Further analysis reveals substantial performance variation across both benchmarks and individual image pairs, with different estimators exhibiting complementary strengths. We introduce PoseAgent, an agentic framework for relative camera pose estimation that dynamically orchestrates pose estimators through learnable ranking and verification. Given an image pair, a profiling agent first extracts appearance, semantic, and geometric features relevant to pose estimation, e.g., scene type. A learned ranking agent then predicts the relative competence of multiple pose estimators given the image-pair profile. The top-ranked estimator is executed, and its predicted pose is assessed by a learned verification agent that estimates the corresponding pose error. When verification fails, PoseAgent adaptively invokes lower-ranked estimators until a candidate is accepted or the execution budget is reached. For pose verification, our verification network predicts pose errors more accurately than prior models. For pose estimation, PoseAgent improves AUC@5 degree up to 4.2% over the strongest standalone estimator on each of ARKitScenes, MegaDepth, ScanNet++, and RealEstate10K. On ARKitScenes, PoseAgent also outperforms VLM-based agents, which include a VLM ranker with the same verifier and fallback policy. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of our learned ranking and verification.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [56] [When Does a Spoken Agent Have Enough Evidence to Act? The PACT-SLM Contract Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38232)
*Mengzhe Geng*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: PACT-SLM is a controlled evaluation framework for measuring whether speech language models can correctly predict actions at partial speech prefixes, separating action identity from timing. Using a probe on WavLM Base Plus, the study finds 26.03% post-onset accuracy, with action identity and timing proving to be distinct measurable aspects of partial-speech decision behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics for spoken language models only assess final-turn scores, which cannot reveal whether early speech prefixes already support the eventual action. The researchers needed a way to measure when during a speech prefix an action becomes identifiable and predictable.

Method: The study introduces PACT-SLM (Partial Speech Action Contract for Turn Taking in Speech Language Models), which assigns a "first valid action time" and measures action identity and timing separately. The diagnostic contains 80 paired contrast groups from four semantic families with 1,600 prefix predictions across clean and 15 dB noise conditions. A refitted WavLM Base Plus probe was used for evaluation.

Result: The WavLM Base Plus probe achieved 26.03% pooled post-onset semantic-label accuracy (95% CI: 22.14%-29.68%). It exposed actions on 18.99% of pre-onset prefixes and predicted 5.94% of complete trajectories exactly. The probe outperformed matched text, scalar-acoustic, and shuffled-representation probes in post-onset label accuracy but scored at the 96th percentile of 100 within-prefix label permutations. For timing, elapsed time was more onset-exact (36.25% vs 23.13%) but less accurate about action identity (9.92% vs 26.03%).

Conclusion: Action identity and timing measure distinct aspects of partial-speech decision behavior in speech language models. This separation is crucial for evaluating streaming spoken agents that must act before speech is complete.

Abstract: Streaming spoken agents may take an external action before the available speech supports it, yet final-turn scores do not reveal whether each observed prefix supports that action. We introduce the Partial Speech Action Contract for Turn Taking in Speech Language Models (PACT-SLM), a controlled evaluation that assigns a first valid action time and measures action identity and timing separately. The primary diagnostic contains 80 paired contrast groups from four held-out semantic families and 1,600 prefix predictions across clean and 15 dB noise renderings. After correcting a mismatch between randomized branch codes and semantic labels, a refitted WavLM Base Plus probe reaches 26.03% pooled post-onset semantic-label accuracy (95% group-bootstrap interval: 22.14%-29.68%), exposes an action on 18.99% of pre-onset prefixes, and predicts 5.94% of complete trajectories exactly. It exceeds matched text, scalar-acoustic, and shuffled-representation probes in post-onset label accuracy, but its score is at the 96th percentile of 100 within-prefix label permutations and below the 97.5th-percentile reference (26.73%). Elapsed time is more onset-exact than WavLM Base Plus (36.25% vs. 23.13%) but less accurate about action identity (9.92% vs. 26.03%). These results show that action identity and timing measure distinct aspects of partial-speech decision behavior.

</details>


### [57] [Multi-Rate Bandwidth Extension by Token Completion in Neural Audio Codecs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38502)
*Benoît Ginies,Olivier Fercoq,Gaël Richard*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper presents a neural bandwidth extension method that formulates the problem as audio token prediction. The approach uses a transformer-based language model trained on discrete representations from a disentangled neural audio codec, where disentanglement is guided by Harmonic-Percussive decomposition. A novel codec design explicitly accounts for the downstream token prediction task, enabling effective coupling between codec structure and transformer modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bandwidth extension (reconstructing high-frequency components from low-passed audio) is a long-standing problem in audio processing. Recent advances in neural architectures have shown promise, but prior approaches haven't adequately aligned codec design with the generative modeling stage. The key motivation is to improve the quality of reconstructed audio by jointly designing the codec and the generative model.

Method: The method frames bandwidth extension as an audio token prediction problem. A transformer-based language model is trained on discrete representations produced by a disentangled neural audio codec. The disentanglement is guided by Harmonic-Percussive decomposition to highlight spectral structures relevant to bandwidth extension. A novel codec design explicitly accounts for the downstream token prediction task, enabling effective coupling between codec structure and transformer modeling.

Result: The approach yields high-quality reconstructions of the original signal, as measured by both objective metrics and subjective evaluations. The results demonstrate the importance of aligning codec disentanglement and representation learning with the generative modeling stage.

Conclusion: The joint design of codec and transformer modeling significantly improves bandwidth extension performance. This highlights the potential of global, representation-aware design for advancing bandwidth extension, demonstrating that aligning codec disentanglement with generative modeling is crucial for achieving high-quality audio reconstructions.

Abstract: Bandwidth extension, the task of reconstructing the high-frequency components of an audio signal from its low-passed counterpart, is a long-standing problem in audio processing. In this work, we extend recent advances in neural architectures by framing bandwidth extension as an audio token prediction problem. Specifically, we train a transformer-based language model on the discrete representations produced by a disentangled neural audio codec, where the disentanglement is guided by a Harmonic-Percussive decomposition of the input signals, highlighting spectral structures particularly relevant for bandwidth extension. Our approach introduces a novel codec design that explicitly accounts for the downstream token prediction task, enabling a more effective coupling between codec structure and transformer modeling. This joint design yields high-quality reconstructions of the original signal, as measured by both objective metrics and subjective evaluations. These results highlight the importance of aligning codec disentanglement and representation learning with the generative modeling stage, and demonstrate the potential of global, representation-aware design for advancing bandwidth extension.

</details>


### [58] [RAST: Resolution-Aware Privileged Structure Transfer for Low-Resolution Audio Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38780)
*Ji Hwan Park,Gautham Krishna Gudur,Yufei Shen,Dawei Liang,Edison Thomaz*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of human activity recognition (HAR) using audio when there is a mismatch between training (high-resolution audio) and inference (low-resolution audio). The authors propose RAST, a resolution-aware transfer learning framework that leverages HR audio during training while requiring only LR audio at inference, achieving up to 7.8% improvement over LR-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution audio provides rich acoustic information for HAR but incurs substantial energy/storage costs and privacy concerns. Low-resolution audio is more resource-efficient and privacy-preserving but loses essential acoustic cues, causing performance degradation. The authors formulate this as a sensor-resolution privileged learning problem to bridge the HR-LR gap.

Method: RAST compresses HR teacher representations by preserving token-level information and neighborhood structure before performing localized HR-LR alignment. This allows knowledge transfer from HR audio to LR models without requiring HR audio at inference time.

Result: Experiments on SAMoSA and AudioIMU datasets show RAST consistently outperforms LR-only training and direct teacher-transfer baselines, improving LR-only recognition by up to approximately 7.8% while requiring only LR audio at inference.

Conclusion: RAST successfully addresses the training-deployment resolution mismatch in audio-based HAR, enabling resource-efficient and privacy-preserving inference without significant performance loss. The approach bridges the gap between HR training and LR deployment through innovative representation compression and alignment techniques.

Abstract: Audio is increasingly used for human activity recognition (HAR) because it captures object interactions, environmental events, and contextual cues in everyday environments. High-resolution (HR) audio provides rich acoustic information for model development but incurs substantial energy and storage costs and may expose sensitive speech content. Low-resolution (LR) audio offers a more privacy-preserving and resource-efficient alternative for deployment, but reduced sampling rates can remove acoustic cues essential for activity recognition, leading to significant performance degradation. We formulate this training-deployment mismatch as sensor-resolution privileged learning, in which HR audio is available during training, while inference relies exclusively on LR audio. We propose RAST, a resolution-aware transfer framework that compresses HR teacher representations by preserving token-level information and neighborhood structure before performing localized HR-LR alignment. Experiments on the SAMoSA and AudioIMU datasets show that RAST consistently outperforms LR-only training and direct teacher-transfer baselines, improving LR-only recognition by up to approximately 7.8% while requiring only LR audio at inference.

</details>


### [59] [Audio Token Attention Is Predictable Before the Language Model Runs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38878)
*Kyoungjun Park,Yunzhe Li,Lili Qiu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Triage is a method for compressing audio tokens in large audio language models (LALMs) by predicting attention rankings before the language model runs. It uses a linear map to predict which audio tokens will receive less attention, allowing pruning before the LM processes them. The method achieves 2.2-5x compression while maintaining accuracy within 0.04 of full audio, and increases the context window from 21.8 to 62 minutes for Qwen2.5-Omni-3B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio tokens are computationally expensive in LALMs (750-1500 tokens per minute of speech), and existing pruning methods cut after the LM's first layers where audio tokens still receive significant attention. The key insight is that attention an audio token will receive is predictable from encoder output before the LM runs, enabling earlier and more effective pruning.

Method: Triage uses a linear map fitted in closed form (without labels) to predict the attention ranking of audio tokens at ρ≥0.69 on eleven of thirteen LALMs. It cuts audio tokens before the LM runs based on this prediction, then makes a second cut at layer 2 using observed attention to correct the prediction. The method sets compression under two budgets that limit output deviation from the model's full-audio output.

Result: At the conservative budget, word error rate and accuracy stay within 0.04 of full audio. At the aggressive budget, Triage beats every baseline in all twelve transcription cases. On multiple choice, at 2.2-5x compression, it outperforms DART (strongest baseline) by 0.043 in mean accuracy. It raises the audio fitting in Qwen2.5-Omni-3B's context window from 21.8 to about 62 minutes, allowing one GPU to serve 4x more concurrent 5-minute streams.

Conclusion: Triage demonstrates that attention rankings in LALMs are predictable from encoder outputs, enabling effective pre-LM audio token pruning. The method achieves significant compression (2.2-5x) while maintaining transcription accuracy and multiple-choice performance, with practical benefits for deploying LALMs with limited computational resources.

Abstract: A large audio language model (LALM) turns a minute of speech into 750-1,500 tokens and prefills every one. Image-token pruning often cuts after the language model's first layers, where image tokens draw little attention. Audio tokens draw much more attention there, and their ranking is still far from final, so audio needs a ranking before the language model runs. Surprisingly, the attention an audio token will receive across the language model is already linearly predictable from its encoder output, before the language model runs. A linear map, fitted in closed form without labels, predicts this all-layer attention ranking at $ρ\geq .69$ on eleven of thirteen LALMs. Our method, Triage, cuts audio tokens by this prediction and, on multiple choice, cuts again at layer 2, correcting the prediction with the attention observed there. Triage sets its compression without labels, under two budgets that limit how far its output may differ from the model's own full-audio output. At the conservative budget, its word error rate and accuracy stay within .04 of full audio. At the aggressive budget, Triage beats every baseline in all twelve transcription cases. On multiple choice, at 2.2-5x compression, it outperforms DART, the strongest baseline on average, by .043 in mean accuracy. Because it cuts before the language model, it raises the audio that fits in Qwen2.5-Omni-3B's context window from 21.8 to about 62 minutes. At its most compressive point, Triage lets one GPU serve 4x as many concurrent 5-minute streams of that model. Project page: https://audio-triage.github.io

</details>


### [60] [FFASR: Benchmarking Far-Field Automatic Speech Recognition using High-Fidelity Simulated RIRs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38897)
*Shivam Saini,Eric Bezzam,Georg Götz,Alessia Milo,Steinar Guðjónsson,Konstantinos Gkanos,Finnur Pind,Daniel Gert Nielsen*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: FFASR is a new benchmark corpus and leaderboard for evaluating far-field automatic speech recognition (ASR) under challenging acoustic conditions. It addresses the gap between mainstream ASR benchmarks (which emphasize close-microphone speech) and real-world far-field scenarios affected by reverberation, noise, and talker motion. The corpus contains 15,637 utterances from 15 talkers, convolved with room impulse responses from 14 furnished rooms, with test waveforms kept private to prevent training-data contamination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ASR benchmarks primarily use close-microphone speech, but real-world deployment involves far-field conditions with reverberation, noise, and talker motion. This mismatch drives model selection toward systems that perform well on close-talk evaluation but may fail in practical applications. There was a need for a comprehensive benchmark that systematically tests ASR performance across varied acoustic factors.

Method: The researchers created FFASR with nine test conditions, each varying a single acoustic factor: (1) anechoic near-field speech, (2) measured vs simulated office-lab pair, (3) static far-field mixtures at high/mid/low SNR, and (4) moving-talker variants at matched SNR. Speech from 15 talkers was convolved with hybrid wave/geometrical-acoustics room impulse responses from 14 furnished rooms. The test waveforms were never released to prevent training-data contamination.

Result: Mean word error rate (WER) increases from 4.4% in near-field conditions to 41.3% in the static low-SNR condition. Moving talkers add a small but consistent penalty at matched SNR. On the office-lab pair, measured and simulated WER agree to within about 1.7 percentage points on average. These results demonstrate that high-fidelity simulation can serve as a scalable proxy for measured far-field evaluation under the tested conditions.

Conclusion: High-fidelity simulation is a valid and scalable alternative to measured far-field evaluation for ASR under the conditions tested. The FFASR benchmark provides a rigorous framework for evaluating and comparing ASR systems in realistic far-field scenarios, with the potential to drive model development toward better real-world performance.

Abstract: Far-field automatic speech recognition(ASR) degrades under reverberation, noise, and talker motion, yet the benchmarks that drive model selection emphasize close-microphone speech. We present FFASR, a held-out corpus of 15,637 utterances and an open leaderboard spanning nine conditions, each varying a single acoustic factor: anechoic near-field speech, a measured-versus-simulated office-lab pair, static far-field mixtures at high/mid/low signal-to-noise ratio(SNR), and moving-talker variants at matched SNR. Dry speech from 15 talkers is convolved with hybrid wave/geometrical-acoustics room impulse responses from 14 furnished rooms; because the speech is newly recorded and the test waveforms are never released, the corpus resists training-data contamination. Across contemporary systems, mean word error rate (WER) rises from 4.4% near-field to 41.3% in the static low-SNR condition; a moving talker adds a small but consistent penalty at matched SNR; and on the office-lab pair, measured and simulated WER agree to within about 1.7 pp on average. These results support high-fidelity simulation as a scalable proxy for measured far-field evaluation under the conditions we test.

</details>


### [61] [Neural Audio Codec for Robust Audio Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39651)
*Jungwoo Kim,Joonyong Park,Junyoung Koh,Jong-Seok Lee*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates how audio coding (compression) affects audio deepfake detection performance. The authors find that lower bitrates lead to higher detection errors, with different codecs causing asymmetric failures in detecting bona fide vs spoof audio. They propose a forensic-preserving neural audio codec (FP-NAC) that maintains detection accuracy while preserving audio quality, achieving up to 49.8 percentage points improvement in equal error rate (EER) at 0.5 kbps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio deepfake detectors are typically evaluated on uncompressed audio, but real-world audio often undergoes low-bitrate coding (compression). This mismatch creates a significant problem as detectors may fail when applied to compressed audio encountered in practical applications.

Method: The authors use a mixed-pair protocol to isolate codec-induced changes in both bona fide and spoof audio. They evaluate multiple codecs (DAC, EnCodec, X-Codec) at various bitrates across different detectors. Based on findings, they propose FP-NAC - a forensic-preserving neural audio codec that fine-tunes a pretrained codec using a detector-guided objective while preserving its native hard quantization path and bitrate.

Result: 1) Higher detection errors occur at lower bitrates
2) Different codecs show asymmetric failures: low-rate DAC and EnCodec mainly degrade bona fide detection, while X-Codec shows stronger spoof-side limitation
3) FP-NAC reduces EER by up to 49.8 percentage points compared with original DAC at 0.5 kbps
4) FP-NAC maintains comparable reconstruction quality
5) Although supervised by only one detector, FP-NAC improves performance across multiple detectors

Conclusion: The study highlights the importance of considering audio coding in deepfake detection evaluation. FP-NAC demonstrates that forensic transparency can be achieved as a codec design objective alongside perceptual quality, improving detection generalization across multiple detectors without sacrificing audio quality.

Abstract: Audio deepfake detectors are typically evaluated on uncompressed audio, although real-world audio often undergoes low-bitrate coding. In this work, we investigate how audio coding affects deepfake detection across codecs, bitrates, and detectors, finding higher errors at lower rates. A mixed-pair protocol isolates codec-induced changes in bona fide and spoof audio, revealing asymmetric, codec-dependent failures: low-rate DAC and EnCodec mainly degrade bona fide detection, whereas X-Codec shows a stronger spoof-side limitation. Motivated by these, we propose a forensic-preserving neural audio codec (FP-NAC), which fine-tunes a pretrained codec using a detector-guided objective while preserving its native hard quantization path and bitrate. On ASVspoof 2019 LA, FP-NAC reduces EER by up to 49.8~pp compared with the original DAC at 0.5~kbps while maintaining comparable reconstruction quality. Although supervised by only one detector, FP-NAC improves performance across multiple detectors, highlighting forensic transparency as a codec design objective alongside perceptual quality. Our codes are available at https://github.com/kjungwoo03/FP-NAC.

</details>


### [62] [How Reliable Are Predicted MOS for Reproducing Human System-Level Preferences in Speech Enhancement?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39032)
*Nahomi Kusunoki,Tsubasa Ochiai,Naohiro Tawara,Marc Delcroix,Naoyuki Kamo,Tetsuji Ogawa,Shoko Araki*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces System-level Preference Accuracy (SPA) to evaluate whether predicted Mean Opinion Scores (MOS) can reliably determine which speech enhancement system is better. The study reveals that current MOS prediction models have significant limitations in practical system comparisons, with even the best models disagreeing with human judgments about 23% of the time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While MOS prediction models are widely used to evaluate speech enhancement systems, they are typically assessed by correlation with human-rated MOS. However, high correlation does not guarantee that the models will agree with humans on which system performs better. This gap motivates the introduction of SPA to directly evaluate preference agreement.

Method: The authors introduce SPA as a metric that directly evaluates whether predicted and human-rated MOS yield the same system preferences. They systematically evaluate three settings: (1) single prediction models, (2) ensembling multiple models, and (3) domain adaptation techniques. Experiments are conducted in both closed condition (known target systems/speakers) and open condition (unknown target systems/speakers).

Result: SPA varies substantially across single prediction models, ranging from 9.4% to 76.8%. Even the best model still disagrees with human judgments in approximately 23% of system comparisons. Ensembling yields only limited improvement. Domain adaptation substantially improves SPA in the closed condition but provides only modest gains in the more practical open condition.

Conclusion: Predicted MOS alone can lead to unreliable conclusions in practical SE system comparison. SPA can reveal errors that correlation-based evaluation alone does not expose. The authors recommend caution when using predicted MOS for system-level comparisons in practical settings.

Abstract: We investigate whether predicted Mean Opinion Scores (MOS) can reliably support system-level comparisons of speech enhancement (SE) methods by introducing system-level preference accuracy (SPA). Although MOS prediction models are widely used to evaluate SE systems, their performance is typically assessed by correlation with human-rated MOS, which does not guarantee agreement on which system is better. SPA addresses this gap by directly evaluating whether predicted and human-rated MOS yield the same system preferences. Using SPA, we systematically evaluate three settings: single prediction models, ensembling, and domain adaptation. Through experiments, SPA varies substantially across single prediction models, from 9.4% to 76.8%. Even the best model disagrees with human judgments in approximately 23% of system comparisons. Ensembling yields only limited improvement, while domain adaptation tends to substantially improve SPA in the closed condition but brings only modest gains in the more practical open condition, where neither the target systems nor the speakers are known. These results suggest that SPA can reveal errors correlation-based evaluation alone does not expose, and that predicted MOS alone can lead to unreliable conclusions in practical SE system comparison.

</details>


### [63] [Game Sound-Effect Completion with Event-Level Transformation Hints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39044)
*Xinrui Jiang,Heng Yu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A method for generating coherent sound effect sets for game character skins, using a fine-tuned latent inpainting model based on Stable Audio 3 that can balance between retaining original audio characteristics and applying requested redesigns through adjustable transformation hints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating sound effects for new game character skins requires maintaining distinct acoustic identities while preserving gameplay-event roles. The challenge is completing a coherent set of related sounds with varying degrees of redesign needed.

Method: The task is formulated as completion conditioned on base-skin audio, completed target assets, and textual design description. A pipeline collects, processes, and aligns corresponding events across League of Legends skins. A latent inpainting model is fine-tuned from Stable Audio 3's pretrained audio prior, using a signed soft retention mask that encodes available audio and specifies the balance between retention and redesign for each missing event.

Result: Experiments on held-out skins show improved reconstruction over evaluated general-purpose audio editors. Target-derived hints further improve paired similarity, with three-level hints retaining most of the benefit of continuous guidance.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully generates coherent sound effect sets for game skins, effectively balancing acoustic identity preservation with requested redesigns through a flexible transformation hint mechanism.

Abstract: Creating sound effects for a new game-character skin requires a distinct acoustic identity while preserving gameplay-event roles. The challenge is to complete a coherent set of related sounds whose required degrees of redesign differ. We formulate this task as completion conditioned on base-skin audio, completed target assets, and a textual design description. We develop a pipeline to collect, process, and align corresponding events across League of Legends skins. Building on Stable Audio 3's pretrained audio prior, we fine-tune a latent inpainting model to jointly complete missing events. A signed soft retention mask encodes available audio and an adjustable transformation hint for each missing event, specifying the requested balance between retention and redesign. Experiments on held-out skins show improved reconstruction over the evaluated general-purpose audio editors. Target-derived hints further improve paired similarity, with three-level hints retaining most of the benefit of continuous guidance.

</details>


### [64] [SCIC: Scope- and Codebook-Aware Instruction Conditioning for Speaker-Adapted Expressive TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39088)
*Longyu Lu,Zongwei Du,Mengtao Xing,Zhuoqun Liu,Zifan Guan,Meiguang Jin,Junfeng Ma*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces Speaker-Relative Inline Prosody Control and Scope- and Codebook-Aware Instruction Conditioning (SCIC) for long-form live-streaming TTS, enabling clause-level relative prosodic control through a Temporal Instruction Router and Tag-Specific Codebook Weighting, with multi-reward GDPO post-training to optimize control accuracy while preserving quality metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-form live-streaming TTS requires context-dependent prosody and paragraph-level coherence, but existing instruction-based TTS systems use global/uniform conditions with limited explicit control over clause-level relative prosodic changes.

Method: 1) Speaker-Relative Inline Prosody Control: Pitch, Energy, Speed instructions target clauses relative to preceding clauses; Pause uses absolute duration. 2) Scope- and Codebook-Aware Instruction Conditioning (SCIC): combines Temporal Instruction Router for frame-level tag activation with Tag-Specific Codebook Weighting over residual codebooks. 3) Multi-reward GDPO post-training for joint optimization of control and quality. Analysis of Qwen3-TTS RVQ codebooks revealed Energy concentrates in early residual codebooks while Pitch accumulates across deeper prefixes.

Result: SCIC improves speaker-relative Pitch and Energy control over standard instruction fine-tuning using text-token tags. Multi-reward GDPO improves control accuracy while preserving CER and speaker similarity. In long-form synthesis, SCIC produces a more distinct paragraph-level expressive hierarchy than speaker-adapted SFT without instructions.

Conclusion: The proposed SCIC framework enables fine-grained, clause-level prosodic control in long-form TTS synthesis, achieving better control accuracy while maintaining speech quality and speaker similarity through codebook-aware conditioning and multi-reward optimization.

Abstract: Long-form live-streaming TTS requires context-dependent prosody and paragraph-level coherence. However, many existing instruction-based TTS systems use global or uniform conditions, providing limited explicit control over clause-level relative prosodic changes. We introduce Speaker-Relative Inline Prosody Control, where each Pitch, Energy, or Speed instruction targets a clause relative to the preceding clause from the same speaker, while Pause uses an absolute duration interval. In codec-based TTS, Speed and Pause affect sequence length, whereas Pitch and Energy rely on residual codebooks. By analyzing Qwen3-TTS RVQ codebooks, we find that Energy concentrates in early residual codebooks, whereas Pitch accumulates across a deeper prefix. We therefore propose Scope- and Codebook-Aware Instruction Conditioning (SCIC), combining a Temporal Instruction Router for frame-level tag activation with Tag-Specific Codebook Weighting over residual codebooks. SCIC improves speaker-relative Pitch and Energy control over standard instruction fine-tuning using text-token tags. We further apply multi-reward GDPO post-training to jointly optimize control and quality, improving control accuracy while preserving CER and speaker similarity. In long-form synthesis, SCIC produces a more distinct paragraph-level expressive hierarchy than speaker-adapted SFT without instructions. Audio demos are available at: https://taoliveaigc.github.io/SCIC/

</details>


### [65] [Training-Free Affinity Fusion of Neural and Embedding-Based Speaker Diarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39162)
*Yehoshua Dissen,Joseph Keshet,Eduard Golshtein*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces Training-Free Affinity Fusion (TFAF), a method to combine speaker embeddings and neural diarization systems by integrating the neural diarizer's speaker structure into an embedding-based system through continuous affinity matrix fusion, requiring no additional training or shared embedding space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speaker embedding-based and neural diarization systems exploit complementary forms of speaker information, but their intermediate representations are not directly compatible, limiting the potential synergy between these two approaches.

Method: TFAF uses the neural speaker partition to condition local speaker representations, constructs a continuous affinity matrix, and combines it with embedding-based acoustic affinity before a single global clustering step. The method requires no additional training, shared embedding space, speaker-label alignment, or hard transfer of speaker count.

Result: Experiments on AMI and CALLHOME show consistent DER (Diarization Error Rate) improvements over both constituent systems. On AMI, fusion also improves speaker-attributed transcription. Ablations indicate the neural speaker partition accounts for most of the gain, while retaining continuous embedding-based affinities provides additional benefit over hard partition fusion.

Conclusion: TFAF successfully integrates complementary speaker information from two different diarization paradigms without requiring additional training or architectural changes, achieving consistent improvements across multiple evaluation datasets.

Abstract: Speaker diarization systems based on speaker embeddings and neural diarization exploit complementary forms of speaker information, but their intermediate representations are not directly compatible. We introduce Training-Free Affinity Fusion (TFAF), which integrates the speaker structure inferred by a neural diarizer into an embedding-based diarization system. The neural speaker partition is used to condition local speaker representations, from which we construct a continuous affinity matrix and combine it with the embedding-based acoustic affinity before a single global clustering step. The method requires no additional training, shared embedding space, speaker-label alignment, or hard transfer of the neural diarizer's speaker count. Experiments on AMI and CALLHOME show consistent DER improvements over both constituent systems; on AMI, fusion also improves speaker-attributed transcription. Ablations show that the neural speaker partition accounts for most of the gain, while retaining the continuous embedding-based affinities provides additional benefit over hard partition fusion.

</details>


### [66] [UniAE-MoE: A Unified Audio Encoder via Mixture of Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39199)
*Shengbo Cai,Zhisheng Zhang,Zichao Nie,Jing Peng,Jingran Xie,Zhiyong Wu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: UniAE-MoE is a unified audio encoder for Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) that combines encoders from Qwen2-Audio and Audio-Flamingo 3 using a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture. It incorporates SwiGLU with shared experts, a two-stage instruction-tuning strategy, and task-specific data scaling (TSDS) to achieve state-of-the-art performance across speech, music, and general audio domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Audio Language Models rely on effective audio encoders for multi-task performance, but existing encoders may not adequately model cross-domain audio representations. This work aims to create a unified audio encoder that excels across diverse downstream understanding tasks.

Method: 1) Integrate encoders from Qwen2-Audio and Audio-Flamingo 3 which demonstrate superior downstream capabilities; 2) Improve encoder using SwiGLU with shared experts to decouple encoder networks; 3) Introduce two-stage instruction-tuning strategy for better adaptation to diverse tasks; 4) Propose task-specific data scaling (TSDS) technique to enhance understanding capabilities.

Result: UniAE-MoE achieves a score of 0.802 on the XARES-LLM benchmark, attaining state-of-the-art performance. It also delivers top-tier performance in the official Interspeech 2026 Audio Encoder Capability Challenge, demonstrating robust generalization across diverse audio tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed UniAE-MoE effectively enables unified audio understanding across speech, music, and general audio domains, validating the effectiveness of the combination of MoE architecture, SwiGLU with shared experts, two-stage instruction-tuning, and TSDS for multi-task audio encoding.

Abstract: Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) rely on effective audio encoders for multi-task performance. We introduce UniAE-MoE, a unified audio encoder designed to model cross-domain audio representations and achieve outstanding downstream understanding performance via a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture. Specifically, we explore mainstream audio encoders and integrate those from Qwen2-Audio and Audio-Flamingo 3, which demonstrate superior downstream capabilities. To facilitate effective model fusion, we improve our encoder using SwiGLU with shared experts to decouple encoder networks, and we further introduce a two-stage instruction-tuning strategy to better adapt the model to diverse downstream tasks. Moreover, we propose the task-specific data scaling (TSDS) technique to enhance \tool's understanding capabilities. On the XARES-LLM benchmark, UniAE-MoE attains a score of 0.802, achieving state-of-the-art performance. It also delivers top-tier performance in the official Interspeech 2026 Audio Encoder Capability Challenge, further demonstrating robust generalization across diverse audio tasks. Together, these results validate the effectiveness of \tool for unified audio understanding across speech, music, and general audio domains.

</details>


### [67] [Who Said What, and Will It Be Remembered? Evaluating Persistent Speaker Attribution Across Meetings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39344)
*Shantanu Vispute,Aditya Mishra,Siddhartha Saxena*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces Speaker Identified cpWER (SI-cpWER) to evaluate persistent speaker attribution across multiple meetings, addressing the limitation that existing metrics cannot track whether the same person retains one identity over time. The benchmark evaluates commercial cascades, academic baselines, and a proposed system (ThyVoice) on CHiME-8 and CHiME-6 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing meeting-transcription metrics either ignore speakers or remap anonymous speakers independently in each recording, making it impossible to measure whether the same person maintains a consistent identity across multiple meetings. This is critical for speech transcripts used as long-term memory that must preserve both words and stable speaker identities.

Method: The authors propose SI-cpWER, which scores a corpus under one global speaker-ID assignment rather than treating each recording independently. They evaluate five commercial diarize-then-identify cascades, two open academic baselines, and their end-to-end system ThyVoice (which repairs overlap and gates evidence for voiceprint creation/update) on the full 129-meeting CHiME-8 NOTSOFAR evaluation set in clean and noise-augmented forms, plus CHiME-6.

Result: ThyVoice achieves lower SI-cpWER than every evaluated commercial cascade in all three conditions (clean, noise-augmented, full panel). The lowest mean SI-cpWER in the full panel is 47.13 (ThyVoice) versus 54.75 for the next best system. Complementary diagnostics (lexical, diarization, per-recording attribution, speaker-clustering) characterize upstream error surfaces.

Conclusion: Persistent speaker attribution must be evaluated directly in systems that reuse conversations across time. The ranking of commercial systems changes when requiring persistent identity, demonstrating the importance of this evaluation paradigm.

Abstract: Speech transcripts used as long-term memory must preserve both words and stable speaker identities. Existing meeting-transcription metrics either ignore speakers or remap anonymous speakers independently in each recording, so they cannot measure whether the same person retains one identity across meetings. We evaluate persistent speaker attribution with Speaker Identified cpWER (SI-cpWER), which scores a corpus under one global speaker-ID assignment. The benchmark covers five commercial diarize-then-identify cascades, two open academic baselines, and ThyVoice on the full 129-meeting CHiME-8 NOTSOFAR evaluation set in clean and noiseaugmented form, plus CHiME-6. ThyVoice is our end-to-end reference system; it repairs overlap and gates the evidence used to create and update voiceprints. Requiring persistent identity changes the commercial ranking: ThyVoice records lower SI-cpWER than every evaluated commercial cascade in all three conditions and the lowest mean in the full panel, 47.13 versus 54.75 for the next system. Complementary lexical, diarization, per-recording attribution, and speaker-clustering diagnostics characterize upstream error surfaces in the final attributed record. These results show why persistent attribution must be evaluated directly in systems that reuse conversations across time.

</details>


### [68] [From Speech to Editable Concepts: Probing Emotion Recognition with Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39453)
*Hezhao Zhang,Thomas Hain*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper adapts concept bottleneck models from image classification to speech emotion recognition (SER) using large language models (LLMs). The approach examines how individual predictions depend on transcripts, acoustic descriptions, and speaker attributes. Experiments on CREMA-D, IEMOCAP, and MELD datasets reveal that LLMs are strongly biased toward transcript information in zero-shot settings, which significantly lowers performance. Fine-tuning removes this bias, and removal experiments show that aggregate performance metrics alone fail to capture the impact on individual predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech emotion recognition performance remains poor on many datasets despite combining multiple modalities. LLMs have attracted interest for SER due to their ability to process diverse inputs with instructions, but direct audio input raises explainability concerns. Concept bottleneck models address explainability in image classification but have not been adapted to SER. This work aims to examine how predictions depend on different input modalities (transcripts, acoustic descriptions, speaker attributes) using concept bottleneck approaches.

Method: The study adapts concept bottleneck models to SER by examining how predictions depend on transcripts, acoustic descriptions, and speaker attributes. Three LLMs were tested on three datasets (CREMA-D, IEMOCAP, MELD) with concepts extracted by separate tools. Experiments included: (1) zero-shot evaluation to measure transcript bias, (2) fine-tuning to remove bias, and (3) concept removal experiments to assess individual prediction changes.

Result: Key findings: (1) In zero-shot setting, LLMs are strongly biased toward transcript on scripted corpora, lowering Macro-F1 from 27.8 to 5.8 on CREMA-D. (2) Fine-tuning removes this bias; transcript raises Macro-F1 from 41.8 to 45.1. (3) Removing speech rate changes 48% of Neutral predictions to Disgust on CREMA-D. (4) Removing intensity level on MELD changes predictions despite little change in Macro-F1. (5) Aggregate performance changes alone do not capture the effects of concept removal on individual predictions.

Conclusion: Concept bottleneck models can be adapted to speech emotion recognition to improve explainability. LLMs exhibit significant bias toward transcript information in zero-shot settings, which can be corrected through fine-tuning. The study demonstrates that relying solely on aggregate performance metrics (like Macro-F1) is insufficient to understand how concept removal affects individual predictions, highlighting the need for more granular analysis in SER model evaluation.

Abstract: Speech emotion recognition (SER) is the task of assigning emotion labels to utterances. Early systems relied on acoustic features, whereas recent approaches combine multiple modalities, most commonly speech and text. Still, performance remains poor on many datasets. Large language models (LLMs) have therefore attracted interest for SER, as they can process diverse inputs jointly with instructions. However, direct audio input raises questions of explainability. To address similar questions in image classification, concept bottleneck models were introduced. This work adapts concept bottlenecks to SER to examine how individual predictions depend on transcripts, acoustic descriptions and speaker attributes. Experiments test three LLMs on CREMA-D, IEMOCAP and MELD, with concepts extracted by separate tools. On scripted corpora, LLMs are strongly biased towards the transcript in the zero-shot setting, which lowers Macro-F1 from 27.8 to 5.8 on CREMA-D. Fine-tuning removes this bias, and the transcript raises Macro-F1 from 41.8 to 45.1. Removing speech rate changes 48% of Neutral predictions to Disgust on CREMA-D; removing intensity level on MELD changes predictions despite little change in Macro-F1. These findings show that aggregate performance changes alone do not capture the effects of concept removal on individual predictions.

</details>


### [69] [Ghost in the Encoder: Decodable Artist Identity Representations in Lyrics-to-Song Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39552)
*Arhan Vohra,Choenden Kyirong,Laura Ibáñez-Martínez,Martín Rocamora*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Researchers investigate how text-to-song models encode artist identity in their internal representations, demonstrating that a trained model (ACE-Step 1.5) can be probed to identify artists from lyrics alone without explicit identifiers, revealing a latent conditioning channel that bypasses prompt-side safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-song generation models can imitate specific artists or regurgitate entire songs from training data, but the internal representations giving rise to this behavior are poorly understood. Prior work on generative audio interpretability focused on semantic concepts like genre or time signature, leaving the mechanism of artist imitation unexplored.

Method: Controlled case study using ACE-Step 1.5 model spanning 2,000 songs across 100 artists. Linear probing was used to decode artist identity from model internal activations, examining whether artist information is encoded in lyric representations and how this signal propagates to the diffusion backbone during inference.

Result: Artist identity can be identified from the model's internal activations when given song lyrics alone, without any additional identifiers. This conditioning signal propagates from the lyric encoder to the diffusion backbone during inference, indicating that lyrics constitute an artist-level conditioning channel.

Conclusion: Lyrics serve as an implicit artist conditioning channel that bypasses prompt-side replication safeguards. This highlights the need for latent-space analysis to audit what generative music models have implicitly learned from training data, revealing hidden conditioning channels that current safeguards do not address.

Abstract: Text-to-song generation models can be prompted to imitate specific artists or regurgitate entire songs from their training data. Although these phenomena have been documented behaviorally on small datasets, little is known about the internal representations that may give rise to them. Prior interpretability work on generative audio has focused on locating semantic concepts such as genre or time signature within model activations. In this work, we show that a trained model can be probed for linearly decodable representations of artist identity from song lyrics alone, without any additional identifiers. Through a controlled case study of ACE-Step 1.5 spanning 2,000 songs across 100 artists, we demonstrate that the artist associated with a given set of lyrics can be identified within the model's internal activations, and that this conditioning signal propagates from the lyric encoder to the diffusion backbone during inference. These findings indicate that lyrics constitute an artist-level conditioning channel not addressed by prompt-side replication safeguards.
