More broadly, our work highlights how latent-space analysis can be used to audit what generative music models have implicitly learned from their training data.

</details>


### [70] [SE-ADD: Self-Evolving Audio Deepfake Detection with Mistake-Driven Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39679)
*Rong Wan,Wei Xie,Jiaxi Li,Wenwu Wang,Lu Yin,Yiliao Song,Xilu Wang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SE-ADD is a self-evolving audio deepfake detection framework that uses low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to iteratively adapt audio language models via mistake-driven supervision. It leverages the model's own verdicts and self-generated forensic cues to provide targeted supervision, particularly for misclassified samples, enabling effective generalization to unseen attacks in evolving spoofing environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audio deepfake detection (ADD) methods rely on predefined supervision from ground-truth labels or verified forensic rationales, but overlook the model's own mistakes as learning signals. In real-world deployments, new spoofing attacks emerge while previous attacks persist, creating an evolving spoofing environment that demands continuous adaptation.

Method: SE-ADD employs a self-evolving framework with mistake-driven supervision. It uses low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to fine-tune audio language models. All training samples receive direct authenticity supervision, while misclassified samples receive additional cue-augmented supervision. The supervision evolves as the updated model regenerates verdicts and forensic cues, creating an iterative adaptation loop.

Result: Experiments on two audio language models (Qwen2-Audio and MOSS-Audio) demonstrate significant improvements. For Qwen2-Audio, EER reduced from 36.72% to 7.52% (79.5% relative reduction). For MOSS-Audio, EER reduced from 19.93% to 3.97% (80.1% relative reduction).

Conclusion: SE-ADD effectively generalizes to unseen attacks in evolving spoofing environments by learning from the model's own mistakes. The mistake-driven supervision approach with LoRA adaptation provides targeted learning signals that significantly improve detection performance on emerging spoofing attacks.

Abstract: Audio deepfake detection (ADD) must remain effective when new spoofing attacks emerge after deployment. Emerging audio language model (ALM)-based ADD methods are built on predefined supervision from ground-truth labels or verified forensic rationales. However, this paradigm overlooks an ALM's own mistakes, which indicate where targeted supervision is most needed. To this end, we first introduce evolving spoofing environments for ALM-based ADD, where a new attack becomes dominant while previously observed attacks persist. Motivated by the above learning-from-mistakes perspective, we further propose SE-ADD, a self-evolving framework that iteratively adapts an ALM via low-rank adaptation (LoRA) using mistake-driven supervision built from its verdicts and self-generated forensic cues. All training samples receive direct authenticity supervision, while misclassified ones receive additional cue-augmented supervision. As verdicts and cues are regenerated by the updated ALM, the resulting supervision evolves accordingly. Experiments on two ALMs demonstrate the effectiveness of SE-ADD in generalizing to unseen attacks, reducing the equal error rate (EER) from $36.72\%$ to $7.52\%$ for Qwen2-Audio and from $19.93\%$ to $3.97\%$ for MOSS-Audio.

</details>


### [71] [Synthetic Speech Attribution via Prototypical Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39722)
*Viola Negroni,Paolo Bestagini,Stefano Tubaro*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes using prototype-based networks (ProtoPNet) as an interpretable alternative to black-box neural networks for synthetic speech attribution - identifying which generative system created a speech signal. The method is evaluated on the MLAAD dataset under three conditions (closed-set, cross-lingual, open-set), achieving competitive or improved performance while providing example-based explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current synthetic speech attribution methods rely on black-box neural networks that lack interpretability. There's a need for models that can explain their decisions by relating predictions to representative training examples.

Method: Adapt ProtoPNet (prototype-based network) to spectrogram-based speech representations. Predictions are made by comparing input to representative training prototypes rather than through opaque neural computations.

Result: Prototype-based reasoning achieves competitive or improved attribution performance compared to baseline methods across closed-set, cross-lingual, and open-set conditions on the MLAAD dataset, while enabling interpretable example-based explanations.

Conclusion: Interpretability and high performance can be jointly achieved in synthetic speech attribution through prototype-based modeling, offering a practical alternative to black-box approaches.

Abstract: Synthetic speech attribution aims to identify the generative system responsible for a speech signal, but current approaches typically rely on black-box neural networks that provide limited insight into their decisions. This work investigates prototype-based networks as an interpretable alternative, where predictions are grounded in comparisons with representative training examples. We adapt ProtoPNet to spectrogram-based speech representations and evaluate the proposed framework on the MLAAD dataset under closed-set, cross-lingual, and open-set conditions. Experiments show that prototype-based reasoning achieves competitive or improved attribution performance compared with the baseline while enabling example-based explanations. These results highlight that interpretability and performance can be jointly achieved in synthetic speech attribution through prototype-based modeling.

</details>


### [72] [SEAR: Spoofing Evidence-Grounded Audio Reasoning Benchmark for Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39847)
*Rong Wan,Suliu Qin,Jiaxi Li,Wei Xie,Wenwu Wang,Xiaolong Han,Lu Yin,Xilu Wang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses the critical gap in evaluating audio language models (ALMs) for audio deepfake detection (ADD) by introducing SEAR, a benchmark that assesses acoustic evidence reasoning rather than just verdict plausibility, and BAEA, an agent that equips ALMs with controlled acoustic tools to improve evidence-based reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for ALM-based ADD only assess verdict correctness or rationale plausibility, without verifying whether the models actually ground their decisions in verifiable acoustic evidence. This creates a gap where models can produce convincing rationales that aren't actually supported by the underlying audio signals.

Method: 1) SEAR benchmark: A four-task AQA (Answering and Question Answering) benchmark with four evaluation tasks focused on acoustic evidence identification/quantification, deepfake detection, and forensic rationale generation. 2) BAEA agent: Equips a frozen ALM with controlled acoustic tools under two policies: fixed (pre-determined evidence acquisition) or adaptive (dynamic evidence acquisition). 3) Experiments: Tested with six different ALMs on two evaluation partitions.

Result: 1) A clear gap exists between plausible rationales and verifiable acoustic evidence reasoning across all six ALMs. 2) BAEA-Fixed improves both final verdicts and forensic rationales on both evaluation partitions. 3) Controlled interventions demonstrate that misleading evidence degrades both detection accuracy and grounding performance.

Conclusion: The research demonstrates the importance of grounding audio deepfake detection in actual acoustic evidence rather than relying on plausible-sounding rationales. BAEA with fixed evidence-acquisition policy provides a promising approach to improve both detection accuracy and reasoning quality. The SEAR benchmark provides a rigorous framework for evaluating acoustic evidence reasoning in ALM-based ADD systems.

Abstract: Audio language models (ALMs) are increasingly used for audio deepfake detection (ADD), yet existing benchmarks assess their verdicts or rationale plausibility without verifying the underlying acoustic evidence. To address this issue, we first introduce spoofing evidence-grounded audio reasoning (SEAR), a four-task AQA benchmark to evaluate ALM-based ADD through acoustic evidence identification and quantification, deepfake detection, and forensic rationale generation. We further propose a bona-fide-based acoustic evidence agent (BAEA), which equips a frozen ALM with controlled acoustic tools under \textsc{fixed} or \textsc{adaptive} evidence-acquisition policies. Experiments with six ALMs reveal a clear gap between plausible rationales and verifiable acoustic evidence reasoning, while BAEA-\textsc{Fixed} improves final verdicts and forensic rationales on both evaluation partitions. Controlled interventions further show that misleading evidence degrades both detection and grounding performance.

</details>


### [73] [MeanVoiceFlow2: Joint Optimization of Mean Flow and Content Encoder for Fast One-Step Zero-Shot Voice Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40087)
*Takuhiro Kaneko,Hirokazu Kameoka,Kou Tanaka,Yuto Kondo*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MeanVoiceFlow2 is a one-step voice conversion model that accelerates inference by ~9x while improving perceptual quality, through joint optimization of a flow-based conversion module and efficient content encoder, using conversion distillation and diffusion-GAN training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: One-step flow-matching models like MeanVoiceFlow offer efficient inference for voice conversion, but their reliance on computationally intensive content encoders creates a performance bottleneck that limits practical deployment.

Method: MeanVoiceFlow2 jointly optimizes a flow-based conversion module and a computationally efficient content encoder through: (1) conversion distillation using MeanVoiceFlow as teacher, (2) reconstruction of real data, and (3) diffusion-GAN training with sample mixing and teacher-guided conditioning augmentation for enhanced realism and disentanglement.

Result: On zero-shot VC tasks, MeanVoiceFlow2 achieved higher perceptual quality and approximately 9× faster inference speed compared to MeanVoiceFlow, while maintaining comparable speaker similarity.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully balances computational efficiency with speech quality, making one-step voice conversion more practical for real-time applications without sacrificing speaker similarity.

Abstract: Flow-matching approaches to voice conversion (VC) have gained attention owing to their high speech quality and strong speaker similarity. Among them, one-step models such as MeanVoiceFlow are particularly attractive because they enable efficient inference; however, their reliance on a computationally intensive content encoder remains a bottleneck. We therefore propose MeanVoiceFlow2, a framework that jointly optimizes a flow-based conversion module and a computationally efficient content encoder. The model is trained through conversion distillation using MeanVoiceFlow and the reconstruction of real data. We further incorporate diffusion-GAN training with sample mixing and teacher-guided conditioning augmentation to enhance realism and disentanglement. Experiments on zero-shot VC showed that MeanVoiceFlow2 achieved higher perceptual quality and approximately $9\times$ faster inference than MeanVoiceFlow while maintaining comparable speaker similarity. Audio samples are available at https://www.kecl.ntt.co.jp/people/kaneko.takuhiro/projects/meanvoiceflow2/.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [74] [Improving OCR Faithfulness via Gated and Attenuated On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38282)
*Baode Wang,Zuming Huang,Kexuan Ren,Jun Huang,Wei Chu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GAD-RL introduces adaptive teacher supervision for post-training vision-language models on OCR tasks, dynamically adjusting distillation strength based on student performance to improve transcription faithfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models may rewrite anomalous text in images into linguistically plausible but incorrect expressions, compromising OCR transcription faithfulness. Fixed teacher supervision becomes progressively less effective as the student improves.

Method: GAD-RL uses a frozen teacher conditioning on reference transcriptions and student-generated prefixes. It disables distillation for response groups with task reward ≥0.95 and attenuates distillation strength as group-mean reward increases. It also weights forward KL by the student's probability of the teacher's Top-1 token.

Result: On Qwen3.5-2B, GAD-RL achieves 59.92% Micro Recall on CHAOS-Bench, surpassing GRPO and GRPO+OPD (fixed-weight) by 8.45 and 4.43 percentage points respectively, while achieving an Overall score of 91.18 on OmniDocBench v1.6.

Conclusion: Adaptive regulation of teacher supervision during joint post-training, based on student's current task performance and local distributions, effectively improves OCR transcription faithfulness in vision-language models.

Abstract: Vision-language models may rewrite anomalous text in images into linguistically plausible expressions, compromising OCR transcription faithfulness. Sequence-level task rewards and local teacher guidance are complementary, but guidance from the same teacher may not remain equally effective as the student improves. Offline analysis shows that supervision from a fixed teacher becomes progressively less favorable as the student improves, both across training checkpoints and across response groups with different task rewards. Motivated by this observation, we introduce GAD-RL, which adaptively regulates teacher supervision during joint post-training according to the student's current task performance and local distributions. A frozen teacher conditions on reference transcriptions and student-generated prefixes. GAD-RL disables distillation for response groups containing an output with task reward at least 0.95 and continuously attenuates distillation strength as group-mean reward increases. It also weights forward KL by the student's probability of the teacher's Top-1 token, moderating local auxiliary updates when student support for that candidate is low. On Qwen3.5-2B, GAD-RL achieves 59.92% Micro Recall on CHAOS-Bench, surpassing GRPO and GRPO+OPD (fixed-weight) by 8.45 and 4.43 percentage points, respectively, while achieving an Overall score of 91.18 on OmniDocBench v1.6.

</details>


### [75] [AREX-2: Advancing Self-Improving Agents through Long-Horizon Reflective Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38288)
*Hongjin Qian,Chaofan Li,Kun Luo,Wenqing Wei,Jianlyu Chen,Shuqi Lu,Yuyang Hu,Hongwang Xiao,Hui Wang,Chaozhuo Li,Qiwei Ye,Zhicheng Dou,Defu Lian,Zheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AREX-2 is a self-improving LLM agent built on Qwen3.8-27B that iteratively refines solutions at test time through reflection and long-horizon execution capabilities. Trained on synthetic improvement trajectories from ML and algorithmic programming tasks, it achieves strong results across multiple benchmarks including MLE-bench Lite (81.8), GAIA (92.2), and DeepSearchQA (93.8), demonstrating continued improvement with increased iteration budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advance the self-improving capability of LLM agents, defined as the ability to iteratively refine solutions at test time. The key insight is that reflection and long-horizon execution are domain-agnostic skills that can be learned from well-suited supervised scenarios.

Method: Synthesize long-horizon improvement trajectories from machine learning and algorithmic programming tasks (domains with verifiable feedback), then train the agent on this data. The agent combines two complementary capabilities: reflection (producing better solutions) and long-horizon execution (maintaining effectiveness over many rounds).

Result: Strong results on multiple benchmarks: MLE-bench Lite (81.8), Frontier-CS (70.7), BrowseComp (84.0), HLE (52.6), GAIA (92.2), DeepSearchQA (93.8). The agent keeps improving as its budget of rounds grows, demonstrating effective self-improvement.

Conclusion: Long-horizon reflective data is an effective route toward self-improving agents. The domain-agnostic nature of reflection and long-horizon execution enables transfer across diverse tasks from deep research to programming.

Abstract: We present AREX-2, an effort to advance the self-improving capability of LLM agents, which we define as the ability to iteratively refine a solution at test time. This ability rests on two complementary capabilities: reflection, which produces a solution better than the current one, and long-horizon execution, which keeps the iteration effective over many rounds. We hypothesize that both capabilities are domain-agnostic, and can therefore be learned in scenarios that are well suited for supervision. Accordingly, we synthesize long-horizon improvement trajectories from machine learning and algorithmic programming tasks, two domains that offer verifiable feedback and reward sustained iteration. Trained on this data, our agent, built on Qwen3.8-27B, achieves strong results on MLE-bench Lite (81.8) and Frontier-CS (70.7), transfers to deep research with 84.0 on BrowseComp, 52.6 on HLE, 92.2 on GAIA, and 93.8 on DeepSearchQA, and keeps improving as its budget of rounds grows. These results show that long-horizon reflective data is an effective route toward self-improving agents.

</details>


### [76] [MoFlow: Multi-Objective Agentic Workflow Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38294)
*Yining Lu,Aurelie Lozano,Xi Yang,Naoki Abe,Yu Deng,Meng Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MoFlow generates agentic workflows optimized across multiple objectives (accuracy, cost, latency, robustness, consistency) by formulating workflow generation as a multi-objective Markov decision process and solving it with Convex-Hull Monte Carlo Tree Search. A single search covers the Pareto front, enabling workflow retrieval for any preference without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing workflow generation methods optimize accuracy alone or a fixed weighted sum of objectives, requiring retraining from scratch when user preferences change. This inflexibility motivates the need for a single generator that can cover the entire Pareto front of trade-offs.

Method: MoFlow formulates workflow generation as a multi-objective Markov decision process and solves it using Convex-Hull Monte Carlo Tree Search with optimistic set-valued backups. Each node stores a set of reachable trade-offs rather than one weighted score, enabling a single search to approximately cover the Pareto front.

Result: MoFlow achieves the highest average hypervolume against six strong baselines on six benchmarks spanning mathematics, code, and question answering. The evaluation setup favored baselines (rerun for each testing preference), yet MoFlow still outperformed.

Conclusion: MoFlow successfully enables a single generator to cover the entire Pareto front of trade-offs, allowing workflow retrieval for any preference without retraining. This addresses the key limitation of existing methods that require retraining when preferences change.

Abstract: We study the generation of agentic workflows that jointly optimize multiple objectives, such as accuracy, cost, latency, robustness, and consistency. Existing methods for workflow generation typically optimize accuracy alone or a weighted sum of objectives, so each trained generator commits to one fixed trade-off and must be retrained from scratch when preferences change. To alleviate this, we propose MoFlow, which generates workflows optimized across varied preferences. Specifically, MoFlow formulates workflow generation as a multi-objective Markov decision process and solves it by leveraging Convex-Hull Monte Carlo Tree Search with optimistic set-valued backups, where every node stores a set of reachable trade-offs rather than one weighted score. A single search thus approximately covers the Pareto front, from which MoFlow can return a workflow for any preference by lookup without retraining. We evaluate MoFlow against six strong baselines on six benchmarks spanning mathematics, code, and question answering. Since the baselines are single-scalar optimizers by design, an apples-to-apples comparison is difficult. We instead adopt an evaluation setup that favors the baselines, in that they are rerun for each testing preference, which MoFlow never sees. Even under this stringent setup, MoFlow achieves the highest average hypervolume.

</details>


### [77] [AI Agents are Vulnerable to Radicalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38296)
*Ozgur Can Seckin,Shalmoli Ghosh,Alessandro Flammini,Kristina Lerman,Maria Elizabeth Grabe,Filippo Menczer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether large language models can radicalize each other through simulated conversations, comparing two pathways: resonance (reinforcing existing beliefs) and persuasion (introducing new beliefs). Using target LLMs role-playing human personas and influencer LLMs, the study finds both mechanisms increase belief extremity, with resonance producing stronger effects than persuasion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs are known to influence human beliefs, it remains unclear whether AI agents can manipulate each other. This gap is critical given the rise of personalized AI agents and multi-agent AI ecosystems that could be vulnerable to radicalization.

Method: The researchers simulated conversations between two LLMs: a target agent role-playing a human persona with specific demographic and psychological attributes, and an influencer agent attempting to make the target's beliefs more extreme. They examined radicalization via two pathways—resonance (reinforcing pre-existing beliefs) and persuasion (promoting initially unimportant beliefs)—using affective and behavioral metrics.

Result: Both resonance and persuasion mechanisms radicalized the target, but resonance produced consistently stronger effects. Different influence tactics (sycophancy, unverified claims) yielded varying radicalization levels, though not consistently across all metrics. Notably, resonance effects propagated to related beliefs, indicating interconnected belief structures within AI agents.

Conclusion: AI agents are susceptible to radicalization, particularly when influence messages align with existing beliefs. This raises significant concerns about the vulnerability of personalized AI agents and multi-agent AI ecosystems, highlighting the need for safeguards against LLM-to-LLM radicalization.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can influence people's beliefs, yet little is known about whether and how they can manipulate each other. To investigate this, we simulate conversations between two agents: a target LLM that role-plays a human persona based on demographic and psychological attributes, and an influencer LLM that aims to make the target's beliefs more extreme. We examine radicalization along two pathways: resonance, where the influencer reinforces a target's pre-existing belief, and persuasion, where the influencer promotes a belief the target initially considers unimportant. Across affective and behavioral metrics, we find that both mechanisms radicalize the target. However, resonance produces consistently stronger effects than persuasion. Different influence tactics, such as using sycophancy and unverified claims, produce different levels of radicalization, but not consistently across metrics. We further show that resonance propagates to related beliefs, suggesting interconnected belief structures within AI agents. These findings indicate that AI agents are susceptible to radicalization, particularly when messages align with their existing beliefs, raising concerns about the vulnerability of personalized AI agents and multi-agent AI ecosystems.

</details>


### [78] [CARAT: Do Materials LLMs Reason or Recite?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38340)
*Jiajun Wu,Jian Yang,Zixiang Ni,Zhenzhu Li,Bin Chong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CARAT is a benchmark that tests whether materials LLMs genuinely reason about crystal structures or simply copy answers from their input context. The authors discovered that apparent reasoning performance often stems from copied information rather than actual structural reasoning, and after rigorously closing shortcuts, they found the frozen model still repeats relations without using them (95.6%), though this becomes learnable with matched supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that accuracy metrics cannot distinguish between genuine structural reasoning and mere copying of information already present in the input. When a materials LLM answers questions about crystal structure, the structural description often contains the very field it is scored against, making it impossible to tell if the model reasoned from the structure or just copied the answer.

Method: The authors created CARAT with eight matched views holding question and gold answer fixed, named each structural relation separately in GraphSpace, and added matched fine-tuning, answer masking, evidence injection, paired inference, and a rule that can withhold claims. They also "attacked their own benchmark" by identifying shortcuts (rules that skip the link and read the list directly) and rebuilt the benchmark until eleven such shortcuts sat near chance.

Result: Three key findings: (1) On hardest families, the grounded view outperforms formula inputs by 17.3 points; (2) GraphSpace beats plain periodic graph by 19.3 points, but this margin combines two effects - where plain rendering carries everything needed it's only 1.96 points, where it omits those fields entirely it's 46.7 points; (3) After closing shortcuts, the frozen model quotes the link yet answers the same when redirected on 95.6% of paired cases (repeats relation without using it), matched supervision reaches 99.8%, and deleting the link drops it to 23.4% (below the 27.0% best shortcut achieves).

Conclusion: The authors demonstrate that materials LLMs often appear to reason about crystal structures but actually rely on copying information from their input. Even after rigorously closing shortcuts in the benchmark, models still exhibit behavior suggesting they repeat relations without genuinely using them. The work highlights the importance of carefully designed benchmarks to distinguish true reasoning from pattern matching, and suggests that apparent performance gains may primarily measure what baseline methods lacked rather than genuine reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: When a materials LLM answers a question about crystal structure, does it reason from the structure or copy an answer already printed in its input? Accuracy cannot tell: a structural description often prints the very field it is scored against. CARAT holds question and gold answer fixed across eight matched views, names each structural relation separately in GraphSpace, and adds matched fine-tuning, answer masking, evidence injection, paired inference, and a rule that can withhold claims. First, on the benchmark's hardest families the grounded view is worth 17.3 points over formula inputs. Second, we turn that scrutiny on ourselves. GraphSpace beats a plain periodic graph by 19.3 points, but that margin is two effects at once: where the plain rendering carries everything the question needs it is 1.96 points, and where it omits those fields entirely, 46.7 points. The headline mostly measures what the baseline lacked, not how evidence is presented. Third, we attack our own benchmark. A rule that skips the link and reads the list directly answers four of seven hardened families, so we rebuilt it until eleven such shortcuts sat near chance. The frozen model quotes that link yet answers the same when we redirect it, on 95.6% of paired cases: it repeats the relation without using it. After matched supervision it reaches 99.8%, and deleting the link drops it to 23.4%, below the 27.0% the best shortcut reaches: both steps are learnable.

</details>


### [79] [Examining Variation in How Guided AI Tutors Resolve Student Impasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38346)
*Bakhtawar Ahtisham,Kirk Vanacore,Alessandra Napoli,Josh Arens,Ksenia Ionova,Clayton Cohn,Shima Salehi,Rene Kizilcec*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study addresses the "assistance dilemma" in AI tutoring systems—when to help stuck students without hindering learning. Using 20,462 student turns from an LLM chemistry tutor, researchers identified 6,630 impasse instances (conceptual errors, uncertainty, help-seeking) and simulated three tutoring conditions. Key findings: each additional impasse turn reduces recovery odds by 12.7%; questioning strategies lose effectiveness over time while directly addressing errors becomes more beneficial; after failed scripted questions, addressing errors (39.8% recovery) outperforms repeating questions (28.1%). The study proposes using impasse depth and type as real-time signals for graduated assistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a critical challenge in AI tutoring: the Assistance Dilemma—balancing productive struggle against wheel spinning (prolonged frustration). With AI tutors increasingly using guardrails to restrict direct answers, there's limited understanding of how these systems should behave when students remain stuck. The study aims to identify actionable dialogue signals that can trigger adaptive assistance.

Method: The study analyzed 20,462 student turns from 1,260 authentic sessions with a guided LLM chemistry tutor, identifying 6,630 impasse turns of three types: conceptual errors, expressed uncertainty, and help-seeking. Three tutoring conditions were simulated (baseline, no-direct-answer, guided tutor) and applied to 150 impasse samples. Trajectory analysis examined how impasse depth affects recovery outcomes, with statistical modeling (AOR) measuring the impact of intervention strategies.

Result: 1) Each additional impasse turn lowers odds of next-turn recovery by 12.7% (AOR=0.873, p<.001)
2) Questioning strategies lose effectiveness as impasses persist (scripted question × depth AOR=0.78; follow-up × depth AOR=0.83)
3) Addressing student errors becomes more beneficial over time (AOR=1.14)
4) After failed scripted questions, repeating them yields 28.1% recovery vs 39.8% when addressing errors instead
5) Early dropouts occur during recursive concept elicitation before task execution
6) Baseline tutor provided direct answers in 50.7% of cases; no-direct-answer asked follow-ups always; guided tutor showed varied contextual responses

Conclusion: Impasse depth and type serve as observable, turn-level dialogue signals for real-time analytics to trigger graduated, state-sensitive assistance in AI tutoring systems. The findings suggest tutoring strategies should adapt dynamically: use questioning early in impasses, but shift to direct error correction as stuckness persists. This provides a framework for adaptive pedagogical interventions in AI-driven educational technologies.

Abstract: When a student is stuck, a tutor faces the assistance dilemma: help given too early can hinder productive struggle, while help withheld too long leaves the student in a frustrating, persistent impasse (i.e., wheel spinning). Generative AI tutors increasingly use guardrails restricting answer-giving, yet little is known about how such tutors behave once an impasse persists. We analyze 20,462 student turns from 1,260 authentic sessions with a guided LLM chemistry tutor, identifying 6,630 impasse turns of three major types: conceptual errors, expressed uncertainty, or help-seeking. We then used these impasses to simulate three tutoring conditions to study variation in AI tutor guidance through impasses: baseline, no-direct-answer, and guided tutor. For a sample of 150 impasses, prompt specificity changed pedagogy: a baseline tutor provided the answer directly in 50.7% of responses, a no-direct-answer tutor asked a follow-up question every time, and the guided tutor responded in a wide variety of ways depending on the context. We then analyzed impasse trajectories in authentic interactions, finding that each additional impasse turn lowered the odds of next-turn recovery by 12.7% (AOR = 0.873, p < .001), and early dropouts were caught in recursive concept elicitation before reaching execution. The benefit of questioning decayed as impasses persisted (scripted question x depth AOR = 0.78; follow-up x depth AOR = 0.83), whereas addressing the student's error grew more beneficial (AOR = 1.14); after a failed scripted question, repeating it was followed by recovery in 28.1% of cases, compared with 39.8% when the tutor addressed the error instead. For learning analytics, these findings identify impasse depth and type as observable, turn-level dialogue signals that analytics can use to trigger graduated, state-sensitive assistance in real time.

</details>


### [80] [Beyond Mode Collapse: Generating Diverse Synthetic Expert Conversations via Generative Flow Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38359)
*Sumit Asthana,Michael Ion,Kevyn Collins Thompson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes using Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) to generate diverse, high-quality synthetic conversation data for post-training LLMs. The method trains GFlowNets on latent conversation structures using Gaussian mixture density over key interaction features, enabling sampling of expert strategies proportional to their prevalence in training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for generating synthetic training data for LLMs (direct prompting or conditioning on end-use scenarios) produce low diversity data that collapses onto dominant modes. There's a need for synthetic data that captures the full diversity of expert strategies and decisions in conversations.

Method: Train GFlowNets to generate latent conversation structure using Gaussian mixture density over key interaction features (confusion episode dynamics, scaffolding directive balance). This enables sampling expert strategies in proportion to their prevalence in training data.

Result: Across two domains (tutoring and emotional support dialogues), GFlowNet-based synthetic data generation offers better balance of fidelity, mode coverage, and authenticity than reinforcement-learning and end-to-end LLM baselines, without copying training data. On three downstream outcome prediction tasks, classifiers trained on GFlowNet-generated conversations provide stronger training signal than competitive synthesis baselines.

Conclusion: GFlowNets provide an effective approach for generating diverse, high-quality synthetic conversation data that captures expert strategies while maintaining fidelity and authenticity. This method outperforms existing baselines and provides better training signals for downstream prediction tasks.

Abstract: High quality synthetic data is central to post training LLMs for adaptive AI applications that represent the diverse expert strategies and decisions in conversations. Prompting LLMs directly or conditioning them on end use scenarios yields low diversity data that collapses onto dominant modes. We propose a method to generate diverse high quality synthetic data using Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets). We show that training GFlowNets to generate latent conversation structure using a Gaussian mixture density over key interaction features (e.g., confusion episode dynamics, scaffolding directive balance) enables sampling expert strategies in proportion to their prevalence in the training data. Across two structurally distinct domains, tutoring and emotional support dialogues, our GFlow based synthetic data generation approach offers a better balance of fidelity, mode coverage and authenticity than reinforcement-learning and end to end LLM baselines, without copying training data. Evaluated on three downstream outcome prediction tasks, classifiers trained on synthetic GFlowNet generated conversations provide a stronger training signal than competitive synthesis baselines.

</details>


### [81] [Can an AI Agent Rediscover a Blaschke-Curve Invariant?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38369)
*Yunus E. Zeytuncu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a case study of using AI to rediscover a mathematical theorem about degree-four Blaschke products. An AI agent fitted a homogeneous cubic to polygon sides that predicts 480 lines with extremely high accuracy (RMS residual ~10^-17), but the experiment did not establish an advantage over traditional polynomial fitting methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a controlled environment for studying AI-assisted mathematical rediscovery, where an AI agent can discover mathematical relationships without being told the target theorem, and to develop a protocol for separating conjecture, numerical validation, and proof in AI-assisted mathematics.

Method: An AI agent receives numerical coordinates of six pair-lines from 80 boundary configurations of a degree-four Blaschke product. The agent generates and tests geometric hypotheses, rejects incorrect ones, and fits a homogeneous cubic to polygon sides. The cubic's coefficients are used to predict lines from 80 unseen parameter values. A deterministic degree-search baseline is also performed for comparison.

Result: The fitted homogeneous cubic predicts 480 lines with RMS residual of 8.88×10^-17, demonstrating extremely high numerical accuracy. Discovery-set diagonals provide out-of-fit consistency check. However, a separate one-configuration run reports insufficient evidence for invariance, and the post-review baseline also recovers the cubic, so no advantage over polynomial fitting is established.

Conclusion: The single-instance case study provides a protocol for separating conjecture, numerical validation, and proof in AI-assisted mathematical discovery. However, explicit limitations exist concerning agent metadata, prior knowledge, and reproducibility. The experiment does not demonstrate an advantage over traditional polynomial fitting methods.

Abstract: We study generalized Blaschke curves as a controlled environment for AI-assisted mathematical rediscovery. For one fixed degree-four Blaschke product, an agent receives numerical coordinates of the six pair-lines determined by each of 80 boundary configurations. The target theorem is withheld from the task instructions. The saved research log reports rejected geometric hypotheses and a homogeneous cubic fitted to polygon sides. Its frozen coefficients predict 480 lines from 80 unseen parameter values, with a recorded RMS scale-free residual of $8.88\times10^{-17}$. Discovery-set diagonals provide an out-of-fit consistency check, not a fully held-out test. A separate one-configuration run reports insufficient evidence for invariance. A post-review deterministic degree-search baseline also recovers the cubic, so the experiment does not establish an advantage over polynomial fitting. We present this single-instance case study as a protocol for separating conjecture, numerical validation, and proof, with explicit limitations concerning agent metadata, prior knowledge, and reproducibility.

</details>


### [82] [Self-Evolving Harness on Multiple Tasks with the Agent as Its Own Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38372)
*Qiankai Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for self-evolving harnesses where the same frozen language model acts as both solver and proposer. The model first solves tasks and then edits the harness that runs it. Evolution uses tasks from five benchmarks across different domains, with strict separation between training and held-out tasks. The framework achieves 4.48 point improvement on in-distribution benchmarks and 12.64 points on out-of-distribution benchmarks, surpassing or matching Codex.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world tasks come from many domains, so both the evolution and evaluation of agent harnesses should cover a diverse range of tasks. Existing methods use separate proposers and evolve harnesses for each benchmark individually, limiting generalization.

Method: Two-stage training approach: (1) Multi-task pretraining using tasks from five benchmarks in different domains, and (2) Continual training on specific out-of-distribution benchmarks. The same frozen model serves as both solver and proposer, directly editing the harness based on run records. Strict separation between training and held-out tasks ensures generalization measurement.

Result: Starting from a 49-line seed harness: Stage 1 improves average score by 4.48 points on in-distribution benchmarks and 12.64 points on out-of-distribution benchmarks, surpassing Codex on the former and matching it on the latter. Stage 2 (continual training on Claw-Eval) raises score from 66.17 to 68.06, exceeding Codex.

Conclusion: The proposed framework achieves generalization across diverse domains through self-evolution. The same model can effectively improve its own harness without separate human-designed proposers. Key emergent mechanisms include output truncation, history compaction, and independent review.

Abstract: A harness is the code around a language-model agent that organizes prompts, calls tools, manages context, and controls execution. As models grow stronger, recent work has begun to let agents improve their own harnesses, a line of work known as self-evolving harnesses. In most existing methods, a separate proposer running on a human-designed harness modifies the solver's harness, and a separate harness is evolved for each benchmark. Real-world tasks come from many domains, so both the evolution and the evaluation of a harness should cover a diverse range of tasks. We propose a framework close to recursive self-improvement: the same frozen model, on the same version of the harness, first solves tasks as the solver and then, as the proposer, reads the complete run records and directly edits the harness that runs it. Each evolution batch draws tasks from five benchmarks in different domains. To measure generalization, training and held-out tasks are strictly separated, and we additionally evaluate on five out-of-distribution benchmarks never used during evolution. We frame the evolution process as deep-learning training with two stages, multi-task pretraining and continual training. Starting from a 49-line seed harness, the harness obtained at the end of the first stage improves the average score by 4.48 points on the in-distribution benchmarks and by 12.64 points on the out-of-distribution benchmarks, surpassing Codex on the former and matching it on the latter. In the second stage, continued evolution on Claw-Eval, one of the out-of-distribution benchmarks, further raises the score on that benchmark from 66.17 to 68.06, exceeding Codex. We also provide an in-depth analysis of the mechanisms that emerged during evolution, including output truncation, history compaction, and independent review.

</details>


### [83] [Aligned Data Can Induce Misalignment via Context Confusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38379)
*Yavuz Bakman,Duygu Nur Yaldiz,Baris Askin,Swastik Roy,Morteza Ziyadi,Salman Avestimehr,Sai Praneeth Karimireddy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a new post-training phenomenon called "context confusion" where aligned training data in one context induces misaligned behavior in different contexts. This differs from emergent misalignment as it causes narrow, domain-specific misalignment rather than general misalignment. The authors demonstrate this across three domains (gender equality, privacy, physical safety) and propose solutions including targeted alignment data or in-context learning. They also provide a mechanistic explanation based on representational shifts during fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the observation that alignment is context-dependent - a recommendation that is appropriate in one context may be inappropriate in another. Current alignment practices don't account for this context dependency, leading to potential misalignments after fine-tuning.

Method: The authors demonstrate context confusion across three domains (gender equality, privacy, physical safety) through experiments. They show that general alignment data doesn't effectively reduce this phenomenon, but targeted alignment data for the misaligned domain or in-context learning examples can substantially reduce it. They also provide mechanistic analysis by observing representational shifts during fine-tuning.

Result: Context confusion causes narrow misalignment (in contrast to emergent misalignment which causes broad misalignment). It is not effectively reduced by general alignment data injection. However, it can be substantially reduced by either: (1) including targeted alignment data for the misaligned domain, or (2) providing in-context learning examples during inference. The mechanistic explanation is that queries from different domains undergo similar representational shifts during fine-tuning, causing cross-domain behavior transfer.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that it is difficult to predict a model's alignment state after training by inspecting training data alone, highlighting the importance of comprehensive post-training alignment evaluations. The context-dependent nature of alignment makes this a challenging problem that requires careful evaluation across multiple contexts.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are frequently updated for various use cases, where filtering out misaligned training samples is a common practice for preventing post-update misalignment. However, alignment is inherently context-dependent: a recommendation that is aligned in one context may be inappropriate in another. For example, in response to the question "What should a researcher do with the research data?", recommending that the researcher preserve the data for reproducibility is aligned. In contrast, recommending data saving in response to "What should a mobile-app developer do with users' sensitive data?" may be inappropriate from a privacy perspective. Starting from this observation, we identify a post-training phenomenon where aligned training induces misaligned behavior in other contexts. We call this phenomenon **context confusion**. We demonstrate context confusion across three domains: (1) Gender Equality, (2) Privacy, and (3) Physical Safety. We further show that context confusion causes narrow misalignment, in contrast to emergent misalignment, and is not effectively reduced by injecting general alignment data, but can be substantially reduced by including targeted alignment data for the misaligned domain or providing in-context learning examples during inference. Lastly, we provide a mechanistic explanation of *context confusion*. We observe that queries from different domains can undergo similar representational shifts during the fine-tuning. Consequently, a query from a different domain may activate the same behavioral feature learned during fine-tuning, which causes the behavior to transfer to a context where it is misaligned. Based on our findings, we argue that it is difficult to predict the alignment state of a model after training by inspecting the training data alone, which highlights the importance of comprehensive post-training alignment evaluations.

</details>


### [84] [Fine-Tuning Diffusion Language Models with Context Selection and Target Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38385)
*Loay Mualem,Lluís Pastor-Pérez,Vinh Tong,Andrei Manolache,Tanja Bien,Steffen Staab,Mathias Niepert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GoldiMask introduces a novel token masking strategy for fine-tuning discrete diffusion language models that uses a submodular objective to select context tokens while weighting prediction targets based on their learning potential and benefit from the selected context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform random masking in discrete diffusion language model training does not optimally balance the trade-off between providing useful context for prediction and preserving valuable tokens as learning targets. The interaction between context selection and target weighting was not explicitly accounted for in prior approaches.

Method: GoldiMask uses a submodular objective to approximately maximize token selection for context, balancing the benefit of revealing tokens against their value as prediction targets. The remaining tokens are then weighted based on how they benefit from the selected context and their remaining learning potential. The method was evaluated across three backbone models and three training datasets.

Result: GoldiMask achieved the highest average accuracy in most evaluated settings across three backbones and three training datasets, with gains on both reasoning and code generation tasks. Component ablations confirmed that both context selection and target weighting contribute to the gains. Additionally, GoldiMask reduced decoding iterations on GSM8K and MATH-500 under confidence-threshold parallel decoding while maintaining comparable accuracy at higher confidence thresholds.

Conclusion: GoldiMask demonstrates that principled token masking via submodular optimization can significantly improve fine-tuning performance of discrete diffusion language models. The approach successfully balances context provision and target preservation, leading to improved accuracy across diverse tasks while also enabling more efficient decoding.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning of discrete diffusion language models masks some response tokens and trains the model to recover their original values from the visible context. The masking pattern therefore determines both the context available to the model and the tokens it learns to predict. Uniform random masking does not explicitly account for the interaction between these choices. We introduce GoldiMask, which selects tokens to reveal as context by approximately maximizing a submodular objective. This objective uses model signals to balance the benefit of revealing tokens against their value as prediction targets. GoldiMask then weights the remaining targets according to how they benefit from the selected context and their remaining learning potential. Across three backbones and three training datasets, GoldiMask achieves the highest average accuracy in most evaluated settings, demonstrating gains on both reasoning and code generation. Component ablations show that both context selection and target weighting contribute to the gains. GoldiMask also reduces decoding iterations on GSM8K and MATH-500 under confidence-threshold parallel decoding, while maintaining comparable accuracy at higher confidence thresholds.

</details>


### [85] [Decode-Latency Feedback Prefill: A Model-Free Controller and Its Generalization Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38386)
*Gaurav Agarwal,Ashish Garg,Isha Singhal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DLFP (Decode-Latency Feedback Prefill) is a model-free controller that dynamically adjusts prefill chunk sizes to reduce inter-token latency interference in concurrent autoregressive inference. It uses observed interval as proportional feedback to resize prefill chunks while keeping isolated prefills unrestricted. The method shows 27.7% P99 inter-token latency reduction on Qwen3-0.6B but fails to generalize to larger models due to scheduler-call interval not accurately representing GPU iteration time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Concurrent autoregressive inference creates interference when prefilling newly arrived long prompts delays tokens for requests already decoding. Fixed prefill chunks reduce this but require manual tuning for different models, hardware, load, and latency objectives. A self-adjusting solution is needed.

Method: DLFP is implemented in vLLM as a model-free controller that adjusts only prefill work overlapping active decodes. After a guarded scheduling cycle, it uses observed interval as proportional feedback to resize the next prefill chunk. Isolated prefills remain unrestricted. Evaluation uses open-loop Poisson arrivals, exact token accounting, raw request traces, and NVIDIA telemetry.

Result: On Qwen3-0.6B (BF16, one A100 80GB), three paired 100-request trials show 24.8%, 30.1%, and 28.2% P99 inter-token latency reduction (mean 27.7%, 95% CI 21.0%-34.3%) with exact output agreement, no failures, and unchanged SLO compliance. However, mean P99 time to first token increases 34.8% (but remains within SLO). The mechanism fails to generalize to Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-32B, or two-GPU tensor-parallel configurations.

Conclusion: DLFP provides significant inter-token latency reduction for small models but fails for larger models due to asynchronous scheduler-call interval being only a proxy for completed GPU iteration time. This negative result defines the boundary of the contribution and motivates future work on completion-timed controllers for concurrent CPU and on-device inference.

Abstract: Concurrent autoregressive inference creates a fundamental interference problem: prefilling a newly arrived long prompt can delay tokens for requests that are already decoding. Fixed prefill chunks reduce this interference, but the best chunk size depends on the model, hardware, load, and latency objective. We introduce Decode-Latency Feedback Prefill (DLFP), a model-free controller that changes only prefill work that overlaps active decodes. After a guarded scheduling cycle, DLFP uses the observed interval as proportional feedback to resize the next prefill chunk; isolated prefills remain unrestricted.
  We implement DLFP in vLLM and evaluate it with open-loop Poisson arrivals, exact token accounting, raw request traces, and NVIDIA telemetry. On Qwen3-0.6B in BF16 on one A100 80 GB GPU, three paired 100-request trials reduce P99 inter-token latency by 24.8%, 30.1%, and 28.2% (mean 27.7%, paired 95% confidence interval 21.0% to 34.3%) with exact output agreement, no failures, and unchanged SLO compliance. The benefit is not free: mean P99 time to first token increases 34.8% while remaining inside the declared SLO.
  Crucially, the mechanism does not generalize to Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-32B, or a two-GPU tensor-parallel configuration. We trace the failure to an asynchronous scheduler-call interval that is only a proxy for completed GPU iteration time. This negative result defines the boundary of the contribution and motivates a completion-timed controller for concurrent CPU and on-device inference. We do not claim mobile-device performance; the present work is a reproducible proof-of-concept and generalization study.

</details>


### [86] [MetaPersona: Task-Grounded Synthetic Populations from Empirical Social Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38392)
*Jinyi Ye,Yuangang Li,Chenxiao Yu,Preyashi Poddar,Priyanka Dey,Longtian Ye,Zihan Wang,Xiyang Hu,Emilio Ferrara,Yue Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaPersona addresses the cold-start problem in LLM social simulation persona creation by introducing a dataset of 11,000+ empirical human-subjects studies and a framework that constructs persona dependency graphs from literature priors. The method reduces persona construction costs while showing strong performance on some downstream tasks but mixed results on others.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that existing persona creation methods for LLM social simulations lack a principled basis for determining which attributes to include and how to assign values. This leads to synthetic populations that may misrepresent demographic composition, latent attributes, and dependency structures that shape downstream behavior. The fundamental gap is the absence of empirical grounding in persona construction.

Method: The approach involves three key components: (1) MetaPersona-DB - a dataset of 11,000+ empirical human-subjects studies annotated with task-relevant variables, reported relationships, and aggregate-level population statistics; (2) MetaPersona framework - retrieves task-relevant evidence, constructs literature-derived persona dependency graphs, and samples synthetic populations from empirical priors linking demographics, latent attributes, and outcomes; (3) MetaPersona-Studio - a prototype interactive interface for empirically grounded persona generation.

Result: Results vary by task and model: MetaPersona performs strongly on misinformation belief and AI-tool sentiment tasks, while results on income redistribution are mixed. The method reduces persona-construction cost to under $0.5 per task using GPT-5.2. Evaluation was conducted across three downstream case studies, three baselines, and three frontier models.

Conclusion: The work presents a data-driven solution to the persona cold-start problem in LLM social simulations. While the approach shows promise for certain tasks (misinformation belief, AI-tool sentiment), the mixed results on income redistribution suggest that empirical grounding alone may not guarantee performance across all task types. The significant cost reduction and the interactive interface make this a practical contribution to persona-based simulation research.

Abstract: Personas used to seed LLM social simulations face a cold-start problem: existing methods lack a principled basis for deciding which attributes to include and how to assign their values. As a result, synthetic populations may misrepresent the demographic composition, latent attributes, and dependency structure that shape downstream behavior. We introduce MetaPersona-DB, a dataset of 11,000+ empirical human-subjects studies annotated with task-relevant variables, reported relationships, and aggregate-level population statistics. Building on this resource, we propose MetaPersona, a framework that retrieves task-relevant evidence, constructs literature-derived persona dependency graphs, and samples synthetic populations from empirical priors linking demographics, latent attributes, and outcomes. Across three downstream case studies, three baselines, and three frontier models, results vary by task and model: MetaPersona performs strongly on misinformation belief and AI-tool sentiment, while results on income redistribution are mixed. It also reduces persona-construction cost to under $0.5 per task using GPT-5.2. Finally, we present MetaPersona-Studio, a prototype interactive interface for empirically grounded persona generation.

</details>


### [87] [SimTrace: Grounded Multimodal User Trajectories Generation for Online User Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38397)
*Yunan Lu,Shuang Xie,Meghna Allamudi,Mingyu Zhao,Han Li,Lingyun Wang,Zhou Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SimTrace is a framework that generates faithful, fine-grained synthetic multimodal clickstreams using a computer-use client agent grounded in real user trajectories and web environment. It addresses the data scarcity problem for virtual client development by anonymizing real interactions, constructing simulated web environment twins, and generating synthetic interaction trajectories that can be shared as alternatives to confidential logs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Virtual clients offer cost-effective solutions for A/B testing, recommender system development, and interface evaluation, but building them requires large-scale, semantically faithful, fine-grained online user trajectories. Existing data sources are limited: public datasets either abstract away fine-grained interaction details or preserve rich context but remain platform-specific and small-scale. Proprietary logs face privacy restrictions, and small businesses lack sufficient traffic.

Method: SimTrace works through three key steps: 1) Anonymizes real interactions to protect privacy, 2) Constructs a simulated twin of the given web environment, 3) Uses a computer-use client agent grounded in real user trajectories and the simulated environment to generate synthetic interaction trajectories. Each generated action is paired with corresponding web observations and user context.

Result: SimTrace outperforms competing baselines on 7 out of 8 fidelity metrics. Models trained on synthetic data achieve performance comparable to those trained on real data on downstream tasks (purchase prediction and recommendation). For next action prediction, augmenting real data with synthetic data improves accuracy by 11.0% relative to training on real data alone.

Conclusion: SimTrace successfully generates high-fidelity synthetic multimodal clickstreams that can serve as shareable alternatives to confidential logs. The synthetic data enables comparable downstream task performance and significant improvements when combined with real data. The open-source release of SimTrace facilitates research on online user behavior modeling.

Abstract: Virtual clients offer a cost-effective approach to support applications such as A/B testing, recommender system development, and interface evaluation. However, building them requires access to large-scale, semantically faithful, fine-grained online user trajectories. These data are difficult to obtain because proprietary logs are subject to privacy restrictions and small businesses often lack sufficient traffic. Consequently, existing public datasets either abstract away fine-grained user interaction details or preserve rich context but remain platform-specific and small-scale. To address this gap, we propose SimTrace, a framework that generates faithful, fine-grained synthetic multimodal clickstreams through a computer-use client agent that is grounded in real user trajectories and the given web environment. SimTrace anonymizes real interactions and constructs a simulated twin of the given web environment, then uses both to generate synthetic interaction trajectories. Each action is paired with its corresponding web observations and user context, yielding a shareable alternative to confidential logs for developing computer-use agent-style virtual clients. We apply SimTrace to an e-commerce setting and evaluate both its fidelity and downstream utility. SimTrace outperforms competing baselines on 7 out of 8 fidelity metrics. Models trained on synthetic data achieve performance comparable to those trained on real data on downstream tasks such as purchase prediction and recommendation. For next action prediction task, augmenting real data with synthetic data further improves accuracy by 11.0% relative to training on real data alone. We release SimTrace as an open-source package to facilitate research on online user behavior modeling.

</details>


### [88] [ArgGYM: A Procedural, Engine-Verified Benchmark for Structured Defeasible Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38409)
*İbrahim Ethem Deveci,Funda Tan Çalık,Barış Deniz Sağlam,Duygu Ataman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ArgGYM is a procedural benchmark and training environment for evaluating and improving large language models' defeasible reasoning (reasoning under incomplete/revisable information). It includes 1,440 verified instances across 15 curriculum configurations with different argument/set orderings, and reveals that frontier and open-weight models show different reasoning profiles with performance declining in more complex configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM reasoning progress is driven by domains with automatically verifiable rewards (math, code, formal logic). However, real-world reasoning involves incomplete and revisable information where conclusions can be supported provisionally, defeated by counter-evidence, or revised - known as defeasible reasoning. It is unclear how success in fixed problem specifications transfers to such domains.

Method: ArgGYM decomposes defeasible reasoning into 12 tasks and uses a symbolic argumentation engine to compute formal states for evaluating model outputs. The benchmark includes 1,440 verified instances across 15 curriculum configurations, two argument preference orderings (weakest-link and last-link), and two set orderings (elitist and democratic). The same generators and verifiers can produce fresh instances for evaluation and RLVR training.

Result: On the frozen benchmark, frontier and open-weight models show sharply different reasoning profiles. Models can recover substantial parts of structured answers without solving the complete task. Performance declines in later curriculum configurations with longer dependencies and more interacting structures.

Conclusion: The authors release the benchmark, generators, and verifiers for reproducible evaluation and RLVR training. This enables reduced dependence on static test sets and provides verifiable-reward training for defeasible reasoning in LLMs.

Abstract: Recent progress in large language model reasoning has been driven by benchmarks and reinforcement learning environments with automatically verifiable rewards, particularly in mathematics, code, and formal logic. These settings make model accuracy easier to evaluate and optimize, but it remains unclear how far success under fixed problem specifications and stable evaluation criteria transfers to reasoning outside such domains. Real-world reasoning often proceeds under incomplete and revisable information: conclusions may be supported provisionally, defeated by counter-evidence, reinstated by further arguments, or revised when stronger reasons become available. Reasoning of this kind is generally referred to as defeasible reasoning. We introduce ArgGYM, a procedural benchmark and RLVR-compatible training environment for structured defeasible reasoning. ArgGYM decomposes this reasoning into twelve tasks and grounds task-specific scoring in a symbolic argumentation engine that computes the formal states used to evaluate model outputs. It includes a frozen benchmark of 1,440 verified instances across fifteen curriculum configurations, two argument preference orderings (weakest-link and last-link), and two set orderings (elitist and democratic), while the same generators and verifiers can produce fresh instances for evaluation that reduces dependence on static test sets and for verifiable-reward training. On the frozen benchmark, frontier and open-weight models show sharply different reasoning profiles: they can recover substantial parts of structured answers without solving the complete task, and performance declines in later curriculum configurations with longer dependencies and more interacting structures. We release the benchmark, generators, and verifiers for reproducible evaluation and RLVR training.

</details>


### [89] [A Competing-Hazards Systematization of Loss of Control in Autonomous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38411)
*Mohamed Aly Bouke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of comparing AI agent failures across different reports by introducing a common framework with four outcome categories: approved completion, safe stopping, scope escape, and continuation. The authors formalize this as a competing-hazards model and analyze 22 incident reports and 102 agent-safety evaluations from January 2025 to September 2026. Key findings include: 87 evaluations recorded out-of-scope effects, only 26 treated safe stopping as a first-class outcome, and in 20 of 22 incidents, the environment enabled out-of-scope effects to succeed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the lack of standardization in reporting AI agent incidents and safety evaluations. Different reports describe agent behavior failures inconsistently, making it difficult to compare failures, trace risk across attempts, or distinguish agent behavior from environmental factors. The UN panel has described AI agents acting beyond approved limits as an early warning of loss of human control, highlighting the urgency of this issue.

Method: The authors: (1) Developed a common framework categorizing outcomes into four types: approved completion, safe stopping, scope escape, or continuation; (2) Formalized the framework as a discrete-time competing-hazards model to derive escape probability within a retry budget, a model-conditional safe-budget limit, and conditions for estimation from execution logs; (3) Conducted an audit of 22 incident reports and 102 agent-safety evaluations published from January 2025 to September 2026 using primary sources.

Result: The audit revealed: (1) Among 22 incidents: 6 involved tasks that could not be completed within scope, 13 involved agents that continued rather than stopped, and 5 did not report stopping behavior; (2) Developers' figures imply a task-level incidence ratio near 47 for out-of-scope coordination in never-solved vs. solved tasks; (3) Among 102 evaluations: 87 recorded an out-of-scope effect or specification violation, 26 treated safe stopping as a first-class outcome, only 20 recorded both safe stopping and out-of-scope effects, and 79 merged budget exhaustion with failure; (4) In 20 of 22 incidents, the environment allowed an out-of-scope effect to succeed; (5) No evaluation reported all fields needed to estimate the full competing-hazards process from published evidence.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that realized loss of control often reflects persistent agent behavior interacting with permissive boundary conditions in the environment, rather than pure agent malfunction. They also find that current evaluation practices are insufficient for proper risk assessment, as critical fields are missing from published reports. The proposed framework enables standardized comparison of agent failures and provides a foundation for better incident reporting and safety evaluation in AI agent systems.

Abstract: Leading AI developers have reported agents acting beyond their approved limits, which a United Nations panel described as an early warning of loss of human control. Yet incident reports and agent-safety evaluations describe these events differently, making it difficult to compare failures, trace risk across attempts, or separate agent behavior from the environment's role in allowing an out-of-scope action to succeed. To address this gap, we introduce a common framework in which each attempt ends in approved completion, safe stopping, scope escape, or continuation. We formalize the framework as a discrete-time competing-hazards model and derive escape probability within a retry budget, a model-conditional safe-budget limit, and conditions for estimation from execution logs. We audit 22 incident reports and 102 agent-safety evaluations published from January 2025 to September 2026 using primary sources. Six incidents involved tasks that could not be completed within scope, thirteen involved agents that continued rather than stopped, and five did not report stopping behavior. Developers' figures imply a task-level incidence ratio near 47 for out-of-scope coordination in never-solved versus solved tasks. Among evaluations, 87 recorded an out-of-scope effect or specification violation, 26 treated safe stopping as a first-class outcome, only 20 recorded both, and 79 merged budget exhaustion with failure. In 20 of 22 incidents, the environment allowed an out-of-scope effect, indicating that realized loss of control often reflected persistent agent behavior interacting with permissive boundary conditions; meanwhile, no evaluation reported all fields needed to estimate the full competing-hazards process from published evidence.

</details>


### [90] [AIM: Agentic Idea Management for Automated Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38445)
*Hyeong Kyu Choi,Bhavana Dalvi Mishra,Jiefeng Chen,Mihir Parmar,Rui Meng,Chun-Liang Li,Xiangru Tang,Sharon Li,Jinsung Yoon,Tomas Pfister*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIM (Agentic Idea Manager) is an autonomous framework for idea-driven automated scientific research using LLMs. It uses Bayesian optimization-inspired mechanisms to organize research ideas, select promising directions, and maintain idea-solution alignment. The framework includes an Agentic Surrogate, Agentic Acquisition, Solution Auditor, and Resource Planner. Experiments on 10 AutoLab benchmark tasks show improvements of 1.6% on System Optimization tasks and 4.9% on Model Development & CUDA tasks, with up to 3.1x speedup in wall-clock time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address three core challenges in using Frontier LLMs for automated scientific research: (1) organizing evolving research ideas, (2) selecting promising directions, and (3) maintaining alignment between ideas and their implementations. The paper distinguishes "idea-driven search" from "solution-driven search" as two different paradigms for automated research.

Method: AIM uses: (1) An Agentic Surrogate for organizing discovered ideas, (2) An Agentic Acquisition mechanism guided by Bayesian optimization principles for idea selection, (3) A Solution Auditor to maintain idea-solution integrity, and (4) A Resource Planner for adaptive allocation of experimental budget across parallel search branches.

Result: AIM surpasses the strongest baseline by 1.6 percentage points on System Optimization tasks and 4.9 percentage points on long-horizon Model Development & CUDA tasks. It reaches baseline performance up to 3.1x faster in wall-clock time. Theoretical analysis shows that explicit idea-level allocation makes semantic coverage directly controllable, with broader coverage becoming more valuable when competitive research directions are sparse among many alternatives.

Conclusion: The framework successfully addresses the three core challenges of idea-driven automated research. The theoretical analysis provides conditions under which searching over ideas becomes beneficial, validating the approach particularly for scenarios with sparse competitive directions among many plausible alternatives.

Abstract: Frontier LLMs are increasingly used to automate scientific research through iterative search. We distinguish idea-driven search from solution-driven search and identify three core challenges: organizing evolving research ideas, selecting promising directions, and maintaining alignment between ideas and their implementations. To address these challenges, we introduce the Agentic Idea Manager (AIM), a fully autonomous framework for managing and exploring research directions in idea-driven automated research. Inspired by Bayesian optimization, AIM uses an Agentic Surrogate and an Agentic Acquisition mechanism to organize discovered ideas and guide their selection. A Solution Auditor maintains idea-solution integrity, while a Resource Planner adaptively allocates the remaining experimental budget across parallel search branches. Experiments on 10 AutoLab benchmark tasks show that AIM surpasses the strongest baseline by 1.6 percentage points on System Optimization tasks and 4.9 percentage points on long-horizon Model Development & CUDA tasks. Notably, AIM reaches the best baseline performance up to 3.1x faster in wall-clock time. We further provide a theoretical analysis of when searching over ideas becomes beneficial. Our analysis shows that explicit idea-level allocation makes semantic coverage directly controllable, and that broader coverage becomes increasingly valuable when competitive research directions are sparse among many plausible alternatives. Project Page: https://imhgchoi.github.io/agentic-idea-manager/

</details>


### [91] [Reach Into The CHOIR: Free-List Elicitation Uncovers Distinct Model Voices in LLM Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38448)
*Ben Wigler,Maria Tsfasman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CHOIR is a framework that addresses false plurality in LLM ensembles by using free-list elicitation from cognitive anthropology. It repeatedly elicits ranked lists, clusters items into concepts, and measures salience across models and prompt variants to distinguish between agreement from constrained answer spaces versus genuine consensus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended LLM homogeneity creates false plurality - multiple systems appear to offer independent perspectives but return similar default answers. Single-pass answers cannot distinguish between agreement due to constrained answer spaces, prompt-vocabulary echo, or broader answer spaces with stable alternatives.

Method: CHOIR adapts free-list elicitation from cognitive anthropology to LLM ensembles. It repeatedly elicits ranked lists, clusters items into prompt-level concepts, and measures concept salience across models, prompt variants, and persona conditions. Includes a source-blind ranking module to prioritize rare-but-stable candidates.

Result: On Infinity-Chat 100: 93/100 prompts showed agreement above chance, successfully separating narrow prompts from broad prompts with recoverable depth. Base-model identity remained the strongest recoverable signature. Persona prompts shifted surfaced concepts within base-model signatures.

Conclusion: CHOIR transforms open-ended homogeneity from a qualitative concern into a diagnostic measurement problem by systematically probing where models converge, why they converge, and what alternatives remain reachable under structured depth probing.

Abstract: Open-ended LLM homogeneity can create false plurality when several systems appear to offer independent perspectives while returning the same familiar default. Single-pass answers obscure the distinction between agreement produced by a tightly constrained answer space, prompt-vocabulary echo, and broader answer spaces with stable alternatives beneath the surface. We introduce CHOIR (Collective Hierarchically-Ordered Inquiry Responses), a framework that adapts free-list elicitation from cognitive anthropology to LLM ensembles. CHOIR repeatedly elicits ranked lists, clusters items into prompt-level concepts, and measures concept salience across models, prompt variants, and persona conditions. We evaluate CHOIR on Infinity-Chat 100, an external prompt bank from recent work on open-ended model homogeneity, and on a 27-question targeted diagnostic bank designed to isolate mechanism-level contrasts. On Infinity-Chat 100, CHOIR reproduces high surface agreement (93/100 prompts above chance) while separating narrow prompts from broad prompts with recoverable depth. Across targeted probes and the external prompt bank, base-model identity remains the strongest recoverable signature, and persona prompts shift surfaced concepts within base-model signatures. A source-blind ranking module prioritises rare-but-stable candidates for later inspection. CHOIR turns open-ended homogeneity into a diagnostic measurement problem by asking where models converge, why they converge, and what remains reachable under structured depth probing.

</details>


### [92] [PrivMeSA: Privacy-Aware Self-Evolving Multi-Agent System for Medicine via Local-Remote LLM Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38458)
*Dannong Wang,Yuran Zhang,Bian Sun,Alex Stinard,Yuzhang Shang,Song Wang,Yu Tian*

Main category: cs.AI
