TL;DR: PrivMeSA is a privacy-aware self-evolving multi-agent system for clinical large language models that balances task accuracy against patient privacy risks. It uses a local agent to manage consultations, consults remote specialists when needed, and employs reinforcement learning to control information disclosure while maintaining accuracy. The system also includes a local lesson memory that distills consultations into reusable guidance without requiring additional labels or parameter updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical LLM agents deployed locally may need to consult more capable remote models, but this risks exposing patient information. While removing explicit identifiers helps, quasi-identifiers can still enable re-identification through multi-turn consultations and repeated visits. Existing approaches place disclosure decisions with local agents but fail to address the cumulative re-identification risk from quasi-identifiers.

Method: PrivMeSA uses a multi-agent architecture where a local agent manages each encounter and consults remote specialists that may request additional information. Reinforcement learning balances task accuracy against direct disclosure and registry-based re-identification risk, evaluating privacy over the complete outbound transcript. A local lesson memory distills completed consultations into generalized clinical guidance and retrieves relevant lessons before transmission, enabling expertise reuse without remote exchange. The system is evaluated on an emergency-department benchmark built from MIMIC-IV-ED records.

Result: PrivMeSA improves mean task accuracy over delegation by up to 15.8 percentage points. It reduces the disclosure of personal details from 98.0% to 0.2% of cases. It also reduces the share of cases where the patient can be narrowed to ten or fewer registry patients from 74% to 0%.

Conclusion: PrivMeSA effectively balances clinical task accuracy with privacy protection in multi-agent clinical LLM systems. By learning to control disclosure through reinforcement learning and retaining expertise locally through a lesson memory, the system maintains high accuracy while dramatically reducing re-identification risks from quasi-identifiers across multiple consultations.

Abstract: Clinical large language model (LLM) agents deployed locally can consult more capable remote models, but doing so risks exposing patient information. Privacy-conscious delegation places disclosure decisions with a local agent, yet removing explicit identifiers is insufficient: quasi-identifiers can accumulate across multi-turn consultations and repeated patient visits to enable re-identification. We introduce PrivMeSA, a privacy-aware self-evolving multi-agent system that learns to control disclosure and retains remote expertise for local reuse. A local agent manages each encounter and consults remote specialists that may request additional information. Reinforcement learning balances task accuracy against direct disclosure and registry-based re-identification risk, with privacy evaluated over the complete outbound transcript of each encounter. A local lesson memory distills completed consultations into generalized clinical guidance and retrieves relevant lessons before transmission, allowing subsequent cases to reuse expertise without another remote exchange. Memory grows without additional outcome labels or parameter updates. On an emergency-department benchmark built from MIMIC-IV-ED records, PrivMeSA improves mean task accuracy over delegation by up to 15.8 percentage points. In the same setting, PrivMeSA reduces the disclosure of personal details from 98.0% to 0.2% of cases and the share of cases in which the patient can be narrowed to ten or fewer registry patients from 74% to 0%.

</details>


### [93] [Trust Is Not a Score: Runtime Assurance Contracts for High-Risk AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39717)
*Serhii Zabolotnii*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses the "assurance-transition gap" - the lack of mechanisms for how observed evidence should change an agent's authority during consequential tasks. The authors propose Runtime Assurance Contract (RAC), a formal schema binding autonomy boundaries, evidence states, transition policies, and non-compensatory gates. Empirical validation includes 280 synthetic failure-injection cases and a prospective holdout of 24 episodes, with results showing the approach matches human judgments while highlighting limitations in deployed safety claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks, audits, and agent protocols describe performance, permissions, and repair mechanisms, but they fail to specify how observed evidence should dynamically alter an agent's authority during consequential tasks. This gap is called the assurance-transition gap. The authors seek to create a formal policy-level mechanism that governs authority transitions based on evidence rather than allowing aggregate performance to authorize risky actions.

Method: The authors define the Runtime Assurance Contract (RAC) as a formal schema with five components: autonomy boundaries, component eligibility, evidence state, transition policy, and human-review capacity with non-compelligatory gates. They validate the approach through: (1) a deterministic failure-injection study with 280 synthetic cases in agentic coding, comparing gate conjunction, score-only rule, and restricted protocol baseline; (2) 18 hand-authored traces checking version-pinned evidence and review transitions; (3) a prospective synthetic holdout of 24 episodes with two blinded LLM judges evaluating 72 action attempts.

Result: The score-only rule at published weights/threshold admits 80 of 100 block-required injections and all 40 review-required injections. When tuned in hindsight, it matches the conjunction rule on this corpus. Mathematical analysis shows exact agreement holds if and only if the threshold does not exceed the smallest weight (for positive weights, positive threshold, binary risk signals, zero-signal controls). In the prospective holdout, both RAC and a separately implemented full stateful baseline match the identical labels assigned by two blinded LLM judges across all 72 action attempts.

Conclusion: The RAC framework provides a formal mechanism for evidence-driven authority transitions in agentic systems. The empirical studies demonstrate that the approach can match human judgments on synthetic cases. However, the authors explicitly acknowledge that these studies test mechanisms only on synthetic cases and establish neither deployed safety nor cross-domain effectiveness. The work focuses on policy-level formalization rather than operational safety guarantees.

Abstract: Benchmarks, audits, and agent protocols describe performance, permissions, and repair, but not how observed evidence should change an agent's authority during a consequential task. We call this the assurance-transition gap. We propose a Runtime Assurance Contract (RAC), a policy-level formal schema binding autonomy boundaries, component eligibility, evidence state, transition policy, human-review capacity, and non-compensatory gates. Under RAC, soft metrics may inform routing, whereas a failed or unknown mandatory gate forces retry, switch, escalation, deferral, or stop; aggregate performance cannot authorize action. We define the contract, an evidence record, a permission rule, and five invariants, and illustrate them in clinical, industrial, and judicial failure probes. We then report a deterministic failure-injection study in agentic coding: 280 constructed cases evaluated by a gate conjunction, a score-only rule, and a restricted protocol baseline. At the published example weights and threshold, the score rule admits 80 of 100 block-required injections and all 40 review-required injections. Tuned in hindsight, it matches the conjunction on this corpus. For positive weights, a positive threshold, binary risk signals, zero-signal controls, and an injected case firing each signal alone, we show that exact agreement holds if and only if the threshold does not exceed the smallest weight. A separate set of 18 hand-authored traces checks version-pinned evidence and review transitions against simpler policy variants. In a further prospective synthetic holdout of 24 episodes, two blinded LLM judges assign identical labels to all 72 action attempts; RAC and a separately implemented full stateful baseline both match these labels. These studies test mechanisms on synthetic cases; they establish neither deployed safety nor cross-domain effectiveness.

</details>


### [94] [NAQD Env: A benchmark for selective withdrawal in language agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38460)
*Mohamed Abouzahra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces NAQD-Env, a synthetic environment for evaluating language agents' ability to selectively withdraw planned actions when circumstances change (evidence changes, permission revoked, or stop instruction arrives). The study evaluates three open-weight models across 3,150 episodes and finds severe limitations: withdrawal recall ≤0.06, no valid resumption observed, and only one episode matching the complete reference policy. Fine-tuning improves decision accuracy but causes unintended side effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents must revise planned actions when conditions change, but existing evaluations don't assess selective withdrawal - the ability to suspend only affected actions while preserving unaffected work. This gap is critical for agent reliability, as inappropriate withdrawal or resumption can lead to task failures or safety issues.

Method: The authors created NAQD-Env, a synthetic environment with 11 dependency families supporting evaluation on development structures, held-out families, and held-out combinations. They evaluated three open-weight models (Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-3B, Llama-3.1-8B) under three prompt conditions on 350 frozen scenarios (3,150 episodes). Metrics distinguish attempted violations from permitted violations and jointly report policy agreement, task value, withdrawal, resumption, and event reporting. Exploratory supervised fine-tuning was also conducted.

Result: Key findings: (1) Withdrawal recall is at most 0.06 across conditions; (2) No valid resumption observed at eligible opportunities; (3) Only 1 episode matches the complete reference policy; (4) Qwen2.5-7B has fewer unsafe-attempt episodes than Qwen2.5-3B and Llama-3.1-8B but completes less useful work and preserves unaffected actions less accurately; (5) Fine-tuning improved decision accuracy from 0.45-0.54 to 0.83-0.92 but caused inappropriate withdrawal after curriculum omissions and loss of event reporting.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that current language agents struggle severely with selective withdrawal, motivating its evaluation as a distinct component of agent reliability. The setting measures policy application with trusted structured inputs and does not establish real-world containment or source-verification ability. Key limitations include the synthetic nature of the environment and reliance on trusted inputs.

Abstract: Language agents must revise planned actions when evidence changes, permission is revoked, or a stop instruction arrives. A useful response is selective: suspend affected actions, preserve unaffected work, and resume only after sufficient repair. We introduce NAQD-Env, a synthetic environment that evaluates these decisions against a deterministic reference policy over explicit evidence, authorization, and constraint dependencies. Eleven dependency families support evaluation on development structures, held-out families, and held-out combinations of structures. Metrics distinguish attempted violations from violations permitted by a simulated execution gate and jointly report policy agreement, task value, withdrawal, resumption, and event reporting. We evaluate three open-weight instruction-tuned models from two families under three prompt conditions on 350 frozen scenarios, yielding 3,150 model-prompt episodes before gate replay. Across the reported conditions, withdrawal recall is at most 0.06, no valid resumption is observed at eligible opportunities, and only one episode matches the complete reference policy. Under the NAQD prompt, Qwen2.5-7B has fewer unsafe-attempt episodes than Qwen2.5-3B and Llama-3.1-8B, but also completes less useful work and preserves unaffected actions less accurately. Exploratory supervised fine-tuning probes increase Qwen2.5-3B decision accuracy from 0.45-0.54 to 0.83-0.92; separate diagnostics reveal inappropriate withdrawal after curriculum omissions and a loss of event reporting. These results motivate evaluating selective withdrawal as a distinct component of agent reliability. The setting measures policy application with trusted structured inputs and does not establish real-world containment or source-verification ability.

</details>


### [95] [VAmoS Part Deux: Harder, More Realistic Voice-Agent Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38512)
*Joshua Meyer,Sahar Shayegan,Ritiz Tambi,Ali Khan,Sun Kim,Victor Shih,Mehdi Jamei,Andi Partovi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VAmoS Energy is a benchmark for evaluating voice agents in production settings, combining multiple challenges (multiple requests, background speech, caller patience) in 100 utility billing calls. It uses a stateful Stripe billing twin and Apache Fineract loan engine with 16 tools, achieving 99.1% agreement with code verifier. Across 14 voice stacks, completion rates range from 17.3% to 44.7%, with background TV reducing completion from 38.7% to 8.6%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Voice agents in production face complex challenges: handling multiple requests, background speech, and customers who lose patience. However, existing benchmarks lack realistic evaluation of these combined challenges. This research aims to create a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates voice agents across the entire call, including what they say, what they change, and how they handle competing speech.

Method: The benchmark uses 100 calls about utility billing and payment assistance with 2-4 requests per caller. It employs 16 tools backed by a stateful Stripe billing twin and Apache Fineract loan engine, with account access blocked until caller verification succeeds. Tasks use public household electricity data and Pennsylvania's residential billing rules. An LLM-as-a-verifier checks agent actions and spoken figures against explicit requirements. Experiments tested 14 voice stacks with three repeats per task.

Result: The LLM verifier agrees with a code verifier on 99.1% of checks. Completion rates across 14 voice stacks range from 17.3% to 44.7%, with Grok Voice leading, followed by Gemini 3.8 Live and GPT-Live at similar cost per call. Background television dramatically reduces pooled completion from 38.7% to 8.6%. The simulated caller often accepts incorrect results because it hears words but cannot inspect actions.

Conclusion: Voice agents need evaluation across the whole call, including verbal output, system state changes, and handling of competing speech. The dramatic impact of background noise (8.6% vs 38.7% completion) demonstrates that current voice agents struggle with real-world conditions. The study highlights that callers may accept incorrect results due to inability to verify agent actions, emphasizing the need for comprehensive benchmark evaluation.

Abstract: Voice agents in production must handle several requests, background speech, and customers who lose patience. We introduce VAmoS Energy, a benchmark that combines these challenges in 100 calls about utility billing and payment assistance. Each caller makes two to four requests. The agent has sixteen tools backed by a stateful Stripe billing twin and the Apache Fineract loan engine, with account access blocked until caller verification succeeds. The tasks use public household electricity data and a policy based on Pennsylvania's residential billing rules. An LLM-as-a-verifier checks the agent's actions and spoken figures against explicit requirements. On a calibration run, it agrees with a code verifier on 99.1% of checks. Across fourteen voice stacks and three repeats per task, completion ranges from 17.3% to 44.7%. Grok Voice leads, and Gemini 3.8 Live and GPT-Live follow at about the same cost per call. Background television reduces pooled completion from 38.7% to 8.6%. The simulated caller often accepts an incorrect result because it hears the agent's words but cannot inspect its actions. These findings show why voice agents need evaluation across the whole call, including what they say, what they change, and how they handle competing speech.

</details>


### [96] [Demographic Pluralism: Inference-Time Modeling of Pluralistic Human Preference Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38555)
*Meng-Chen Wu,Qipin Chen,Ansh Jain,Tess Wood,Zhe Du,Si-Chi Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "Demographic Pluralism," an inference-time framework for large language models to estimate population-level opinion distributions without requiring opinion-distribution training data or task-specific fine-tuning. The method generates multiple perspectives within demographically grounded groups to better capture within-group variation that previous coarse demographic methods missed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly deployed in culturally sensitive settings where they must represent diverse preferences within populations. However, existing methods model populations at coarse demographic or community levels, failing to capture the significant variation that exists within groups. This limitation hinders proper alignment of LLMs to diverse population preferences.

Method: The proposed Demographic Pluralism framework works at inference-time to estimate population-level opinion distributions. It generates multiple perspectives within demographically grounded groups rather than treating each demographic group as monolithic. The method was evaluated using three aggregation strategies: weighted, equal-weighted, and inverse-weighted aggregation.

Result: Across four different LLM backbones tested on GlobalOpinionQA and VITAL benchmarks, Demographic Pluralism reduces Jensen-Shannon distance (a measure of distribution difference) by 8.4%-26.4% compared to Modular Pluralism. Among the three aggregation methods, equal weighting performs best overall. Additionally, the study found that group-level error increases with group weight, which helps explain why weighted aggregation performs weaker than equal weighting.

Conclusion: The Demographic Pluralism framework successfully captures within-group variation in population opinions, significantly outperforming previous approaches like Modular Pluralism. The finding that equal weighting outperforms weighted aggregation suggests that simply weighting groups by size or importance does not improve opinion distribution estimation; instead, treating all demographic subgroups equally yields better results. This work provides a practical approach for improving LLM alignment in culturally sensitive applications without requiring additional training data or fine-tuning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in culturally sensitive settings, where alignment requires representing diverse preferences within populations. Yet existing methods model populations at coarse demographic or community levels and overlook within-group variation. We introduce Demographic Pluralism, an inference-time framework that estimates population-level opinion distributions without opinion-distribution training data or task-specific fine-tuning by generating multiple perspectives within demographically grounded groups. Across four backbones on GlobalOpinionQA and VITAL, it reduces Jensen-Shannon distance by 8.4%-26.4% over Modular Pluralism. Among weighted, equal-weighted, and inverse-weighted aggregation, equal weighting performs best overall; group-level error also increases with group weight, helping explain weighted aggregation's weaker performance.

</details>


### [97] [Defining and Categorising Human-AI Interactions in Clinical Trials: A Multidimensional Human-AI Classification Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38559)
*Sandra Woolley,Tim Collins,Khalid Khattak,Illia Chernomorets,Ariane Arevalo,Chris Richardson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multidimensional framework for classifying human-AI interactions (HAIIs) in clinical trials, considering four dimensions: AI tasks, human-AI relationships, interaction configurations, and interacting human groups. The framework was validated through 15 sampled clinical trials using both human reviewers and LLM classifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing taxonomies for HAII in clinical trials are limited; there is a need for a more comprehensive framework to consistently identify, compare, and synthesize human-AI interactions across clinical-trial records.

Method: Purposive sampling of 15 clinical trials from a previously reported dataset. Each trial was independently categorized by two human reviewers and six large language model (LLM) classifiers. The framework integrates four dimensions: AI tasks, human-AI relationships, interaction configurations, and interacting human groups.

Result: The proposed framework provides a structured method for consistent identification, comparison, and synthesis of HAIIs across clinical-trial records. LLM-assisted categorization shows potential, though human judgment remains important for incomplete or ambiguous records.

Conclusion: The multidimensional framework successfully brings together AI tasks, human-AI relationships, interaction configurations, and interacting human groups within a single approach. It has potential to support more systematic identification, comparison, and synthesis of human-AI interactions in clinical trials.

Abstract: This paper examines human-AI interactions (HAIIs) in clinical trials and presents a multidimensional categorisation framework that classifies interactions according to AI tasks, human-AI relationships, interaction configurations and interacting human groups. We define HAII, examine existing taxonomies and extend existing categorisation approaches through this novel multidimensional framework. We purposively sampled 15 clinical trials from a previously reported dataset. Each trial was independently categorised by two human reviewers and six large language model (LLM) classifiers. The proposed categorisation provides a structured method for the consistent identification, comparison and synthesis of human-AI interactions across clinical-trial records. The framework is intended to support more consistent comparison and synthesis of AI-related clinical trials and to make explicit the different forms of human involvement associated with AI interventions. The results demonstrate the potential for LLM-assisted categorisation while indicating the continuing importance of human judgement where trial records are incomplete or ambiguous. The principal contribution is a proposed multidimensional framework that brings together AI tasks, human-AI relationships, interaction configurations and interacting human groups within a single approach designed for clinical-trial records. Its significance lies in its potential to support more systematic identification, comparison and synthesis of how humans and AI interact in clinical trials.

</details>


### [98] [Towards Model as a Library: Offline, Community-Sourced AI for Low-Resource African Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38574)
*Fendji K. E. Jean Louis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes Model as a Library (MaaL), a software architecture that packages small, community-enrolled speech models as on-device dependencies for offline voice-based data collection in African low-resource language communities. It addresses the problem that LLMs fail in these contexts by enrolling vocabulary directly from speakers at deployment and transpiling existing digital form tools into voice-first interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are least reliable for African communities despite being proposed as AI-powered services. All African languages are low-resource, and models trained on scraped, standardized text misrepresent actual dialectal and regional variation. Current approaches fail exactly where need is greatest.

Method: The paper proposes a software architecture called MaaL that: (1) packages small speech models as versioned on-device dependencies, (2) uses keyword spotting to convert closed-vocabulary text forms into voice forms, (3) enables on-device filling and submission, and (4) transpiles existing digital form tools into MaaL schemas for low-friction deployment.

Result: This is a position and system-design paper presenting a concept, mechanism, and analytical feasibility case. No empirical results are reported; the work identifies requirements for a working implementation.

Conclusion: MaaL offers a promising approach for offline, voice-first data collection for low-literacy populations in low-resource language communities. The key insight is enrolling vocabulary directly from speakers rather than relying on web-scraped corpora. Implementation requires further development of the proposed architecture.

Abstract: Large language models are frequently proposed as a route to AI-powered services for African communities, but they are least reliable exactly where the need is greatest: all African languages remain low-resource by any standard measure, and models trained on scraped, standardised text systematically misrepresent the dialectal and regional variation of how people actually speak. We introduce \textbf{Model as a Library (MaaL)}, a software architecture that packages small, community-enrolled speech models as versioned on-device dependencies, enabling offline structured data collection that cannot generatively hallucinate, for populations that current language models serve worst. Rather than relying on web-scraped corpora, MaaL's vocabulary is enrolled directly from a small number of example recordings by the speakers themselves, at the point of deployment. We describe the architecture and its central mechanism - keyword spotting that turns a closed-vocabulary text form into a voice form, filled and submitted entirely on-device - and propose transpiling the closed-vocabulary elements already present in widely-deployed digital form tools into MaaL schemas, a low-friction path to voice-first, offline data collection for the low-literacy populations these tools already reach. This is a position and system-design paper: we describe the concept, the mechanism, and an analytical feasibility case, and identify what a working implementation still requires.

</details>


### [99] [Conditional Generation of Creative Chess Puzzles with Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38577)
*Aatu Selkee,Severi Rissanen,Xidong Feng,Tom Zahavy,Eric Malmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel approach for generating creative chess puzzles using masked diffusion models, incorporating a best-move prediction auxiliary task and reinforcement learning (DDPO) to improve solution uniqueness and theme-conditioning accuracy. The work also releases the first open-weights models for chess puzzle generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern language models struggle with constrained, counter-intuitive creative tasks; chess puzzle generation serves as a rigorous testbed for computational creativity and reasoning where a single piece change can invalidate an entire solution.

Method: The approach uses masked diffusion models with non-directional diffusion for conditional generation of chess puzzles, allowing conditioning on specific tactical themes and partial board positions. An auxiliary task of simultaneous best-move prediction is introduced. A reinforcement learning framework adapted from Denoising Diffusion Policy Optimization (DDPO) is established to optimize solution uniqueness and theme conditioning.

Result: The auxiliary task improves solution uniqueness by 11.6% and theme-conditioning accuracy by 2.5%. The RL training increases the yield of unique and theme-matching positions by 89.1%. The first open-weights models for chess puzzle generation are released.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides a new pathway for controllable, creative generation in chess puzzles, with the RL framework significantly improving both solution uniqueness and theme-matching performance.

Abstract: While modern language models demonstrate impressive generative capabilities, they often struggle with constrained, counter-intuitive creative tasks. To address this limitation, we explore chess puzzle generation as a rigorous testbed for computational creativity and reasoning, a domain where altering a single piece can invalidate an entire solution. We propose a novel approach for conditional generation of creative chess puzzles using masked diffusion models. Unlike previous methods, our non-directional diffusion approach allows for conditioning on specific tactical themes and partial board positions. We introduce a novel auxiliary task of simultaneous best-move prediction, which improves solution uniqueness by 11.6% and theme-conditioning accuracy by 2.5%. To further optimize solution uniqueness and theme conditioning, we establish a reinforcement learning framework adapted from Denoising Diffusion Policy Optimization (DDPO). This RL training increases the yield of unique and theme-matching positions by 89.1%. Finally, we release the first open-weights models (Appendix B) for chess puzzle generation, offering a new pathway for controllable, creative generation.

</details>


### [100] [Sense and Sensitivity: Benchmarking LLM Clinical Triage Recommendations with Physician Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38600)
*Abinitha Gourabathina,Haoran Zhang,Yuexing Hao,Walter Gerych,Marzyeh Ghassemi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates LLM reliability in clinical triage compared to physicians under realistic text perturbations. The study uses a benchmark of 6,000+ clinical scenarios, 7,000 physician annotations, and 225,000 model responses to assess how LLMs handle clinically irrelevant textual changes (gender, tone) while preserving the underlying clinical situation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly deployed in clinical settings, it is critical to evaluate their reliability under realistic variations in clinical text that could affect their recommendations.

Method: The researchers created a benchmark with over 6,000 clinical scenarios, 7,000 physician annotations, and 225,000 model responses. They compared LLM recommendations to practicing physicians under text perturbations that preserve the underlying clinical setting but vary factors like gender and tone.

Result: Two key findings: (1) LLMs are more likely than physicians to recommend unnecessary care at baseline, and this tendency increases under perturbed inputs. (2) LLM recommendations are more sensitive to gender and tone perturbations than human recommendations.

Conclusion: LLMs can vary under clinically irrelevant textual changes, highlighting the need for deployment-oriented evaluations grounded in expert physician behavior to ensure patient safety.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in clinical settings, it is critical to evaluate their reliability under realistic variation in clinical text. We study this question in clinical triage, comparing LLMs to practicing physicians under text perturbations that preserve the underlying clinical setting. We introduce a benchmark of over 6,000 clinical scenarios, 7,000 physician annotations, and 225,000 model responses. Using this benchmark, we make two key observations. First, LLMs are more likely than physicians to recommend unnecessary care at baseline, and this tendency increases under perturbed inputs. Further, we find that LLM recommendations are more sensitive to gender and tone perturbations than human recommendations. Together, these results demonstrate that LLMs can vary under clinically irrelevant textual changes, highlighting the need for deployment-oriented evaluations grounded in expert physician behavior.

</details>


### [101] [When Scientific Contradictions Are Lost in Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38621)
*Tal Zeevi,Trey W. Jensen,Maxwell Strome*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The study investigates whether language models can determine when two scientific findings conflict, focusing on how they handle comparable measurements. Using an unsatisfiable XOR constraint system translated into scientific reports, the authors create a dilemma between an assignment that satisfies more constraints versus one that better matches biological expectations. The key finding is that while models perform well with direct constraints (90-96% accuracy), they struggle in scientific prose - Claude Opus 5 often prefers biologically expected assignments. Removing biological preference dramatically improves recovery (27%→79%), and adding formalization cues pushes it to 92%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two scientific findings can disagree without contradicting each other, and determining conflict requires knowing whether they describe comparable measurements. This study investigates how language models behave at this critical decision point for scientific verification.

Method: The researchers generated an unsatisfiable XOR constraint system and translated its constraints into scientific reports from different laboratories. They created a dilemma: one assignment satisfies more constraints while another satisfies fewer but better matches expected biology. They tested GPT-5.6 Sol and Claude Opus 5 in two conditions - direct constraint statement and scientific prose - and measured recovery of the better-supported assignment.

Result: With direct constraints, GPT-5.6 Sol and Claude Opus 5 recovered the best-supported assignment in 90% and 96% of cases. In scientific prose, Claude Opus 5 often preferred the biologically expected assignment. Removing biological preference increased recovery from 27% to 79% (p<.001); with formalization request and paired-design cue, recovery reached 92% (p<.001). GPT-5.6 Sol showed no significant changes.

Conclusion: Reliable scientific verification depends not only on formal reasoning, but also on how models decide which findings should be compared and what relations they imply. Models need appropriate cues to distinguish between better-supported assignments and biologically expected ones.

Abstract: Two scientific findings can disagree without contradicting each other. Determining whether they conflict requires knowing whether they describe comparable measurements. We study how language models behave at this decision point. In a controlled task, we generate an unsatisfiable XOR constraint system and translate its constraints into scientific reports from different laboratories. One assignment satisfies more constraints, while another satisfies fewer but better matches expected biology. This creates a simple dilemma: does the model choose the assignment that best fits the constraints, or the one that better matches biological expectations? When the constraints are stated directly, GPT-5.6 Sol and Claude Opus 5 recover the best-supported assignment in 90% and 96% of cases, respectively. In scientific prose, however, the models behave differently. Claude Opus 5 often prefers the biologically expected assignment. Removing that biological preference increases recovery of the better-supported assignment from 27% to 79% (p<.001); recovery reaches 92% when the same Biology-favored record is accompanied by a formalization request and an explicit paired-design cue (p<.001). GPT-5.6 Sol is less sensitive, with neither corresponding change reaching statistical significance. These results suggest that reliable scientific verification depends not only on formal reasoning, but also on how models decide which findings should be compared and what relations they imply.

</details>


### [102] [Component-Aware Feedback for Self-Evolving Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38639)
*Ethan Lin,Jinming Nian,Yi Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces component-aware feedback for LLM-guided evolutionary search, which tracks which component edits produce which fitness changes by comparing each program with its parent and logging changes in both local (parent) and global (seed) reference frames. Applied to LLM reranking (multi-objective optimization balancing quality vs. cost), the method achieves 7.2% higher nDCG@10 and 11% fewer tokens per query compared to baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-guided evolutionary search methods discard information about which component edits produce which fitness metric changes, forcing the mutator LLM to infer effects from cluttered histories. This makes program search slow and unstable, especially for locally servable LLMs evolving multi-component systems.

Method: Component-aware feedback: (1) Compare each evaluated program with its parent to identify changed components; (2) Log components with associated metric differences into attribution memory; (3) Store changes in two reference frames - local (against parent) and global (against seed program); (4) Later mutations read from this memory to understand immediate effects and cumulative progress.

Result: Across 12 Bright datasets: (1) Reaches strongest baseline's final quality after median of one-third the search budget; (2) Ends 7.2% higher in held-out nDCG@10; (3) Under cost-aware objective, finds pipelines that are more accurate while using 11% fewer tokens per query.

Conclusion: Component-aware feedback is a promising direction for more efficient self-evolving systems, enabling faster convergence and better quality-cost tradeoffs in LLM-guided evolutionary search for multi-component systems.

Abstract: LLM-guided evolutionary search can discover complex programs, but existing methods mostly only save candidate programs and fitness scores while discarding which component edits produced which fitness metric changes. Existing methods force the mutator LLM to infer the effect of prior edits from cluttered histories, making program search slow and unstable. This is especially true for locally servable LLMs to evolve multi-component systems. We introduce component-aware feedback, which compares each evaluated program with its parent, identifies the components that changed, and logs them with the associated metric differences into an attribution memory that later mutations read. The memory keeps each change in two reference frames, local against the parent it came from and global against the seed program, which shows both the immediate effect of a change and the cumulative progress made since the seed. We study this on LLM reranking, a multi-objective optimization problem where a multi-stage pipeline must balance quality against serving cost. Across twelve \textsc{Bright} datasets, our method reaches the strongest baseline's final quality after a median of one third of the search budget and ends 7.2\% higher in held-out nDCG@10, and under a cost-aware objective it finds pipelines that are on average more accurate while using 11\% fewer tokens per query, showing component-aware feedback to be a promising direction for more efficient self-evolving systems.

</details>


### [103] [ChartRevise: A Dataset and Evaluation Protocol for Exact Chart Editing via Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38642)
*Jiaxiang Tang,Yi Zhou,Chad DeLuca,Rogerio Feris,Ahmed Khalil Omran,Zhi-Li Zhang,Pengyuan Li,Ali Anwar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChartRevise is a benchmark for evaluating chart editing models that require making visual changes in code while ensuring necessary related updates and avoiding unrelated changes. The dataset contains 92,438 records across 344 edit types, 20 chart types, and 3 plotting libraries. The evaluation protocol measures atomic requirement completion, detects gratuitous changes, and identifies missed coupled updates, achieving 16% relative gain in requirement recall and 22% in exact-edit rate through fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing chart editing benchmarks focus on code executability or chart quality but fail to distinguish request completion from missed coupled updates and gratuitous changes. This gap prevents proper evaluation of whether models complete the exact requested edits without introducing unnecessary modifications or missing related updates.

Method: 1) Dataset construction: Built on grammar of graphics to systematically cover chart-editing operations; uses source-program checks to verify applicability across chart types and libraries; pipeline checks individual requirements and guides repair or exclusion when unmet. 2) Evaluation protocol: Reference-free; separately measures atomic requirement completion, identifies gratuitous changes, and detects missed coupled updates; combined with execution and rendering success for exact-edit success.

Result: Dataset: 92,438 records covering 344 edit types across 20 chart types and three plotting libraries. Evaluation: Across five models and four external benchmarks, fine-tuning yields relative gains of 16% in mean requirement recall and 22% in mean exact-edit rate.

Conclusion: ChartRevise provides a structured dataset and evaluation protocol that enables precise assessment of chart editing models by distinguishing successful exact edits from missed coupled updates and gratuitous changes. The significant improvements from fine-tuning demonstrate the benchmark's effectiveness in driving model improvement.

Abstract: Chart editing requires cross-modal edit grounding, realizing a requested visual change in the code that draws it, with necessary related updates and without altering unrelated content. Existing benchmarks emphasize either code executability or chart quality, but their metrics do not clearly distinguish request completion from missed coupled updates and gratuitous changes. We introduce ChartRevise, a structured dataset and evaluation protocol for exact program-grounded chart editing. For dataset construction, we build on the grammar of graphics to systematically cover chart-editing operations, using source-program checks to verify their applicability across chart types and libraries. To improve edit exactness, our pipeline checks individual requirements and guides repair or exclusion when they are unmet. The resulting dataset contains 92,438 records covering 344 edit types across 20 chart types and three plotting libraries. For evaluation, our reference-free protocol separately measures atomic requirement completion, identifies gratuitous changes, and detects missed coupled updates. These checks are combined with successful execution and rendering to determine exact-edit success. Across five models and four external benchmarks, fine-tuning yields relative gains of 16\% in mean requirement recall and 22\% in mean exact-edit rate.

</details>


### [104] [AgBench: Agentic AI Benchmarks for Personal AI Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38652)
*Yizhou Han,Di Wu,Dhananjay Saikumar,Blesson Varghese*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgBench is a benchmark suite for evaluating agentic AI systems on personal devices, comparing local, hybrid, and cloud execution across task success, latency, cost, and data exposure. Testing over 162 million data points reveals trade-offs: local execution eliminates costs and data exposure but has lower success rates, while cloud execution offers higher success but with costs and privacy risks. No single architecture dominates all metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems increasingly rely on cloud-hosted LLMs, raising concerns about API costs and data exposure. While personal AI devices enable local execution, limited resources may affect task success. Existing benchmarks fail to systematically characterize trade-offs across devices, workloads, and deployment architectures.

Method: Developed AgBench, a benchmark suite with open artifacts for reproducible evaluation of agentic AI on personal devices. Evaluated local, hybrid, and cloud execution across agentic workloads, examining task success, latency, cloud API cost, and data exposure using over 162.07 million data points.

Result: Personal AI devices can complete many agent tasks locally. Local-only execution generally has lower task success and longer completion times than cloud-only execution, especially as concurrency increases. Local execution eliminates cloud model API costs and sensitive-information exposure. Hybrid execution can improve task success but its cloud cost and data exposure depend on how agents divide work. No single architecture performs best across all metrics (task success, goodput, cloud cost, and data exposure).

Conclusion: Deployment architecture choices should reflect the intended workload and device capabilities. The study provides the first systematic characterization of trade-offs between local, hybrid, and cloud execution for agentic AI on personal devices.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems increasingly rely on cloud-hosted large language models for planning, tool use, and iterative execution, raising concerns about API cost and data exposure. Advances in personal AI devices enable agents to execute locally, but limited resources on device may affect task success and performance. Existing benchmarks are inadequate for systematically characterizing these trade-offs across devices, workloads, and deployment architectures. We present AgBench, a benchmark suite and open artifacts for reproducible evaluation of agentic AI on personal devices. Using AgBench, we evaluate local, hybrid, and cloud execution across agentic workloads, examining task success, latency, cloud API cost, and data exposure. Our results, drawn from over 162.07 million data points, show that personal AI devices can complete many agent tasks locally, but local-only execution generally has lower task success and longer completion times than cloud-only execution, especially as concurrency increases. Local-only execution eliminates cloud model API costs and sensitive-information exposure to cloud agents. Hybrid execution can improve task success, but its cloud cost and data exposure depend on how agents divide work and share information. No single architecture performs best across task success, goodput, cloud cost, and data exposure; deployment choices should reflect the intended workload and device capabilities. AgBench is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/AgBench-2777.

</details>


### [105] [EvoSteer: Online Self-Evolving Graph Orchestration via Reference-Anchored Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38661)
*Mingda Zhang,Hanwen Zhang,Qiang Huang,Zijia Wang,Pengfei Guo,Yuchen Zhang,Jionghao Zhu,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoSteer introduces a new paradigm of Online Self-Evolving Graph Orchestration for LLM-based multi-agent systems, addressing three key challenges: post-hoc evolution, credit diffusion, and uncalibrated skill admission. It uses Anchored Trajectory Balance (AnchorTB) for credit assignment and Validated Skill Admission for skill promotion, demonstrating significant improvements across 12 datasets in QA, math reasoning, code generation, and interactive decision making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-evolving orchestration in LLM-based multi-agent systems suffers from three critical limitations: (1) Post-hoc evolution - team revision only occurs after trajectory ends; (2) Credit diffusion - all actions receive the same terminal advantage under confounded baselines; (3) Uncalibrated skill admission - skills are never retired once admitted. These issues limit the adaptability and efficiency of multi-agent systems in tool-using scenarios.

Method: EvoSteer proposes two main technical innovations: (1) Anchored Trajectory Balance (AnchorTB), a regression-style flow-matching loss that assigns each orchestration action a coefficient by balancing subtrajectories against a frozen reference, combining task-level reference reward statistics with prefix-dependent corrections; (2) Validated Skill Admission, where candidate skills are trialed before promotion and promoted only if paired evidence passes a sequential test under a shared nominal testing budget. The orchestrator builds a running team and repairs plausible but failing steps from execution features and learned value estimates.

Result: Experimental results on twelve datasets show that EvoSteer significantly outperforms baselines across question answering, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and interactive decision making tasks.

Conclusion: EvoSteer successfully addresses the three key challenges in LLM-based multi-agent orchestration through online self-evolving graph orchestration. The combination of AnchorTB for credit assignment and Validated Skill Admission for skill management provides a more effective and adaptive framework for tool-using multi-agent systems. The code is available at https://github.com/beita6969/evosteer.

Abstract: In recent years, LLM-based multi-agent systems have been widely applied to orchestrate tool-using agents into executable communication graphs. However, existing self-evolving orchestration still faces key challenges, including post-hoc evolution that revises the team only after the trajectory ends, credit diffusion that gives every action the same terminal advantage under confounded baselines, and skill admission that is uncalibrated and never retired. To address these challenges, we propose EvoSteer, a new paradigm of Online Self-Evolving Graph Orchestration -- the orchestrator builds a running team and repairs its plausible but failing steps from execution features and a learned value estimate. To support this paradigm, we introduce Anchored Trajectory Balance (AnchorTB), a regression-style flow-matching loss that assigns each orchestration action a coefficient by balancing subtrajectories against a frozen reference. Built on the learned flow, we further propose Validated Skill Admission, in which a candidate skill is tried before promotion and promoted only if paired evidence passes a sequential test under a shared nominal testing budget. Moreover, AnchorTB combines measured task-level reference reward statistics with prefix-dependent corrections. Experimental results on twelve datasets show that EvoSteer significantly outperforms baselines across question answering, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and interactive decision making. Our code is available at https://github.com/beita6969/evosteer.

</details>


### [106] [Where Scientific Search Agents Fail: Decision-Checkpoint Auditing of Exposure and Inspection Attempts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38670)
*Hongmin Li,Wanli Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a decision checkpoint protocol to analyze scientific-search agent behavior beyond simple accuracy metrics. The method tracks whether agents encounter target papers, attempt to inspect them, and return accepted answers. Across experiments on 540 AutoResearchBench Deep questions, keyword search (24.6% accuracy) and read-first (24.6% accuracy) show similar final accuracy but different internal behaviors—keyword search leaves more targets uninspected after exposure, while read-first makes more evidence-search calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that final-answer accuracy alone cannot distinguish whether a scientific-search agent failed to encounter a target paper, failed to inspect it after encountering it, or failed to return an accepted answer after inspection. This lack of granularity limits understanding of agent behavior and failure modes.

Method: The authors introduce decision checkpoints that record observations and tool actions without benchmark labels during inference. They then join target identities and evaluator labels to assign outcome categories from recorded events, enabling analysis of target exposure, inspection attempts, and answer correctness at the question level.

Result: Keyword search achieves 24.6% accuracy vs 17.8% for raw search. Both keyword and read-first conditions achieve 24.6% accuracy despite different behaviors: keyword search has fewer incorrect answers without target exposure/inspection but more incorrect answers after target exposure without inspection. Read-first makes 27.4% more evidence-search calls. Target inspection attempts occurred on 199 questions (read-first) vs 191 (keyword search).

Conclusion: The checkpoint protocol successfully distinguishes target exposure and inspection from aggregate accuracy and total tool use, revealing that similar accuracy can mask different agent behaviors. This fine-grained analysis makes question-level differences explicit and enables better understanding of agent failure modes in scientific search tasks.

Abstract: Final-answer accuracy does not reveal whether a scientific-search agent failed to encounter a target paper, attempt to inspect it, or return an accepted answer after inspection. We introduce decision checkpoints that record observations and tool actions without benchmark labels during inference, then join target identities and evaluator labels to assign outcome categories from recorded events. Across five conditions on 540 answerable AutoResearchBench Deep questions in a fixed, target-enriched environment, keyword search achieves 24.6\% accuracy, compared with 17.8\% for raw search. The keyword condition has fewer incorrect answers with neither target exposure nor inspection, but more incorrect answers after the target is exposed and left uninspected. Compared with keyword search, read-first has 27.4\% more recorded evidence-search calls. Target inspection attempts occur on 199 questions under read-first and 191 under keyword search; both conditions achieve 24.6\% accuracy. The checkpoint protocol makes these question-level differences explicit, distinguishing target exposure and inspection from aggregate accuracy and total tool use.

</details>


### [107] [Concept-Grounded Attention: A Controlled Evaluation of Graph-Injected Attention, Temporal Versioning, and Epistemic Status](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38684)
*Sachin Dev Duggal,Pradyumna Swarnalatha Ramanna,Alexandros Vassiliades*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Concept Lifecycle Model (CLM) and Concept-Grounded Attention (CGA) to address limitations in knowledge-intensive language models, specifically lack of concept evolution support, point-in-time reasoning, and epistemic status tracking. The framework represents concepts as temporally versioned, graph-grounded entities with explicit provenance. Evaluation across multiple benchmarks shows that explicit temporal representation and epistemic status improve performance, but graph-attention mechanisms provide minimal benefit beyond added capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge-intensive language model systems typically represent external knowledge as text chunks or static graphs, with limited support for: (1) concept evolution, (2) point-in-time reasoning, and (3) distinctions between validated and inferred knowledge. This limits their ability to handle dynamically changing information and reason about knowledge at specific timepoints.

Method: The paper proposes two components: (1) Concept Lifecycle Model (CLM) - represents concepts as persistent, graph-grounded, temporally versioned entities with explicit provenance and epistemic status; (2) Concept-Grounded Attention (CGA) - injects concept-graph structure into transformer computation through two mechanisms: Form A (graph-biased self-attention) and Form B (gated cross-attention over concept nodes). Evaluation uses disabled-mechanism baselines as controls.

Result: Key findings: (1) On MuSiQue/HotpotQA: concept-graph retrieval recovers multi-hop paths but doesn't improve evidence recall; Form A shows no improvement over baseline; Form B improves F1 from 0.188 to 0.221 but control concepts yield 0.213, indicating most gain is from added capacity. (2) On LongMemEval: temporal representation improves accuracy by 13-25 points across generators up to 122B parameters. (3) On source-independence task: epistemic status reduces unsupported assertions from 28% to 0.1% in small models and from 19-68% to 0-5% in 72-122B models.

Conclusion: The results support making temporal validity and epistemic status explicit in knowledge-intensive systems. However, graph-attention diagnostics (Form A/B) are not informative without disabled-mechanism controls, and most gains from graph-attention mechanisms appear to reflect added capacity rather than true structural reasoning. The study demonstrates the importance of proper control experiments when evaluating complex neural architectures.

Abstract: Knowledge-intensive language-model systems typically represent external knowledge as text chunks or static graphs, with limited support for concept evolution, point-in-time reasoning, and distinctions between validated and inferred knowledge. We introduce the Concept Lifecycle Model (CLM), which represents concepts as persistent, graph-grounded, temporally versioned entities with explicit provenance and epistemic status, and Concept-Grounded Attention (CGA), which injects concept-graph structure into transformer computation through graph-biased self-attention (Form A) and gated cross-attention over concept nodes (Form B). We evaluate the framework in controlled settings using disabled-mechanism baselines. On 200 MuSiQue and HotpotQA questions with retrieval fixed, concept-graph retrieval recovers explicit multi-hop paths but does not improve evidence recall. Form A appears to steer attention, with 2.76 times more attention on gold than distractor concepts, but the same ratio occurs when Form A is disabled; the learned bias is negligible and no answers change. An identity-preserving Form B improves F1 from 0.188 to 0.221, but control concepts yield 0.213, indicating that most of the gain reflects added capacity. On LongMemEval, explicit temporal representation improves answer accuracy by 13 to 25 points across all tested generators, up to 122B parameters, while simplified CLM version resolution performs similarly to dated serialization because concept identity is not established reliably. On a synthetic source-independence task, protocol-derived epistemic status reduces unsupported assertions from 28% to 0.1% in a fine-tuned small model and from 19-68% to 0-5% in 72-122B models. Overall, the results support making temporal validity and epistemic status explicit, while showing that graph-attention diagnostics are not informative without disabled-mechanism controls.

</details>


### [108] [GATE-ST: Gene-Aware Text-image Encoder for Spatial Transcriptomics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38690)
*Lucas Ni,Jian Luo,Wentao Huang,Chao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GATE-ST is a novel framework that combines text-based gene descriptions with image-based spatial transcriptomics predictions using cross-attention layers, outperforming existing image-only and image-text fusion approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial transcriptomics enables spatially resolved gene expression analysis but typically requires expensive and time-consuming tests. While image-based prediction methods exist, they focus mainly on positional embeddings and image features, leaving text-based optimization unexplored.

Method: GATE-ST incorporates text-based inputs into image-based spatial gene expression predictions. Gene summaries are processed through a text encoder to generate embeddings, which then integrate with image embeddings via cross-attention layers to align with morphological features.

Result: GATE-ST outperforms random gene embeddings and multiple other image-text fusion architectures in benchmarking tests.

Conclusion: Text-guided spatial gene expression prediction is effective in pathology imaging and has potential to greatly reduce the time and cost of accurate spatial transcriptomic predictions.

Abstract: Spatial transcriptomics enables spatially resolved gene expression analysis from slide-level images while preserving morphological features, providing valuable information for studying disease mechanisms and developing treatments. However, spatial gene expression profiling typically requires expensive and time-consuming tests. While existing image-based prediction optimizations mostly revolve around including positional embeddings and further image-based changes, text-based optimizations remain relatively unexplored. We present GATE-ST, which incorporates text-based inputs into image-based spatial gene expression predictions. With this approach, generated text descriptions of genes are utilized to better spatial transcriptomics prediction results. Gene summaries are put through a text encoder, generating embeddings that integrate with image embeddings through cross-attention layers to align with morphological features. We demonstrate the effectiveness of such text inputs by benchmarking performance against random gene embeddings and multiple other image-text fusion architectures, and show that GATE-ST outperforms these alternatives. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of GATE-ST in pathology imaging, which may greatly reduce the time and cost of accurate spatial transcriptomic predictions, proving the potential of text-guided spatial gene expression prediction.

</details>


### [109] [Budget Boundary Effects in Test-Time Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38699)
*Guilin Zhang,Ziqi Tan,Wulan Guo,Kai Zhao,Hongyun Yang,Mei Luo,Qi Ning,Feng Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates the trade-offs between strict and advisory stopping rules when LLMs hit cumulative token caps during inference on mathematical problems. Using 19,200 public traces from math competition problems (AIME, BrUMO, HMMT), the study reveals that advisory stopping converts abstentions to correct answers, that cost-aware comparisons differ from same-cap comparisons, and that increased candidate coverage doesn't guarantee higher accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand how to handle token cap boundaries in LLM inference—specifically whether to strictly stop at a cap or allow current attempts to finish. This has practical implications for controlling compute costs and accuracy trade-offs in production LLM systems.

Method: The study uses paired offline replays of 19,200 public traces from 120 math problems (AIME, BrUMO, HMMT). Two archive configurations of one model were tested with fixed candidate order and a 16-attempt cap. Answer selection was blind to reference answers and correctness labels.

Result: Three key findings: (1) At 4k cap, advisory gains replace abstention with correct answers while strict stopping loses value from unfinished prefixes; (2) Cost-aware comparisons show advisory 4k outperforms strict 8k at comparable cost, but strict 32k outperforms advisory 4k in high-cost settings; (3) Increased candidate coverage doesn't guarantee accuracy gains—a log-probability selector loses accuracy as coverage rises, and same-cap accuracy differences shrink below 1.3 percentage points at 32k.

Conclusion: The conclusion emphasizes that budget curves should jointly report: cap, realized cost, eligible candidates, stopping rule, and selector information. The findings suggest that simple comparisons at equal caps or equal compute are insufficient for understanding the trade-offs.

Abstract: A cumulative token cap can fall inside a mathematical derivation, forcing a test-time controller to choose between stopping at the cap (strict) and allowing the current attempt to finish (advisory). We measure this boundary choice with paired offline replays of 19,200 public traces: 120 AIME, BrUMO and HMMT problems and two archive configurations of one model. Candidate order and a 16-attempt cap are fixed, and answer selection is blind to reference answers and correctness labels. Three findings emerge. First, at the 4k cap, most advisory accuracy gains replace abstention with a correct answer; strict stopping pays for an unfinished prefix that the completed-only selector cannot use. Second, comparisons along realized cost differ from same-cap comparisons: advisory 4k in low has higher accuracy than strict 8k at comparable mean completion cost, while in high its observed accuracy is 0.42 points below strict 32k using 59% of its mean tokens. These aggregate comparisons do not establish equal-compute superiority or accuracy equivalence. Third, increased candidate coverage does not guarantee higher answer accuracy: a log-probability selector loses accuracy while coverage rises, including after a source-grade consistency repair. Same-cap majority-accuracy differences shrink below 1.3 percentage points at 32k. Budget curves should jointly state the cap, realized cost, eligible candidates, stopping rule and selector information.

</details>


### [110] [Staying on Task: Testing the Foundations of Long-Horizon Agent Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38712)
*Jeffrey Willette,Krishna C. Puvvada,Boris Ginsburg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Long-Transduction is a diagnostic benchmark testing AI models' ability to sustain repeated state-dependent operations (arithmetic, sorting, variable lookups, table transformations) during long-generation tasks with growing context, varying input formats, and changing task complexity. Evaluation of seven open-weight models reveals significant performance degradation across all three axes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agentic workflows require models to maintain consistent operations across thousands of outputs while context grows, sub-task complexity changes, and new data arrives. Existing models may accept entire inputs but lose track of progress or fail to apply operations consistently as generation proceeds. This represents a critical liability in real-world agentic applications.

Method: Long-Transduction is a controlled diagnostic that independently varies three axes: (1) local task complexity, (2) input data formatting, and (3) context length. It tests models' abilities to continuously read, mutate, and output input-context dependent operations including arithmetic, sorting, variable lookups, and table transformations.

Result: Seven open-weight models were evaluated, showing: 62.8% performance decrease when scaling context length from 4K to 128K tokens; 36.5% decrease when varying input format; and 39.9% decrease when increasing local task complexity.

Conclusion: These three failure modes (context length, input format, task complexity) represent critical liabilities in long-horizon agentic workflows, indicating that current models struggle to maintain consistent task execution as these factors scale.

Abstract: Long-horizon agentic workflows require models to sustain repeated state-dependent actions all while the context grows, sub-task complexity changes, and new data arrives. Each situation represents an independent axis along which an agent may fail. An agent reconciling a long ledger, for example, must repeatedly read its state, update the correct record, and preserve alignment across thousands of outputs. A model may accept the entire ledger yet lose its place or stop applying the operation consistently as generation proceeds. We introduce Long-Transduction, a controlled diagnostic that tests a model's ability to stay on task during long generation while continuously reading, mutating, and outputting input-context dependent operations such as arithmetic, sorting, variable lookups, and table transformations. Long-Transduction evaluation independently varies local task complexity, input data formatting, and context length isolate failures along each axis. We evaluate seven open-weight models, finding a 62.8\% decrease when scaling context length from 4-128K, a 36.5\% decrease when varying input format, and a 39.9\% decrease by increasing local task complexity. Together, these failures represent critical liabilities in long-horizon agentic workflows.

</details>


### [111] [UniEvo-VL: An On-policy Self-Distillation Training Recipe for Multimodal Model Self-improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38721)
*Fang Wu,Da Xing,Yanjie Huang,Junxi Wang,Ji Wang,Hejia Geng,Guancheng Wan,Bowen Zuo,Xiaomin Li,Shixiang Tang,Xinyu Xiang,Zehong Wang,Shiyi Du,Peng Xia,Shuangjia Zheng,Yining Hong,Li Erran Li,Jure Leskovec,Yejin Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UniEvo-VL is a self-evolving framework for multimodal models that enables them to learn from their own constructive self-correction feedback during test-time compute, without relying on external teachers. A single model acts as both teacher (conditioned on privileged critique) and student (seeing only the vanilla question), with training minimizing divergence between their denoising diffusion distributions over sampling trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern multimodal models have unified generation and understanding capabilities, enabling them to provide and learn from their own feedback. This work aims to leverage this unified capacity for self-improvement without external supervision or guidance, addressing the hot research line of recursive self-improvement.

Method: Uses a single multimodal model as both teacher and student with different contexts. The student only sees the vanilla question, while the teacher conditions on privileged critique. Training minimizes per-state divergence between their denoising diffusion distributions over the student's own sampling trajectories.

Result: Built on Qwen-image-2512, UniEvo-VL achieved significant improvements: GenEval increased from 0.747 to 0.808, and GenEval2 Soft-TIFA increased from 32.97 to 35.53. Experiments with stronger external critics (GPT5.6-Luna) suggest models with strong judge capabilities can achieve higher self-evolving ceiling. However, self-improvements are not uniform across different tasks (e.g., mixed text-rendering outcomes).

Conclusion: Self-improvements from the UniEvo-VL framework may not be uniform across different tasks. Multimodal models with stronger judge capabilities can anticipate higher self-evolving potential. This work provides insights for the recursive self-improvement research line to enhance user experience with multimodal models without external supervision.

Abstract: Modern multimodal models bring generation and understanding into a single unified system, which enables them to provide and learn from their own feedback. Motivated by this unified capacity, we introduce UniEvo-VL, a self-evolving framework for multimodal models to learn from this constructive self-correction feedback during test-time compute. Instead of relying on a separate, often larger, teacher, we leverage their self-critiques as privileged information and ask a single multimodal model to act as both teacher and student with different contexts. The student only sees the vanilla question, while the teacher conditions on the privileged critique. Then training minimizes the per-state divergence between their denoising diffusion distributions over the student's own sampling trajectories. Experiments demonstrate that UniEvo-VL improves the image generation capabilities of multimodal models, while maintaining their sensitivity to additional reflection information. Specifically, we build on top of the open-source Qwen-image-2512 and observe a significant performance gain from 0.747 to 0.808 on GenEval and from 32.97 to 35.53 on GenEval2 Soft-TIFA. Moreover, attempts with more powerful external critics (e.g., GPT5.6-Luna) show that multimodal models with strong judge capabilities can anticipate a higher self-evolving ceiling. Last but not least, mixed text-rendering outcomes show that our self-improvements may not be uniform across different tasks. Our study aims to shed light on the current hot recursive self-improvement research line to enhance the user experience when using multimodal models without external supervision or guidance.

</details>


### [112] [Code to Control: Synthesizing Parameterized Reactive Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38733)
*Zergham Ahmed,Joshua B. Tenenbaum,Chris Bates,Samuel J. Gershman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Code to Control (C2C) is a novel approach that synthesizes Python controllers as executable policies, separating program structure (generated by LLM) from parameters (fitted via derivative-free search). This eliminates LLM inference and planning at decision time, enabling real-time control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based control methods suffer from latency issues: either invoking LLM for action selection or requiring planning at every decision step, which limits real-time applicability. This work aims to achieve fast decision-making while maintaining the flexibility of LLM-based synthesis.

Method: C2C uses a two-stage approach: (1) LLM synthesizes the controller structure (program logic), (2) Derivative-free search optimizes the parameters using environment feedback. Once trained, the resulting Python controller executes directly without LLM inference or planning.

Result: C2C outperforms planning-based program synthesis methods across Atari games, Flappy Bird, and MuJoCo tasks. It remains competitive with deep RL (PPO) while using fewer environment interactions, transfers across substantial environment dynamics changes, and scales to complex locomotion tasks. Under the timing protocol, it achieves faster action selection than PPO.

Conclusion: Code to Control successfully bridges the gap between LLM-based program synthesis and real-time control by decoupling synthesis from execution. This enables efficient, deployable controllers that combine the flexibility of LLM synthesis with the speed of direct policy execution.

Abstract: Recent LLM-based approaches to control either invoke a language model to select actions or synthesize world models that require planning at every decision, introducing latency that can limit real-time use. We introduce Code to Control, an approach that synthesizes Python controllers which execute directly as policies. Code to Control separates program structure from parameters. An LLM synthesizes the controller structure, while derivative-free search fits its parameters for continuous control using feedback from the environment. Once learned, the resulting controllers require neither LLM inference nor planning at decision time, enabling real-time gameplay and, under our timing protocol, faster action selection than a PPO policy. Across a suite of Atari games, Flappy Bird, and MuJoCo tasks, Code to Control outperforms planning-based program synthesis methods, remains competitive with deep reinforcement learning while using fewer environment interactions, transfers across substantial changes in environment dynamics, and scales to complex locomotion tasks.

</details>


### [113] [Learning to Route in Visual Space via Multi-Step Embedding Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38743)
*Tianyu Chen,Mingyuan Zhou,Jiaxing Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VHOP addresses the bottleneck of visual agentic search by offloading multi-step navigation to the retrieval tool itself. The framework introduces a benchmark with five difficulty levels and VHOP-Router, an end-to-end training pipeline that transforms standard embedding models into autoregressive multi-step retrievers operating in visual latent space. This approach achieves 76.3% retrieval performance (up from <5%), improves task success rates by 52.7%, and reduces token length by 61% while maintaining generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual agentic search is bottlenecked by standard single-step retrievers. Agents must issue text queries for every intermediate step, struggling when visual clues are difficult to describe or when retrievers fail to surface necessary intermediate evidence within top results. The key hypothesis is that offloading multi-step navigation across the embedding space directly to the retrieval tool resolves this performance bottleneck.

Method: 1) VHOP framework: A flexible data generation framework and benchmark with five core difficulty levels testing both visual matching and search planning. 2) VHOP-Router: An end-to-end training pipeline combining supervised fine-tuning, online imitation learning, and reinforcement learning that transforms a standard embedding model into an autoregressive multi-step retriever. Operates directly in the visual latent space, retrieving linked image chains in a single tool call without requiring the agent to formulate intermediate text queries.

Result: Retrieval performance boosts from under 5% to 76.3%. In agentic search, task success rates improve by 52.7% and average token length reduces by 61% (from 1886 to 728). Upgrading the agent alone yields only 3.7% gain. Compared to a strong baseline retrieving top-50 results per step, VHOP-Router maintains superior performance while reducing in-context images by 23x and cutting cumulative API payload by 35x. Models generalize robustly to unseen difficulty levels and realistic test sets.

Conclusion: VHOP and VHOP-Router provide an efficient and effective solution for visual agentic search that leaves native LLM capabilities entirely intact. The approach demonstrates that embedding multi-step reasoning directly into the retriever significantly outperforms traditional agent-upgrading strategies.

Abstract: LLM agents rely on retrieval tools to access external knowledge, yet visual agentic search remains severely bottlenecked by standard single-step retrievers. In current pipelines, the agent must issue text queries for every intermediate step, struggling when visual clues are difficult to describe or when the retriever fails to surface necessary intermediate evidence within its top results. We hypothesize that offloading multi-step navigation across the entire embedding space directly to the retrieval tool resolves this performance bottleneck. To study this systematically, we introduce VHOP, a flexible data generation framework and benchmark with five core difficulty levels testing both visual matching and search planning. Using this framework, we develop VHOP-Router, an end-to-end training pipeline---combining supervised fine-tuning, online imitation learning, and reinforcement learning---that transforms a standard embedding model into an autoregressive multi-step retriever. Operating directly in the visual latent space, VHOP-Router retrieves linked image chains in a single tool call without requiring the agent to formulate intermediate text queries. Experiments show VHOP-Router boosts retrieval performance from under 5\% to 76.3\%. In agentic search, it improves task success rates by 52.7\% and reduces the average token length by 61\% from 1886 to 728, whereas upgrading the agent yields only a 3.7\% gain.
