Compared to a strong baseline where the agent retrieves the top 50 results per step, VHOP-Router maintains superior performance while reducing in-context images by $23\times$ and cutting the cumulative API payload by $35\times$. The models also generalize robustly to unseen difficulty levels and realistic test sets. Ultimately, VHOP and VHOP-Router provide an efficient and effective solution for visual agentic search that leaves native LLM capabilities entirely intact.

</details>


### [114] [Self-Evolving Algorithm-Design Agents: Escaping In-Context Evolutionary Stagnation via Population-Curated Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38757)
*Chen Lu,Ke Xue,Siyuan Xu,Mingxuan Yuan,Chao Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes Population-Curated Policy Optimization (PCPO), a sample-efficient parametric self-evolution method that enables LLM agents to learn from self-generated algorithms. By addressing in-context evolutionary stagnation through the Improvement Chain proposition and a hybrid policy update scheme, PCPO achieves state-of-the-art results in learning rate schedule design for chip placement and GPU kernel optimization, outperforming in-context evolutionary methods while requiring minimal training data (4 chip cases) and reducing inference costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from limitations of pure in-context evolutionary frameworks for LLM-based algorithm-design agents, which plateau in domains requiring specialized knowledge. While parametric adaptation offers a solution by internalizing specialized knowledge, conventional training requires abundant domain-specific corpora, which are scarce in complex algorithm-design scenarios where high-quality algorithms are limited.

Method: The paper proposes two key components: (1) Improvement Chain proposition - an analytical characterization of in-context evolutionary stagnation, showing how learning successive self-generated algorithms can locally increase the likelihood of neighboring algorithms; (2) Population-Curated Policy Optimization (PCPO) - a hybrid policy update scheme that utilizes a global population to retain and reuse high-quality, diverse self-generated algorithms, shifting the policy toward stronger algorithms through prompt distillation.

Result: In learning rate schedule design for global placement in EDA (trained on 4 chip cases, tested on 16), PCPO outperforms state-of-the-art in-context evolutionary methods (OpenEvolve and ShinkaEvolve) on average. With an 8B-size base model, PCPO achieves competitive performance compared to GPT-5.5 while reducing inference-time token cost. For GPU kernel designs, PCPO achieves an average of 8.27× speedup against the PyTorch Eager baseline.

Conclusion: The conclusion demonstrates that sample-efficient parametric self-evolution is viable for LLM-based algorithm-design agents. PCPO successfully internalizes specialized domain knowledge with minimal training data (4 examples), outperforms in-context evolutionary methods, rivals frontier closed-source models, and achieves significant speedups in practical kernel optimization tasks while reducing computational overhead.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly participating in complex real-world tasks in the form of algorithm-design agents, designing and refining algorithms. Many successful algorithm-design agents adopt pure in-context evolutionary frameworks, but they may quickly plateau in domains that require specialized knowledge. Parametric adaptation offers a way to internalize specialized knowledge, but conventional training requires abundant domain-specific corpora while high-quality algorithms are scarce in complex algorithm-design scenarios. In this paper, we propose sample-efficient parametric self-evolution where agents can explore and learn from self-generated algorithms. First, we characterize in-context evolutionary stagnation and analytically propose the Improvement Chain proposition, showing how learning successive self-generated algorithms can locally increase the likelihood of neighboring algorithms. Motivated by this local-transfer perspective, we further propose Population-Curated Policy Optimization (PCPO) to utilize a global population and a hybrid policy update scheme for retaining and reusing high-quality, diverse self-generated algorithms, shifting the policy towards stronger algorithms. In the task of learning rate schedule design for global placement in electronic design automation, trained only on 4 chip cases, PCPO outperforms the state-of-the-art in-context evolutionary methods (e.g., OpenEvolve and ShinkaEvolve) on average across 16 chip cases. With an 8B-size base model, PCPO achieves competitive performance compared to frontier closed-source models such as GPT-5.5. PCPO also reduces inference-time token cost by internalizing grounded domain knowledge and prompt distillation. Moreover, PCPO achieves significant speedups on four GPU kernel designs, with an average of 8.27$\times$ speedup against the PyTorch Eager baseline.

</details>


### [115] [PathAnchor: Path-Structured Evidence for Scientific Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38766)
*Qiuhui Chen,Jiafan Lu,Shuaimin Tang,Tao Dai,Suyuan Wang,Chenrui Ji,Zhenglei Zhou,Weimin Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PathAnchor is a bounded scientific reasoning system that uses path-structured evidence workspaces (Material-Sensor-Signal-System trajectories) to improve retrieval and citation completeness in scientific agents. It outperforms six evaluated systems on 120 flexible-sensor questions, achieving 82.6% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific agents often lose functional order, mix evidence across sources, or make conclusions that exceed the retrieved evidence, even when retrieving relevant passages. This indicates that evidence organization significantly impacts reasoning quality.

Method: PathAnchor retrieves source-linked Material-Sensor-Signal-System trajectories that preserve role, direction, and supporting evidence for each transition. A controller uses three read-only tools: (1) search paper-specific trajectories, (2) trace paths across candidate sources, and (3) open exact evidence before producing claim-cited answers with explicit evidence boundaries.

Result: PathAnchor achieves 82.6% accuracy on 120 single- and cross-paper flexible-sensor questions, leading six evaluated systems. Compared to unordered concept graphs, path-structured records increase source recall from 61.3% to 82.9%, raise answers with fully cited evidence from 69.2% to 90.0%, and reduce tool calls.

Conclusion: Evidence organization significantly affects retrieval and citation completeness under fixed agent resources. Path-structured representations that preserve functional relationships and directional transitions outperform traditional unordered concept graphs in scientific reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Scientific agents can retrieve relevant passages yet still lose functional order, mix evidence across sources, or state conclusions that exceed the retrieved record. We introduce PathAnchor, a bounded scientific reasoning system built on path-structured evidence workspaces. Instead of treating passages or extracted concepts as independent units, the system retrieves source-linked Material-Sensor-Signal-System trajectories that preserve role, direction, and the evidence supporting each transition. A controller uses three read-only tools to search paper-specific trajectories, trace paths across candidate sources, and open exact evidence before producing a claim-cited answer and an explicit evidence boundary. On 120 single- and cross-paper flexible-sensor questions, PathAnchor scores 82.6% and leads six evaluated systems. Under a matched controller, corpus, and six-call budget, replacing unordered concept graphs with path-structured records raises source recall from 61.3% to 82.9%, increases answers whose claims all cite opened evidence from 69.2% to 90.0%, and reduces tool calls. These results show that evidence organization affects retrieval and citation completeness under fixed agent resources.

</details>


### [116] [Action Conditioned Bisimulation For GUI Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38778)
*Hongbo Zhang,Liuyang Song,Quanquan Li,Daqian Yang,Yan Wen,Zhengtao Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of how an agent should determine when two web pages count as the "same" for memory purposes. The key insight is that observation similarity (pages looking alike) is insufficient because visually similar pages may behave differently. The proposed solution defines a merge rule based on action-conditioned bisimulation over the empirical predictive state graph, where two states merge only when their shared actions lead to agreeing outcomes under an affordance label. This rule-based approach (no training) replaces the merge rule in an existing outcome-value memory system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that memories built on observation similarity incorrectly merge pages that look alike but behave differently. In web interfaces, two tabs of one widget or two rows of one menu answer the same click differently, yet observation-based merging would treat them as identical. This leads to incorrect memory formation and poor agent performance.

Method: The method defines a merge rule as an action-conditioned bisimulation over the empirical predictive state graph that a frozen agent fills as it acts. Two states merge only when: (1) their shared actions lead to agreeing outcomes, and (2) successor blocks under an affordance label match. Observation similarity is explicitly excluded from the rule, and nothing is trained—the approach is purely rule-based. This replaces the merge rule of an existing outcome-value memory, enabling closed-loop comparison to isolate the effect.

Result: On MiniWoB++, the proposed method raises success rate over a memoryless agent. Control experiments demonstrate that: (1) identical exploratory detours produce no improvement, (2) the prior successor-representation merge produces no improvement, and (3) the same criterion without action conditioning produces no improvement. This isolates the effectiveness of the action-conditioned bisimulation approach.

Conclusion: The action-conditioned bisimulation merge rule successfully addresses the page identity problem in web navigation agents. By conditioning on actions and outcomes rather than observation similarity, the method correctly identifies when pages should be merged in memory. The empirical results on MiniWoB++ demonstrate that this approach improves agent performance, while control experiments validate that each component of the method is necessary for the improvement.

Abstract: An agent that remembers what it did on a web page must decide when two pages count as the same. Memories built on observation similarity merge pages that look alike but behave differently, and GUIs are full of such pages: two tabs of one widget or two rows of one menu answer the same click differently. We define the merge rule as an action-conditioned bisimulation over the empirical predictive state graph a frozen agent fills as it acts. Two states merge only when their shared actions lead to agreeing outcomes and successor blocks under an affordance label. Observation similarity never enters the rule, and nothing is trained. It replaces the merge rule of an existing outcome-value memory, so a closed-loop comparison isolates it. On MiniWoB++ it raises success rate over a memoryless agent, while a control taking identical exploratory detours, the prior successor-representation merge, and the same criterion without action conditioning change nothing.

</details>


### [117] [Persona and Persuasive Framing in AI Voice Agents: A $2\times2$ Field Experiment with Children](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38782)
*Thilo Tamme,David Steck,Anton Hantel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study examines how conversational agent design (persona authority and message framing) affects children's susceptibility to persuasion in a real-world setting (German Santa Claus telephone hotline). It uses a 2×2 randomized field experiment with 89 qualifying calls from children (median age 6).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While conversational agents increasingly interact with children, existing evidence comes almost entirely from lab settings. This study addresses the gap by examining how agent design shapes children's persuasion susceptibility in a naturalistic, real-world context.

Method: A 2×2 randomized field experiment embedded in a public German Santa Claus telephone hotline. Calls were randomly routed to one of four LLM voice agents varying in persona (Santa: high authority vs. Helper: low authority) and framing (persuasive nudges toward prosocial wishes vs. neutral). Of 1,072 logged calls, 89 met inclusion criteria. Analysis included both main effects and robustness checks with controls.

Result: Persuasive framing dramatically increased prosocial wish probability from 11.6% to 45.7%, robust to controls. Persona authority showed near-zero effect—Santa did not outperform Helper. However, persona significantly affected engagement: children hung up on Helper much more often within the first minute (65% vs. 39%).

Conclusion: When context already provides legitimacy (e.g., Santa hotline), how the agent speaks (framing) shapes children's compliance more than who it claims to be (persona authority). This challenges assumptions about authority-based persuasion in conversational agents for children.

Abstract: Conversational agents increasingly interact with children, yet evidence on how their design shapes children's susceptibility to persuasion comes almost entirely from the lab. We report a $2\times2$ randomized field experiment embedded in a public German Santa Claus telephone hotline. Children's calls were randomly routed to one of four LLM voice agents varying persona (Santa, high authority, vs. Helper, low authority) and framing (persuasive nudges toward prosocial wishes vs. neutral). Of 1,072 logged calls, 89 conversations (median age 6) met inclusion criteria. Persuasive framing raised the probability of a prosocial wish from 11.6% to 45.7%, robust to controls. Persona authority showed a near-zero effect: Santa did not outperform the Helper. Persona instead shaped engagement; children hung up on the Helper far more often within the first minute (65% vs. 39%). Where context already lends an agent legitimacy, how it speaks shapes children's compliance more than who it claims to be.

</details>


### [118] [Positive Ratings, Hidden Concerns: Employee Voice Disclosure in AI-Mediated Organizational Listening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38788)
*Thilo Tamme,Michael Saatkamp,Alma Bonte,Daniel Weiss,Anton Hantel,Andrej Levin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study examines how employees communicate in AI voice interviews versus structured surveys when disclosure carries hierarchical risk. Through a field study in a global consulting firm, researchers found that 20-41% of sessions showed employees giving favorable ratings followed by substantive concerns - revealing that employees often "soften concerns, deflect accountability, and bounded how far they went" in AI interviews. The study develops a grounded model of "bounded disclosure" and proposes that silence can persist inside expression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what conversational AI agents change in employee voice compared to structured surveys, particularly when disclosure carries hierarchical risk. The research addresses a gap in understanding how different listening channels affect what employees are willing to disclose.

Method: Field study inside a global management consulting firm using a process that pairs pre-survey with an adaptive AI voice interview on the same themes within one session. Analysis used Gioia methodology drawing on 158 protective quotes from 65 eligible sessions across 44 first-session interviews (132 matched theme observations).

Result: 20-41% of sessions showed favorable rating co-occurring with substantive concern voiced later, depending on the favorability threshold. Disclosure rarely arrived unguarded: employees softened concerns, deflected accountability, and bounded how far they went. This protective work tracked the perceived legitimacy of the listening structure.

Conclusion: The study develops a grounded model of bounded disclosure and derives four propositions for voice, channel, and listening research. The key insight is that "silence can persist inside expression" - employees may appear to speak but still hold back substantive concerns through various protective mechanisms.

Abstract: Organizations started listening to employees through conversational AI agents alongside structured surveys. Little is known about what these channels change in what employees say when disclosure carries hierarchical risk. We report a field study inside a global management consulting firm whose process pairs a pre-survey with an adaptive AI voice interview on the same themes within one session. Across 44 first-session interviews (132 matched theme observations), 20-41% of sessions showed a favorable rating co-occurring with a substantive concern voiced later, depending on the favorability threshold. The Gioia analysis drew on 158 protective quotes from 65 eligible sessions. Disclosure rarely arrived unguarded: employees softened concerns, deflected accountability, and bounded how far they went, and this protective work tracked the perceived legitimacy of the listening structure. We develop a grounded model of bounded disclosure and derive four propositions for voice, channel and listening research. Silence, we argue, can persist inside expression.

</details>


### [119] [GraphCert: Bootstrap Agentic Graph Reasoning with Certified Evidence Rubrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38798)
*Weiqi Jiang,Yuchen Ying,Rui Wang,Kaixuan Chen,Bingde Hu,Shunyu Liu,Yu Wang,Tongya Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphCert is a self-training method for graph reasoning agents that uses executable self-certification to generate training data without human annotation. It bootstraps graph-grounded QA pairs via a Bootstrapped Graph Quizzer, certifies evidence through execution and semantic curation, and trains a Graph Solver using GRPO with evidence alignment rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training capable graph agents requires large collections of QA pairs and reasoning trajectories, which are costly and difficult to scale. Using proprietary LLMs to generate supervision also risks exposing sensitive graph data to external services.

Method: GraphCert uses a Bootstrapped Graph Quizzer guided by generation controls to produce graph-grounded QA pairs with supporting evidence. These undergo execution certification and semantic curation. Accepted evidence is canonicalized into certified evidence rubrics that reward Graph Solver during GRPO training, considering both answer correctness and evidence alignment.

Result: GraphCert consistently outperforms substantially larger LLM agents and other post-training methods on five graph reasoning domains in GRBENCH. The learned policy transfers robustly across heterogeneous graph domains.

Conclusion: Executable self-certification is an effective approach to self-training compact graph reasoning agents. GraphCert acquires reusable graph-reasoning capabilities rather than domain-specific patterns, enabling transfer across heterogeneous graph domains.

Abstract: Graph agents extend large language models (LLMs) with the ability to actively explore and reason over knowledge graphs through multi-step interactions with graph tools. However, training capable graph agents typically requires large collections of question-answer pairs and reasoning trajectories, whose manual construction is costly and difficult to scale. Moreover, employing proprietary LLMs to generate such supervision further risks exposing sensitive graph data to external services. Therefore, we propose GraphCert to bootstrap agentic graph reasoning with certified evidence rubrics during post-training. Specifically, the Bootstrapped Graph Quizzer guided by generation controls produces graph-grounded QA pairs and marks supporting evidence, which undergo execution certification and semantic curation. The accepted evidence is then canonicalized into certified evidence rubrics that later reward Graph Solver evidence alignment alongside answer correctness during GRPO training. Experiments on five graph reasoning domains in GRBENCH demonstrate that GraphCert consistently outperforms substantially larger LLM agents and post-training method. Furthermore, our analysis demonstrates that the learned policy transfers robustly across heterogeneous graph domains, suggesting that GraphCert acquires reusable graph-reasoning capabilities rather than domain-specific patterns. These results establish executable self-certification as an effective approach to self-training compact graph reasoning agents. Our code will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [120] [When Reasoning Goes Astray: Attention Dynamics of Uncontrolled Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38817)
*Yuanhe Zhang,Ziwei Wang,Jie Ren,Haoran Gao,Zhenhong Zhou,Fanyu Meng,Cong Wu,Li Sun,Sen Su*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of uncontrolled reasoning in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), which can lead to resource exhaustion and service degradation. The authors introduce RADAR, a method that identifies reasoning states in real-time and detects abnormal attention patterns before repetition occurs. They also propose Attention Realignment to correct these deviations, reducing excessive reasoning loops while preserving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mitigations for LRM reasoning failures only truncate long outputs or react to surface repetition, failing to distinguish normal thinking from uncontrolled reasoning or explain how benign reasoning degenerates into harmful behavior. This research aims to provide a mechanistic understanding of reasoning degradation and enable targeted runtime interventions.

Method: The authors operationalize LRM generation as four distinct states and develop RADAR (Reasoning-state Analysis via Dynamic Attention Responses) to identify the current reasoning state in real-time by analyzing attention distributions. They then apply Attention Realignment to correct abnormal attention patterns toward normal generation patterns.

Result: Temporal analyses reveal that uncontrolled reasoning is characterized by attention distributions that deviate from normal generation. Abnormal trends become detectable before repetition begins. Attention Realignment consistently reduces looping while largely preserving benign performance.

Conclusion: RADAR provides a mechanistic account of how reasoning becomes uncontrolled, offering actionable guidance for identifying critical failure stages and designing targeted runtime interventions. The approach enables early detection of reasoning degradation and effective correction through attention realignment.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) improve performance on complex tasks through extended reasoning, yet the same process can degenerate into redundant verification and persistent generation loops. Such uncontrolled reasoning increases inference cost and creates risks of resource exhaustion and service degradation. However, existing mitigations largely truncate long outputs or react to surface repetition, and thus fail to distinguish normal thinking from uncontrolled reasoning or explain how benign reasoning degenerates into harmful behavior. In this paper, we operationalize LRM generation as four states and further introduce Reasoning-state Analysis via Dynamic Attention Responses (RADAR), which identifies the current reasoning state in real time and characterizes how effective reflection can develop into uncontrolled generation. Guided by RADAR's analysis, we further realign abnormal attention distributions toward patterns observed in normal requests and examine how this correction affects excessive reflection and persistent looping. Temporal analyses show that uncontrolled reasoning is characterized by attention distributions that deviate from normal generation, with abnormal trends becoming detectable before repetition begins. Correcting these deviations through Attention Realignment consistently reduces looping while largely preserving benign performance. Together, RADAR provide a mechanistic account of how reasoning becomes uncontrolled, offering actionable guidance for identifying critical failure stages and designing targeted runtime interventions.

</details>


### [121] [Whose Voice Survives the Summary? A Voice-Retention Audit of LLM Employee Listening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38818)
*Thilo Tamme,Anton Hantel,Bijan Khosrawi-Rad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a Voice Retention/Representation Ratio metric to measure representational bias in LLM-based employee feedback summarization systems. Using a bilingual corpus of 2,586 responses, the study reveals that these systems filter by popularity rather than sentiment, causing unique concerns to be systematically dropped while criticism survives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations increasingly use LLM summaries to route employee feedback to leaders, but this unaudited layer may silence already-spoken voice. The researchers aimed to understand how these summarization pipelines affect the representation of employee feedback, particularly distinguishing between sentiment-based and prevalence-based filtering.

Method: The study analyzed a bilingual (English/German) corpus of 2,586 free-text responses from a global professional service company. They introduced a Voice Retention/Representation Ratio metric and examined 45 leader-summaries, controlling for frequency and sentiment to determine what drives content retention.

Result: Employees supply criticism more reliably than praise (withholding praise is 82x more common). The pipeline filters by popularity, not sentiment: criticism survives, but concerns voiced once are dropped 86% of the time. Short and German-only content is disproportionately lost (theme retention 0.14 vs 0.74). Sentiment has no independent effect when controlling for frequency; the harm is prevalence-driven. Sentiment-only audits miss this issue. A targeted prompt recovers only named themes.

Conclusion: The study contributes a new metric (Voice Retention/Representation Ratio), field evidence demonstrating prevalence-driven bias in LLM summarization, and a disaggregated voice-retention card for practical auditing. The findings suggest current sentiment-based audits are insufficient and that LLM summarization systems systematically silence minority voices regardless of sentiment.

Abstract: Organizations increasingly route employee feedback to leaders through large language model (LLM) summaries, an unaudited layer that silences already-spoken voice. We introduce a Voice Retention / Representation Ratio metric for representational bias in summarization and apply it to a bilingual (English/German) corpus of 2,586 free-text responses from a global professional service company. First, employees supply criticism more reliably than praise (withholding praise is 82 times more common). Second, across 45 leader-summaries the pipeline filters by popularity, not sentiment: criticism survives, yet a concern voiced once is dropped 86% of the time, with short and German-only content lost on the same axis (theme retention 0.14 vs 0.74; German directional). Controlling for frequency, sentiment has no independent effect; the harm is prevalence-driven, which sentiment-only audits miss. A targeted prompt recovers only named themes. We contribute the metric, field evidence, and a disaggregated voice-retention card.

</details>


### [122] [More Choices, Fewer Decisions: Ordinal-Scale Bias in JEV-like Direct-Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38827)
*Tianxiang Gao,Jinzhe Li,Zhiyuan Li,Yi Chang,Yuan Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates "ordinal scale-utilization bias" in direct-decision models (like JEV and KEV models), which compress the candidate decision space and fail to fully utilize the ordinal scales provided by users. This bias is distinct from accuracy, gold imbalance, fixed positions, and candidate count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While direct-decision models are attractive for low-latency text classification and automatic evaluation, they must not only be accurate but also faithfully use the ordinal decision scales supplied by users. The researchers noticed that JEV heavily relies on "Neutral" predictions on ANLI despite balanced conditions, motivating a systematic investigation.

Method: The authors analyzed JEV 1.13 and three open KEV models across 36 ordinal datasets and 4 nominal tasks. They conducted experiments including: (1) examining prediction distributions on ANLI, (2) measuring "gold support utilization" across datasets, (3) randomizing candidate order, (4) refining scales from K=2 to 14 while controlling for gold support and positions, and (5) applying BA-LoRA post-training to address the bias.

Result: Models use only 67-76% of effective gold support on ordinal tasks vs 87-102% on nominal tasks. JEV assigns 38.8% of predictions to Neutral on ANLI despite balanced labels. Utilization falls for every model as K increases (reaching 26-75% at K=14). BA-LoRA post-training raised gold-relative utilization from ~47% to 86% on eight supervised scales.

Conclusion: Ordinal scale-utilization bias is a learned phenomenon rather than an immutable architectural limit. It can be modified through targeted post-training (BA-LoRA), achieving significant improvements in scale utilization while maintaining model performance.

Abstract: Direct-decision models turn text into low-latency structured labels and scores, making them attractive for classification and automatic evaluation. Yet reliability requires more than accuracy: a model must also use the ordinal decision scale supplied by the user faithfully. We analyze JEV~1.13 and three open KEV models. Our investigation begins with ANLI, where JEV assigns 38.8\% of all predictions and 51.3\% of errors to Neutral despite 74.95\% accuracy, nearly balanced gold labels, and balanced candidate positions. Across 36 ordinal datasets, final decisions use only 67--76\% of the effective gold support, versus 87--102\% on four nominal tasks. Randomizing candidate order weakens but does not remove this compression. Holding items and source scores fixed while balancing gold support and positions, we refine scales from $K=2$ to $14$; utilization falls for every model and reaches 26--75\% at $K=14$, although candidate probabilities remain broad for most models. Targeted BA-LoRA post-training raises gold-relative utilization from roughly 47\% to 86\% on eight supervised scales at both KEV sizes, showing that the compression is learned and modifiable rather than an immutable architectural limit. We call this ordinal scale-utilization bias: decision-stage candidate-space compression distinct from accuracy, gold imbalance, fixed position, and candidate count alone. The code and data are available at https://github.com/Glax147/jev_ordinal_scale_bia

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [123] [Gaussian Stippling: Efficient Sorting-Free 3D Gaussian Rendering through Hybrid Sampling and Spatiotemporal Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38488)
*Zijian Huang,Suiliang Mai,Chuankun Zheng,Yuan Meng,Yuchi Huo*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper presents a hybrid Gaussian Stippling method for order-independent rendering of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) assets, combined with a spatiotemporal reconstruction network to recover high-quality images from sparse stochastic samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional 3DGS requires expensive depth sorting and ordered alpha blending. Stochastic transparency enables sorting-free rendering but introduces spatial and temporal noise at low sample counts.

Method: The method combines primitive-based and fragment-based stippling adaptively, leveraging their complementary strengths. A Gaussian-aware spatiotemporal reconstruction network exploits Gaussian attributes to aggregate stochastic clues across space and time, suppressing stippling noise.

Result: The approach generalizes to unseen scenes without retraining/preprocessing, enabling interactive, temporally stable, and visually plausible rendering on mobile devices. With scene-specific training, it outperforms baselines in visual quality.

Conclusion: The proposed hybrid Gaussian stippling with spatiotemporal reconstruction achieves an efficient order-independent rendering framework that works directly on unmodified 3DGS assets, offering both performance (mobile deployment) and quality (high-fidelity configuration) options.

Abstract: Conventional 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) requires depth sorting and ordered alpha blending to correctly render overlapping Gaussian primitives. Stochastic transparency enables sorting-free rendering by replacing fractional alpha contributions with discrete stochastic visibility samples, but produces substantial spatial and temporal noise at low sample counts. We refer to this conversion from continuous Gaussian splats to discrete visibility samples as \textit{Gaussian Stippling}. Based on this, we present an efficient order-independent rendering and reconstruction framework that operates directly on unmodified 3DGS assets. Our method adaptively integrates primitive-based and fragment-based stippling, leveraging their complementary strengths across different rendering regimes to significantly improve rendering throughput. To recover high-quality images from sparse stochastic samples, we further introduce a lightweight Gaussian-aware spatiotemporal reconstruction network. By exploiting the Gaussian attributes retained by each stipple, the network aggregates structured stochastic clues across both space and time, effectively suppressing stippling noise. Experiments show that our hybrid Gaussian stippling method, coupled with a spatiotemporal reconstruction network trained on diverse scenes, generalizes to unseen scenes and enables interactive, temporally stable, and visually plausible rendering on mobile devices without retraining or preprocessing. With scene-specific training and appropriately scaled sampling and network capacity, our method further outperforms the baselines in visual quality, offering a high-fidelity configuration for quality-prioritized applications.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [124] [Travel Time Prediction in Supply Chain Management Using Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38190)
*Balaji Venkateswaran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This research applies machine learning and deep learning techniques to predict travel time for transportation and logistics in supply chain systems, using historical data to build an accurate prediction model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supply chain systems are complex and heavily rely on transportation and logistics of raw materials and finished goods. Accurate travel time estimation is critical for improving logistics consistency, performance, planning, demand forecasting, lead time management, assembly planning, and customer satisfaction.

Method: The research utilizes historical data combined with machine learning and deep learning techniques to develop a prediction model for travel time of inventory in supply chain logistics.

Result: The research builds an accurate model capable of predicting travel time of inventory in supply chain systems.

Conclusion: Accurate travel time prediction using ML/DL approaches can significantly improve supply chain operations including logistics planning, demand forecasting, lead time management, and customer satisfaction on the delivery side.

Abstract: The purpose of this research is to find data and methods using machine learning and deep learning to correctly predict the estimated travel time for transportation and logistics in a supply chain system. The supply chain ecosystem is very complex and heavily relies on the transportation and logistics of raw materials and finished goods. Accurate travel time estimation is critical because it helps supply chain members to improve logistics consistency and performance. This helps in planning, demand forecasting, lead time management and assembly planning. The logistics on the delivery side of the customer also plays a crucial role in customer satisfaction and voice of customer. With the collection of huge historical data and using novel techniques, the research builds an accurate model to predict travel time of inventory.

</details>


### [125] [EHR2Trace: Auditable EHR Data Infrastructure for Patient World Models and Clinical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38193)
*Xinye Yang,Yuli Wang,Cheng Ting Lin,Harrison Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EHR2Trace is a system that converts electronic health records from different sources into traceable patient events for training and evaluating patient world models and clinical agents. It addresses inconsistencies in EHR event recording by linking events to source records, separating event time from information availability, and distinguishing medication orders, dispensing, and administration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Patient world models and clinical agents require reliable histories of patient conditions, treatments, and information available at each decision point. Electronic health records contain these histories but are difficult to use consistently due to differences in how events are recorded across different sources.

Method: EHR2Trace implements several key mechanisms: (1) linking events to source records for traceability, (2) separating event time from information availability, (3) distinguishing medication orders, dispensing, and administration. The system supports both OMOP and MEDS exports with automated validation and reproducible builds. Evaluation was conducted across three clinical datasets using automated checks and injected fault detection.

Result: EHR2Trace converted 846.4 million events across three clinical datasets. Every applicable check passed except one unit-consistency check on MIMIC-IV. The system detected all 28 injected faults. A controlled prediction experiment demonstrated that assigning later diagnoses to admission time substantially inflated measured performance, and that models trained on such data lost accuracy when deployed on histories filtered by information availability.

Conclusion: EHR2Trace provides a reusable data foundation for patient world models and clinical agents, enabling researchers to inspect patient histories, check conversion decisions, and evaluate models with explicit data rules. The system's automated validation and reproducible builds ensure data quality and consistency across different EHR sources.

Abstract: Patient world models and clinical agents aim to predict changes in patients' health and support clinical work. Developing these systems requires reliable histories of patient conditions, treatments, and the information available at each decision. Electronic health records (EHRs) contain these histories, but differences in how events are recorded make them difficult to use consistently. We present EHR2Trace, a system that converts EHRs from different sources into traceable patient events for model training and evaluation. It links events to source records, separates event time from information availability, and distinguishes medication orders, dispensing, and administration. A shared event representation supports both OMOP and MEDS exports, with automated validation and reproducible builds. Across three clinical datasets, EHR2Trace converted 846.4 million events, with every applicable check passing except one unit-consistency check on MIMIC-IV, and detected all 28 injected faults. A controlled prediction experiment showed that assigning later diagnoses to admission time substantially inflated measured performance, and that a model trained on such data lost accuracy when deployed on histories filtered by availability. EHR2Trace provides a reusable data foundation for patient world models and clinical agents, helping researchers inspect patient histories, check conversion decisions, and evaluate models with explicit data rules.

</details>


### [126] [A Moving-Horizon Approximate Branch-and-Reduce Method for Deep Classification Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38194)
*Chenxuanyin Zou,Jiayang Ren,Qiangqiang Mao,Jing Liu,Marcus Lai,Yankai Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a moving-horizon approximate branch-and-reduce method for training near-optimal deep classification trees on large-scale datasets with continuous features, combining global optimality guarantees with improved efficiency through lookahead approximations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decision trees are important for interpretability but face scalability challenges; existing global optimal methods are limited by binary feature selection and shallow tree depths, while heuristic approaches sacrifice predictive accuracy.

Method: The method uses a hierarchical root-subtree optimization framework: solving the root-level problem via branch-and-reduce while approximating induced subtree problems using greedy heuristics (functioning as lookahead rollout in reinforcement learning), with a low-cost moving-horizon strategy for iterative refinement.

Result: The method exceeds testing accuracy of existing heuristic baselines while offering significantly greater scalability in terms of dataset size and tree depth compared to global optimal solvers.

Conclusion: The proposed moving-horizon approximate branch-and-reduce method successfully balances optimality and scalability, enabling near-optimal deep classification trees on large-scale datasets with continuous features.

Abstract: Despite the importance for interpretability, decision trees face severe scalability challenges. Existing global optimal methods are often limited by binary feature selection and shallow tree depths, whereas traditional heuristic approaches frequently sacrifice predictive accuracy. To overcome these limitations, this paper proposes a moving-horizon approximate branch-and-reduce method to train near-optimal deep classification trees on large-scale datasets with continuous features. Built on a hierarchical root-subtree optimization framework, the method solves the root-level problem via branch-and-reduce while approximating the induced subtree problem using greedy heuristics. Although the underlying framework is capable of guaranteeing global optimality, the approximation, which functions as a lookahead rollout in a reinforcement learning context, significantly boosts efficiency for deeper structures. A low-cost moving-horizon strategy is then employed to iteratively refine model accuracy. Extensive numerical results demonstrate that our method exceeds the testing accuracy of existing heuristic baselines while offering significantly greater scalability, in terms of both dataset size and tree depth, than global optimal solvers.

</details>


### [127] [A Data-Free Physics-Informed Neural Operator for Level-Set Interface Advection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38195)
*Muhammad Akbar Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a data-free physics-informed neural operator for level-set interface advection that trains without any reference solutions, using only the transport residual and geometric (eikonal) constraints. The method outperforms supervised baselines in terms of area conservation (2.7x better) despite larger field error, and a hybrid approach with fewer training samples (8 vs 16) outperforms fully supervised training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop a neural operator that can replace traditional solvers for interfacial problems without requiring expensive reference solutions from the solver it aims to replace. Current supervised approaches compete between anchoring terms and residuals when no solution data are available.

Method: The method uses a spacetime Fourier backbone that emits the entire trajectory in one pass. Training employs only the transport residual and a geometric constraint (eikonal equation |∇φ| = 1). The initial condition is imposed by construction rather than penalty, eliminating the competition between anchoring and residual terms that arises in data-free training.

Result: On reversed single vortex: data-free achieves 1.614% relative L2 error vs 0.369% for supervised (4.4x gap). On solid-body rotation: 3.804% vs 2.576% (1.5x gap). The eikonal constraint is valid for 99.7% of rotation domain but only 13.1% of vortex domain. Physics-trained operator conserves enclosed area 2.7x better than supervised baseline. A hybrid arm with 8 reference solutions outperforms supervised with 16 solutions.

Conclusion: The data-free physics-informed approach achieves comparable accuracy to supervised methods while being more physically consistent (better area conservation). The eikonal constraint validity critically affects performance rankings between benchmarks. The hybrid approach demonstrates that even minimal physics-informed regularization can significantly reduce data requirements, achieving superior results with half the training samples.

Abstract: Operators for interfacial problems are trained on reference solutions produced by the solver they are intended to replace. This work develops a data-free physics-informed neural operator for level-set interface advection, in which the interface is the equation's unknown and the operator maps an initial interface to the full spatiotemporal trajectory under a prescribed flow. Training uses only the transport residual and a geometric constraint; no reference solution enters the objective at any point. A spacetime Fourier backbone emits the entire trajectory in one pass, and the initial condition is imposed by construction rather than by penalty, which removes the competition between the anchoring term and the residual that otherwise arises when no solution data are available. Supervised and hybrid operators are trained under an identical architecture, family, budget and test set, and are reported throughout as baselines that quantify what refusing labels costs. On a reversed single vortex the data-free operator reaches 1.614 +/- 0.067% relative L2 error on 100 held-out initial interfaces against 0.369 +/- 0.035% for the supervised baseline, a factor of 4.4; on solid-body rotation the corresponding figures are 3.804 +/- 1.075% and 2.576 +/- 0.159%, a factor of 1.5. The two benchmarks rank the arms differently, and the difference is attributable to the eikonal constraint: the exact solution violates |grad phi| = 1 over 0.3% of the domain under rotation and 86.9% under the vortex. Where the constraint is valid the physics-trained operator conserves enclosed area 2.7 times better than the supervised baseline despite a larger field error, and a hybrid arm using eight reference solutions outperforms a supervised arm using sixteen.

</details>


### [128] [Conformal Adversarial Generative Ensemble](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38196)
*Ahmad Shahi,Mamehgol Yousefi,Brendon J. Woodford,Farhaan Mirza,Tapabrata Chakraborti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAGE (Conformal Adversarial Generative Ensemble) is a novel time series forecasting framework that combines generative modeling, adversarial discrimination, and conformal prediction to address the problem of extreme forecasts disproportionately influencing traditional ensemble methods. It uses p-values from nonconformity scores to dynamically adjust model weights, ensuring only the most credible predictions contribute to the final output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional ensemble methods for time series forecasting suffer from the disproportionate influence of extreme forecasts, which can significantly degrade forecast reliability and accuracy. There is a need for methods that can robustly handle outliers and noisy data while maintaining statistical rigor.

Method: CAGE employs multiple generative models to produce initial forecasts, which are then evaluated by a discriminative component using conformal prediction techniques. P-values derived from nonconformity scores help dynamically adjust model weights, minimizing the impact of unreliable forecasts. Only the most credible predictions contribute to the final ensemble output.

Result: Empirical and statistical analyses on two datasets (New Zealand's milk collection data and OWID monkeypox dataset) demonstrate that CAGE outperforms traditional ensemble methods, particularly in handling outliers and noisy data. The approach delivers accurate and statistically rigorous forecasts.

Conclusion: CAGE offers a versatile solution potentially applicable across finance, weather, and supply chain management. By incorporating conformal prediction, it enhances decision-making through accurate and statistically rigorous forecasts.

Abstract: Accurate time series forecasting is critical across various domains, yet traditional ensemble methods often suffer from the disproportionate influence of extreme forecasts. We introduce the Conformal Adversarial Generative Ensemble (CAGE), a novel framework that combines generative modeling, adversarial discrimination, and conformal prediction to enhance forecast reliability and accuracy. CAGE employs multiple generative models to produce initial forecasts, which are then evaluated by a discriminative component using conformal prediction techniques. P-values derived from nonconformity scores help dynamically adjust model weights, minimizing the impact of unreliable forecasts. This approach ensures that only the most credible predictions contribute to the final ensemble output. Our empirical and statistical analyses of time series data from New Zealand's milk collection and the global health data from the public owid-monkeypox dataset show that the CAGE outperforms traditional ensemble methods, especially in handling outliers and noisy data. By incorporating conformal prediction, CAGE delivers accurate and statistically rigorous forecasts, enhancing decision-making. We have demonstrated performance on two different datasets deliberately to showcase that the proposed method offers a versatile solution potentially applicable across finance, weather, and supply chain management.

</details>


### [129] [DualCast: A Dual-Path Language Model for Bimodal Financial Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38197)
*Wentao Zhao,Hongqiang Wu,Shanghang Liu,Zhaochen Zan,Yu Zhang,Biqing Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DualCast is a dual-path framework that combines a fast numerical forecaster (using a frozen Qwen3-8B language model with learned financial tokens) with a slow path (LoRA adapter) that revises predictions based on news. It uses adaptive frequency-equalizing residual vector quantization for improved codebook utilization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time-series forecasting needs to capture price dynamics across heterogeneous assets while incorporating news available at prediction time. Existing methods either lack semantic understanding from news or fail to share patterns across different assets efficiently.

Method: DualCast uses a dual-path architecture: (1) Fast path trains only financial-token embeddings and output heads on frozen Qwen3-8B, representing log-return patches as one summary token + three residual shape tokens; (2) Slow path uses a toggleable LoRA adapter conditioned on fast forecast and news to produce revised predictions. The method uses adaptive frequency-equalizing residual vector quantization to rebalance overloaded codewords.

Result: In zero-shot evaluations on equities and energy prices at 5-minute, daily, and weekly resolutions, the slow path achieved the lowest MAPE in 8 of 12 dataset-horizon settings, including all longest-horizon settings. News ablations showed additional gains in most settings, though magnitude varied across markets.

Conclusion: DualCast effectively combines a fast numerical forecaster with an optional text-conditioned revision mechanism, achieving superior performance in most tested settings, particularly for long-horizon predictions.

Abstract: Financial time-series forecasting must capture price dynamics across heterogeneous assets while incorporating news available at prediction time. We introduce DualCast, a dual-path framework that extends a frozen language model with a discrete financial vocabulary. Each log-return patch is represented by a learned summary token and three residual shape tokens, preserving local drift and volatility while allowing shape patterns to be shared across assets. To improve codebook utilization, we develop adaptive frequency-equalizing residual vector quantization, which rebalances overloaded codewords without compromising reconstruction accuracy. The fast path trains only the new financial-token embeddings and output heads on a frozen Qwen3-8B backbone. A toggleable LoRA adapter enables a slow path that conditions on the fast forecast and news available at the forecast origin to produce a revised prediction. The reviser is initialized by supervised fine-tuning and further optimized with a return-space group relative policy optimization objective that rewards improvements over the fast forecast. In zero-shot evaluations covering equities and energy prices at five-minute, daily, and weekly resolutions, the slow path achieves the lowest mean absolute percentage error among the compared methods in 8 of 12 dataset-horizon settings, including every longest-horizon setting. News ablations indicate additional gains in most tested settings, although their magnitude varies across markets. DualCast thus combines a fast numerical forecaster with an optional text-conditioned revision mechanism.

</details>


### [130] [FlashDiffusion: Fused Tiled Kernel Spectral Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38198)
*Julio Candanedo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlashDiffusion is a matrix-free method for efficient computation of diffusion maps and kernel matrices in geometric learning, addressing the O(N²) memory bottleneck of dense Gaussian kernels through GPU-accelerated block evaluation and multi-resolution eigensolver coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The geometric limit of diffusion maps with small bandwidths leads to high-rank matrices, and materializing dense Gaussian kernels requires O(N²) memory, which becomes computationally prohibitive for large datasets. Existing methods struggle with the trade-off between resolution (small bandwidth) and computational efficiency.

Method: The paper introduces FlashDiffusion, which combines three key innovations: (1) Matrix-free evaluation of dense Gaussian kernel blocks in fused GPU tiles for efficient computation, (2) Coupling the eigensolver to an empirical β-flow that selects the finite-sample resolution scale adaptively, and (3) A continuation strategy over sample size and bandwidth that warm-starts increasingly expensive spectral solves from coarser resolutions.

Result: The method enables efficient computation of diffusion maps at small bandwidths (high resolution) while avoiding the O(N²) memory bottleneck of traditional dense kernel approaches.

Conclusion: FlashDiffusion provides a scalable solution for nonlinear spectral representation in geometric learning, effectively bridging the gap between theoretical geometric limits and practical finite-sample computations through adaptive resolution selection and multi-resolution warm-starting.

Abstract: Diffusion maps, and kernel methods more generally, provide an interpretable nonlinear spectral representation basis for geometric learning. In the geometric limit, small bandwidth, these matrices tend to be high rank and thus require materializing dense Gaussian kernels requires $O(N^2)$ memory. We introduce FlashDiffusion, a matrix-free method that evaluates dense Gaussian kernel blocks in fused GPU tiles and couples the eigensolver to an empirical $β$-flow that selects the finite-sample resolution scale. A continuation over sample size and bandwidth warm-starts increasingly expensive spectral solves from coarser resolutions.

</details>


### [131] [Kinematic signatures of impairment: Detecting alcohol intoxication in e-scooter riders using sensor data and machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38276)
*Rahul Rajendra Pai,Marco Dozza,Alexander Rasch,Ali Mohammadi,Marco Capuccini*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study develops a real-time intoxication detection system for e-scooter riders using onboard kinematic sensors and entropy-based signal analysis. The method achieved 85% classification accuracy in distinguishing sober from intoxicated riders (0.05% and 0.08% BAC).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alcohol intoxication is a leading cause of fatal and severe e-scooter crashes. Current countermeasures (temporal restrictions, pre-ride cognitive screening) cannot continuously assess impairment in real time, necessitating a passive monitoring approach that preserves mobility for sober riders.

Method: Controlled experiment with 25 participants riding an instrumented e-scooter through a test track under three conditions: sober, 0.05% BAC, and 0.08% BAC. Data was collected from a six-axis IMU and throttle/brake sensors at 100 Hz. Two signal features were computed: normalized permutation entropy (temporal complexity) and standard deviation (signal amplitude). Repeated measures correlation identified significant features, and an entropy-based multi-class logistic regression classifier was evaluated using leave-one-participant-out cross-validation.

Result: Seven kinematic features (all IMU and throttle signals) showed significant changes with increasing intoxication: entropy decreased (p < 0.001) while standard deviation increased (p < 0.01). This indicates intoxicated riders shift from continuous low-amplitude micro-corrections to fewer high-amplitude reactive corrections. The classifier achieved 85% overall accuracy, weighted one-vs-rest AuROC of 0.94, and sober-vs-high AuROC of 1.00. Steering rate and lateral acceleration were the most important predictive features.

Conclusion: Onboard kinematic sensing combined with entropy-based signal analysis can reliably distinguish sober from intoxicated e-scooter riding, providing a foundation for automatic intoxication detection systems that preserve mobility for sober riders.

Abstract: Alcohol intoxication is a leading contributor to fatal and severe-injured e-scooterist crashes. Current countermeasures, such as temporal restrictions or pre-ride cognitive screening, cannot continuously assess an e-scooterist's physical motor control or impairment in real time. We conducted a controlled experiment in which 25 participants rode an instrumented e-scooter through a test track while sober and at two targeted blood alcohol concentration levels (0.05% and 0.08%). The e-scooter was instrumented with a six-axis inertial measurement unit (IMU), and throttle and brake lever position sensors, all sampled at 100 Hz. Two complementary signal features were computed: normalised permutation entropy, which quantifies temporal complexity, and standard deviation, which quantifies signal amplitude. Repeated measures correlation identified seven kinematic features (all IMU and throttle signals) whose entropy decreased (p < 0.001) while standard deviation increased (p < 0.01) with increasing intoxication, indicating that intoxicated riders shift from continuous, low-amplitude micro-corrections to fewer, high-amplitude reactive corrections. An entropy based multi-class logistic regression classifier, evaluated through leave-one-participant-out cross-validation, achieved 85% overall accuracy and a weighted one-vs-rest area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AuROC) of 0.94, with a sober-vs-high AuROC of 1.00. Steering rate and lateral acceleration were the most important predictive features, indicating that alcohol induces a distinct collapse in lateral equilibrium during riding. Ultimately, these results demonstrate that onboard kinematic sensing combined with entropy-based signal analysis can reliably distinguish sober from intoxicated e-scooter riding, providing a foundation for automatic intoxication detection systems that preserve mobility for sober riders.

</details>


### [132] [Hermes: Learning Contextual Reasoning Unlocks Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38332)
*Xinyu Li,Mononito Goswami,Hao Liu,Nikos Kanakaris,Langlin Huang,Prithwish Jana,Patrick Blöbaum,Purak Jain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hermes and Hermes-Learn enable models to learn contextual reasoning—adaptively deciding how to allocate and reuse context during inference—which improves test-time scaling performance, with training closing the gap between capable and smaller models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time scaling approaches prescribe context allocation decisions through their harness. The authors argue these decisions should be shifted to the model itself, enabling adaptive contextual reasoning that varies with problem complexity and reasoning progress.

Method: 1) Hermes: a family of configurable harnesses that progressively vary model control over context allocation and reuse; 2) Hermes-Learn: a two-stage framework for training models to learn contextual reasoning capabilities.

Result: Capable models can exploit flexibility to scale with additional inference-time compute. Smaller open-source models initially struggle but training with Hermes-Learn induces adaptive strategies that vary with problem and reasoning progress. Gains generalize across benchmarks and models, extrapolate beyond training compute, and transfer to other test-time scaling methods.

Conclusion: Shifting context allocation decisions to the model enables effective test-time scaling. With appropriate training (Hermes-Learn), models develop adaptive contextual reasoning strategies that generalize beyond training conditions and transfer to complementary test-time scaling methods.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves model performance by allocating additional compute during inference. Using this compute effectively across multiple context windows requires deciding how to allocate fresh contexts and what information to carry between them. We call a model's ability to make these decisions contextual reasoning. Existing approaches largely prescribe these decisions through their harness; we instead shift them to the model. We introduce 1) Hermes, a family of simple, configurable harnesses that progressively varies model control over context allocation and reuse, and 2) Hermes-Learn, a two-stage framework for learning these capabilities. We find that capable models can exploit this flexibility to scale with additional inference-time compute, while smaller open-source models initially struggle to do so. Training with Hermes-Learn closes this gap, inducing adaptive contextual reasoning strategies that vary with both the problem and the progress of reasoning. These gains generalize across benchmarks and models, extrapolate beyond the inference-time compute seen during training, and transfer to complementary test-time scaling methods beyond Hermes.

</details>


### [133] [Activation-Conditioned Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38342)
*Zhexi Lu,Subhajit Chaudhury,Tejaswini Pedapati,Keerthiram Murugesan,Lei Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACSD (Activation-Conditioned Self-Distillation) is a novel self-distillation method that extracts a steering vector by contrasting activations of correct self-generated trajectories versus incorrect ones. This vector is applied at each prediction position to provide dense token-level supervision without requiring reference solutions or teacher updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) uses a model as its own teacher but faces challenges: providing privileged information doesn't ensure effective token-level supervision throughout long reasoning responses. Reference-solution conditioning alone is insufficient for dense supervision across lengthy generations.

Method: ACSD contrasts activations between self-generated trajectories that reach verified correct answers within a generation budget versus all remaining trajectories. A frozen copy of the base model applies this steering vector at each prediction position. The student learns from next-token distributions on student-generated prefixes. Key features: outcome verification for direction construction and calibration; no problem-specific reference text needed; no teacher parameter updates required; the distilled student is used alone at inference.

Result: On five models, ACSD achieves highest mean accuracy over four mathematical benchmarks. On DeepSeek-R1-0528-Qwen3-8B: 71.9% mean mathematical accuracy and 70.9% LiveCodeBench v6 pass@12, compared to 69.0% and 66.3% for reference-conditioned OPSD baseline. Contrasts among correct trajectories support distillation. Extracted directions can be reused across mathematical training datasets. ACSD maintains more stable late-position logit-update magnitudes than OPSD on fixed student trajectories.

Conclusion: ACSD successfully enables effective token-level self-distillation without reference solutions. The steering vector approach provides dense supervision throughout long responses, achieving state-of-the-art results across multiple models and benchmarks. The method is generalizable (directions reusable across datasets) and stable (consistent logit updates).

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation uses a model as its own teacher to provide dense supervision for reasoning, often through reference-solution conditioning. Providing privileged information does not by itself ensure effective token-level supervision throughout long responses. We introduce Activation-Conditioned Self-Distillation (ACSD), which extracts a steering vector by contrasting activations of self-generated trajectories that reach verified correct answers within a generation budget with those of all remaining trajectories. A frozen copy of the base model applies this vector at each prediction position, and the student learns from its next-token distributions on student-generated prefixes. Outcome verification is used for direction construction and calibration; distillation requires neither problem-specific reference text nor teacher parameter updates. The distilled student is used alone at inference. On each of five models, ACSD achieves the highest mean accuracy over four mathematical benchmarks among the evaluated methods. On DeepSeek-R1-0528-Qwen3-8B, mean mathematical accuracy reaches 71.9\% and LiveCodeBench v6 pass@12 reaches 70.9\%, compared with 69.0\% and 66.3\% for the reference-conditioned OPSD baseline. Contrasts among correct trajectories also support distillation, and extracted directions can be reused across mathematical training datasets. On fixed student trajectories, ACSD maintains more stable late-position logit-update magnitudes than OPSD.

</details>


### [134] [Function-Space Transformer with Adaptive Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38348)
*Guorui Sang,Pedram Rooshenas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Function-Space Transformer (FST) is a novel framework that learns from functions through a spatially adaptive continuous latent representation. It predicts anchor locations from input observations and recursively refines anchor features through function-space interactions, allowing the representation to adapt its spatial organization to each input rather than inheriting that of the observation grid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The mismatch between continuous function representation and discrete observation samples creates a trade-off when using fixed uniform grids - resolving localized variation requires increasing computation across the domain. Neural operators address this by learning mappings between functions, while latent-attention architectures provide flexible processing of sampled observations. However, existing approaches lack explicit spatial organization in their latent representations.

Method: FST stores features at anchor locations predicted from input observations and recursively refines these anchor features through function-space interactions. This approach allows the representation to adapt its spatial organization to each input rather than inheriting that of the observation grid, while supporting both spatially resolved and finite-dimensional outputs.

Result: On PDE solution prediction using PDEBench Burgers and Darcy flow, FST substantially outperforms Perceiver IO baseline and is highly competitive with Fourier Neural Operator. On ImageNet-1K, FST achieves higher classification accuracy than Vision Transformer baseline with fewer parameters. Ablations support the benefits of function-space updates and recursive refinement.

Conclusion: The results highlight the potential of adaptive continuous representations for both scientific prediction (PDE solving) and visual recognition (ImageNet classification), demonstrating that spatially adaptive anchor-based representations can effectively bridge discrete observations and continuous function spaces.

Abstract: Many forms of data, including physical fields, geometric shapes, and visual signals, are naturally described by functions over continuous domains but are observed through discrete samples. Representing these functions on fixed uniform grids imposes a trade-off between resolving localized variation and increasing computation across the domain. Neural operators address this mismatch by learning mappings between functions, while latent-attention architectures provide flexible processing of sampled observations. We introduce the Function-Space Transformer (FST), a framework for learning from functions through a spatially adaptive continuous latent representation. FST stores features at anchors whose locations are predicted from the input observations and recursively refines these anchor features through function-space interactions. This allows the representation to adapt its spatial organization to each input rather than inherit that of the observation grid, while supporting both spatially resolved and finite-dimensional outputs. On PDE solution prediction using PDEBench Burgers and Darcy flow, FST substantially outperforms the Perceiver IO baseline, whose latent representation lacks explicit spatial organization, and is highly competitive with the Fourier Neural Operator. On ImageNet-1K, FST achieves higher classification accuracy than the Vision Transformer baseline, with fewer parameters across these comparisons. Ablations further support the benefits of function-space updates and recursive refinement. Together, these results highlight the potential of adaptive continuous representations for both scientific prediction and visual recognition.

</details>


### [135] [MILO: Automated Harness Discovery via Orchestrated Multi-Agent Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38349)
*Prithwish Jana,Mononito Goswami,Hao Liu,Xinyu Li,Langlin Huang,Zhehui Huang,Zhishen Huang,Patrick Blöbaum,Anoop Deoras,Purak Jain,Nikos Kanakaris,Sahika Genc*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Modern agentic systems combine an AI model with a harness that controls execution and environmental interactions. Harness design strongly affects long-horizon performance, yet its combinatorial search space demands substantial human effort that must be repeated as models change. Existing automated methods explore this space narrowly, optimizing only components such as prompts or skills or becoming trapped by fixed, exploitative search strategies. We introduce MILO (Meta-evolutionary Island Orchestration), a framework that co-evolves agent harnesses and the strategy used to discover them. MILO combines: (i) hierarchical lineage memory over island-based trees, using rejected mutations as negative evidence; (ii) per-island mutator agents that rewrite complete harnesses using global search history and parent-specific feedback; and (iii) an orchestrator that adapts search through lineage grafting and speciation, mutator reassignment and curriculum revision. Across Terminal-Bench 2.1, PaperBench, and DeepSWE, MILO-discovered harnesses outperform eight state-of-the-art harnesses and six search methods using frontier (Opus 4.8) and open-weight (gpt-oss-120b) models. With Opus 4.8, MILO improves resolution over its initial harness by $+12.0\%$, $+28.3\%$, and $+10.3\%$, respectively, compared with best prior-search gains of $+4.5\%$, $+18.3\%$, and $0\%$. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, it achieves $86.1 \pm 2.0\%$, exceeding the official leaderboard's top entry ($83.8 \pm 2.3\%$) while using 26\% fewer tokens than its initial harness. On EinsteinArena open problems, MILO improves best-known upper bounds for Erdős minimum-overlap ($0.3808586 \to 0.3808568$) and the first and third autocorrelation inequalities ($1.50274365 \to 1.50274360$; $1.45081 \to 1.44889$).

</details>


### [136] [Continual Learning of Dynamical Systems in Recurrent Neural Networks through Recyclable Unit Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38356)
*Sima Hashemi,Daniel Durstewitz,Georgia Koppe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses Continual Dynamical Systems Reconstruction (cDSR), where models must learn new systems while preserving previously learned dynamics. The authors benchmark existing continual learning methods on the interpretable AL-RNN architecture and introduce CRUG (Continually-Recyclable Unit-Gating), which uses differentiable gates with L0-based penalty to selectively allocate and recycle task-specific units, achieving zero forgetting and strong forward transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge in cDSR is that even small parameter updates in recurrent models can qualitatively alter their behavior over long autonomous rollouts. Existing continual learning methods had not been systematically evaluated for this problem, and parameter isolation approaches risk exhausting fixed-size networks when learning many tasks sequentially.

Method: The authors benchmark three categories of continual learning methods (parameter regularization, replay, and parameter isolation) on the AL-RNN architecture. They introduce CRUG, which uses: (1) differentiable gates with L0-based penalty for task-specific unit selection, (2) unit recycling for capacity conservation, and (3) directed connections allowing later tasks to reuse earlier representations without affecting previously committed units' dynamics.

Result: CRUG achieves the strongest reconstruction-capacity trade-off among tested methods with zero forgetting. It reliably learns heterogeneous sequences of nonlinear and chaotic systems. Forward transfer is more pronounced and useful when tasks share similar underlying dynamics. The advantages extend beyond autonomous cDSR to sequential cognitive tasks.

Conclusion: CRUG effectively addresses the capacity and forgetting challenges in continual dynamical systems reconstruction. The method enables efficient unit allocation through gating and recycling, preserves previously learned dynamics, and enables beneficial forward transfer when tasks have similar dynamics. This represents a promising approach for lifelong learning in dynamical systems modeling.

Abstract: Dynamical Systems Reconstruction (DSR) aims to infer models from observed time series that reproduce a system's qualitative long-term behavior. Continual DSR (cDSR) requires learning new systems while preserving previously learned dynamics, yet even small parameter updates in recurrent models can qualitatively alter their behavior over long autonomous rollouts. We benchmark established continual learning (CL) methods spanning parameter regularization, replay, and parameter isolation on the fully trainable and interpretable Almost-Linear RNN (AL-RNN). Parameter isolation preserves earlier dynamics most effectively, but excessive task-specific allocations can rapidly exhaust a fixed-size network. We therefore introduce Continually-Recyclable Unit-Gating (CRUG), which conserves capacity through compact allocation and forward transfer. Differentiable gates trained with an $L_0$-based penalty select task-specific units, while unused units are recycled for subsequent tasks. Directed connections allow later tasks to reuse earlier representations without affecting the dynamics of previously committed units. CRUG achieves the strongest reconstruction--capacity trade-off among the tested methods with zero forgetting and reliably learns a heterogeneous sequence of nonlinear and chaotic systems. Furthermore, we show that forward transfer is more pronounced and useful when tasks share similar underlying dynamics. Lastly, we demonstrate that CRUG's advantages extend beyond autonomous cDSR to sequential cognitive tasks.

</details>


### [137] [On the Off-Policy Teacher in On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38360)
*Langlin Huang,Hao Liu,Mononito Goswami,Xinyu Li,Prithwith Jana,Nikos Kanakaris,Patrick Blöbaum,Purak Jain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCOUT addresses a fundamental asymmetry in on-policy distillation where the teacher must supervise student-generated prefixes it was not optimized for. The method adapts the teacher using RL with verifiable rewards to improve its continuation performance from student prefixes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation creates a mismatch: while student trajectories are on-policy for the student, they are off-policy for the teacher. The teacher was optimized to continue from its own prefixes but must supervise student-generated prefixes, causing performance degradation as prefixes grow longer.

Method: SCOUT is a co-training framework that adapts the teacher to student-generated prefixes using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. Alongside standard OPD updates, it periodically optimizes the teacher's conditional ability by having the teacher generate continuations from student prefixes and learn from outcome rewards.

Result: SCOUT improves the teacher's ability to continue from student-generated prefixes, supporting the intended mechanism of student-conditioned teacher adaptation. Across multiple teacher-student configurations, model scales, and reasoning domains, SCOUT consistently improves the effectiveness of on-policy distillation.

Conclusion: Student-conditioned teacher adaptation via SCOUT successfully addresses the asymmetry problem in on-policy distillation, providing a general framework that improves distillation outcomes across various configurations and domains.
