Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has recently emerged as a promising post-training paradigm in which the student learns from trajectories generated by its own policy under dense teacher supervision. However, OPD introduces a fundamental asymmetry: although the sampled trajectories are on-policy for the student, they are off-policy for the teacher. The teacher is typically optimized to continue from prefixes generated by its own policy, but during OPD it must instead supervise prefixes generated by the student. Empirically, we find that its continuation performance degrades as these prefixes grow longer. To address this issue, we propose Student-COnditioned Updates of the Teacher (SCOUT), a co-training framework that adapts the teacher to student-generated prefixes. Alongside standard OPD updates, SCOUT periodically optimizes the teacher's conditional ability using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, where the teacher generates continuations from student prefixes and learns from outcome rewards. Controlled experiments show that SCOUT improves the teacher's ability to continue from student-generated prefixes, supporting the intended mechanism of student-conditioned teacher adaptation. Across multiple teacher--student configurations, model scales, and reasoning domains, SCOUT also consistently improves the effectiveness of on-policy distillation.

</details>


### [138] [Simulator-Refined Diffusion for Radio-Frequency Inverse Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38363)
*Jinhao Liang,Jacob K. Christopher,Michael Frei,Tommaso Dreossi,Nando Fioretto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Simulator-Refined Diffusion (SRD), a novel method that combines a low-fidelity differentiable surrogate with a high-fidelity non-differentiable simulator for PCB layout generation using diffusion models. The approach addresses the challenge of meeting quantitative electromagnetic specifications in inverse design by using the surrogate's gradient to guide perturbation direction while the simulator refines the design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models show promise for inverse design of PCBs conditioned on target S-parameters, but struggle to meet quantitative electromagnetic specifications. Current approaches using gradient-based guidance face a dilemma: full-wave EM simulators are accurate but expensive and non-differentiable, while differentiable surrogates are informative but not always reliable.

Method: The proposed SRD method combines a low-fidelity differentiable surrogate with a high-fidelity non-differentiable simulator within the diffusion sampling process. Unlike standard zeroth-order optimization requiring many random perturbations, SRD uses the surrogate's gradient to propose the perturbation direction, then employs the simulator to search based on this direction to identify effective design updates.

Result: Experimental results across different settings show that SRD consistently outperforms current state-of-the-art methods. The generated layouts' simulated S-parameters match target specifications up to 21.2% closer for in-distribution targets and up to 19.8% for out-of-distribution targets.

Conclusion: The Simulator-Refined Diffusion approach successfully addresses the limitations of both full-wave simulators and differentiable surrogates in PCB layout generation. By intelligently combining both tools within the diffusion sampling process, SRD achieves superior performance on both in-distribution and out-of-distribution targets, demonstrating a promising direction for AI-driven electronic design automation.

Abstract: Diffusion models have shown potential in inverse design of printed circuit boards (PCBs), enabling the generation of layouts conditioned on target S-parameters. Despite this promise, applying diffusion models to PCB layout generation remains challenging due to their difficulty in meeting the quantitative electromagnetic specifications. A common approach is gradient-based guidance, which biases the diffusion sampling process with the gradient of an objective used for evaluation. However, full-wave electromagnetic simulators are accurate but expensive and typically non-differentiable, whereas differentiable surrogates are informative but not always reliable. To address these limitations, this paper proposes Simulator-Refined Diffusion (SRD), a novel combination of a low-fidelity differentiable surrogate and a high-fidelity non-differentiable simulator within the diffusion sampling process. Unlike standard zeroth-order optimization, which requires a great number of random perturbations, our approach uses the surrogate's gradient to propose the perturbation direction while the simulator then searches based on this direction to identify an effective design update. Experimental results across different settings show that this method consistently outperforms current state-of-the-art methods, producing layouts whose simulated S-parameters match the target specifications up to 21.2% closer for in-distribution targets and up to 19.8% for out-of-distribution targets.

</details>


### [139] [Learning to Plan from Random Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38383)
*Deqian Kong,Guangyan Sun,Sheng Cheng,Sirui Xie,Bo Pang,Jianwen Xie,Tony Geng,Caiwen Ding,Ying Nian Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a method for long-range planning in environments using only random exploration data, without requiring policy-improvement training. The approach learns temporal relations between states at different horizons using a conditional energy-based model, then uses these learned relations to guide planning toward goals at test time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key insight is that random exploration can reveal how an environment is traversed before any goal is specified, and this experience should be able to support long-range planning without expensive policy-improvement training. Traditional approaches require learning policies through reinforcement learning, which is data-inefficient. The authors ask whether random walk data alone can encode useful spatial/temporal relations for planning.

Method: The method has two components: (1) A conditional energy-based model that learns temporal log-density ratios through horizon-conditioned embeddings, trained on observation pairs using noise-contrastive estimation without action or reward labels. (2) At test time, a separate local dynamics model predicts candidate action outcomes, and the temporal model evaluates progress toward the goal by selecting/aggregating improvements across different horizons. The agent executes one action and replans with both models fixed.

Result: The approach demonstrates successful long-range maze planning from random exploration using both states and images. The learned score fields, embedding probes, and planned routes exhibit properties of a multiscale cognitive map. Additional experiments show egocentric navigation from random exploration and manipulation planning from suboptimal data.

Conclusion: Random exploration alone can provide sufficient information for long-range planning tasks. The learned temporal relations form a multiscale cognitive map that supports planning at different horizons. This approach eliminates the need for reward-based policy training while still enabling complex navigation and manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Random exploration reveals how an environment can be traversed before a goal is specified. Can this experience support long-range planning without policy-improvement training? Our random-walk analysis explains what temporal relations contain: short horizons reveal geodesic geometry in the diffusion limit, while longer horizons reveal connectivity between regions before mixing removes these distinctions. We learn these relations with a conditional energy-based model that estimates temporal log-density ratios through horizon-conditioned embeddings. The model is trained on observation pairs by noise-contrastive estimation, without action or reward labels. The planner queries these learned relations at different horizons as it moves toward the goal. At test time, a separate local dynamics model predicts candidate action outcomes, and the temporal model evaluates their progress toward the goal by selecting or aggregating estimated improvements across horizons. The agent executes one action and replans with both models fixed. Experiments demonstrate long-range maze planning from random exploration using states and images. Learned score fields, embedding probes, and planned routes exhibit properties of a multiscale cognitive map. We further demonstrate egocentric navigation from random exploration and manipulation planning from suboptimal data.

</details>


### [140] [Role-guided Speaker Deletion Verification in Clinical Psychiatry Speech Recordings with Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38491)
*Joseph T Colonel,Daniel Katzman,Kelsey Kirker,Adam N Davidson,Shalaila S Haas,Cheryl Corcoran,René S Kahn,Guillermo Checci,Baihan Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the verification problem of confirming speaker removal in dyadic psychiatric interview recordings. The authors develop two symmetric pipelines (for clinician and patient speech removal verification) that use audio-language and large-language models to scan redacted audio for missed deletions. Testing four open-weight models on 48 recordings, a disjunctive OR ensemble achieved an F1 of 0.478 (precision 0.330, recall 0.870), with recall gains indicating complementarity across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from practical needs in clinical psychiatry research: investigators often need to remove designated speakers from multi-speaker recordings due to consent and protocol requirements, but verifying complete removal at scale is infeasible manually. This creates a need for automated verification systems.

Method: The study employs two parallel, symmetric pipelines: one confirming clinician speech removal and another confirming patient speech removal. Each pipeline redacts raw audio for its target role and scans the output using audio-language and large-language models. Four open-weight models were tested (Gemma-4-12B, Gemma-4-31B, Nemotron-3-Nano, Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni) in inference-only mode. A disjunctive OR ensemble over fourteen model-view configurations was used for final predictions.

Result: The disjunctive OR ensemble achieved a combined F1 of 0.478 with precision 0.330 and recall 0.870. This represents an improvement over individual model estimates, driven primarily by recall gains. The results suggest substantial complementarity across models and context views.

Conclusion: The ensemble approach with multiple models and context views can effectively identify missed speaker deletions in psychiatric interview recordings, with high recall (0.870) being a key strength. However, the precision is relatively low (0.330), indicating a trade-off and room for improvement. The complementarity across different models and views enables the high recall performance.

Abstract: Clinical research in psychiatry increasingly relies on large scale collection of spoken language data to identify acoustic and linguistic biomarkers. Yet evolving consent and protocol requirements can oblige investigators to remove a designated speaker from multi-speaker recordings and to verify said removal at a scale infeasible for manual review of entire corpora. We study this verification problem for role-driven dyadic clinical dialogue in psychiatry and investigate it with two parallel, symmetric pipelines: confirming that clinician speech has been removed from psychiatric interview recordings, and confirming that patient speech has been removed from the same recordings. Each pipeline redacts the raw audio for its target role and then scans the surviving output with audio-language and large-language models to identify missed deletions. We evaluate this approach on a corpus of 48 dyadic recordings drawn from psychiatry settings, testing four open-weight models in an inference-only setting: Gemma-4-12B, Gemma-4-31B, Nemotron-3-Nano, and Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni. A disjunctive OR ensemble over fourteen model-view configurations had a combined F1 of 0.478 (precision 0.330, recall 0.870), an improvement over individual model estimates driven by recall gains that point to substantial complementarity across models and context views.

</details>


### [141] [LeanPolish: Verified Supervision for Lean Proof Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38384)
*Pauline Bourigault*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeanPolish is a symbolic Lean 4 pipeline that releases 33,402 accepted local edits and 65,596 failed attempts to study what language models learn from verified proof edit supervision. The study reveals that naive training from first-success search introduces artifacts, but continuing evaluation beyond first success and training rankers yield significant improvements (70.1% vs 36.9% baseline). For compression, iterative symbolic passes raise miniF2F savings from 19.7% to 27.5%, and fine-tuning on whole-proof rewriting raises verified token reduction from 2.8% to 5.5% on PutnamBench proofs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verified proof edits provide supervision for improving language-model-generated Lean proofs, but verification only confirms correctness—not that the training signal is free of search artifacts. This work investigates what models actually learn from this supervision and how to separate learning to imitate a search policy from improving on that search.

Method: The authors introduce LeanPolish, a symbolic Lean 4 pipeline that releases 33,402 accepted local edits and 65,596 same-state failed attempts. They study learning from first-success search, teacher-selected evaluation sites, and menu evaluation beyond first success. They train rankers, test compression via iterative symbolic passes, and evaluate verified neural editing with matched frozen-model controls.

Result: 1) First-success search admits a goal-independent rule with perfect ranking accuracy; teacher-selected evaluation sites reward trivial deletions. 2) Continuing menu evaluation beyond first success removes the ordering shortcut: trained ranker selects best candidate on 70.1% of held-out states vs 36.9% for strongest frozen baseline. 3) For compression, iterating symbolic pass raises miniF2F savings from 19.7% to 27.5%. 4) Verified neural editing helps on other proof sources, but matched frozen-model controls show gains need not come from training. 5) Fine-tuning raises verified token reduction from 2.8% to 5.5% on 19 PutnamBench proofs.

Conclusion: The supervision from verified proof edits improves proof improvement when correctness, compression, and edit policy are kept distinct. The released edits, complete candidate pools, and controlled evaluations provide a reproducible basis for studying proof improvement while separating learning to imitate a search policy from improving on that search.

Abstract: Verified proof edits offer a natural source of supervision for improving language-model-generated Lean proofs. Yet verification establishes that an edit is correct, not that its training signal is free of search artifacts. We introduce LeanPolish, a symbolic Lean 4 pipeline that releases 33,402 accepted local edits and 65,596 same-state failed attempts, and use it to study what models learn from this supervision. First-success search admits a goal-independent rule with perfect ranking accuracy; teacher-selected evaluation sites also reward trivial deletions. Continuing menu evaluation beyond the first success removes the ordering shortcut: a trained ranker selects the best candidate on 70.1% of evaluated held-out states, versus 36.9% for the strongest frozen baseline. For compression, iterating the symbolic pass raises miniF2F savings from 19.7% to 27.5%, exceeding the neural hybrids we test there. Verified neural editing helps on other proof sources, but matched frozen-model controls show that its gains need not come from training. The supervision does improve whole-proof rewriting: fine-tuning raises verified token reduction from 2.8% to 5.5% on 19 PutnamBench proofs. Together, the released edits, complete candidate pools, and controlled evaluations separate learning to imitate a search policy from improving on that search. They provide a reproducible basis for studying proof improvement while keeping correctness, compression, and edit policy distinct.

</details>


### [142] [Which Tasks Survive Self-Supervised Learning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38393)
*Achleshwar Luthra,Lucas Bryant,Tracy Zhu,Tomer Galanti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates what downstream tasks remain recoverable from same-instance self-supervised learning (SSL) representations through the lens of "semantic recoverability." The authors show that for centered and whitened representations, recoverability determines directional class-distance-normalized variance (CDNV) and controls few-shot classification. They derive a closed-form spectral characterization showing that SSL preserves tasks whose posterior lies in the leading cross-view-stable spectral subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Same-instance SSL enforces consistency across two views of the same instance, but this principle alone doesn't determine which downstream tasks remain recoverable from learned representations. The authors seek to understand what information survives SSL and how it appears in downstream geometry and transfer.

Method: Theoretical analysis using centered and whitened representations, analyzing a canonical two-view SSL objective and deriving its population optimum in closed-form spectral terms. Validation on synthetic and real datasets across multiple SSL methods.

Result: 1) Recoverability exactly determines CDNV and controls few-shot nearest-centroid classification
2) Task-relevant semantic directions are governed by recoverability
3) Population linear probe and centroid axis coincide
4) Multiple well-recovered tasks approach factorial centroid geometry
5) SSL population optimum spans leading cross-view-stable modes of the two-view operator
6) Downstream task preservation depends on whether its posterior lies in the selected spectral subspace
7) Predictions validated across synthetic and real data with several SSL methods

Conclusion: This work provides a task-level account of what information survives same-instance SSL and how retained information appears in downstream geometry and transfer. The spectral structure of the two-view operator determines semantic recoverability, offering a principled way to understand and predict SSL transfer performance.

Abstract: Same-instance self-supervised learning (SSL) learns representations by enforcing consistency across two views of the same underlying instance. This principle alone, however, does not determine which downstream tasks remain recoverable from the learned representation. We study this question through \emph{semantic recoverability}, defined as the amount of a task's posterior score captured by the represented function space. We show that, for centered and whitened representations, recoverability exactly determines directional class-distance-normalized variance (CDNV), controls few-shot nearest-centroid classification, and governs the strength of task-relevant semantic directions. The population linear probe and centroid axis coincide, and multiple well-recovered tasks approach a factorial centroid geometry. We then analyze a canonical two-view SSL objective and show that its population optimum spans the leading cross-view-stable modes of the associated two-view operator. This yields a closed-form spectral characterization of semantic recoverability: a downstream task is preserved to the extent that its posterior lies in the selected spectral subspace. We validate these predictions on synthetic and real datasets across several SSL methods, testing the predicted relationships among recoverability, directional geometry, spectral structure, and few-shot transfer. Together, these results give a task-level account of what information survives same-instance SSL and how the retained information appears in downstream geometry and transfer.

</details>


### [143] [Disagreement-Regularized Imitation Learning for Image-Based Continuous Control with Gaussian and Beta Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38407)
*Irving Giovani Bronzatti Petrazzini,Eric Aislan Antonelo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper evaluates Disagreement-Regularized Imitation Learning (DRIL) for image-based continuous control in CarRacing, comparing Gaussian and Beta learner policies under limited (1) vs abundant (20) demonstrations. DRIL achieves 61-112% improvement over behavior cloning in few-demonstration settings, while Beta behavior cloning outperforms DRIL with more data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Behavior cloning suffers from compounding errors when encountering out-of-distribution states. This study investigates whether DRIL—which uses disagreement among ensemble policies as a reinforcement learning reward—can improve performance in image-based continuous control tasks.

Method: Controlled CarRacing experiments varying: (1) learner policy types (Gaussian vs Beta), (2) expert demonstrations (clipped Gaussian vs bounded Beta), (3) demonstration count (1 vs 20 trajectories), (4) evaluation mode (deterministic vs stochastic). Three checkpoint stages evaluated: behavior cloning, best training-score checkpoint, and final DRIL checkpoint. Disagreement ensemble uses five Gaussian policies.

Result: With few demonstrations (1 trajectory), DRIL improved 61% over BC with clipped-action demos and 112% with bounded-action demos. With 20 trajectories, the advantage narrowed; Beta BC remained ~7% above best DRIL. The disagreement reward's informativeness varies with learner representation and training stage.

Conclusion: DRIL substantially improves few-demonstration visual continuous control. Bounded Beta policies excel when more demonstrations are available. Success depends on learner support, ensemble response, and proper checkpoint selection.

Abstract: Purpose: Behavior cloning can accumulate errors when a learned controller visits states outside the demonstrated distribution. This study evaluates whether Disagreement-Regularized Imitation Learning (DRIL), which converts disagreement among cloned policies into a reinforcement-learning reward, improves image-based continuous control. Methods: A controlled CarRacing study combines Gaussian and Beta learner policies, demonstrations from either a clipped Gaussian expert or an intrinsically bounded Beta expert, one or 20 trajectories, deterministic and stochastic evaluation, and three retained stages: behavior cloning, the highest 10-episode training-score checkpoint, and the final DRIL checkpoint. The disagreement ensemble contains five Gaussian policies in every variant. Each retained policy is evaluated over 100 procedurally generated episodes. Results: Score-selected DRIL produced its largest gains in the few-demonstration setting, improving over the strongest behavior-cloning mean by 61% with clipped-action demonstrations and by 112% with bounded-action demonstrations. With 20 trajectories, the advantage of DRIL narrowed; in the bounded-action regime, Beta behavior cloning remained about 7% above the best DRIL checkpoint. The experiments also show that the informativeness of the disagreement reward changes with the learner representation and training stage. Conclusion: DRIL can substantially improve few-demonstration visual continuous control, while bounded Beta policies provide strong behavior-cloning performance when more demonstrations are available. The results highlight the joint importance of learner support,ensemble response, and checkpoint selection.

</details>


### [144] [Synthesis Without Training: An Inference-Only Pipeline for Tabular, Temporal, and Relational Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38414)
*Zilong Zhao,Abdul Raheem,Jiayu Li,Sohei Arisaka,Darius Lim Hong Yi,Milad Abdollahzadeh,Uzair Javaid,Biplab Sikdar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GENSCRIPT is an inference-only synthetic data generation pipeline that eliminates model training by computing deterministic statistical profiles of source data and using a language model to infer semantics and constraints, which are then compiled into an executable sampler.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fit-then-sample paradigm faces three challenges: (1) requiring new training runs for each dataset; (2) needing task-specific models for different data modalities; and (3) producing opaque models that are difficult to inspect. The authors seek a unified, training-free approach.

Method: GENSCRIPT computes a deterministic statistical profile of source data (column types, ranges, missingness, categories, correlations) and passes this profile to a language model to infer field semantics and cross-column integrity constraints. A coding agent then compiles the profile and constraints into an executable, auditable sampler. The approach supports single-table, temporal, and relational data without task-specific modeling.

Result: Across four single-table benchmarks, GENSCRIPT builds generators in 2 minutes and samples 50k rows within 6 seconds, while remaining within a few points of leading methods in marginal fidelity. It is the only method that perfectly preserves a 1-to-1 mapping between columns in the Adult dataset. On a smart-building dataset, it produces conditional time series that more closely match the real distribution than two baselines and perfectly preserves primary- and foreign-key relationships in relational databases.

Conclusion: GENSCRIPT offers a unified, training-free approach to synthetic data generation that achieves competitive fidelity with significantly faster generation times, while uniquely preserving column mappings and relational integrity constraints.

Abstract: Synthetic data generation is dominated by the fit-then-sample paradigm: a generative model is trained on a private dataset and then sampled from. Despite its widespread adoption, this paradigm faces three challenges: (1) a new training run is required for every dataset; (2) different data modalities, such as single tables, time series, and relational databases, require task-specific models and feature engineering; and (3) the resulting model is opaque, making its behavior under data constraints difficult to inspect. We propose GENSCRIPT, an inference-only pipeline that eliminates model training. GENSCRIPT computes a deterministic statistical profile of the source data (column types, ranges, missingness, categories, correlations, etc.) and passes it--rather than raw rows--to a language model to infer field semantics and cross-column integrity constraints. A coding agent then compiles the profile and constraints into an executable, auditable sampler. This unified approach supports single-table, temporal, and relational data without task-specific modeling. Across four single-table benchmarks, GENSCRIPT builds generators in 2 minutes and samples 50k rows within 6 seconds, while remaining within a few points of leading methods in marginal fidelity. Notably, it is the only method that perfectly preserves a 1-to-1 mapping between columns in the Adult dataset. On a smart-building dataset, it produces conditional time series that more closely match the real distribution than two baselines and perfectly preserves primary- and foreign-key relationships in the corresponding relational database.

</details>


### [145] [Graph Anomaly Detection as Finite-Horizon Control: Training-Free Scoring via Empirical Bayes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38424)
*Fred Xu,Thomas Markovich,Florence Regol,Yizhou Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EB-GAD is a training-free graph anomaly detection framework that uses Empirical Bayes with Generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (GOU) relaxation to model normality. It generates multiple scoring methods from a single fitted prior, with a label-free selector to choose the best score family based on graph characteristics. Achieves state-of-the-art AUROC on 9/11 benchmarks including large financial fraud networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GAD models indirectly encode normality through architectures, message passing, reconstruction or contrastive objectives, which entangles graph trust, graph-spectral weighting, and anomaly-score choice. This results in scores that are costly, opaque, and unstable across different graph regimes. The authors seek a more transparent and robust approach.

Method: 1) Model normality as graph-aware Generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (GOU) relaxation toward a graph-filtered template
2) Use Empirical Bayes to fit graph precision from residual-field likelihood
3) Convert scoring to closed-form finite-horizon control energy (minimum effort to steer feature-neutral node to observed endpoint)
4) Generate bank of scores sharing one fitted prior (equilibrium Mahalanobis, finite-horizon control-energy, scale-normalized ratio)
5) Label-free selector chooses score family based on feature homophily, edge density, and feature dimension
6) Rank candidates by fitted-null deviation and rank stability

Result: Best or tied-best AUROC on 9 out of 11 benchmarks: four financial fraud networks (up to 3.7M nodes), YelpChi, Amazon review graphs, Weibo, Reddit, and Facebook. Margins up to 21.7 points. Second place on BlogCatalog and ACM. No labels used at any step.

Conclusion: EB-GAD successfully provides a training-free, interpretable framework for graph anomaly detection that adapts to different graph regimes through its bank of scores and automatic selector. It outperforms existing methods on most benchmarks, particularly on large-scale financial fraud detection, while being computationally efficient (no training required) and theoretically grounded (using GOU process and Empirical Bayes).

Abstract: Node-level graph anomaly detection (GAD) identifies nodes whose attributes and interactions deviate from dominant graph regularities. Existing GAD models encode normality and anomaly scoring indirectly through architectures, message passing, reconstruction or contrastive objectives, and tuned score families. This entangles graph trust (how strongly graph structure should define normality), graph-spectral weighting, and anomaly-score choice, yielding scores that are costly, opaque, and unstable across graph regimes. We propose EB-GAD (Empirical-Bayes GAD), a training-free framework that models normality as graph-aware generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (GOU) relaxation toward a graph-filtered template. Empirical Bayes fits the graph precision from the residual-field likelihood; the GOU then turns scoring into a closed-form finite-horizon control energy, the minimum effort to steer a feature-neutral node to its observed endpoint along graph-spectral relaxation. Sweeping relaxation horizon and endpoint tolerance yields a bank of scores that share one fitted prior: equilibrium Mahalanobis scoring is one limit, while finite-horizon control-energy and scale-normalized ratio scores reveal anomalies that static equilibrium scoring can mask. A label-free selector chooses the score family from feature homophily, edge density, and feature dimension, then ranks candidates by fitted-null deviation and rank stability. On 11 benchmarks and without labels at any step, EB-GAD has the best or tied-best AUROC on 9: the four financial fraud networks (up to 3.7M nodes), the YelpChi and Amazon review graphs, Weibo, Reddit and Facebook, with margins of up to 21.7 points. It is second on BlogCatalog and ACM.

</details>


### [146] [What Pretraining and Midtraining Make Learnable from Rewards?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38446)
*Chiwun Yang,Xiaoyu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates how pretrained language models acquire information and computation through reward signals, specifically how pretraining and midtraining supply the information and computation that make reward adaptation effective. The authors characterize mechanisms that agree on training rewards but demand different held-out answers, and propose task-independent source observations to resolve this ambiguity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how reward signals can identify correct answers while leaving the computation for new inputs undetermined, and to characterize the division of labor between information acquisition (prediction) and task-specific learning (reward adaptation) in neural networks.

Method: The authors construct finite sampled Adam paths from specified random initializations through source prediction and reward adaptation in the same parameters. They test with pretrained Qwen2.5 checkpoints across eight worlds, using sequential models with source and first-operation supervision, memory replay, and independent confirmation tasks. They also test on GSM8K (math reasoning) and HotpotQA (multi-hop question answering).

Result: Sequential models with correct source and first-operation supervision achieved 82.61% success vs 44.15% for private-random source control. Memory replay preserved retrieval during reward adaptation. Independent eight-world confirmation achieved 75.32% vs 49.86% after matched alternative-retrieval training. GSM8K and HotpotQA showed separation at reward entry, subsequent gain, and final performance.

Conclusion: The results connect information acquisition, executable computation, and reward-guided task learning, demonstrating a clear division of labor between prediction (acquiring execution/retrieval) and reward adaptation (learning task-specific use).

Abstract: A reward can identify a correct answer while leaving the computation needed for new inputs undetermined. We study how pretraining and midtraining supply the information and computation that make reward adaptation effective. In sequential state computation and contextual memory, we characterize mechanisms that agree on every training reward yet demand different held-out answers. Task-independent source observations resolve this ambiguity. We construct finite sampled Adam paths from specified random initializations through source prediction and reward adaptation in the same parameters, proving how prediction acquires execution or retrieval and rewards learn their task-specific use. Experiments with pretrained Qwen2.5 checkpoints test this division of labor. Across eight worlds, Sequential models trained with correct source and first-operation supervision reach 82.61% success, versus 44.15% for a private-random source control. Memory replay preserves retrieval during reward adaptation, and an independent eight-world confirmation achieves 75.32% task success versus 49.86% after matched alternative-retrieval training. GSM8K and HotpotQA separate accuracy at reward entry, subsequent gain and final performance. Together, these results connect information acquisition, executable computation and reward-guided task learning.

</details>


### [147] [An Input-Frugal Deep Learning Framework for Weather-Driven National Crop-Yield Forecasting: A Case Study of Brazilian Soybean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38447)
*Fernando Dupin da Cunha Mello,Prashant Kumar,Erick G. Sperandio Nascimento*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A frugal deep learning framework using routine weather data plus two lightweight static inputs (crop year and agro-environmental label) for soybean yield forecasting in Brazil. The Transformer encoder achieves the best performance (RMSE 149 kg/ha, rRMSE 5.3%, R² = 0.784), reducing error by 47.6% relative to a farmer baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable, timely crop-yield forecasts are essential for market stability and risk management, but many existing approaches rely on costly or hard-to-scale inputs. This paper aims to develop a simple, transferable, and architecture-agnostic framework that uses only routine weather data.

Method: A deep learning framework using routine weather as the only time-varying input plus two lightweight static context inputs (crop year and agro-environmental label). The study uses a 20-season Brazilian soybean case study (2001/02-2020/21) with leave-one-year-out cross-validation. Six deep learning models (MLP, CNN, LSTM, CNN-LSTM, Transformer, Mamba) are benchmarked against linear ridge regression and a five-year moving-average baseline.

Result: The Transformer achieved the best national accuracy (RMSE 149 kg ha⁻¹; rRMSE 5.3%; R² = 0.784), reducing error by 47.6% relative to the farmer baseline. All deep learning variants outperformed ridge regression, and all sequential encoders surpassed the non-sequential MLP. In-season forecasts improve monotonically from early to late season, reaching approximately 50% lower error than the baseline at the latest forecast point. Ablations show the agro-environmental label and spatial instance expansion contribute positively without increasing input complexity. SHAP analysis indicates crop year explains most of the long-run trajectory, while within-season weather and agro-environmental context drive interannual deviations, with moisture/cloud and thermal-demand variables dominating.

Conclusion: The proposed framework is straightforward to deploy across other crops and geographic regions and is naturally compatible with operational weather forecasts for routine monitoring. The framework is frugal, transferable, and architecture-agnostic, making it highly practical for operational crop-yield forecasting.

Abstract: Reliable, timely crop-yield forecasts are essential for market stability and risk management, yet many approaches rely on costly or hard-to-scale inputs. We present a frugal, transferable, and architecture-agnostic deep learning framework that uses routine weather as the only time-varying input plus two lightweight static context inputs (crop year and an agro-environmental label) to capture long-run change and regional heterogeneity, while supporting multiple sequence encoders under identical data requirements. Using a 20-season Brazilian soybean case study (2001/02-2020/21) with leave-one-year-out cross-validation, we benchmark MLP, CNN, LSTM, CNN-LSTM, a Transformer encoder and the Mamba state-space model against linear ridge regression and a five-year moving-average "farmer" baseline. All deep learning variants outperform ridge, and all sequential encoders surpass the non-sequential MLP. The Transformer achieves the best national accuracy (RMSE 149 kg ha^-1; rRMSE 5.3%; R^2 = 0.784), reducing error by 47.6% relative to the farmer baseline. In-season forecasts improve monotonically from early- to late-season issuance, reaching approximately 50% lower error than the baseline at the latest forecast point. Ablations indicate that the agro-environmental label and spatial instance expansion (multiple grid-node weather sequences per municipality-year) contribute positively without increasing input complexity. SHAP diagnostics suggest crop year explains most of the long-run trajectory, whereas within-season weather and agro-environmental context primarily drive interannual deviations, with moisture/cloud and thermal-demand variables dominating. Overall, the framework is straightforward to deploy across other crops and geographic regions and is naturally compatible with operational weather forecasts for routine monitoring.

</details>


### [148] [Grokking through the Lens of Minimum-Norm Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38453)
*Gil Kur,Ileana Rugina,Clémentine Carla Juliette Dominé,Marco Mondelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a statistical theory characterizing how regularization geometry and signal sparsity govern generalization near interpolation, addressing the grokking phenomenon where fitting training data and learning the underlying signal occur at different stages. The work focuses on high-dimensional regression and identifies regimes where sparsity-promoting regularization enables more accurate exact interpolation than approximate fitting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theories offer limited quantitative insight into how delayed generalization (grokking) depends on inductive bias and signal structure. The authors aim to fill this gap by understanding how regularization and sparsity affect generalization near the interpolation threshold.

Method: The authors develop a statistical theory for high-dimensional regression, analyzing ℓ_r-regularization paths. They prove a zero-one generalization law for strongly overparameterized noiseless problems and construct a family of convex norms. They also analyze the proportional regime where feature dimension and sample size are proportional.

Result: The paper establishes: (1) A zero-one generalization law in strongly overparameterized noiseless settings; (2) A family of convex norms whose interpolators transition from trivial risk to exact recovery while maintaining zero training error; (3) Precise characterization of training and generalization errors along ℓ_r-regularization paths; (4) Quantification of generalization gain near interpolation - showing it increases with more sparsity-promoting norms and sparser targets, with a sharp drop for noiseless data and ℓ_1 regularization; (5) Experimental validation on diagonal linear networks and transformers trained on modular arithmetic.

Conclusion: The generalization gain near interpolation increases with sparsity-promoting norms and target sparsity. The work reveals a statistical instability in minimum-norm interpolation where small perturbations in regularization strength lead to drastically different generalization while preserving small training error. This provides new quantitative understanding of grokking and the role of inductive bias in generalization dynamics.

Abstract: Grokking shows that fitting the training data and learning the underlying signal can occur at very different stages. However, existing theories offer limited quantitative insight into how this delayed generalization depends on inductive bias and signal structure. Our work addresses the gap by developing a statistical theory that characterizes how regularization geometry and signal sparsity govern generalization near interpolation. In particular, we focus on the prototypical setting of high-dimensional regression and identify regimes in which sparsity-promoting regularization makes exact interpolation much more accurate than approximate fitting. In strongly overparameterized noiseless problems, we prove a zero--one generalization law and construct a family of convex norms whose interpolators transition from the trivial risk of the all-zero predictor to exact recovery, while keeping the training error equal to $0$. Furthermore, when feature dimension and sample size are proportional, we provide a precise characterization of training and generalization errors along $\ell_r$-regularization paths. This in turn allows us to quantify the generalization gain that remains near interpolation: we show that this gain increases as the norm becomes more sparsity-promoting and as the target becomes sparser, with a sharp drop in generalization reached for noiseless data and $\ell_1$ regularization. Experiments on diagonal linear networks and transformers trained on modular arithmetic demonstrate the generality of our theoretical predictions. Finally, beyond grokking, our work reveals a statistical instability in minimum-norm interpolation: small perturbations in the regularization strength can lead to drastically different generalization, while preserving small training error.

</details>


### [149] [Does Gradient Conflict Predict the Understanding--Generation Trade-off? A Controlled Audit of Conflict-Metric Validity in Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38465)
*Shuyang Jiang,Fucheng Deng,Yuchuan Luo,Zhenyu Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper audits the common assumption that gradient conflict between understanding and generation objectives in Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) is a useful diagnostic target. Using a controlled testbed (GRIDUMM), the authors find that directional conflict metrics show poor correlation with the actual trade-off, and interventions that suppress conflict leave the trade-off unchanged.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The premise that reducing gradient conflict between understanding and generation objectives improves downstream performance has been assumed but never directly tested. The authors sought to audit this assumption in a controlled environment where the ground-truth trade-off is exactly computable.

Method: The authors created GRIDUMM, a controlled testbed mirroring key structural ingredients of UMM training. They conducted 63 configurations across 372 measured checkpoints, testing directional conflict metrics, dose-response interventions, and comparing functional interference measures against directional conflict metrics.

Result: No directional conflict metric achieved an absolute Spearman correlation above 0.3 with the training conflict against the eventual trade-off (confidence interval excludes zero). A dose-response intervention monotonically suppressing conflict left the trade-off flat. The norm ratio functions as a generation-failure detector but becomes null among configurations that master generation. Functional interference measures outperformed directional conflict metrics, while training loss strongly tracked the trade-off.

Conclusion: The validity of gradient conflict as a diagnostic target must be established empirically, not assumed. The authors release their audit protocol as a reusable standard for future research.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) are increasingly designed around gradient conflict between understanding and generation objectives. The premise that reducing these metrics improves the downstream understanding-generation trade-off has never been tested directly. We audit it in a controlled testbed, GRIDUMM, which mirrors key structural ingredients of UMM training while making the ground-truth trade-off exactly computable. Across 63 configurations and 372 measured checkpoints, no directional conflict metric reaches an absolute Spearman correlation of 0.3 with a confidence interval excluding zero for conflict measured during training against the eventual trade-off. A dose-response intervention that monotonically suppresses conflict leaves the trade-off flat, separating correlation from causation. The norm ratio is a generation-failure detector and becomes null among configurations that master generation. Functional interference measures outperform directional conflict metrics, while training loss tracks the trade-off strongly. Our results do not show that conflict is useless; they show that its validity as a diagnostic target must be established, not assumed, and we release the audit protocol as a reusable standard.

</details>


### [150] [RetroGEF: Dynamic Graph Edit Flow for Single-Step Retrosynthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38484)
*Xiaozhuang Song,Xuemin Chen,Xinjian Zhao,Yaoyao Xu,Tianshu Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RetroGEF is a flow-based generative model for single-step retrosynthesis that constructs reactant molecules from target molecules by adding atoms and changing bonds in the molecular graph, modeling both connectivity changes and graph size changes within a unified generative process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrosynthesis is crucial for drug discovery and materials design, but it involves complex molecular graph transformations that can change connectivity and graph size, introducing reactant components absent from the target while revising product-derived structures. Existing methods often rely on fixed-size graph canvases, limiting their ability to handle variable molecular transformations.

Method: RetroGEF is a flow-based generative model that learns directly from product-reactant pairs without requiring a prescribed edit order. Starting from the target molecule, it constructs possible reactants by adding atoms and changing bonds in the molecular graph, modeling molecular transformations and changes in graph size within the same generative process rather than relying on a fixed-size graph canvas.

Result: Experiments on representative retrosynthesis benchmarks demonstrate that RetroGEF achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Conclusion: RetroGEF successfully addresses the challenge of modeling variable-size molecular graph transformations in retrosynthesis through a unified flow-based generative approach, outperforming existing methods on benchmark datasets.

Abstract: Retrosynthesis enables the discovery of viable synthetic routes to target molecules. It plays a central role in modern drug discovery and materials design. Retrosynthesis involves molecular graph transformations that can change both connectivity and graph size. These transformations may introduce reactant components absent from the target while revising the product-derived structure. To model these transformations, we propose RetroGEF, a flow-based generative model for single-step retrosynthesis. Starting from the target molecule, it constructs possible reactants by adding atoms and changing bonds in the molecular graph. RetroGEF models molecular transformations and changes in graph size within the same generative process, rather than relying on a fixed-size graph canvas. It learns this process directly from product--reactant pairs without requiring a prescribed edit order. Experiments on representative retrosynthesis benchmarks demonstrate that RetroGEF achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [151] [Autoregressive Frontier Expansion: Growing Trees with Graph Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38506)
*Umer Gupta,Saku Peltonen,Martin Ritzert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A generative framework called Autoregressive Frontier Expansion that constructs tree-like branching structures (neurons, botanical trees) through iterative biological growth simulation, using a flow-matching model with SO(2)-equivariant GNN to predict branch bifurcation or termination at each step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for generating tree-like structures either constrain topology generation or use hand-tuned mechanistic procedures, while generic 3D graph generators don't exploit tree-specific structure. Real-world 3D data is expensive to acquire, making realistic generative models valuable for simulation and data augmentation.

Method: The framework uses an iterative expansion process simulating biological growth. At each step, a flow-matching model parameterized by an SO(2)-equivariant GNN expands the frontier by predicting whether each active branch bifurcates or terminates.

Result: Generated morphologies agree closely with reference distributions across both cortical neurons and botanical trees, in unconditional, class-conditioned, and morphology-guided generation settings.

Conclusion: Autoregressive Frontier Expansion successfully generates realistic tree-like branching structures across multiple domains, with the iterative growth approach effectively mimicking biological tree development while maintaining morphological fidelity.

Abstract: Tree-like branching structures are common in nature, from botanical trees to neurons, blood vessels and respiratory trees. Their branching shape often reflects function, making structural modelling central to understanding how these systems work. Because acquiring real-world 3D data is often expensive or infeasible, realistic generative models are valuable for simulation and data augmentation. Existing morphology-specific models either constrain how topology is generated or rely on hand-tuned, mechanistic procedures. Generic 3D graph generators, by contrast, do not exploit or enforce the structure of trees. We propose Autoregressive Frontier Expansion, a generative framework that constructs trees through an iterative expansion process, simulating the biological growth of real trees. At each step, a flow-matching model parameterised by an SO(2)-equivariant GNN expands the frontier by predicting whether each active branch bifurcates or terminates. We evaluate our method on cortical neurons and botanical trees in unconditional, class-conditioned, and morphology-guided generation. Across both domains, the generated morphologies agree closely with the reference distributions and, in conditional experiments, with the specified targets.

</details>


### [152] [Does Text Steer Neural PDE Surrogates? A Controlled Diagnostic with OperatorCLIP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38517)
*Aadi Dash,Lennon J. Shikhman,Michael Galarnyk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the "attribution problem" in text-conditioned neural surrogates: whether lower error means the model actually understands text semantics or merely learns statistical patterns. The authors compare multiple conditioning approaches (unconditioned FNO, constant-sentence FiLM control, contrastive alignment with task text) across 2D/3D physics simulations, finding that constant conditioning often outperforms text conditioning, and that standard contrastive losses fail in single-description regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core motivation is to determine whether text-conditioned neural surrogate models truly comprehend the semantic meaning of text prompts, or if they simply exploit statistical correlations. The authors argue that lower error alone cannot demonstrate semantic understanding—this is the attribution problem they aim to examine.

Method: The authors use OperatorCLIP as their testbed, comparing three approaches: (1) unconditioned FNO (Fourier Neural Operator) with no text input, (2) constant-sentence FiLM control where all inputs receive the same fixed text description, and (3) task text with contrastive alignment training. Experiments span three seed conditions across three physical systems: Darcy2D, ShallowWater2D, and 3D compressible Navier-Stokes (CNS3D). The text encoder is trained from scratch, with each conditioned model seeing only one description during training. They employ pairwise InfoNCE loss and conduct prompt interventions to test semantic ordering.

Result: Counterintuitively, constant conditioning (the same sentence for all inputs) achieves lower mean test error than text conditioning on both 2D tasks (Darcy2D and ShallowWater2D). Relative to this control, task text plus alignment shows similar means on ShallowWater2D and CNS3D but higher mean on Darcy2D. These comparisons exhibit substantial seed uncertainty. The InfoNCE loss fails because it cannot identify matched pairs when each model sees only one description, resulting in minimum log B. Prompt interventions reveal no reliable semantic ordering, indicating the model does not learn meaningful text semantics in this regime.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that pathway controls are essential for proper evaluation of text-conditioned models. Their study neither establishes semantic competence of the text encoder nor tests text effectiveness under varying physical contexts. The key methodological insight is that standard contrastive alignment approaches fail in single-description training regimes, and that lower error does not imply semantic understanding—controlled experiments are necessary to isolate whether models genuinely comprehend text meaning.

Abstract: Lower error from a text-conditioned neural surrogate does not, by itself, show that the model uses the meaning of the text. We examine this attribution problem with OperatorCLIP, comparing an unconditioned FNO, a constant-sentence FiLM control, and a fixed task description trained with contrastive alignment. Three-seed experiments cover Darcy2D, ShallowWater2D, and three-dimensional compressible Navier-Stokes (CNS3D). Constant conditioning has lower mean test error on both 2D tasks. Relative to this control, task text plus alignment has a similar mean on ShallowWater2D and CNS3D and a higher mean on Darcy2D; these descriptive comparisons have substantial seed uncertainty. The latter comparison changes both prompt content and loss, so it isolates neither effect. The text encoder is trained from scratch, and each conditioned model sees only one description during training. In this regime, pairwise InfoNCE cannot identify matched pairs and has minimum $\log B$. Prompt interventions show no reliable semantic ordering. This methodological caution demonstrates why pathway controls are needed; it neither establishes semantic competence of the encoder nor tests the effectiveness of text under varying physical context.

</details>


### [153] [ShamAN-Q: Shampoo Augmented NanoQuant for Sub-1-bit LLM Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38521)
*Jonathan Mei,Sang Hyub Kim,Oliver Knitter,Chi Chen,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ShamAN-Q is a sub-1-bit post-training quantization method that extends NanoQuant by using a dense curvature metric (Mahalanobis reconstruction loss) based on Kronecker product approximation of the Fisher information matrix, with ADMM updates replaced by Sylvester equation solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NanoQuant uses diagonal reconstruction geometry, which may be suboptimal. ShamAN-Q aims to improve quantization quality by replacing this with a tractable dense curvature metric, using a paradigm from the Shampoo optimizer, to achieve better perplexity at lower bits per weight (bpw).

Method: ShamAN-Q replaces diagonal reconstruction geometry with a tractable dense curvature metric using Kronecker product fitting to the empirical Fisher information matrix via KL minimization, forming a Mahalanobis reconstruction loss. Continuous ADMM updates become Sylvester equation solutions. Input curvature is re-measured for each layer before factorization with periodic refresh on the partially quantized model. Uniform rank is redistributed across layers at the same total bits.

Result: On Qwen3-Base at ~1 bpw: 0.6B model: 27.56→22.96; 1.7B: 19.21→16.72; 4B: 14.29→13.80. Zero-shot accuracy on Eleuther LM Evaluation Harness matches or improves. At ~0.8 bpw on 0.6B, matches NanoQuant's perplexity at ~1.0 bpw.

Conclusion: ShamAN-Q successfully improves perplexity at similar bit rates while matching or improving zero-shot accuracy. It enables quantization to ~0.8 bpw while achieving comparable performance to NanoQuant at ~1.0 bpw, demonstrating the effectiveness of dense curvature metrics over diagonal reconstruction in post-training quantization.

Abstract: We introduce ShamAN-Q, a sub-1-bit post-training quantization method that extends NanoQuant by replacing each its diagonal reconstruction geometry with a tractable dense curvature metric, using a general paradigm popularized by the Shampoo optimizer. For each linear weight, ShamAN-Q fits a Kronecker product to the empirical Fisher information matrix of a small calibration set by Kullback--Leibler minimization, forming a Mahalanobis reconstruction loss from the result. The continuous ADMM updates from NanoQuant become solutions to Sylvester equations, while its discrete projection and deployment format remain unchanged. Because the curvature is local to a given set of weights, ShamAN-Q re-measures the input curvature statistic for each layer immediately before layer factorization, periodically refreshing all statistics on the partially quantized model. ShamAN-Q also redistributes the uniform rank from NanoQuant across layers at the same total number of bits. On Qwen3-Base, ShamAN-Q lowers WikiText-2 perplexity at $\approx$1 bpw from 27.56 to 22.96 (0.6B), 19.21 to 16.72 (1.7B), and 14.29 to 13.80 (4B) while matching or improving zero-shot accuracy on the Eleuther LM Evaluation Harness. On 0.6B, ShamAN-Q at $\approx$0.8 bpw matches the published perplexity of NanoQuant at $\approx$1.0 bpw.

</details>


### [154] [MM-FinEval: A Multi-Task Multimodal Benchmark for Real-World Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38523)
*Dong Shu,Yanguang Liu,Huopu Zhang,Saisai Hu,Haiyan Zhao,Hekun Huang,Mengnan Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MM-FinEval is a benchmark for evaluating multimodal large language models (LLMs) on financial forecasting tasks, using 2,045 S&P 500 earnings conference calls spanning 2019-2022. Each sample includes three modalities: text transcripts, presentation slides, and audio recordings, with 12 financial task labels. The study evaluates 19 baseline models across Image-Text, Audio-Text, and Any-to-Any configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial benchmarks are limited to unimodal inputs or single-task settings, making it difficult to evaluate whether multimodal LLMs can support real-world financial analysis. The need is to assess models' ability to reason over complex corporate disclosures, market expectations, and subtle communication signals in a more realistic, multi-modal setting.

Method: The authors created a tri-modal dataset with 2,045 S&P 500 earnings conference calls (2019-2022), containing text transcripts, presentation slides, and audio recordings as inputs, with 12 financial task labels as outputs. They evaluated 19 baseline models across three categories: Image-Text, Audio-Text, and Any-to-Any configurations.

Result: Small-size Any-to-Any models processing all three modalities achieve strong performance, even compared to larger proprietary models restricted to two-modality inputs. This indicates that the tri-modal dataset design introduces useful, non-redundant information, validating that text, audio, and visual data serve as important, complementary signals.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that multimodal LLMs can effectively leverage complementary signals from text, audio, and visual data to mimic the decision-making process of expert human analysts. The MM-FinEval benchmark provides a rigorous evaluation framework for assessing multimodal LLMs in real-world financial analysis tasks.

Abstract: Financial forecasting from earnings conference calls requires models to reason over complex corporate disclosures, market expectations, and subtle communication signals. However, existing financial benchmarks are often limited to unimodal inputs or single-task settings, making it difficult to evaluate whether multimodal large language models (LLMs) can support real-world financial analysis. In this paper, we introduce MM-FinEval, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate multimodal LLMs across multiple financial tasks. MM-FinEval spans a diverse timeline from 2019 to 2022. The entire proposed dataset contains 2,045 S\&P 500 conference earning calls as inputs and 12 financial task labels as outputs. Each input contains three modalities: a word-to-word text transcript of the earning call, the corresponding presentation slides used during the call, and the entire audio recording. To establish a rigorous evaluation framework, we analyze 19 baseline models across three distinct model categories: Image-Text, Audio-Text, and Any-to-Any configurations. We observe that small-size Any-to-Any models processing all three modalities achieve strong performance, even when compared against larger proprietary models restricted to two-modality inputs. This indicates that our tri-modal dataset design introduces useful, non-redundant information. These results validate that text, audio, and visual data serve as important, complementary signals that mimic the decision-making process of expert human analysts.

</details>


### [155] [Revisiting scaling laws for reward optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38526)
*Ali Aouad,Aymane El Gadarri,Vivek F. Farias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a scaling law for AI alignment optimization that characterizes how performance jointly depends on the amount of preference data (M comparisons) and the KL-divergence budget (K). The key finding is that performance scales as Θ(√(min{log(M), K})), validated empirically with R² values of 97-99% across various conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on scaling laws for optimization against reward models has pinned down performance dependence on optimization effort (KL-divergence budget), but it fails to cleanly identify how performance jointly scales with the amount of training data and the divergence budget. The problem of over-optimization or reward hacking arises when optimizing against a proxy reward model rather than true rewards, causing performance to plateau or degrade. Understanding this joint scaling is crucial for effective AI alignment.

Method: The authors develop an information-theoretic model to establish an upper bound for performance scaling, proving it is tightly achievable through a constructive procedure. They conduct extensive empirical evaluations using a real-world annotation setup with a large 70B gold reward model generating feedback data, while proxy reward models are trained from less capable models (0.6B to 4B). The scaling law is tested across different model sizes, noise levels, and optimization procedures (best-of-N or policy tilting).

Result: The proposed scaling law provides an excellent fit with R² values ranging from 97% to 99%, outperforming alternative specifications. The law remains robust across model sizes, noise conditions, and optimization procedures. The key insight emerging from the evidence is that reward optimization is analogous to a surprisingly simple selection task: choosing from a sequence of IID Gaussian random variables using noisy preference feedback.

Conclusion: The paper successfully establishes a theoretically grounded and empirically accurate scaling law for AI alignment optimization. The performance scales as Θ(√(min{log(M), K})), where M is the number of comparisons in training data and K is the policy's divergence budget. This provides practitioners with a practical guide for balancing data collection and optimization effort, while the insight that reward optimization resembles a simple selection task offers fundamental understanding of the underlying dynamics.

Abstract: Scaling laws for optimization against reward models in AI alignment have pinned down how performance depends on optimization effort---measured by a KL-divergence budget relative to a reference policy. Beyond a certain budget, over-optimization (or reward hacking) can arise: because we optimize against a proxy reward model (distinct from true rewards), performance can plateau or degrade. Naturally, the proxy reward's accuracy depends on how much preference data (often in the form of pairwise comparisons) was used to train it. However, existing research does not cleanly identify how performance jointly scales with the amount of training data and the divergence budget. Our main contribution is to provide an empirically accurate and theoretically grounded scaling law in such context. Performance roughly scales as $Θ(\sqrt{\min\{\log(M),K\}})$, where $M$ is the number of comparisons in training data and $K$ is the policy's divergence budget. We develop an information-theoretic model to establish this upper bound and prove it is tightly achievable through a constructive procedure. Informed by this, we conduct extensive empirical evaluations using a real-world annotation setup, whereby a large 70B gold reward model generates feedback data and proxy reward models are trained from less capable models (0.6B to 4B). Our scaling law provides an excellent fit (R2 from 97\% to 99\%), outperforms alternative specifications, and remains robust across model sizes, noise, and optimization procedures (best-of-$N$ or policy tilting). Our evidence suggests that reward optimization is analogous to a surprisingly simple selection task: choosing from a sequence of IID Gaussian random variables using noisy preference feedback.

</details>


### [156] [Does This Action Still Explain the Task? Reverse Scoring for Diffusion Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38536)
*Jiacheng Qiu,Christopher E. Mower,Jan Peters,Haitham Bou-Ammar,Matthieu Zimmer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Diffusion-based LLMs (dLLMs) suffer from retry loops in embodied agent tasks where agents repeatedly re-issue failed actions. This paper identifies the cause as masked decoding adaptivity creating "task-blind corruption" in action probabilities, and proposes "Reflect Reverse" - a training-free remedy that evaluates reverse conditional probabilities via masked denoising to cancel out this distortion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While dLLMs promise parallel decoding efficiency over autoregressive models, this efficiency does not transfer to embodied agentic competence. dLLM-backed agents repeatedly fall into retry loops, re-issuing actions that have already failed. The authors sought to understand why this happens and how to fix it without additional training.

Method: The authors trace the failure to masked decoding adaptivity: the sampler commits confident positions and defers uncertain ones. At failure states, the context already offers a confident fill for the deferred decision (the failed action itself), so the retry is committed without confronting failure feedback. They model this as task-blind corruption where contextually salient actions receive inflated probability. They then propose an invariance proposition: the task-blind factor cancels from the reverse conditional (likelihood of task given state and action). This is computed by masking task tokens and denoising - a process native to masked dLLMs but requiring a few parallel passes per candidate action.

Result: The method "Reflect Reverse" was evaluated on four multi-turn embodied benchmarks, where it improved task success and progression rates over forward-scoring baselines.

Conclusion: The retry loop failure in dLLM-backed agents stems from task-blind corruption in action probabilities due to masked decoding adaptivity. The training-free remedy "Reflect Reverse" leverages the reverse conditional to cancel this distortion, improving embodied agent performance without requiring additional training or model modifications.

Abstract: Diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) promise to break the sequential latency bottleneck of autoregressive agents through parallel decoding, but recent evaluations show this efficiency does not transfer to embodied agentic competence: dLLM-backed agents repeatedly fall into retry loops, re-issuing an action long after it has failed. We give a mechanistic account of this failure and a training-free remedy. We trace the retry loop to the adaptivity of masked decoding: the sampler commits the positions it is most confident about and defers the uncertain ones, and at a failure state the context already offers a confident fill for the deferred decision, i.e. the failed action itself, so the retry is committed without the failure feedback ever being confronted. We model the resulting distortion of the action distribution as a task-blind corruption: contextually salient actions (e.g., the action just taken) receive inflated probability by a factor that depends on the state and the action but not on the task. Under this model, we analyse an invariance proposition: the task-blind factor cancels exactly from the reverse conditional, i.e. the likelihood of the task given the state and a candidate action, which coincides with the task posterior of an idealized uncorrupted model. Masked dLLMs evaluate the reverse conditional natively, unlike autoregressive models, by masking the task tokens and denoising, at the cost of a few parallel passes per candidate. We instantiate the rule as Reflect Reverse and evaluate it on four multi-turn embodied benchmarks, where it improves task success and progression rates over forward-scoring baselines.

</details>


### [157] [ReLaG: A Scalable Framework Generalizing Random Splits to Data with Latent Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38538)
*Anthony Lavertu,Jacob Cote,Sophie Gobeil,Jacques Corbeil,Isabeau Premont-Schwarz,Pascal Germain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReLaG is a framework that addresses data leakage in train-test splits when datasets contain related samples (common in biochemical studies), by modeling sample relatedness through hierarchical latent variables and community detection to produce independent splits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random splitting can yield non-independent train-test subsets when datasets contain related samples, leading to overly optimistic generalization estimates. This is particularly problematic in biochemical studies where samples may be related (e.g., molecular or protein datasets).

Method: ReLaG uses a hierarchical latent-variable process to model sample relatedness, infers groups of related samples using proximity graphs and community detection, and produces independent train-test subsets. It also includes a label-free procedure that adapts splitting resolution to production data.

Result: ReLaG matches existing relation-aware methods on molecular and protein datasets while scaling substantially better, enabling splits at previously impractical dataset sizes. The inferred groups provide a cheap estimate of effective dataset size, enabling diversity-aware dataset scaling.

Conclusion: ReLaG provides a modality-agnostic solution for creating independent train-test splits in datasets with related samples, with improved scalability and practical utility for deployment settings. It is open source and available via pip.

Abstract: Random splitting can yield non-independent train--test subsets when a dataset contains related samples, as is common in certain applications such as biochemical studies. This leads to overly optimistic generalization estimates. Here, we introduce ReLaG, a modality-agnostic framework that models sample relatedness through a hierarchical latent-variable process and infers groups of related samples using proximity graphs and community detection to produce independent train--test subsets. Across molecular and protein datasets, ReLaG matches existing relation-aware methods while scaling substantially better, enabling splits at previously impractical dataset sizes. We further introduce a label-free procedure that adapts the splitting resolution to production data, aligning evaluation with the intended deployment setting. ReLaG's inferred groups provide a cheap estimate of effective dataset size, enabling diversity-aware dataset scaling. ReLaG is open source and can be installed with pip install relag.

</details>


### [158] [When a Flatness Proxy Is Not a Function: Robustness Certificates and Training Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38540)
*Vicente Opazo,Jose Calatayud-Mateu,Cristobal Rojas,Cristian Buc Calderon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that a valid curvature upper bound does not automatically justify its use as a robustness certificate or as a training intervention. Using a "last-layer relative-flatness proxy" as a case study, the authors show two key failures: (1) retained certificate expressions can underestimate loss increases by over 210× at finite empirical-risk minima, and (2) common-row softmax shifts preserve predictions while making the proxy unbounded, with standard reference-class choices doubling it on average.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to challenge the assumption that valid curvature upper bounds can be directly inverted into robustness certificates or differentiated into intrinsic training interventions. They investigate this distinction using a specific last-layer relative-flatness proxy.

Method: The authors: (1) derive a globally valid gauge-invariant feature-space repair; (2) analyze common-row softmax shifts and their effects on predictions and the proxy; (3) demonstrate that row centering gives orbit-minimized bounds and restores gradient invariance; (4) conduct 45 paired one-step tests on algorithmic and image models; (5) perform long-horizon CIFAR-10 experiments.

Result: At finite global empirical-risk minima, retained certificate expressions underestimate loss increases by over 210×. Common-row softmax shifts preserve predictions while making the proxy unbounded—standard reference-class choices double it on average. Row centering gives the orbit-minimized bound and restores full-model gradient invariance. Across 45 tests, amplified shifts separate raw-regularized predictors while quotient-regularized predictors remain aligned. CIFAR-10 experiments show substantial, reversible suppression of generalization, with less consistent evidence for selective delay after memorization.

Conclusion: Validity as a curvature upper bound does not by itself justify inversion into a robustness certificate or differentiation into an intrinsic training intervention. The paper provides evidence that careful consideration of the proxy's behavior under various transformations is necessary before using it for certification or intervention purposes.

Abstract: A valid curvature upper bound need not justify either a robustness certificate or an intervention on an intrinsic predictor property. We demonstrate this distinction for a last-layer relative-flatness proxy used in both settings. First, empirical-risk stationarity does not eliminate pointwise first-order loss terms: at a finite global empirical-risk minimum, the retained certificate expression underestimates a loss increase by over $210\times$. We derive a globally valid, gauge-invariant feature-space repair. Second, common-row softmax shifts preserve predictions and the exact contraction while making the proxy unbounded. Even standard reference-class choices double it on average relative to the centered representation. For a single fixed-feature example with at least three classes, scalar retuning generically cannot align the induced probability updates. Row centering gives the orbit-minimized bound and restores value and full-model gradient invariance under this symmetry. Across 45 paired one-step tests on algorithmic and image models, amplified shifts separate raw-regularized predictors while quotient-regularized predictors remain aligned. Long-horizon CIFAR-10 experiments show substantial, reversible suppression of generalization, while evidence for selective delay after memorization is less consistent. Together, these results show that validity as a curvature upper bound does not by itself justify either inversion into a robustness certificate or differentiation into an intrinsic training intervention.

</details>


### [159] [Towards Universal Wasserstein Barycenters through Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38547)
*Eduardo Fernandes Montesuma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Universal Barycenter Approximation - the problem of approximating the entire family of Wasserstein barycenters over the simplex (Wasserstein simplex). The authors propose BaryFM, a flow matching model that can transport marginal measures into any barycenter in the Wasserstein simplex, enabling sample generation through an ODE after training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
