Motivation: While computing Wasserstein barycenters for a fixed weight vector is common, approximating the whole family of barycenters over the simplex remains underexplored. This is important for probabilistic machine learning tasks requiring flexible probability measure transformations.

Method: BaryFM uses a flow matching model architecture. Once trained, the network can draw samples from measures in the Wasserstein simplex through an ordinary differential equation (ODE).

Result: BaryFM achieves the best average rank among 15 competing methods across 10 domain adaptation benchmarks, matching or surpassing non-universal solvers. Validated on 4 downstream tasks: domain adaptation, generalization, Bayesian posterior aggregation, and algorithmic fairness.

Conclusion: BaryFM successfully provides a universal approach to approximating the entire Wasserstein simplex, demonstrating strong performance across multiple downstream tasks and establishing new state-of-the-art results in domain adaptation benchmarks.

Abstract: Defining a weighted mean over probability measures under probability metrics is a central tool in probabilistic machine learning. Under the Wasserstein metric, these are called \emph{Wasserstein barycenters}. While most approaches compute barycenters for a fixed weight vector, approximating the whole family of barycenters over the simplex, which we call the \emph{Wasserstein simplex}, remains underexplored. We refer to this problem as \emph{Universal Barycenter Approximation}, and propose \texttt{BaryFM}, a flow matching model transporting the marginal measures into any barycenter in the Wasserstein simplex. Once trained, the network can draw samples from measures in the Wasserstein simplex through an ordinary differential equation. We validate our method on 4 downstream tasks: domain adaptation, generalization, Bayesian posterior aggregation and algorithmic fairness. \texttt{BaryFM} achieves the best average rank among 15 competing methods across 10 domain adaptation benchmarks, matching or surpassing non-universal solvers.

</details>


### [160] [The Advantages of Fresh Sketching for Ridge Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38565)
*Linkai Ma,Qilin Li,Petros Drineas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses a fundamental design choice in iterative randomized solvers for (under-constrained) ridge regression: whether to reuse the same sketch or draw fresh randomness at each iteration. The authors demonstrate that fresh sketching provides provable advantages by enabling error analysis along the current residual direction rather than uniformly over the Gram matrix. This directional view yields sharper convergence guarantees and leads to residual-aware sampling rules, including an oracle distribution and practical approximations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether fresh sketches offer provable advantages over reused sketches in iterative ridge regression with column sampling, which has remained an open problem. The goal is to develop sharper convergence guarantees and more efficient sampling rules.

Method: The key insight is to analyze error only along the current residual solution direction rather than uniformly over the entire Gram matrix. This directional view is used to derive an oracle distribution that minimizes the variance of the relevant sketched matrix-vector product. Practical approximations to this oracle are developed, including a mixture sampling distribution with theoretical convergence guarantees.

Result: The paper provides sharper convergence guarantees for leverage score and ridge leverage score sampling. It introduces residual-aware sampling rules and derives an oracle distribution with practical approximations. Experiments on synthetic and real data (including ridge probes on Qwen2.5 representations) demonstrate substantially faster convergence.

Conclusion: Fresh sketching does offer provable advantages over reused sketches in iterative ridge regression. The directional analysis approach leads to better convergence guarantees and practical sampling methods that significantly improve convergence speed in large-scale regression problems.

Abstract: Over the past 25 years, sketching and sampling have become widely used tools for accelerating large-scale regression. In iterative randomized solvers, a basic design choice is whether to $\textit{reuse}$ the same sketch or draw $\textit{fresh}$ randomness at every step. For (under-constrained) iterative ridge regression with column sampling, whether fresh sketches offer provable advantages has remained open: $\textit{We show that they do.}$ Fresh sketching lets us analyze error only along the current residual solution, rather than uniformly over the entire Gram matrix. This directional view yields sharper convergence guarantees for leverage score and ridge leverage score sampling and, more importantly, leads to residual-aware sampling rules. By minimizing the variance of the relevant sketched matrix-vector product, we derive an oracle distribution and practical approximations to the oracle distribution, including a mixture sampling distribution with (somewhat weaker) convergence guarantees. Experiments on synthetic and real data, including ridge probes on Qwen2.5 representations, support our theory, showing substantially faster convergence.

</details>


### [161] [FlexRouter: Learning Complementary Model Sets for Flexible LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38585)
*Wang Wei,Harry Yang,Tiankai Yang,Samyadeep Basu,Hongjie Chen,Andy Zhao,Franck Dernoncourt,Ryan A. Rossi,Hoda Eldardiry*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlexRouter is a novel LLM routing framework that optimizes for answer coverage by explicitly modeling model complementarity using Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs), rather than independently scoring and selecting top-k models. This approach avoids selecting redundant models with similar failure modes and enables adaptive subset sizing during inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM routing methods score models independently and select top-k models, which ignores model correlations and enforces rigid computational budgets. This often leads to selecting redundant models that share failure modes, limiting the overall probability of success.

Method: FlexRouter uses DPPs to model routing policy, capturing both model competence and redundancy. The training objective marginalizes over failure sets to directly optimize coverage without requiring ground-truth target subsets. During inference, a greedy strategy based on marginal log-determinant gains enables adaptive subset size determination without a predefined budget.

Result: Extensive experiments on the large-scale RouterEval benchmark demonstrate that FlexRouter achieves higher coverage with lower redundancy across both in-domain and out-of-domain tasks while maintaining flexible inference cost.

Conclusion: FlexRouter provides a more effective LLM routing approach by explicitly modeling model complementarity and optimizing for answer coverage, outperforming strong baselines in coverage and redundancy metrics.

Abstract: Existing Large Language Model (LLM) routing methods score LLMs independently to select top-$k$ models. However, this ignores model correlations and enforces a rigid computational budget. Consequently, routers often select redundant models that share failure modes, limiting the overall probability of success. To address this, we propose FlexRouter, a routing framework that explicitly models model complementarity. FlexRouter optimizes for \textit{answer coverage}, maximizing the probability that at least one selected model yields a correct response. This objective aligns with practical inference pipelines where multiple candidate outputs are generated and a downstream verifier or user selects the final one. We formulate routing as a coverage-oriented subset selection problem and model the routing policy using Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs), which naturally capture both model competence and redundancy. To directly optimize coverage without requiring a ground-truth target subset, we introduce a training objective based on marginalizing over failure sets. During inference, we employ a greedy strategy based on marginal log-determinant gains, enabling the router to adaptively determine subset sizes without a predefined budget. Extensive experiments on the large-scale RouterEval benchmark demonstrate that our proposed FlexRouter achieves higher coverage with lower redundancy across both in-domain and out-of-domain tasks than strong baselines while maintaining flexible inference cost.

</details>


### [162] [NeurDuo-EEG: A Long-Sequence EEG Foundation Model with Persistent State and Explicit Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38587)
*Yifan Wang,Haiping Liu,Yang Cui,Wenhao Cai,Shuhang Li,Xiaoyang Huang,Xianyang Liu,Jingyu Sun,Yizheng Sun,Cunhang Fan,Tianming Du,Jiancheng Yang,Zhenhong Li,Yunhao Zhang,Hongpeng Zhou,Jingyuan Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeurDuo-EEG is a causal EEG foundation model with channel-resolved persistent memory that introduces multi-timescale memory management, enabling persistent modelling of continuous EEG with fixed-size state. It is pre-trained on 3,955 hours of EEG from 17 public datasets and achieves state-of-the-art performance on 4 out of 5 benchmarks, including seizure detection with significant AUC-PR improvement from 0.285 to 0.471.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key limitation in existing EEG foundation models is their inability to capture long-timescale dynamics because they process fixed windows independently. While state-space architectures enable persistent recurrent processing, long-range information remains implicitly compressed in recurrent states. This work aims to address this gap by introducing persistent memory with learned consolidation and selective retrieval.

Method: NeurDuo-EEG introduces multi-timescale memory management with learned consolidation and selective retrieval, enabling persistent modelling of continuous EEG with fixed-size state. It uses multichannel autoregressive prediction of discrete spectral codes for pre-training on 3,955 hours of EEG from 17 public datasets.

Result: NeurDuo-EEG achieves the best performance on 4 of 5 benchmarks (all three short-window tasks and seizure detection). For seizure detection, AUC-PR improved from 0.285 to 0.471 over the strongest non-NeurDuo baseline. It remains competitive on sleep staging and supports efficient streaming inference with nearly constant per-chunk latency as history grows to one hour. The Small variant achieves this with only 4.7M backbone parameters.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the value of persistent, multi-timescale modelling for both long-sequence and short-window EEG analysis. NeurDuo-EEG successfully addresses the limitation of capturing long-timescale dynamics while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is recorded continuously over hours, with relevant dynamics spanning timescales from milliseconds to hours. Most EEG foundation models nevertheless process fixed windows independently, limiting their ability to capture information encoded in long-timescale dynamics. State-space architectures enable persistent recurrent processing, but long-range information remains implicitly compressed in recurrent states. We present NeurDuo-EEG, a causal EEG foundation model with channel-resolved persistent memory. NeurDuo-EEG introduces multi-timescale memory management with learned consolidation and selective retrieval, enabling persistent modelling of continuous EEG with fixed-size state. It is pre-trained on 3,955 hours of EEG from 17 public datasets using multichannel autoregressive prediction of discrete spectral codes. Across three short-window and two long-sequence downstream tasks, NeurDuo-EEG achieves the best performance on four of five benchmarks, including all three short-window tasks and seizure detection, where AUC-PR improves from $0.285$ to $0.471$ over the strongest non-NeurDuo baseline. NeurDuo-EEG also remains competitive on sleep staging and supports efficient streaming inference, with nearly constant per-chunk latency as the available history grows to one hour. Notably, the Small variant achieves this with only 4.7M backbone parameters. These results demonstrate the value of persistent, multi-timescale modelling for both long-sequence and short-window EEG analysis. Our code is available at https://github.com/YifaNNW/NeurDuo-EEG.

</details>


### [163] [Reinforcement Learning with Complex (valued) Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38598)
*Sathya Kamesh Bhethanabhotla,Efstratios Gavves,André Biedenkapp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using Unitary recurrent networks (uRNNs) as drop-in replacements for recurrent layers in PPO architectures to handle partially observable environments in deep reinforcement learning. The complex vector space representation with norm-preserving unitary dynamics enables better gradient flow and information propagation through long sequences, achieving 2-3x performance gains over baselines on memory-intensive tasks like rocksample and Craftax.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Partially observable environments require agents to compress temporal information and maintain memory for effective decision-making. While various approaches exist (gated recurrence, attention, model-based RL), finding effective representational techniques that capture long-term dependencies remains an active research challenge. The authors revisit uRNNs which demonstrated superior gradient flow and associative recall but have not been explored in RL contexts.

Method: The authors proposed three different versions of uRNNs as drop-in replacements for recurrent PPO architectures. They leverage the complex vector space representation where the phase of the complex hidden states provides additional degrees of freedom. They explored preserving phase information for a phase-aware policy by drawing parallels to quantum state measurement. Experiments were conducted on memory-improvable tasks including continuous control environments.

Result: The proposed uRNN-based methods achieved significant gains over baseline approaches. Performance reached 2-3 times the reward compared to baselines in environments like rocksample and Craftax, demonstrating the effectiveness of unitary dynamics for handling partial observability in RL.

Conclusion: This work points towards an exciting new research direction for representations in RL, specifically for addressing the problem of partial observability. The simple recurrence mechanism combined with the additional degrees of freedom from complex representations (phase information) provides a promising approach for memory-based decision making in partially observable environments.

Abstract: Partially observable environments pose a fundamental challenge in deep reinforcement learning, requiring agents to compress temporal information from observations and maintain a memory to make effective decisions. While there exist many approaches ranging from gated recurrence to attention mechanisms and model-based RL, the search for effective representational techniques that can capture long-term dependencies remains an active area of research. In this work we revisit Unitary recurrent networks (uRNNs) [Arjovsky et al., 2016, Jing et al., 2017], that demonstrated superior gradient flow and associative recall, expressing the recurrence and the hidden state in a complex vector space. Their norm preserving unitary dynamics enable information propagation through long sequences. To this end, we propose three different versions of uRNNs as drop-in replacements for recurrent PPO architectures, and demonstrate that the simple recurrence and the added degree of freedom from the phase of the complex representations enable significant gains over baselines on several memory-improvable tasks, including continuous control. We further explore how to preserve the phase information of the complex hidden state for a phase-aware policy by drawing a parallel to how quantum states are measured. With our methods reaching up to 2-3 $\times$ the reward in environments like rocksample and Craftax compared to the baselines, this work points towards an exciting new direction of representations for RL and the problem of partial observability. Code is available at: https://github.com/Sathya98/qurl

</details>


### [164] [JARQ: Joint Alternating Refinement for Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38599)
*Xinyu Wang,Sicheng Lyu,Xiao-Wen Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JARQ is a plug-in refinement method for group-wise post-training quantization of LLMs that alternates joint least-squares fitting of group scales with bounded Babai proposals to improve weight rounding accuracy. It addresses the limitation that existing quantizers use fixed grids not optimized for the resulting integer codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Group-wise post-training quantizers for LLMs round weights onto a grid that is not refit to the resulting integer codes. This leaves accuracy on the table because: (1) the best grid depends on the codes, (2) input correlations couple errors of different groups, and (3) useful code changes often involve many codes at once.

Method: JARQ alternates between two steps: (1) joint least-squares fit of all group scales, and (2) bounded Babai proposals that move many codes of a group together on the current grid. This solves a bilinear box-constrained mixed-integer least-squares problem. The solver is backpropagation-free, does not increase the layer-wise objective under exact scale solves, and preserves the host's bit width, groups, zero points, and inference cost.

Result: Across Llama-2, Llama-3, and Qwen models with RTN, GPTQ, OmniQuant, and AWQ hosts, JARQ lowers perplexity in 90 of 96 comparisons, cuts three-bit RTN perplexity by up to 36%, raises mean multiple-choice accuracy in 23 of 24 configurations, and improves QEP, QuaRot, and OJBKQ outputs, at under a minute per 7B block.

Conclusion: JARQ effectively refines existing group-wise quantizers by jointly optimizing scales and codes, achieving significant perplexity improvements and accuracy gains without changing the quantization infrastructure or inference cost.

Abstract: Group-wise post-training quantizers for large language models round weights onto a grid that is not refit to the resulting integer codes. We show that this leaves accuracy on the table: the best grid depends on the codes, input correlations couple the errors of different groups, and useful code changes often involve many codes at once. We propose JARQ , a plug-in refinement that starts from any group-wise quantizer and alternates a joint least-squares fit of all group scales with bounded Babai proposals that move many codes of a group together on the current grid. The problem is a bilinear box-constrained mixed-integer least-squares problem; the solver is backpropagation-free, does not increase the layer-wise objective under exact scale solves, and keeps the host's bit width, groups, zero points, and inference cost. Across Llama-2, Llama-3, and Qwen models with RTN, GPTQ, OmniQuant, and AWQ hosts, JARQ lowers perplexity in 90 of 96 comparisons, cuts three-bit RTN perplexity by up to 36%, raises mean multiple-choice accuracy in 23 of 24 configurations, and improves QEP, QuaRot, and OJBKQ outputs, at under a minute per 7B block.

</details>


### [165] [Learning-Enabled Estimation: Tight Characterizations under Sample Selection Biases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38608)
*Vikram Kher,Jane H. Lee,Anay Mehrotra,Manolis Zampetakis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Provides a complete characterization of when regression is identifiable under sample selection bias, identifying minimal assumptions needed and showing regression can be identified even when the selection filter itself cannot be.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sample selection bias pervades many domains (clinical trials, labor markets, auctions) where outcomes are only observed after passing through selection filters depending on both covariates and outcomes. Existing approaches (Heckman's model and extensions) provide sufficient conditions but lack complete characterization, limiting practical applicability in modern complex selection settings.

Method: Theoretical analysis identifying minimal assumptions on functional forms of selection processes, combined with development of oracle-efficient algorithms with finite-sample estimation guarantees under natural strengthenings of identification conditions.

Result: Established characterization of when regression remains possible under selection bias; proved that regression function can be identified even when selection filter cannot (going beyond the "estimate selection, then debias" paradigm); derived explicit convergence rates and provided first general-purpose estimation method for this class of selection problems.

Conclusion: Results enable regression analysis in settings where existing methods fail, with practical implications for econometric settings involving complex selection mechanisms such as auctions with entry costs and labor markets. The framework extends beyond traditional two-stage approaches by identifying conditions where direct regression identification is possible without fully specifying the selection process.

Abstract: When can we learn from biased samples? We study regression when outcomes are observed only after passing through selection filters that depend on both covariates and outcomes themselves, a ubiquitous challenge spanning clinical trials with patient dropout, labor markets with self-selection, and auctions with strategic entry. Ignoring such selection yields systematically biased conclusions with real-world consequences. This challenge has a long history in econometrics and statistics, starting with Heckman's seminal two-stage model and followed by numerous generalizations. While these works provide various sufficient conditions for identification, a complete characterization of when such regression is possible has remained elusive.
  In this work, we provide a characterization for when regression is possible in the presence of sample selection bias. Our results establish the minimal assumptions required on the functional forms of selection processes under which regression remains possible, which are particularly relevant in modern settings where selection mechanisms are increasingly complex and opaque. As a corollary of our characterization, we show that there are settings where the regression function can be identified even when the selection filter itself cannot. This observation already goes beyond the ``estimate selection filter, then debias regression'' paradigm that is followed by virtually all existing approaches. Under natural strengthenings of our identification conditions, we also establish finite-sample estimation guarantees with explicit convergence rates and provide oracle-efficient algorithms. This yields the first general-purpose estimation method for this broad class of selection problems. Finally, we explore the implications of our results for several well-studied econometric settings with complex selection mechanisms such as auctions with entry costs and labor markets.

</details>


### [166] [Differentiable Structure Learning for Cyclic Linear Gaussian Models with Latent Confounders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38618)
*Sadegh Khorasani,Ali Najar,Saber Salehkaleybar,Negar Kiyavash*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses causal structure learning in linear Gaussian SCMs with two key challenges: directed cycles and unknown latent confounders. The authors introduce marginal quasi-equivalence to characterize observational equivalence classes, formulate structure learning as penalized Gaussian negative log-likelihood with a logarithmically scaled complexity penalty, and use Bernoulli gates for continuous optimization. They prove consistency of global minimizers and demonstrate superior recovery error compared to prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current causal discovery methods typically assume acyclic graphs (DAGs) and often require knowledge of latent confounders. This work aims to relax both assumptions simultaneously, enabling structure learning from observational data in more general SCMs with cyclic dependencies and unknown latent variables.

Method: The authors: (1) Derive the covariance matrix for observed variables in linear Gaussian SCMs with cycles and latent confounders; (2) Introduce marginal quasi-equivalence to characterize when different models generate the same observational distributions; (3) Formulate structure learning as optimization of Gaussian negative log-likelihood with logarithmically scaled complexity penalty counting edges and latent variables; (4) Parameterize edges and latent confounders using Bernoulli gates, optimizing continuous probabilities jointly with structural coefficients; (5) Obtain a closed-form differentiable complexity penalty through expectation over Bernoulli gates.

Result: The authors establish that global score minimizers are consistent up to marginal quasi-equivalence under algebraic faithfulness, structural minimality, and model-overlap assumptions. The expected objective achieves the same global infimum as the discrete structure-learning objective. Experiments demonstrate lower recovery error than previous methods across several settings.

Conclusion: This work provides a principled framework for causal structure learning in the presence of both directed cycles and latent confounders, with theoretical consistency guarantees and empirical performance improvements over existing approaches.

Abstract: We study causal structure learning from observational data in linear Gaussian structural causal models in the presence of directed cycles and an unknown number of exogenous latent confounders, bounded by a given maximum. We derive the covariance of the observed variables and introduce marginal quasi-equivalence, which characterizes when different causal models share a full-dimensional subset of the observational distributions they can generate. We formulate structure learning as minimization of the Gaussian negative log-likelihood with a logarithmically scaled complexity penalty that counts directed edges and latent variables. For a fixed number of observed variables and a fixed upper bound on latent variables, we establish consistency of global score minimizers up to marginal quasi-equivalence under algebraic faithfulness, structural minimality, and model-overlap assumptions. We parameterize the inclusion of directed edges and candidate latent variables using Bernoulli gates, whose continuous probabilities are optimized jointly with the structural coefficients. Averaging the penalized negative log-likelihood over these gates yields an objective with a closed-form differentiable complexity penalty. We prove that this expected objective has the same global infimum as the corresponding discrete structure-learning objective. Experimental results show that our approach achieves lower recovery error than previous methods in several experimental settings.

</details>


### [167] [Geometry-physics confounding impairs PDE learning across varying domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38623)
*Yinghao Cheng,Gengxiang Chen,Xu Liu,Qinglu Meng,Yixin Jing,Xiangguo Tang,Wenping Mou,Lihui Wang,Yingguang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses "geometry-physics confounding" in learning partial differential equations across varying domains. The authors propose a de-confounding framework that explicitly models geometry-to-operator transformations, improving both forward operator learning and equation discovery when geometric variations are present.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometric variations in PDE dynamics alter both field representation and governing differential operators, confounding geometric effects with intrinsic physical properties. This leads to reduced data efficiency, poor generalization in forward operator learning, and misspecified candidate libraries in equation discovery, causing biased parameters and spurious terms.

Method: The authors propose a de-confounding framework that makes the known geometry-to-operator transformation explicit. This involves: (1) Geometry-induced coefficient fields for operator learning to improve prediction and data efficiency; (2) Geometry-complete candidate libraries for equation discovery to recover true governing equations and reduce residuals.

Result: The framework demonstrates: (1) Improved prediction and data efficiency across five operator-learning benchmarks; (2) Recovery of generating equations with geometry-complete candidate libraries; (3) Reduction of held-out PDE residuals by more than two orders of magnitude in evolving-domain systems.

Conclusion: By separating known geometric action from intrinsic physics, the proposed framework enables more reliable and data-efficient PDE learning across scientific and engineering problems with varying geometries. This addresses a fundamental challenge in data-driven PDE modeling where domain geometry varies.

Abstract: Learning partial differential equation (PDE) dynamics across varying domains is central to predictive modelling and data-driven discovery of governing equations. However, geometric variation alters both field representation and the governing differential operators, confounding geometric effects with intrinsic physical properties in the observed dynamics. This work identifies geometry-physics confounding as a unified failure mechanism for PDE learning across varying domains. In forward operator learning, this confounding increases the burden of inferring geometry-dependent operator changes from finite data, reducing data efficiency and generalisation. In equation discovery, omitting geometry-induced operators misspecifies the candidate library, leading to biased parameters, missed governing terms and spurious terms. We propose a de-confounding framework that makes the known geometry-to-operator transformation explicit. Geometry-induced coefficient fields improve prediction and data efficiency across five operator-learning benchmarks, while geometry-complete candidate libraries recover the generating equations and reduce held-out PDE residuals by more than two orders of magnitude in both evolving-domain systems. By separating known geometric action from intrinsic physics, the proposed framework supports more reliable and data-efficient PDE learning across scientific and engineering problems with varying geometries.

</details>


### [168] [Interpretable but Fragile? Robustness of Concept Bottlenecks under Geometric-Semantic Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38625)
*Hanwei Zhang,Tianma Hu,Gaojie Jin,Xu Cheng,Ronghui Mu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates how Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) affect model robustness compared to standard classifiers, resolving conflicting findings in prior literature through a generator-based evaluation framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies on CBM robustness report contradictory findings, which the authors attribute to conflating different robustness notions and perturbation regimes rather than fundamental disagreements about CBMs themselves.

Method: The authors introduce a generator-based evaluation framework enabling controlled comparisons between standard classifiers and CBMs under two perturbation types: continuous geometric perturbations in latent space and discrete semantic interventions in concept space. They evaluate robustness empirically (prediction and concept-level sensitivity metrics) and certifiably (randomized smoothing in latent and concept spaces).

Result: The study reconciles previously conflicting findings by clarifying when and in what sense concept bottlenecks do or do not improve robustness. They find that interpretability does not inherently confer robustness; instead, concept bottlenecks shift where and how sensitivity manifests, revealing a nuanced interpretability-robustness trade-off depending on perturbation regime and task structure.

Conclusion: Interpretability and robustness are distinct objectives: interpretable intermediate representations do not uniformly improve robustness but redistribute sensitivity across perturbation spaces and model families.

Abstract: Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) are designed to provide interpretable intermediate representations, yet how such bottlenecks affect robustness remains unclear, with existing studies reporting mixed and sometimes contradictory findings. We argue that these discrepancies arise from conflating different robustness notions and perturbation regimes, rather than from fundamental disagreements about CBMs themselves. To disentangle these factors, we introduce a generator-based evaluation framework that enables controlled comparisons between standard classifiers and CBMs under two distinct perturbation types: continuous geometric perturbations in latent space and discrete semantic interventions in concept space. Within this framework, we evaluate robustness both empirically, via prediction and concept-level sensitivity metrics, and certifiably, using randomized smoothing in latent and concept spaces. Across experiments, we reconcile previously conflicting findings by clarifying when, and in what sense, concept bottlenecks do or do not improve robustness. By further analyzing robustness under varying task conditions, including class semantic similarity and concept vocabulary size, we show that interpretability does not inherently confer robustness. Instead, concept bottlenecks shift where and how sensitivity manifests, revealing a nuanced interpretability robustness trade off that depends critically on the perturbation regime and task structure. Together, our results show that interpretability and robustness are distinct objectives: interpretable intermediate representations do not uniformly improve robustness, but instead redistribute sensitivity across perturbation spaces and model families.

</details>


### [169] [Proper Scoring Rule-based Diffusion for Probabilistic Weather Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38632)
*Joonhyeong Park,Giung Nam,Hyungi Lee,Kyunghyun Cho,Byoungwoo Park,Juho Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces auxiliary conditional denoising tasks to improve probabilistic weather forecasting by learning predictive distributions more effectively at longer horizons. The method extends existing CRPS-based forecasters by adding corrupted future information as conditioning inputs, while maintaining single forward pass generation capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: At longer forecast horizons, uncertainty is high, making it difficult for stochastic predictors to learn predictive distributions from forecast context alone. The goal is to reduce prediction ambiguity by providing partial future information through corrupted versions of the future state.

Method: The method uses auxiliary conditional denoising tasks built on distributional diffusion models. A single stochastic predictor learns conditional distributions by minimizing a proper scoring rule across noise levels. The predictor generates ensemble members in a single forward pass at the fully corrupted endpoint, with standard CRPS training as a special case.

Result: Controlled experiments show improved one-step forecasting across architectures, with larger gains at longer forecast horizons. The method successfully extends to high-dimensional global weather forecasting under both training from scratch and fine-tuning, with improved calibration and potential benefits for generalization under distribution shift.

Conclusion: The auxiliary denoising task framework effectively extends CRPS-based probabilistic forecasters, providing significant improvements in forecasting accuracy, especially at longer horizons, while maintaining computational efficiency through single forward pass generation.

Abstract: Recent probabilistic weather forecasters train stochastic predictors with the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) to generate each ensemble member in a single forward pass. These models learn the predictive distribution from the forecast context alone, which becomes difficult at longer forecast horizons where uncertainty is high. To learn the predictive distribution more effectively, we introduce auxiliary conditional denoising tasks that predict the same future state from the context and its corrupted version, which provides partial future information that can reduce prediction ambiguity. Building on distributional diffusion models, we learn the conditional distributions of these tasks with a single stochastic predictor by minimizing a proper scoring rule across noise levels. At inference, the predictor can still generate each ensemble member in a single forward pass at the fully corrupted endpoint. Standard CRPS training is recovered as the endpoint-only special case of our formulation, so our framework extends existing CRPS-based forecasters with only additional conditioning inputs. Controlled experiments show that the auxiliary tasks improve one-step forecasting across architectures, with larger gains at longer forecast horizons. The gains extend to high-dimensional global weather forecasting under both training from scratch and fine-tuning, along with improved calibration and potential benefits for generalization under distribution shift.

</details>


### [170] [Alignment via Training Against Probes Without Losing Monitorability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38645)
*Lena Libon,Alexander Panfilov,Ben Rank,Xin Chen,Jonas Geiping,Maksym Andriushchenko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "probe-guided fine-tuning" as an alignment method that uses probes detecting undesired properties in model activations as training signals, rather than relying on observed outputs. This approach addresses the problem where capable models may superficially comply with alignment objectives without internalizing intended behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current alignment methods (demonstrations, preference data, reward signals) only reward outputs that appear aligned. More capable models can learn to "fake" compliance during training without internalizing the intended behavior. The authors hypothesize that defining objectives on model internals (rather than outputs) would make superficial compliance harder to achieve.

Method: The method involves training linear and non-linear probes across different layers to detect undesired properties (harmfulness and honesty) in model activations. These probes serve as direct training signals. The study compares: (1) static probes that don't update during training vs. (2) continuously updated probes. Performance is evaluated against DPO (Direct Preference Optimization) and inference-time steering.

Result: Training against static probes is easily exploitable by models. However, continuously updated probes substantially reduce harmfulness and improve honesty while preserving utility. Probe-guided fine-tuning achieves better safety-utility trade-offs than DPO and inference-time steering, and is substantially more robust against jailbreak and abliteration attacks. Importantly, concepts remain linearly encoded after fine-tuning, meaning oversight is not lost.

Conclusion: Probe-guided fine-tuning offers a promising approach to shape what models represent internally, not just what they output. This is increasingly important as models become better at making their outputs appear aligned while potentially having misaligned internal representations. The method provides stronger guarantees against alignment faking by targeting model internals rather than just outputs.

Abstract: Models are usually aligned based on their observed outputs, using demonstrations, preference data, or reward signals. These objectives reward responses that look aligned. More capable models may learn to satisfy them without internalizing the intended behavior, for example by faking compliance during training. Such superficial compliance could be harder when the objective is defined on model internals rather than outputs. Therefore, we study probe-guided fine-tuning, using probes that detect undesired properties in model activations as a direct training signal. We evaluate linear and non-linear probes with different numbers of probes per layer across two alignment objectives: harmlessness and honesty. We find that training against probes that do not update during training is an easily exploitable objective, while continuously updated probes substantially reduce harmfulness and improve honesty while preserving utility. Probe-guided fine-tuning achieves better safety-utility trade-offs than DPO and inference-time steering, while being substantially more robust against jailbreak and abliteration attacks. Moreover, the concepts stay linearly encoded after fine-tuning, meaning oversight is not lost by our method. Training against probes thus offers a way to shape what models represent rather than only what they output, which may become increasingly important as models get better at making their outputs look aligned.

</details>


### [171] [Uncertainty-Normalized Margins for Direct Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38647)
*Sadegh Khorasani,Petrus Mikkola,Matthias Grossglauser*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces UNM-DPO, a novel preference optimization method that extends DPO by incorporating preference strength margins and learned prompt-dependent uncertainty scales. The method includes two variants (AO and WR) plus a length-normalized version (ULNM-DPO-WR), achieving state-of-the-art results on HelpSteer2/3 and AlpacaEval benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) models binary preferences using a Bradley-Terry model with a common noise scale, which fails to capture two important aspects: (1) preference strength - some preferences are stronger than others, and (2) prompt-dependent uncertainty - different prompts may have varying levels of human feedback ambiguity. This work aims to address these limitations by introducing strength-dependent margins and learned prompt scales.

Method: The proposed UNM-DPO combines: (1) Strength-dependent margins that account for varying preference strengths, and (2) A learned prompt scale capturing prompt-dependent uncertainty. Two training objectives are developed based on a heteroskedastic Bradley-Terry model: (a) Advantage-only (AO) - divides reward difference by prompt scale before subtracting margin; (b) Whole-residual (WR) - subtracts margin before dividing by scale. A theoretical identifiability condition for the prompt scale is established. ULNM-DPO-WR adds length normalization to WR for additional improvement.

Result: Using Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: (1) On HelpSteer2/3 evaluation panels, ULNM-DPO-WR achieves 68.00% and 65.31% tie-adjusted win rates against matched DPO, with higher mean rewards and shorter responses on average. (2) On AlpacaEval with GPT-4.1 judge, ULNM-DPO-WR achieves 21.62% length-controlled win rate, compared to 16.39% for DPO and 15.30% for SimPO.

Conclusion: Combining preference-strength margins, learned prompt scales, and length normalization significantly improves policy optimization. The heteroskedastic Bradley-Terry modeling approach effectively captures preference strength and prompt-dependent uncertainty, leading to consistent improvements over DPO and related baselines across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Direct preference optimization (DPO) models binary preferences through a Bradley-Terry model with a common noise scale, without explicitly accounting for preference strength or prompt-dependent uncertainty from human feedback. We introduce uncertainty-normalized margin DPO (UNM-DPO), which combines strength-dependent margins with a learned prompt scale. Motivated by a heteroskedastic Bradley-Terry model, we develop two training objectives. Both compare the implicit rewards of preferred and rejected responses, derived from response log-probability ratios to a reference policy. Advantage-only (AO) divides this reward difference by the prompt scale before subtracting the margin; whole-residual (WR) subtracts the margin before dividing by the scale. For the WR comparison model, we establish a necessary and sufficient condition under which known margins make the prompt scale identifiable. We introduce a practical procedure for learning the scale. Building on WR, we introduce ULNM-DPO-WR, which normalizes each response's implicit reward by its length. We evaluate our methods against DPO and related baselines on HelpSteer2 and HelpSteer3, using the Skywork reward model as a judge. With Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, ULNM-DPO-WR achieves tie-adjusted win rates against matched DPO of 68.00% and 65.31% on evaluation panels, with higher mean rewards and shorter responses on average. On AlpacaEval with a GPT-4.1 judge and GPT-4-Turbo reference answers, the same 8B policy achieves a length-controlled win rate of 21.62%, compared with 16.39% for DPO and 15.30% for SimPO. These results demonstrate the potential of combining preference-strength margins, learned prompt scales, and length normalization for policy optimization.

</details>


### [172] [Understanding Off- vs On-Policy Distillation: A Tale of Distinct Training Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38666)
*Qiwei Di,Xuheng Li,Kaixuan Ji,Chenggong Zhang,Heyang Zhao,Quanquan Gu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies on-policy distillation (OPD) from multiple teachers, comparing forward and reverse KL divergence aggregation targets. It develops algorithms with logarithmic regret bounds and identifies mechanisms explaining OPD's benefits (better retention under uninformative feedback) and fragility (sensitivity to low-probability correct responses and dependence on continuation distributions).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While on-policy distillation has shown promise in reducing forgetting relative to supervised fine-tuning, its benefits and fragility remain incompletely understood. This work aims to understand the theoretical mechanisms behind OPD by analyzing different KL divergence aggregation targets.

Method: The authors study sequential distillation from multiple teachers, where the student minimizes average divergence from teachers. They analyze forward KL (yielding weighted arithmetic mixture) and reverse KL (yielding normalized weighted geometric aggregate). They develop algorithms for both off-policy and on-policy feedback settings, establishing logarithmic regret bounds in the tabular setting and extending to function approximation.

Result: Relative to forward KL, reverse KL better retains a confident expert's preferences under uninformative feedback. However, reverse KL is more sensitive to teachers that assign very low probabilities to correct responses. The token-level conditionals reveal a dependence on continuation distributions that can favor incorrect prefixes over long horizons.

Conclusion: The analysis identifies mechanisms explaining both the benefits and fragility of OPD: reverse KL offers advantages in certain feedback scenarios but carries risks related to teacher confidence and prefix continuation distributions, providing guidance for practitioners when choosing between forward and reverse KL aggregation.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) learns from teacher feedback on student-generated responses and has shown promise in reducing forgetting relative to supervised fine-tuning (SFT). However, its benefits and fragility remain incompletely understood. We study sequential distillation from multiple teachers, where the student minimizes its average divergence from the teachers. Forward Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence yields a weighted arithmetic mixture, while reverse KL yields a normalized weighted geometric aggregate. We develop algorithms that learn these targets under off-policy and on-policy feedback, respectively, establishing logarithmic regret bounds in the tabular setting and extending the analysis to function approximation. By analyzing these aggregation targets, we identify mechanisms that help explain both the benefits and fragility of OPD. Relative to forward KL, reverse KL can better retain a confident expert's preferences under uninformative feedback, but is more sensitive to teachers that assign very low probabilities to correct responses. Its token-level conditionals also reveal a dependence on continuation distributions that can favor incorrect prefixes over long horizons.

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [173] [PrecipJEPA: JEPA-Regularized Future-State Prediction with Motion-Source Rendering for Precipitation Nowcasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38926)
*Yufeng Zhu,Dan Niu,Qiliang Wu,Weiwei Huang,Yixiao Liang,Yongchao Feng,Chunlei Shi*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: PrecipJEPA is a deep learning model for long-term precipitation nowcasting that combines a structured forecasting path with an auxiliary path (History-Masked JEPA) to improve radar-echo evolution modeling. The model uses an online encoder, Task-Driven Future-State Predictor (TFP), and Parallel Motion-Source Renderer (PMSR) to generate motion and source-sink fields for transforming observations into future frames.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for precipitation nowcasting fail to adequately preserve localized high-intensity structures during long-term predictions. Current encoders learn representations mainly from final forecast errors, ignoring the rich historical information in observed radar sequences. There was a need for location-aware prediction that separates echo displacement from intensity change.

Method: PrecipJEPA employs a dual-pathway architecture: (1) Forecasting path with an online encoder converting observations into spatiotemporal tokens, a Task-Driven Future-State Predictor (TFP) combining tokens with recent-dynamics summary and spatiotemporal queries, and a Parallel Motion-Source Renderer (PMSR) decoding states into motion and source-sink fields that transform the latest observation into future frames. (2) Auxiliary path with History-Masked JEPA (H-JEPA) that predicts masked historical features from visible context to directly supervise the online encoder during joint training.

Result: PrecipJEPA achieves substantial improvements over strongest baselines: 118.6% improvement in highest-threshold CSI on SEVIR dataset and 35.1% on MeteoNet dataset, while maintaining the highest mean CSI throughout the 3-hour forecast horizon.

Conclusion: The proposed PrecipJEPA successfully addresses long-term precipitation nowcasting by separating motion (displacement) from intensity changes (source-sink) and using history-masked self-supervised learning to improve encoder representations. The dual-pathway architecture with auxiliary supervision enables better preservation of high-intensity precipitation structures, demonstrating significant practical value for operational weather forecasting.

Abstract: Long-term precipitation nowcasting requires modeling radar-echo evolution while preserving localized high-intensity structures. Recent radar-specific studies motivate location-aware prediction and separating echo displacement from intensity change. However existing encoders learn historical representations mainly from final forecast errors. We propose PrecipJEPA, which couples a structured forecasting path with an auxiliary path that enriches its encoder from observed radar history. In the forecasting path, an online encoder first converts the observations into spatiotemporal tokens. The Task-Driven Future-State Predictor (TFP) combines these tokens with a recent-dynamics summary and spatiotemporal queries to construct future radar states. The Parallel Motion-Source Renderer (PMSR) decodes these states into motion and source-sink fields that transform the latest observation into future frames. During joint training, the History-Masked JEPA (H-JEPA) operates on the auxiliary path to predict masked historical features from visible context, directly supervising the same online encoder from the observed sequence. Experiments on SEVIR and MeteoNet show that PrecipJEPA improves highest-threshold CSI by 118.6% and 35.1%, respectively, over the strongest baselines, while maintaining the highest mean CSI throughout the 3-hour forecast.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [174] [How Should Diffusion Language Models Edit Code?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38257)
*Xijia Tao,Ziru Liu,Shansan Gong,Jiacheng Ye,Kecheng Chen,Zirui Wu,Lin Zheng,Xinyu Fu,Rui Liu,Lingpeng Kong*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper studies how masked diffusion language models perform code editing across four different interfaces (whole-file rewriting, search-and-replace, locate-then-infill, and token-level editing), revealing a "composition gap" where models excel at generating coordinated changes when edit locations are provided but fail when locations must be predicted.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why strong infilling capability alone doesn't ensure reliable editing, and to identify the key requirements for successful code editing with diffusion models.

Method: Experiments on CanItEdit dataset, varying editable regions while holding the generation model and decoding procedure fixed. Also tested sentence-level Wiki editing to probe generalizability beyond code.

Result: Two distinct requirements for successful editing: (1) covering every required change and (2) placing precise boundaries around it. Missing a required region prevents the change, while widening regions to ensure coverage sharply reduces success by requiring regeneration of unchanged code. A composition gap exists between supplied and predicted locations in both code and text editing.

Conclusion: Strong infilling capability alone does not ensure reliable editing; the interface must expose all required changes while limiting regeneration of unchanged code. The editing interface design is critical for model performance.

Abstract: Code editing requires a model to decide where to make changes, generate the new content, and preserve everything else. We study how masked diffusion language models divide these responsibilities across four editing interfaces: whole-file rewriting, search-and-replace, locate-then-infill, and token-level editing. Experiments on CanItEdit reveal a composition gap: diffusion models can generate coordinated changes when the correct edit locations are supplied, but much of this capability is lost when those locations must be predicted. Access to the intact original code helps the model fill multiple edit regions, yet does not resolve the difficulty of selecting those regions. By varying the editable regions while holding the generation model and decoding procedure fixed, we identify two distinct requirements for successful editing: covering every required change and placing precise boundaries around it. Missing a required region prevents the corresponding change, while widening regions to ensure coverage can sharply reduce success by requiring unchanged code to be regenerated. A sentence-level Wiki editing probe shows the same qualitative gap between supplied and predicted locations beyond code. These findings show why strong infilling capability alone does not ensure reliable editing: the interface must expose all required changes while limiting regeneration of unchanged code.

</details>


### [175] [Zero2Repo: Can Coding Agents Build Repositories from Scratch?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38269)
*Pei Yang,Tianyu Shi,Yuhang Yao,Wanyi Chen,Tongyun Yang,Dun Pei,Haonan Wang,Pengbin Feng,Guanxu Yu,Jingchun Huang,Zeyu Zhang,Shuhan Sun,Hao Li,Xiang Li,Jie Xiao,Xinyu Wang,Hanxin Chen,Daqi Li,Qi Jia,Hongshan Lin,Zhizhou Gu,Zijun Tian,Weizhi Du,Lynn Ai,Eric Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Zero2Repo is a benchmark for evaluating coding agents' ability to build complete software repositories from scratch. It uses a language-agnostic pipeline to convert real open-source projects into tasks with behavioral specifications, reproducible environments, and hidden acceptance tests. The benchmark runs agents in isolated containers with binary reward (pass/fail only) based on execution, not LLM judgment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks for coding agents are limited to single programming language and rely on manually curated tasks. There is a need for a more comprehensive benchmark that can evaluate agents' ability to construct complete repositories from scratch across multiple languages and ecosystems.

Method: The benchmark uses a language-agnostic authoring pipeline that transforms real, version-pinned open-source projects into: (1) behavioral specifications, (2) reproducible environments, and (3) hidden acceptance tests. Validation occurs through execution - reference implementations must pass, and adversarial validation ensures tests reject incorrect implementations. Agents run in isolated containers with tests withheld until explicit submission.

Result: On 11 tasks from repositories likely seen during training, the strongest agent solves only 10. Failing submissions pass 90-99% of hidden tests. For the two strongest agents, 67-100% of failed tests trace to single omissions or low-frequency rules in specifications rather than missing subsystems.

Conclusion: The benchmark reveals that even frontier models struggle with complete repository construction, though they pass most tests. Failures are typically due to specific omissions or overlooked rules, providing concrete targets for improvement. The language-agnostic design supports Python, TypeScript, Go, and C++.

Abstract: Coding agents are increasingly asked to build software rather than patch it, yet benchmarks for from-scratch repository construction are mostly limited to a single language and depend on manually curated tasks. We introduce Zero2Repo, a benchmark in which an agent receives a product requirements document, an interface contract, and an empty workspace, and must deliver a complete repository in the project's native ecosystem. Tasks are produced by a language-agnostic authoring pipeline that converts real, version-pinned open-source projects into behavioral specifications, reproducible environments, and hidden acceptance tests. Each task is validated by execution: a reference implementation derived from the upstream project must pass, and adversarial validation must show that the tests reject incorrect implementations. Evaluation runs production coding agents in isolated containers, withholds the acceptance tests until an explicit submission, and assigns a binary reward only when every test passes, with no LLM judge. The pipeline and harness make no language-specific assumptions and apply to mainstream programming ecosystems; the current release contains Python, TypeScript, Go, and C++ tasks. Even on 11 tasks drawn from repositories that frontier models have very likely seen during training, the strongest agent solves only 10, and every failing submission passes 90-99% of the hidden tests; for the two strongest agents, 67-100% of failed tests trace to a single omission or a low-frequency rule stated in the specification rather than to a missing subsystem, so each failure is a concrete target for improvement.

</details>


### [176] [E2E-SWE: Benchmarking LLMs on Building Working Codebases from Scratch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38335)
*Hantian Ding,Chloe Bi,Jiacheng Zhu,John Yang,Matt Deitke,Pengcheng Yin,Zijian Wang,Rui Hou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: E2E-SWE is a benchmark for evaluating LLM-powered coding agents on end-to-end repository-scale software generation, containing 186 tasks across 11 programming languages. Given only natural-language specifications, agents must implement complete, installable projects that pass hidden test suites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating repository-scale generation for coding agents is challenging because tasks must demand system-level reasoning while ensuring precise specification and implementation independence. Existing benchmarks fail to capture the full complexity of building complete software repositories from scratch.

Method: E2E-SWE benchmark contains 186 whole-repository generation tasks. Tasks are constructed through collaboration between software engineers and LLMs, developing test suites and implementation-independent specifications. An iterative verification process uses autonomous agents to audit and repair task defects via static inspection and real model rollouts.

Result: Evaluating 13 frontier models shows substantial variation in end-to-end repository generation ability, with pass@1 ranging from 11.7% to 67.7%, providing strong model differentiation while leaving considerable headroom for future progress.

Conclusion: The benchmark reveals that successful repository-scale generation requires long, front-loaded reasoning patterns, highlighting the importance of planning and system-level reasoning when constructing working codebases from scratch. The benchmark provides strong model differentiation with significant room for improvement.

Abstract: Coding agents powered by large language models (LLMs) are evolving from making localized code changes to developing complete software repositories. However, evaluating repository-scale generation remains challenging: tasks must demand system-level reasoning while ensuring that all evaluated behaviors are precisely specified and independent of any particular implementation. We introduce E2E-SWE, a benchmark for evaluating whether coding agents can build complete, functional software repositories end to end. E2E-SWE contains 186 whole-repository generation tasks spanning 11 programming languages. Given only a natural-language specification and an empty workspace, an agent must implement a complete, installable project that satisfies a comprehensive suite of hidden tests. Each task is constructed by a software engineer in collaboration with an LLM; together, they develop the test suite and a corresponding implementation-independent specification. To ensure that tasks are well specified and practically solvable, we further subject them to an iterative verification process in which autonomous agents audit and repair task defects using static inspection and failures observed from real model rollouts. Evaluating 13 frontier models, we find substantial variation in end-to-end repository generation ability, with pass@1 ranging from 11.7% to 67.7%, providing strong model differentiation while leaving considerable headroom for future progress. Analysis of agent trajectories further reveals long, front-loaded reasoning patterns, highlighting the planning and system-level reasoning required to construct working codebases from scratch.

</details>


### [177] [OpenCollab: A Multi-Agent Coding Framework with Programmable Collaboration and Controllable Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38345)
*Chun-Wah Hsu,Kai Gong,Yu Wu,Xianhe Chen,Mengyang Liu,Jie Li,Hanyu Li,Zhixuan Liu,Naisheng Tang,Jiaying Chi,Ziheng Fan,Xuning He,Xiaokang Yang,Xue Jiang,Yihong Dong*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: OpenCollab is a multi-agent coding framework that addresses evaluation gaps in multi-agent software engineering systems by providing unified infrastructure for programmable collaboration, controllable runtime, and fine-grained execution tracking. It introduces "Adherence" metric to measure whether declared organizational configurations are actually realized, and demonstrates that a two-coder workflow achieves SOTA performance while single-agent config uses fewest tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent coding system evaluations assume configured organizations are followed faithfully, but reality differs. This behavioral gap combined with differences in underlying system components prevents clear attribution of observed performance gains. The field lacks a unified infrastructure for programmable collaboration and controllable runtime that enables causal evaluation.

Method: OpenCollab provides: (1) unified organization design, (2) enforced experimental control on shared runtime, (3) fine-grained event stream tracking. The Adherence metric quantifies whether declared organization is actually realized in practice.

Result: Experiments reveal significant behavioral variation: changing any single dimension shifts Adherence from 47.2% to 97.2%. A two-coder workflow built on OpenCollab achieves SOTA performance compared to Mini-SWE-agent, Codex CLI, and Claude Code. The single-agent configuration uses the fewest tokens across all evaluated suites.

Conclusion: OpenCollab establishes a unified multi-agent infrastructure enabling easy programmable collaboration and controlled causal evaluation. Well-designed organization configurations can outperform existing strong harnesses, while different configurations offer different trade-offs (performance vs. efficiency).

Abstract: Multi-agent coding systems are designed to tackle complex software engineering tasks through collaboration. However, existing evaluations typically assume configured organizations are followed faithfully, whereas reality differs. This behavioral gap, combined with differences in underlying system components, prevents clear attribution of observed gains. To this end, we introduce OpenCollab, a multi-agent coding framework that provides a unified infrastructure for programmable collaboration and controllable runtime. Specifically, OpenCollab unifies organization design, enforces experimental control on a shared runtime, and tracks execution through fine-grained event streams. On this basis, we define Adherence to quantify whether the declared organization is actually realized. Our experiments reveal that agents collaborate very differently across configurations: changing any single dimension shifts Adherence, from 47.2% to as high as 97.2%. Furthermore, extensive agentic coding benchmarks show that a two-coder workflow built on OpenCollab establishes new SOTA performance compared to the mainstream harnesses such as Mini-SWE-agent, Codex CLI, and Claude Code, showing that a well-designed organization can outperform strong existing harnesses, while OpenCollab's single-agent configuration uses the fewest tokens across all evaluated suites. OpenCollab establishes a unified multi-agent infrastructure for easy programmable collaboration and controlled causal evaluation.

</details>


### [178] [From Codebase to Culprit (C2C): Reducing the Search Space for Bugs with Semantic Retrieval and Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38402)
*Ankur Garg,Corey Yang-Smith,Rishav Rishav,Ahmad Abdellatif,Samira Ebrahimi Kahou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: C2C is a bug localization framework that uses semantic retrieval and Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning to progressively narrow down bug locations from files to functions to specific lines of code, improving upon prior single-granularity approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior bug localization approaches operate at a single granularity level, which doesn't match developers' natural top-down debugging workflow. There is a need for multi-resolution localization that maintains contextual consistency across different levels of code hierarchy.

Method: Two-stage process: (1) Recall-oriented retrieval using semantic vector similarity with CodeBERT embeddings fine-tuned via contrastive learning, incorporating bug report text and stack-trace information; (2) HRL framework that incrementally localizes bugs from files → functions → lines, using hierarchical decomposition, structured learning signals, and reward shaping.

Result: Experiments on real-world Java and Python datasets demonstrate improved retrieval precision and localization accuracy. Ablation studies validate the contributions of hierarchical decomposition, structured learning signals, and reward shaping.

Conclusion: C2C enables precise multi-resolution bug localization while maintaining contextual consistency across granularity levels, outperforming prior single-granularity approaches.

Abstract: We introduce C2C (From Codebase to Culprit), a framework for precise bug localization that progressively reduces the debugging search space across multiple levels of granularity: files, functions, and lines of code. To mirror developer's natural top-down debugging workflows, C2C integrates semantic retrieval and Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning (HRL) in a two-stage process. First, it performs recall-oriented retrieval of buggy candidates via semantic vector similarity search using bug-report text, including available stack-trace information, against a database of embeddings, where the embeddings are fine-tuned via contrastive learning with CodeBERT. Building on this reduced search space, the HRL framework incrementally localizes bugs, reasoning from files to functions and ultimately to individual lines of code. Unlike prior approaches which operate at a single granularity, C2C enables multi-resolution localization while maintaining contextual consistency across decisions. Experiments on real-world Java and Python datasets demonstrate that C2C improves retrieval precision and localization accuracy. Ablation studies further highlight the contributions of hierarchical decomposition, structured learning signals, and reward shaping in advancing multi-level bug localization.

</details>


### [179] [MallocSan: A Memory Safety Tool for Native Closed-Source Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38499)
*Adel Belkhiri,Michel Dagenais,Maxime Lamothe*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: MallocSan is a heap sanitizer for native x86-64 Linux applications that requires no source access, recompilation, or specialized hardware. It works by interposing on memory allocation through LD_PRELOAD and embedding object identifiers in unused high bits of pointers, causing faults at offending instructions which are then patched at runtime for per-object bounds checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-corruption errors remain a leading cause of high-impact vulnerabilities in C and C++ software. Existing deterministic detection methods (compiler sanitizers, hardware-assisted schemes, dynamic binary translation) have limitations: they require source code, specific hardware, or impose significant performance overhead (order-of-magnitude slowdowns).

Method: MallocSan uses LD_PRELOAD to interpose on memory allocation and embeds an object identifier in the unused high bits of each protected pointer. Dereferencing the resulting noncanonical pointer faults at the offending instruction, which is then decoded and patched at runtime. Frequently executed residual sites are rewritten offline via profile-guided pass. Fallback mechanisms include in-handler emulation or single-stepping. Also extends to vector gather/scatter instructions and supports policy-scoped coverage.

Result: Across seven SPEC CPU 2017 benchmarks, MallocSan outperforms Valgrind Memcheck on five, with a geometric-mean execution-time factor of 4.82x relative to native execution, compared with 18.55x for Memcheck. Preserves parallel scaling on multithreaded workloads (644.nab_s and pigz). On Juliet tests, detects all seeded violations with no false reports. Also detects a known one-byte overread in LibTIFF's tiffcrop utility.

Conclusion: MallocSan provides an effective heap sanitization solution for closed-source x86-64 Linux applications with significantly lower overhead than Valgrind Memcheck (4.82x vs 18.55x), while maintaining detection accuracy with no false positives on good executions and successful detection of real-world vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Memory-corruption errors remain a leading cause of high-impact vulnerabilities in C and C++ software. Deterministic detection, however, remains difficult to deploy: compiler-based sanitizers require source code and control of the build pipeline, hardware-assisted schemes depend on specific platforms, and dynamic binary translation can impose order-of-magnitude slowdowns. This paper presents MallocSan, a heap sanitizer for native, potentially closed-source x86-64 Linux applications that requires no source access, recompilation, or specialized hardware. MallocSan interposes on memory allocation through LD_PRELOAD and embeds an object identifier in the unused high bits of each protected pointer. Dereferencing the resulting noncanonical pointer faults at the offending instruction, which MallocSan decodes and patches at runtime so that subsequent executions perform per-object bounds checks entirely in userspace. Sites that cannot be patched fall back to in-handler emulation or single-stepping, while an optional profile-guided pass rewrites frequently executed residual sites offline. MallocSan also extends identity-based checking to vector gather/scatter instructions and supports policy-scoped coverage. Across seven SPEC CPU 2017 benchmarks, MallocSan outperforms Valgrind Memcheck on five, with a geometric-mean execution-time factor of 4.82x relative to native execution, compared with 18.55x for Memcheck, while preserving substantial parallel scaling on both evaluated multithreaded workloads: 644.nab_s and pigz. On the in-scope Juliet tests, MallocSan detects all seeded violations with no false reports on the good executions. It also detects heap-buffer errors in real-world applications, including a known one-byte overread in LibTIFF's tiffcrop utility.

</details>


### [180] [An Empirical Study of Architectural Shift from Traditional to AI-Enabled Simulink Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38504)
*Hadiza Umar Yusuf,Khouloud Gaaloul*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses the gap in understanding how traditional and AI-enabled controller architectures differ in cyber-physical systems (CPS). Through an empirical study of 62 real-world Simulink models and a survey of 13 practitioners, the research identifies three key architectural tensions: (1) subsystem organization dominates controller structures regardless of paradigm, (2) AI-enabled controllers rely on discrete dynamics and user-defined abstraction absent from AI literature, and (3) constraint enforcement mechanisms shift from explicit structure to implicit training-time artifacts, breaking traceability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from a critical gap in AI adoption in CPS: as AI-enabled components increasingly replace analytically derived control laws, there is no systematic understanding of how controller architectures differ or remain similar across paradigms. This lack of understanding hinders effective AI integration into engineering practice.

Method: The study employed a mixed-methods approach: (1) Empirical analysis of 62 real-world Simulink models spanning 8 controller types and 10 application domains, guided by a literature-derived taxonomy of 10 structural categories and 9 functional roles; (2) Survey of 13 practitioners to validate findings and capture industry perspectives.

Result: Three architectural tensions were identified: (1) Subsystem organization dominates all controller structures (68-72% of footprint) while core control logic occupies minimal space; (2) AI-enabled controllers heavily rely on discrete dynamics and user-defined abstraction—categories largely absent from AI literature, exposing a gap between described and implemented architectures; (3) Constraint enforcement blocks largely disappear from AI-enabled models despite practitioner expectations, indicating a misalignment where safety mechanisms shift from explicit structure to implicit training-time artifacts, breaking traceability.

Conclusion: The study reveals a fundamental misalignment in AI adoption for CPS: safety mechanisms transition from explicit architectural structures to implicit training-time artifacts, which breaks traceability. This highlights the need for better integration of design knowledge into engineering practice and addresses the gap between described and implemented AI controller architectures.

Abstract: Effective AI adoption in cyber-physical systems (CPS) depends on embedding design knowledge into engineering practice. Yet as AI-enabled components increasingly replace analytically derived control laws, this occurs without a systematic understanding of how controller architectures differ or remain similar across paradigms. We address this gap with an empirical study of traditional and AI-enabled Simulink controllers, guided by a literature-derived taxonomy of ten structural categories and nine functional roles. The study analyzes 62 real-world models spanning 8 controller types and 10 application domains, and surveys 13 practitioners, identifying three architectural tensions. First, subsystem organization dominates all controller structures regardless of paradigm, occupying 68-72% of controller footprint, while core control logic occupies minimal space. Second, AI-enabled controllers rely heavily on discrete dynamics and user-defined abstraction, categories largely absent from AI literature, exposing a gap between described and implemented architectures. Third, constraint enforcement blocks largely disappear from AI-enabled models despite practitioner expectations. This reveals a misalignment where safety mechanisms shift from explicit structure to implicit training-time artifacts, breaking traceability.

</details>


### [181] [Adaptive-GEPA: Make Your Harness Fit Heterogeneous Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38762)
*Tianyu Chen,Yasi Zhang,Ruiyi Wang,Xinran Zhao,Taoran Li,Mingyuan Zhou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Adaptive-GEPA addresses the limitation of prior reflective optimizers (like GEPA) that optimize a single shared program for heterogeneous requests. It introduces a dual evolution approach: simultaneously learning a router to categorize requests and a library of specialist programs to handle each category. The method achieves 70.6 test score (x100) vs GEPA's 62.5 on a 4-task family mixture, with perfect routing agreement on 651 test requests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reflective optimizers like GEPA optimize one shared program for all requests, leaving the division of work implicit in source-code search. This causes inefficiency when requests require different tools, reasoning modes, and control flow. The paper aims to explicitly learn both routing and specialist solutions.

Method: Adaptive-GEPA evolves a router and specialist programs under one search budget. The router's instructions and each specialist's description/program are plain text editable from feedback. Specialists are aligned by the requests they handle, with descriptions inherited alongside programs. The system runs on Qwen3-8B with 18,000 scored calls budget.

Result: On a fixed mixture of four task families, Adaptive-GEPA evolves four experts without supplying family labels. Its routing matches the task partition on all 651 test requests. Family-mean test score (x100) rises from 52.6 to 70.6, compared with 62.5 for GEPA's full-program adapter and 54.0 for GRPO.
