Conclusion: Adaptive-GEPA successfully learns both request division and solution strategies. By evolving separate specialists with a routing mechanism, it significantly outperforms GEPA and GRPO baselines. The perfect routing agreement (651/651) demonstrates the approach's effectiveness in handling heterogeneous request distributions.

Abstract: Reflective optimizers such as GEPA improve language model prompts from execution traces and evaluator feedback; full-program extensions can also rewrite tools and control flow. In practice, a user hands the same endpoint heterogeneous requests whose effective solutions require different tools, reasoning modes, and control flow. Optimizing one shared program leaves this division of work implicit in source-code search, while optimizing a separate program per request family fixes it beforehand.
  We introduce Adaptive-GEPA, which learns both how to divide requests and how to solve them. It evolves a router and a library of specialist programs under one search budget. The router's instructions, each specialist's description, and its program code are plain, human-readable text, edited from feedback. To combine branches, it aligns specialists by the requests they handle and inherits descriptions together with programs. On a fixed mixture of four task families, the reported Qwen3-8B run evolves four experts without supplying family labels to the router or reflection model; its routing matches the task partition on all 651 test requests. Its family-mean test score (x100) rises from 52.6 to 70.6, compared with 62.5 for GEPA's full-program adapter and 54.0 for GRPO at a nominal budget of 18,000 scored calls. These counts do not equate total compute. Figure 1 summarizes the learning curves, final test scores, and routing agreement.

</details>


### [182] [Toward Quantum Software Automation: A Quantum-Aware Harness for LLM-Guided Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38883)
*Lily Jiaxin Wan,Deming Chen,Klara Nahrstedt,Bo Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: QSA is a quantum-aware evolutionary search framework that uses LLM-guided search to automate quantum software design, featuring three quantum-specific guidance mechanisms: evolution-hardness-guided coreset for cost reduction, static/snapshot analyses for execution context, and task-specific rewards for optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum software design relies on ad-hoc handcrafted heuristics that are suboptimal and quickly become obsolete as quantum hardware evolves. Existing LLM-guided search frameworks lack quantum-specific support for efficient evolution due to expensive verification, sparse feedback, and heterogeneous optimization objectives.

Method: QSA provides three forms of quantum-specific guidance: (1) evolution-hardness-guided coreset and approximate scoring to reduce verification cost; (2) static and snapshot analyses to provide fine-grained execution context; (3) task-specific rewards for compiler passes and runtime policies. The approach is evaluated on IBM Quantum platform across three benchmark suites.

Result: For multiprogramming, QSA improves QPU utilization by 4.2%-9.5% and Hellinger fidelity by 15.2%-19.5% over state-of-the-art methods. For error mitigation, QSA reduces mitigation time by at least 96.8% while achieving comparable or better fidelity. The total cost is $6.9 in LLM API cost over 11.3 hours.

Conclusion: QSA demonstrates that LLM-guided evolutionary search can effectively automate quantum software design with minimal computational cost, achieving significant improvements in both multiprogramming efficiency and error mitigation performance on real quantum hardware.

Abstract: Quantum software is critical for improving the efficiency and reliability of scarce quantum hardware. However, its design still relies heavily on ad-hoc, handcrafted heuristics that are often suboptimal and quickly become obsolete as quantum hardware evolves. LLM-guided evolutionary search offers a promising way to automatically explore complex software designs, but existing search frameworks lack the quantum-specific support needed for efficient evolution: verification is expensive, feedback is sparse, and heterogeneous quantum programs require different optimization objectives. In this paper, we present QSA, a quantum-aware harness for LLM-guided evolutionary search toward automating quantum software design. QSA equips the search with three forms of quantum-specific guidance: an evolution-hardness-guided coreset and approximate scoring to reduce verification cost, static and snapshot analyses to provide fine-grained execution context, and task-specific rewards for compiler passes and runtime policies. We evaluate QSA on the IBM Quantum platform across three benchmark suites. For multiprogramming, QSA improves QPU utilization by 4.2%-9.5% and Hellinger fidelity by 15.2%-19.5% over the state of the art. For error mitigation, QSA reduces mitigation time by at least 96.8% while achieving comparable or better fidelity. These gains require only $6.9 in LLM API cost over 11.3 hours.

</details>


### [183] [Doing More with Less Tokens: Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning for Efficient Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38885)
*Haobin Li,Liang Jiang,Zhenyu Huang,Mouxing Yang,Xi Peng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents HERO, a reinforcement learning framework for training token-efficient coding agents in software engineering scenarios. The approach addresses the trade-off between resolution rate and token efficiency by leveraging two key observations: efficiency variation (successful resolution possible with fewer tokens) and entropy correlation (unproductive behaviors linked to turn-level entropy).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current coding agents in SWE scenarios require frequent environment interactions, leading to substantial token overhead, high costs, and latency. Existing inference-time approaches (context manipulation, interaction limits) sacrifice task-relevant information and struggle to balance resolution rate with token efficiency. There is a need for a training-based approach that achieves both high resolution and token efficiency.

Method: HERO uses a novel reinforcement learning framework with two strategies: (1) prioritizes task resolution over token efficiency during policy optimization, and (2) encourages efficient reasoning patterns at both trajectory and turn levels. The framework is based on two core observations: Efficiency Variation (successful resolution achievable with fewer tokens) and Entropy Correlation (unproductive behaviors correlate with turn-level entropy).

Result: Extensive experiments on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Multilingual demonstrate that HERO achieves a favorable trade-off between resolution rate and token efficiency compared to state-of-the-art coding agents and reinforcement learning methods.

Conclusion: Training token-efficient coding agents is a viable and highly-practical approach. HERO successfully balances resolution performance with token efficiency through reinforcement learning that prioritizes task resolution while encouraging efficient reasoning patterns at multiple levels.

Abstract: Recently, coding agents have emerged as a dominant paradigm for real-world software engineering (SWE) scenarios, which solve complex tasks through multi-turn interactions with development environments. However, frequent interactions with environments would inevitably introduce substantial token overhead, leading to high usage costs and latency. Although recent studies have explored reducing token usage by context manipulation and interaction limits at inference time, these approaches focus on improving token efficiency while overlooking the risk of discarding task-relevant information, thus struggling to balance the trade-off between resolution rate and token efficiency. In this paper, we study a more general paradigm without suffering from the limitation, i.e., training token-efficient coding agents with promising resolution performance, which is a highly-practical yet less-explored problem. To this end, we reveal two core observations in SWE scenarios: i) Efficiency Variation: successful resolution could be achieved with fewer tokens; ii) Entropy Correlation: unproductive behaviors are associated with turn-level entropy. Motivated by observations, we propose a novel reinforcement learning framework, dubbed HERO. Specifically, HERO prioritizes task resolution over token efficiency during policy optimization and encourages efficient reasoning patterns at both trajectory and turn levels. Extensive experiments on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Multilingual demonstrate that HERO achieves a favorable trade-off between resolution rate and token efficiency compared with state-of-the-art coding agents and reinforcement learning methods.

</details>


### [184] [From Verification Failures to Reusable Guidance for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39022)
*Yuqing Zhai,Xiaohong Chen,Lingming Zhang,Sriram Vishwanath,Grigore Rosu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a framework combining the K framework with verification procedures to help coding agents establish program correctness against specifications. The approach achieves 100% success rate (164/164) on HumanEval benchmark and successfully identifies defects in author-reviewed packages through AI auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents need to verify that programs satisfy specifications and that specifications capture intended behavior. The challenge is creating reusable guidance for verification work when proofs fail, requiring expert diagnosis of verification failures.

Method: The approach combines: (1) Executable language definitions in the K framework, (2) A kit of procedures for constructing specifications, repairing proofs, and auditing adequacy. The method involves human-guided development campaigns with targeted repairs, tested on HumanEval (164 Python tasks), 12 author-reviewed clean/defective package pairs, and KleverBench (31 programs with changed operator meanings). Human-reviewed Optimism proofs tested pause reverts under London semantics.

Result: Key results: (1) 164/164 success rate on HumanEval with K semantics and the kit, verified by AI audit Pass verdicts after two targeted repairs; (2) All 12 defects identified and all clean packages accepted in audit tests; (3) Mixed results on KleverBench comparing acceptance rules with generic advice across two model and budget settings; (4) Established expected pause reverts for six operations within input bounds under London semantics with unbounded gas.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates that combining executable semantic definitions with verification procedure kits can enable agents to deliver programs with checkable correctness arguments. However, further work is needed on selecting useful guidance within resource limits, as comparisons with generic advice yielded mixed results.

Abstract: Coding agents need to establish that a program satisfies a specification and that the specification captures the requested behavior. We study how expert diagnosis of verification failures can become reusable guidance for this work. Our approach combines executable language definitions in the K framework with a kit of procedures for constructing specifications, repairing proofs, and auditing their adequacy. A human-guided development campaign on HumanEval, a benchmark of 164 Python programming tasks, achieves a 164/164 success rate with the semantics and the kit, measured by final AI audit Pass verdicts after two targeted repairs. To examine whether auditing detects problems that successful proofs leave unresolved, we construct 12 author-reviewed pairs of clean and defective packages. Every package passes its K proofs, and completed audits identify all defects and accept all clean packages. We then use KleverBench to test specification and proof construction for 31 programs with changed operator meanings. Comparisons with complete acceptance rules and equally long generic advice yield mixed results across two model and budget settings, motivating further work on selecting useful guidance within resource limits. Human-reviewed Optimism proofs establish expected pause reverts for six operations within declared input bounds under London semantics with unbounded gas. We report progress, difficulties, and lessons toward agents that deliver programs with checkable correctness arguments.

</details>


### [185] [Trustworthy Runtime Error Healing in Real-World Repositories: A Benchmark and Guardrail](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39086)
*Gou Tan,Pengfei Chen,Zhensu Sun,Jieke Shi,Junkai Chen,Ting Zhang,Weifeng Sun,Junda He,Shuai Liang,Chuanfu Zhang,Lwin Khin Shar,David Lo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper advances LLM-based runtime error healing from small competition programs to real-world repositories. It introduces HealBench (265 errors from 18 repositories) and HealGuard (a safety system using static/dynamic taint analysis to prevent healing code from reaching protected operations). The best configuration achieves 38.11% resume rate and 28.68% test pass rate, but 17.4% of successful heals may have unsafe side effects. HealGuard detects all unsafe cases but with 68.42% false positive rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior LLM-based runtime healing work only evaluated on small competition programs and lacks safety mechanisms for executing LLM-generated code in live processes. This work aims to bring runtime healing to practical use in real-world repositories.

Method: Build HealBench benchmark with 265 runtime errors from 18 real-world repositories, each with reference execution on patched version. Design HealGuard requiring healing code in HealCore (an analyzable Python subset) with static and dynamic taint analysis to check if healing-induced state changes reach protected operations. Evaluate dedicated healing method and three general coding agents with three backbone LLMs.

Result: Best setting achieves 38.11% resume rate and 28.68% target test pass rate. Among passing executions, HealGuard flags 17.4% where healing-changed state may reach protected operations. HealGuard detects all unsafe cases (on 684 controlled cases) at cost of 68.42% false positive rate.

Conclusion: Existing LLM agents can already heal meaningful share of real repository-level crashes, but safety concerns remain significant. HealGuard provides safety guarantees but needs improvement to reduce false positives before practical deployment.

Abstract: Runtime error healing lets a crashed program continue by generating code that repairs its live runtime state. Recent work shows that LLMs can generate such healing code, but it is evaluated only on small competition programs, and executing LLM-generated code inside a live process raises safety concerns that remain unaddressed. In this paper, we take LLM-based runtime healing toward practical use in real-world repositories. We first build HealBench, a benchmark of 265 runtime errors from 18 real-world repositories, each paired with a reference execution on the patched version. HealBench also provides a unified framework that lets LLM agents heal with cross-file context and live runtime state. We then design HealGuard, which requires healing code to be written in HealCore, an analyzable subset of Python, and uses static and dynamic taint analysis to check whether state changed by healing reaches operations protected by developers. We evaluate a dedicated healing method and three general coding agents with three backbone LLMs. The best setting resumes execution in 38.11% of instances and passes the target test in 28.68%, showing that existing agents can already heal a meaningful share of real repository-level crashes. However, among executions that pass, HealGuard flags 17.4% whose healing-changed state may reach a protected operation. On 684 controlled cases, HealGuard detects all unsafe cases, at the cost of a 68.42% false positive rate.

</details>


### [186] [EngramBench: A Capability-Grounded Benchmark for Skill-Evolution Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39284)
*Zhixuan Tan,Pengjie Gu,Zhao Li,Yihan Hu,Xu He,Dong Li,Jianye Hao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: EngramBench is a new benchmark that solves the identifiability problem in autonomous agent evaluation by ensuring capability overlap without solution overlap. It comprises 30 learning tasks and 13 unseen transfer tasks, challenging agents with multi-hour development cycles with LLM-simulated users. The study reveals that while static skill banks cannot bypass the "last mile" of exact code implementation (bottlenecked by base model's reasoning limits), they serve as crucial execution compass that reduces redundant context bloat and cuts coding time by over 55%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of skill evolution in autonomous agents suffer from a critical identifiability problem: they structurally confound genuine capability abstraction with rote solution leakage (copying highly similar code from historical training data). There was a need to distinguish authentic capability transfer from trivial pattern matching.

Method: EngramBench is introduced as a rigorous, capability-grounded benchmark governed by the strict axiom of capability overlap without solution overlap. It comprises 30 diverse learning tasks and 13 unseen transfer tasks, challenging agents to navigate interactive, multi-hour development cycles driven by LLM-simulated users. Evaluation covers 48 multi-hour execution trajectories, corroborated by human-expert validation.

Result: 1) Static skill banks do not magically bypass the "last mile" of exact code implementation, which remains bottlenecked by the base model's inherent reasoning limits. 2) However, they serve as an indispensable execution compass by navigating agents away from catastrophic, token-heavy trial-and-error. 3) Genuine capability abstraction slashes redundant context bloat and reduces overall coding time by over 55%.

Conclusion: EngramBench shifts the evaluation paradigm from trivial pattern matching to the verifiable measurement of deep, cross-domain capability transfer. The benchmark demonstrates the importance of distinguishing between genuine capability abstraction and solution leakage in evaluating autonomous software engineering agents.

Abstract: While large language models have achieved remarkable success in isolated code generation, authentic software engineering requires sustained reasoning, complex state management, and continuous cross-domain abstraction. However, current evaluations of skill evolution in autonomous agents suffer from a critical identifiability problem: they structurally confound genuine capability abstraction with rote solution leakage (i.e., copying highly similar code from historical training data). To resolve this, we introduce EngramBench, a rigorous, capability-grounded benchmark governed by the strict axiom of capability overlap without solution overlap. Comprising 30 diverse learning tasks and 13 unseen transfer tasks, EngramBench challenges agents to navigate interactive, multi-hour development cycles driven by LLM-simulated users. Our extensive evaluation across 48 multi-hour execution trajectories -- corroborated by human-expert validation -- reveals a profound insight into procedural memory. We demonstrate that static skill banks do not magically bypass the "last mile" of exact code implementation, which remains bottlenecked by the base model's inherent reasoning limits. However, they serve as an indispensable execution compass. By navigating agents away from catastrophic, token-heavy trial-and-error, genuine capability abstraction slashes redundant context bloat and reduces overall coding time by over 55%. Ultimately, EngramBench shifts the evaluation paradigm from trivial pattern matching to the verifiable measurement of deep, cross-domain capability transfer.

</details>


### [187] [Self-Spec Verifiable Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39568)
*Jiaru Qian,Yihong Dong,Yongmin Li,Hao Zhu,Bin Gu,Ge Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VeriCodeBench is a benchmark for self-spec verifiable code generation, where LLMs must generate both specifications and code without oracle guidance. It contains 400 problems across C, Java, Rust, and Python. The paper also introduces CodeNova, a method using constraint-guided specification and verifier feedback to improve LLM performance. Experiments show self-generated specifications are a major bottleneck, and CodeNova substantially improves performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for evaluating LLMs in generating formally verifiable code have two key limitations: (1) They evaluate specification and code generation stage-wise with oracle specifications, missing end-to-end success evaluation; (2) They focus on single proof-oriented languages and mathematically structured tasks, lacking coverage of common software development tasks.

Method: The paper introduces VeriCodeBench, a benchmark with 400 language-native problems across C, Java, Rust, and Python covering practical software development concerns. It evaluates specification coverage, code validity, and joint problem-level success. The paper also proposes CodeNova, which makes requirements explicit through constraint-guided specification and uses verifier feedback to guide targeted implementation repairs.

Result: Self-generated specifications remain a major bottleneck. Providing more sophisticated specifications may not necessarily lead to higher verification success rates. CodeNova substantially improves performance across all evaluation metrics, enabling Claude Sonnet 5 to achieve the strongest results under the self-spec protocol.

Conclusion: The self-spec verifiable code generation task remains challenging, with specification generation being the primary bottleneck. CodeNova effectively addresses this challenge by guiding LLMs through constraint-based specification and iterative verification, significantly improving end-to-end success rates.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) may generate unreliable code on corner cases missed by testing, while formal verification can provide machine-checkable guarantees. Recently, researchers have proposed several benchmarks to evaluate the capabilities of LLMs in generating formally verifiable code, where LLMs need to formulate formal specifications, generate the corresponding code, and verify its correctness. However, existing benchmarks have two key limitations: (I) They primarily evaluate specification and code generation stage-wise, with code generation typically conditioned on an oracle specification. This setup overlooks whether strong stage-wise performance translates into end-to-end success. (II)They mainly focus on a single proof-oriented language and mathematically structured tasks, offering limited coverage of tasks common in software development. In this paper, we introduce VeriCodeBench, a benchmark for self-spec verifiable code generation, where the LLM relies solely on its own generated specification and code throughout the entire process. VeriCodeBench contains 400 language-native problems across C, Java, Rust, and Python, covering practical concerns in software development. We evaluate specification coverage, code validity, and joint problem-level success. We further introduce CodeNova to enhance the capabilities of LLMs in self-spec verifiable code generation. CodeNova makes requirements explicit through constraint-guided specification and uses verifier feedback to guide targeted implementation repairs. Experimental results reveal that self-generated specifications remain a major bottleneck, while providing more sophisticated specifications may not necessarily lead to higher verification success rates. CodeNova substantially improves performance across all evaluation metrics, enabling Claude Sonnet 5 to achieve the strongest results under the self-spec protocol.

</details>


### [188] [COMPASS: Predicting the Relationship of Multiple Patches for Vulnerabilities with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39783)
*Yi Song,Dongchen Xie,Xiaoyuan Xie,He Zhang,Lin Xu,Chunying Zhou,Zhi Jin*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: COMPASS is an automated approach using large language models to predict relationships between multiple vulnerability patches for the same CVE. It addresses the challenge that downstream maintainers face when manually adopting patches, as different patch relationships (Merge, Mirror, Better Solution, Fixing-of-Fixing, Collaboration, Separation) require different adoption strategies. The method achieves 85.04% average improvement over state-of-the-art approaches on a benchmark of 300 multi-patch CVEs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern software relies heavily on code reuse, but upstream vulnerability fixes don't automatically propagate to downstream codebases. Downstream maintainers must manually adopt patches to eliminate known risks. The key challenge is that a single vulnerability often corresponds to multiple patches, and different patch relationships imply different adoption strategies, making patch adoption complex and error-prone.

Method: COMPASS follows a four-phase pipeline: (i) identifies the patch group and pre-scans explicit relationships, (ii) performs individual patch analysis, (iii) infers relationship instances via a hierarchy-guided prompt, and (iv) validates completeness and consistency of inferred results. The approach was developed based on manual inspection of approximately 1,000 multi-patch vulnerabilities in the real world and interviews with experienced developers, identifying six typical patch relationship types.

Result: COMPASS achieves strong and consistent prediction effectiveness and outperforms state-of-the-art methods by 85.04% on average on a benchmark of 300 multi-patch CVEs. The approach successfully predicts and visualizes patch relationships as a relationship graph.

Conclusion: This work provides an automated solution for predicting vulnerability patch relationships, significantly improving the efficiency of downstream patch adoption. The public release of an online querying website enables community reuse of patch relationship knowledge, potentially reducing security risks in software supply chains.

Abstract: Modern software heavily relies on code reuse, so upstream vulnerability fixes do not automatically propagate to downstream codebases. Downstream maintainers must manually adopt patches to eliminate known risks. In practice, a single vulnerability often corresponds to multiple patches, which greatly complicates downstream patch adoption because different patch relationships imply different adoption strategies. To address this challenge, we first manually inspect large-scale multi-patch vulnerabilities (about 1K) in the real world and interview experienced developers, summarizing six typical types of patch relationships, i.e., Merge, Mirror, Better Solution, Fixing-of-Fixing, Collaboration, and Separation. Based on these observations, we propose COMPASS, an automated approach that predicts the relationships of multiple vulnerability patches with large language models. Given a CVE as input, COMPASS follows a four-phase pipeline that (i) identifies the patch group and pre-scans explicit relationships, (ii) performs individual patch analysis, (iii) infers relationship instances via a hierarchy-guided prompt, and (iv) validates completeness and consistency of the inferred results. As output, COMPASS reports the predicted relationships within the patch group and visualizes them as a relationship graph. We evaluate COMPASS on a benchmark of 300 multi-patch CVEs and compare it against mainstream learning-based and LLM baselines. Results show that our method achieves strong and consistent prediction effectiveness and outperforms SOTA by 85.04% on average. We publicly release an online querying website to support community reuse of patch relationships knowledge: https://patch-relation.com.

</details>


### [189] [DoGBench: Can Agents Meet Expert Standards for User-Facing Documentation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39909)
*Frances Liu,Manny Silva,Paige Calvert,Ayu Adiati,Sarah Sanders*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DoGBENCH is the first benchmark for generating and maintaining real user-facing software documentation, containing 292 items from open source projects. It evaluates whether agents can produce documentation that experienced technical writers would accept, scoring on accuracy, completeness, reader guidance, placement, and repository conventions. The highest-scoring agent achieved 47.3/100, with common failures including task-completion gaps (45.5%), technical inaccuracies (36.6%), and incomplete coverage (32.5%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for documentation tasks focus on isolated skills like code summarization or API documentation generation, missing the full complexity of real-world documentation maintenance. This benchmark addresses the need to evaluate whether AI agents can produce documentation that meets professional standards in realistic scenarios.

Method: The benchmark contains 292 items from open source projects (Helm, PostHog, Mautic). Agents receive a pre-change repository and a trigger (code PR or documentation gap). They must decide whether documentation needs updating, then produce an acceptable patch or abstain. Task-specific rubrics validated with project maintainers score each patch on five dimensions. Seven agents were evaluated on a 117-item held-out split, with an audit of 1,267 patches identifying failure patterns.

Result: The highest-scoring agent achieved 47.3 out of 100. Analysis of 1,267 patches revealed three key failure modes: (1) describing interfaces without examining reader usage (36.0%), (2) missing decisive evidence and filling gaps with plausible assumptions (33.1%), and (3) stopping after finding first plausible documentation surface and leaving other pages stale (30.1%).

Conclusion: Current AI agents are far from meeting professional documentation standards. Key challenges include understanding actual reader needs, gathering sufficient evidence before documenting, and comprehensively covering all affected documentation. The benchmark provides a rigorous framework for evaluating documentation generation capabilities in real-world scenarios.

Abstract: We introduce DoGBENCH (Documentation Generation Benchmark), to our knowledge, the first benchmark for generating and maintaining real user-facing software documentation. It asks whether an agent can produce documentation that experienced technical writers would accept in review. The benchmark contains 292 items from open source projects, including Helm, PostHog, and Mautic. Each item gives the agent a pre-change repository and a trigger, such as a code pull request or a reported documentation gap. The agent must first decide whether the documentation needs an update. For items that need one, the agent must produce an acceptable patch in one attempt. For items that do not need updates, the agent must abstain. Task-specific rubrics, validated with project maintainers, score each patch on accuracy, completeness, reader guidance, placement, and repository conventions. The composite score combines patch quality with correct abstention, and a score of 100 means an agent meets every requirement for the task. Scores should not be interpreted as a percentage of an expert's capability. We evaluated seven agents. The highest-scoring agent reached 47.3 out of 100 on the 117-item held-out split. In a separate audit of 1,267 patches, the most common failure modes were task-completion gaps (45.5%), technical inaccuracies (36.6%), and incomplete conceptual or reference coverage (32.5%). Analysis of the corresponding trajectories identified three key patterns associated with these failures: (1) describing interfaces without examining how readers use them (36.0%), (2) missing decisive evidence and filling the gaps with plausible assumptions (33.1%), and (3) stopping after finding the first plausible documentation surface and leaving other affected pages stale (30.1%).

</details>


### [190] [Learning When and How to Intervene: A Hindsight-Distilled Sentinel for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39957)
*Jiangrui Zhao,Chenglong Li,Meng Zhang,Xiaoting Du*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: HiSentinel is a hindsight-distillation framework that trains lightweight sentinel models (0.6B and 1.7B) to make pre-execution intervention decisions for coding agents, preventing error propagation in repository-level tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In repository-level coding tasks, a single erroneous action can misdirect subsequent decisions and increase recovery costs. Existing approaches use execution feedback for recovery or specialized checks to block errors, but determining whether pre-execution intervention will benefit task completion remains challenging.

Method: HiSentinel uses a privileged teacher that leverages recorded execution outcomes as evidence for intervention judgments, which are distilled into a causal student that receives only pre-action context and proposed action. The system introduces SWE-Intervene, an action-level dataset annotating whether actions should be allowed, autonomously redirected, or paused for human assistance, with corresponding intervention feedback.

Result: HiSentinel consistently improves task completion across different sentinel scales and coding-agent families, with gains of up to 14% on SWE-bench Verified Mini and 10% on Ask or Assume, while maintaining competitive token consumption.

Conclusion: Lightweight pre-execution intervention can effectively prevent error propagation and improve the reliability of autonomous coding agents, demonstrating that selective intervention is more efficient than correcting every imperfect action.

Abstract: Coding agents solve repository-level tasks through sequences of actions, where a single erroneous action can misdirect subsequent decisions and increase recovery costs. Existing approaches use execution feedback for recovery or specialized checks to block errors, but deciding before execution whether intervention will benefit eventual task completion remains challenging. To address this challenge, we propose HiSentinel, a hindsight-distillation framework that trains lightweight 0.6B and 1.7B sentinels to select pre-execution interventions aimed at improving task completion rather than correcting every imperfect action. A privileged teacher uses recorded execution outcomes as evidence for intervention judgments, which are distilled into a causal student that receives only the pre-action context and proposed action. Beyond identifying whether and when to intervene, the sentinel must also provide actionable feedback that helps the coding agent recover or obtain necessary human input. To support these capabilities, we introduce SWE-Intervene, an action-level dataset constructed from software-engineering trajectories that annotates whether an action should be allowed, autonomously redirected, or paused for human assistance, together with corresponding intervention feedback. Across SWE-bench Verified Mini and Ask or Assume, HiSentinel consistently improves task completion across Sentinel scales and coding-agent families, with gains of up to 14% and 10%, respectively, while maintaining competitive token consumption. These results demonstrate that lightweight pre-execution intervention can effectively prevent error propagation and improve the reliability of autonomous coding agents.

</details>


### [191] [Leveraging Game-Based Platform to Teach Code Refactoring: An Experience with Refactoria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40086)
*Eman Abdullah AlOmar,Christopher Engelbart,Stephen Pachucki,Nerissa Lundquist,David Van Hist*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents Refactoria, a game-based educational tool for teaching code smell identification and refactoring concepts. The experiment involved 30 student developers who used the tool to learn refactoring skills through gamification, with positive results in perceived usefulness and understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses two key problems: 1) the difficulty in acquiring skills for identifying and refactoring code smells due to the need for training and experience, and 2) the lack of motivation that hinders developers from adopting refactoring tools in practice.

Method: The researchers conducted a classroom experiment with 30 student developers. Participants used Refactoria, a game-based tool where players act as an expert chef with a sidekick "Watson the Whiskbot" to refactor code instructions into efficient, readable, and maintainable code. The study evaluated perception, effectiveness, and usefulness of gamification for code smell identification and refactoring.

Result: The evaluation showed high perceived usefulness among students. Participants reported that Refactoria facilitated their understanding of refactoring concepts.

Conclusion: Gamification through Refactoria is effective for engaging developers in code smell identification and refactoring activities. The game-based approach successfully improved students' understanding of refactoring concepts, suggesting potential for broader adoption in software engineering education.

Abstract: Refactoring is the art of improving the internal structure of the code without altering its external behavior. Because of the topic's significance, several teaching methods and strategies have been proposed in the literature. However, skills in identifying and refac- toring code smells come from training and experience, and a lack of motivation may hinder developers' adoption of refactoring tools. In this paper, we discuss the results of an experiment in the classroom that involved performing various refactoring activities to remove antipatterns using Refactoria, an innovative game-based tool that supports the acquisition of code smell and refactoring concepts. The players play as an expert chef with their sidekick Watson the Whiskbot to refactor Watson's instructions into efficient, readable, and easily maintainable code. We present an experiment with 30 student developers. In particular, we study the perception, effectiveness, and usefulness of gamification for engaging developers in code smell identification and refactoring. Our evaluation indicates a high perceived usefulness among students, and participants reported that Refactoria facilitated their understanding of the refactoring concepts.

</details>
